--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page is the title page of the volume, following the blank protective flyleaves previously translated. It introduces a collection of medical treatises by the Flemish physician and chemist Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of "iatrochemistry" who sought to explain bodily functions through chemical processes.</meta>

# JOHN BAPTIST
## VAN
# HELMONT,

->*Lord of Royenborch,*<- <note>The title "Toparcha" refers to a local ruler or lord of a specific territory.</note>
->*Pellines, etc.*<-

## MEDICAL TREATISES
### UNPRECEDENTED. <note>original: "inaudita" — This translates to "unheard of," signaling to the reader that Helmont’s theories were revolutionary and challenged the established medical wisdom of his time.</note>

---

->I. **ON THE STONE.**<- <note>Referring to "lithiasis," the formation of stones in the kidney or bladder, which Helmont studied extensively from a chemical perspective.</note>
->II. **ON FEVERS.**<-
->III. **ON THE HUMORS OF GALEN.**<- <note>A critique of the ancient Greek physician Galen, whose theory of the "four humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) dominated medicine for over a millennium until challenged by thinkers like Helmont.</note>
->IV. **ON THE PLAGUE.**<-

---

<summary>The title page for a collection of four medical treatises by Jan Baptist van Helmont, published posthumously in Cologne in 1644. The work covers revolutionary chemical theories on kidney stones, fevers, the plague, and a rejection of ancient Greek medical traditions.</summary>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and features a dedicatory poem by Jan Walhorn. The catchword "DOCTRI-" from the OCR likely refers to the beginning of the "Doctrine" or main text on the subsequent page. This page serves as a polemical introduction, framing Helmont as a savior of medicine compared to the "deadly" traditions of Galen.</meta>

*Regarding the Noble & most distinguished Lord*

# THE WORKS
## OF J. B. VAN HELMONT
## Against the Galenists <note>The Galenists were physicians who followed the ancient Greek doctor Galen. They believed illness was caused by an imbalance of four "humors" and relied heavily on bloodletting and purging—practices Helmont vehemently opposed.</note>

->A Poem<-

*By the Noble and most Distinguished Lord*

# JAN WALHORN, DOCTOR,
## Counselor to His Majesty.

> Close your schools, Galen, there are enough of the slain,
> Oh, now it is enough: the full graves cry out!
> For blood and the <term>clyster</term> <note>An enema, a standard treatment in 17th-century medicine used to "purge" the body.</note> are your only medicines; often
> You provide nothing: your only hope lies in the <term>critical day</term>. <note>Galenic doctors believed that diseases followed a set calendar and that a "crisis" or turning point would occur on specific days (like the 7th or 14th); Helmont argued this was an excuse for doing nothing while the patient suffered.</note>
> Meanwhile, in the feeling of the pulse, and the scorched tongue,
> You are entirely dazed by urine and excrement. <note>Traditional doctors diagnosed patients primarily by examining their waste and their pulse, which Helmont found insufficient for true healing.</note>
> This does not help the sick man: Medicine must be prepared for him.
> There is no need for witnesses of the disease, but for a Doctor.
> Yet you expect a great reward after the funeral:
> Thus the dog watches, and loves, the sick cattle.
> Only Helmont is able, through the <term>Apolline art</term>, <note>Referring to Apollo, the Greek and Roman god of healing and light.</note>
> To rescue those whom the other had left for death!

<summary>A promotional and polemical poem by Jan Walhorn that harshly criticizes traditional Galenic medicine as ineffective and deadly, presenting Jan Baptist van Helmont as the only physician capable of truly saving patients.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Baptist van Helmont, Galenism, Jan Walhorn, medical polemic, iatrochemistry, clyster, critical days, Apolline art, 17th-century medicine, medical satire</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page is the primary title page for a specific treatise within Van Helmont's collected works. Following the previous page’s polemical poem, which established Helmont's opposition to traditional Galenic medicine, this page introduces his "unheard-of" theories on "Lithiasis" (stone disease) and neurological conditions. It serves as a formal entry point into his radical chemical approach to medicine.</meta>

# UNHEARD-OF DOCTRINE,
## On the causes, the manner of formation, the contents, the root, and the resolution
# OF STONE DISEASE <note>original: "Lithiasis". This refers to the formation of stony concretions within the body, most commonly kidney or bladder stones, which was a major cause of suffering and death in the 17th century.</note>.

<margin>1</margin>

->And also<-
->On sense, sensation, pain, lack of feeling, stupor, motion, and immobility.<-

->*Just as*<-
->On diseases of this class: Leprosy, the Falling Sickness <note>original: "Caduco". An early medical name for epilepsy, so-called because the patient "falls" during a seizure.</note>, Apoplexy <note>A historical term for a stroke.</note>, Paralysis, Spasm, Coma, etc.<-

->*Everything here is new and, until now, paradoxical.*<-

### *A treatise equally useful to the Physicist and the Physician, as to the <term>Spagyrist</term> <note>A Spagyrist was an alchemist-physician who followed the principles of Paracelsus, using chemical processes like distillation and extraction to create potent medicines from minerals and plants.</note>: but most useful of all to the sufferer.*

->BY THE AUTHOR<-
## JAN BAPTIST van HELMONT,
->*of Brussels.*<-

---

<summary>This is the title page for Jan Baptist van Helmont’s groundbreaking medical treatise on kidney stones and neurological disorders, published in Cologne in 1644. It frames his chemical and physical theories as radical, "unheard-of" alternatives to the traditional medical knowledge of his time.</summary>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page immediately follows the title page of Van Helmont’s treatise on Lithiasis. The catchword "Illustri" on the previous page's footer suggests this glossary serves as a bridge to the formal dedication or the main body of the text, providing the reader with a "cheat sheet" for Van Helmont’s notoriously difficult chemical vocabulary.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF SEVERAL
## technical terms.

1. **T**he *Alkahest* <note>original: "Altahest". This is Van Helmont’s famous universal solvent, capable of reducing any material to its liquid "prime matter."</note> liquor of Paracelsus resolves every visible body into its primary matter, while the power of its seeds is preserved. Of this, the Chemists say:
> The common folk burn by fire; we burn by water. <note>original: "Vulgus cremat per ignem, nos per aquam." This refers to the alchemical belief that liquid solvents are more "natural" and less destructive than the harsh heat of a furnace.</note>

2. The *Archeus* <note>In Helmontian medicine, the Archeus is the "internal chemist" or vital spirit that governs the organs and directs growth.</note> of Paracelsus is the vital aura, the director of seeds and of life, and the **impelling force** <note>original: "τὸ ἔνορμον" (to enormon), a term used by Hippocrates to describe the fundamental principle of life and motion.</note> of Hippocrates.

3. *Blas*—for lack of a better root-word, I call by this name the power of motion, both that which alters a thing and that which moves it from place to place. <note>Van Helmont coined the term "Blas" to describe the astral or internal pulses that drive natural phenomena, like the weather or the human heartbeat.</note>

4. *Duelech* of Paracelsus is the proper name for the human stone <note>original: "calculi".</note>. For "stone" is merely a metaphor.

5. *Gas* is a spirit that cannot be solidified, such as that which comes from fermenting wine, and also that red spirit which is belched out when <term>gold-dissolving water</term> <note>original: "chrysulca". This is likely nitric acid or aqua regia.</note> is at work, etc. <note>Van Helmont is credited with inventing the word "Gas," derived from the Greek "Chaos."</note>

6. *Magnale* is a sheath in the air, a created thing that is midway between a body and a non-body.

7. *The Great Necessity* <note>original: "Magnum oportet."</note>. This is the thistle and thorn of the earth, found in the "middle life" of man, concerning which there is a separate treatise.

8. *Leffas* of Paracelsus is the juice of the earth, recently drawn into the root, which acts as the vegetable's kitchen.

9. *Zenexton* of Paracelsus is an amulet used against the plague.

10. *Vigo’s Powder* <note>original: "Pulvis Vigonis". Named after Giovanni de Vigo, a 16th-century surgeon. This powder, often containing mercury, was a standard treatment for wounds and sores.</note>, well known to barbers.

11. *The Element of the Fire of Venus* is an oil made from the sulfur of copper.

12. *Gold-dissolving water* <note>original: "Aqua chrysulca".</note> and *Royal water* <note>original: "regis", i.e., Aqua Regia.</note> is <term>strong water</term> <note>original: "aqua fortis", or nitric acid.</note>, and this same water "married" with nearly a quarter-part of sal ammoniac.

13. *Horizontal Gold* is gold that has the correct weight, but has not yet become yellow.

14. *Diaceltateffon* of Paracelsus is the common <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum vivum", or mercury.</note> solidified in the Alkahest and tinted with the "water of eggs." It becomes a coral-like secret remedy <note>original: "arcanum".</note> possessing the essence and condition of "goldness," because it is also "horizontal."

15. *Relolleum* of Paracelsus is a quality that does not contain a "seminal being" within itself—such as the basic qualities of the elements, or the color and "signature" of simple things.

The rest of the less common words are either medical, or are at least described and explained in the Author’s present text, and are thus accessible to the reader.

<summary>A glossary of technical and alchemical terms used by Van Helmont, defining key concepts like Gas, Archeus, and Alkahest. It serves as an essential guide for readers to navigate his "unheard-of" chemical philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Alkahest, Archeus, Blas, Gas, Duelech, Paracelsus, chemical medicine, glossary, alchemical terms, vitalism</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the technical glossary. Having defined his unique chemical vocabulary, Van Helmont now addresses his patron, Baron Caspar Uldaric, setting a somber tone by recounting the death of the Baron's uncle—an event Van Helmont uses to critique the medical establishment of his day.</meta>

->To the Illustrious Man,<-
->L O R D<-
# CASPAR ULDARIC,
->Baron of the Holy Roman Empire of Hoens-<-
->broeck, Commander of the Teutonic Order<-
->in Gemert,<-
->Patron of the liberal arts,<-
->His singular friend and Patron.<-

*Y*ou remember, *Illustrious Lord,* that *Lord Werner Spies von Bullesheim, Provincial Commander of the Bailiwick of Koblenz* <note>original: "Confluentiæ"</note> *of the Teutonic Order, and Commander of the house of Pitzenburg in Mechelen* <note>original: "Mechliniæ"</note>, **Lord in Elsen and Herrn-Mülheim, etc., formerly your uncle and the most favoring of my friends, sent his litter to me three days before his death, because he was bedridden with a terrible tertian fever** <note>A fever that recurs every third day, historically associated with malaria.</note>; **and when I had arrived, he still greeted me with a nod, and gestured toward an embrace with both arms: I wanted to help him immediately, because his intermittent pulse commanded haste: but his attendants** <note>original: "suos"</note> **had delayed the promised aid until the afternoon, so that the physicians might be present. When they had explained their work—that for 13 days now they had opened a vein twice** <note>The practice of bloodletting, intended to balance the "humors."</note>, **and purged him as many times, while nourishing him with broths and whey: and finally had strengthened him with confections of Alkermes** <note>A famous medicinal cordial made from the kermes insect, pearls, and spices, used for heart health.</note> **and Hyacinths** <note>A cordial made from crushed gemstones, believed to possess cooling and restorative powers.</note>: **therefore they intended to proceed in the same track, except that finally his legs and arms were to be blistered with Spanish flies** <note>original: "Cantharidibus". These beetles were used to raise blisters on the skin to "draw out" harmful fluids.</note>. **But I responded: "You see, my friends, how much..."**

<summary>Van Helmont dedicates his work to Baron Caspar Uldaric, beginning with a narrative about the death of the Baron’s uncle, Werner Spies. He describes how the patient was subjected to aggressive traditional treatments like bloodletting and blistering, which Van Helmont implies were ineffective or harmful.</summary>
<keywords>Dedication, Caspar Uldaric von Hoensbroeck, Werner Spies von Bullesheim, Teutonic Order, medical practice, tertian fever, bloodletting, Alkermes, Spanish fly, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s address to Baron Caspar Uldaric. On the previous page, Van Helmont had arrived to find his friend, the Baron's uncle, being treated with traditional Galenic remedies like bloodletting and cooling cordials. He had just begun to question the attending physicians about the effectiveness of their treatments.</meta>

...[I asked whether] those remedies had given, increased, or left behind any hope. Wherefore, if you continue on this same path, tomorrow will provide the horrors and the agony of death as the epilogue to this tragedy. I beg you, let me have at least five hours; and even today it will be seen whether that Illustrious man, who is most friendly to me, ordered me to be summoned yesterday in vain. They easily agreed, except for one man, **Fonseca** <note>Rodrigo de Castro Fonseca, a prominent physician of the era who adhered to traditional methods.</note>, perhaps because he was Portuguese <note>original: "Lusitanus"</note>; he despised **chemical remedies** <term>chemical remedies: medicines derived from alchemy and mineral processing, which were controversial compared to traditional herbal "Galenic" mixtures</term> on the grounds that they were "fiery," and would only pour oil on a fire. And so, by the vote of this one man, that knight met his death. For although priest friends and noble household members were present, they hoped more for the usual ways and the opinion of the majority than for remedies not yet known to them. 

And so he began to be missed by good men; for you were away while these things were happening. For indeed, just as righteous indignation gives birth to poetry <note>A reference to the Roman poet Juvenal's phrase "facit indignatio versum," suggesting that anger at injustice inspires creativity.</note>, so, provoked by these ignorant men, I decided to publish a little book of unheard-of doctrine concerning **fevers**. And timely enough, **Cardinal Ferdinand** <note>Infante Ferdinand of Austria (1609–1641), the Governor of the Spanish Netherlands and brother to King Philip IV of Spain.</note>, the brother of our King, was extinguished by his Portuguese **chief physicians** <term>chief physicians: "Archiatris," the highest-ranking medical officials at court</term> through excessive bloodletting and immoderate cooling. I had predicted in writing to his Carmelite confessor that this would happen. 

Afterward, following his death, the matter was disputed in a legal controversy. The debate was won by **Fortunatus Vopiscus Plempius**, a most learned Dutchman <note>original: "Batavus"</note> and Professor at Leuven <note>original: "Lovanii"</note>. I, however, prefixed a poem to my book, so that it might be clear that it was not so much malice as the brutal and unpunished stupidity of those practitioners. I added, therefore, that no one was ever made immune to fever by the **method of Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon was an ancient Greek physician whose theories on "humors" dominated European medicine for 1,500 years; Van Helmont was a famous critic of this establishment.</note>, so that

<summary>Van Helmont recounts how the rejection of his "chemical" treatments by traditional physicians led to the death of a nobleman and the Governor of the Netherlands, prompting him to write a revolutionary book on fevers.</summary>
<keywords>bloodletting, chemical remedies, Galenism, medical controversy, Fortunatus Vopiscus Plempius, Cardinal Ferdinand, Fonseca, fevers</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of the medical establishment. On the previous page, he described how a prominent patient died because traditional physicians rejected "chemical" remedies in favor of outdated methods. This page contains the "Catchword" (the first word of the next page) "Discans" at the bottom right, and notes the presence of a digital watermark.</meta>

...nor could anyone else who was seriously ill escape, unless the strength of nature snatched them from their hands <note>Van Helmont refers to the hands of the traditional Galenic physicians.</note> in a timely manner. This is because in the **Schools** <term>Schools: the traditional university medical faculties of the 17th century, which relied heavily on ancient Greek and Roman texts rather than experimentation</term>, *both the true nature of fevers and their remedies have remained unknown until now. I therefore published a book to establish this fact; but it was so scattered with errors from the printer that I would blush to acknowledge it as my own. Yet, even as it was, because of the novelty of the subject, it began to be sought after almost as soon as it appeared, for the supply had run out. For I demonstrate that fever is misunderstood and its remedies unknown; likewise, that the theory of the **four humors** <note>The ancient medical theory that health depended on the balance of four bodily fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. Van Helmont was a famous opponent of this view.</note> consists of old wives' tales <note>original: "aniles nugas"</note>, with which gullible mortals are still fattening graveyards today.* 

Therefore, let this be the Problem: Either I myself have erred completely in this matter, or indeed I have not, and it is the **Humorists** <term>Humorists: physicians who followed the traditional theory of the four humors</term> who have erred. The entire Humoristic School has gone to ruin, for the very hinge upon which the doorposts of healing rest now lies broken. Since this matter touches upon life itself, the State <note>original: "Respub." (Respublica)</note>, and most families, I ask the Christian world, out of charity, to watch over the decision of this unexpected and momentous problem. 

I am likewise adding a book in which I have demonstrated that the causes, remedies, and the very manner in which **stones** <note>original: "calculi"; referring to kidney or bladder stones, a common and painful ailment of the era.</note> are formed have been consistently misunderstood until now. I show that the Plague, Apoplexy, Paralysis, Leprosy, Lethargy, Spasms, and that entire class of diseases are equally unknown by the Schools.

*I have written these **Paradoxes** <note>In this context, "paradoxes" refers to statements that go against prevailing medical opinion or "common sense."</note> as a down payment for a promised larger Volume, in which I shall reveal the physical beginnings and new axioms of healing for the public good. Namely, so that the Schools may learn and return to their senses.*

<summary>Van Helmont denounces the traditional "Humoral" medical establishment as fundamentally ignorant of the causes of disease and responsible for many deaths. He introduces his own writings on fevers and kidney stones as the foundation for a new, revolutionary approach to medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Baptist van Helmont, medical reform, four humors, fevers, kidney stones (calculi), Humorists, Galenic medicine, medical paradoxes</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal preface to Joannes Baptista van Helmont’s treatise. It follows the preceding critique of traditional medicine by addressing the reader directly and defending the author's radical new theories on fevers against the inevitable backlash from the academic establishment. The catchword at the bottom of the previous page "Discans" (learning) led into this section, and the catchword here is "sed" (but).</meta>

# TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE
## *Peace, Joy, Knowledge*
## *Wished by the Philosopher by Fire,* <note>original: "Philosophus per Ignem" — This is a common title for a chemist or "iatrochemist" who believes that the secrets of nature and medicine are revealed through laboratory work and the use of heat/fire.</note>
# JOANNES BAPTISTA VAN HELMONT,
### OF BRUSSELS,
### LORD OF ROYENBORCH,
### PELLINES, &c.

I recently published a new teaching on **fevers**. *In which I showed that fever—in its essence, root, properties, and remedy—is unknown to the* **Schools** <term>Schools: the traditional university medical faculties of the 17th century, which relied heavily on ancient Greek and Roman texts like those of Galen rather than modern experimentation</term>. *This matter* affected practitioners of medicine in various ways, and particularly distressed those who refuse to learn. For those who have persuaded themselves that they know enough already, have said: "Will the Academies really endure this insult with impunity? And have so many brilliant minds of men, and we ourselves, been mere logs of wood? Does **Helmont** alone recline at the table of the **Sun** <note>The "Table of the Sun" is a metaphor for divine or natural enlightenment, suggesting Van Helmont claims a unique, inspired source of truth.</note>, so that from those banquets he dares to claim for himself the title of **Adept of Healing** <term>Adept: a term often used in alchemy and early chemistry to describe someone who has attained the highest level of secret or practical knowledge.</term>?" But although my ignorance accompanies my intention in a most poor fashion, and the confession of this does not erase the stain of ignorance: nevertheless, the integrity and sincerity of my intention deserves pardon. Since, in **Healing**, the truth of any person must be judged by the work they leave behind <note>Van Helmont argues that the validity of a medical theory should be proven by its success in curing patients, not by academic debate.</note>. Nor indeed should it be considered slander if the errors of those who came before are uncovered, while suppressing their names. I am dealing with a human and public matter, solely for the sake of charity. If I should say, therefore, that there is still lacking even the first among those who seek the foundations of medicine from the **Pagans** <note>original: "Ethnicis" — Van Helmont frequently refers to the ancient Greeks and Romans as "pagans" to undermine their authority in a Christian society.</note>, who [knows] anything of fevers;

<summary>In this preface, Van Helmont introduces his revolutionary work on fevers and defends himself against the accusations of arrogance from the medical establishment, arguing that medical truth is proven by successful cures rather than ancient tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Joannes Baptista van Helmont, medical reform, fevers, iatrochemistry, Schools of medicine, Adept of Healing, charity in medicine, Galenic critique</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader Who Loves Medicine." On the previous page, Van Helmont discussed the essence of fevers; he now transitions into his justification for criticizing the medical establishment and introduces his specific study on "the stone" (lithiasis).</meta>

*but if he has recognized the root of whatever diseases,*
*and their proper remedies, and I shall have demonstrated that: I*
*am free from blame, nor will I seem injurious. If not, having*
*pitied my ignorance, I pray, let them instruct me: as I believe,*
*I am moved by the sole compassion for the human race, lest*
*anyone hereafter trust his life to unfaithful helpers,*
*who have made no one immune from disease through certain knowledge*
*thus far; but, however many have escaped, they*
*recovered by the bounty of God alone, and by the*
*goodness of nature herself. For this is that paradox which*
*I promise I will demonstrate, and I will stand by my*
*promises. Moreover, I had said in the aforementioned book on fevers, that*
*I owed a treatise also to the stone: because* **Stone-disease** <term>Lithiasis: the formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</term>*, like a monster, and therefore must be separated into a unique stable.*
*For indeed other diseases are born nowhere except within us:*
*but the* **stone** <term>calculus: a medical "stone" or pebble formed within an organ.</term> *alone grows even in the* **chamber pot** <note>original: "matula" — Van Helmont points out that unlike other diseases, the "stone" continues to solidify or grow even after being expelled from the body, proving its unique mineral-like nature.</note>*. It indeed turns to stone, as a product*
*of universal nature, but, insofar as it is modified, it*
*grows: and so that it becomes a medical stone, and not just a* **rock** <note>original: "petra" — common mineral stone found in the earth.</note>*; it*
*requires matter disposed by the human body. By this boundary, therefore,*
*the* **Academies** <term>Academies: the traditional university medical faculties.</term> *will see that they have not touched upon the*
*causes of Stone-disease even on the outermost shores: and those who grieve*
*being blamed for ignorance of fevers will recognize that they have many*
*partners in their calamity. For I never wished to be*
*injurious to all those before me: and it is enough that I have protested that I*
*lack the intention of injuring. For far be it that I should not know that an*
*unknown thing, demonstrated for the use of one's neighbor, lacks*
*calumny: especially while the ignorance of the* **Healer** <term>Medentis: one practicing the art of medicine.</term> *behaves*
*in the manner of a crime, and a man will eventually give* **skin for skin** <note>A reference to Job 2:4, "Skin for skin, yea, all that a man hath will he give for his life," emphasizing that medical ignorance is a grave matter because human life is at stake.</note>*. Not otherwise than a* **Magistrate** <term>Prætor: a Roman judicial officer; here meaning any high legal official or judge.</term>*, accusing someone*
*of a crime, is excused from calumny. I have always*
*grieved greatly that in the religion of Healing alone, such deaf,*
*sluggish, and frivolous principles are subscribed to: whereas in other*
*professions, work is so ingeniously elaborated.*
*Truly, what subtlety has not been devised,*

<summary>Van Helmont argues that his critique of traditional medicine is motivated by compassion rather than malice, asserting that most patients recover through God's grace rather than a doctor's skill. He introduces his study of "the stone" (lithiasis), describing it as a unique "monster" that behaves like a mineral even outside the human body.</summary>
<keywords>lithiasis, calculus, medical ethics, academic criticism, divine healing, iatrochemistry, bladder stones, Jan Baptista van Helmont</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader Who Loves Medicine" (Philiatro Lectori). The author is transitioning from a discussion of his previous work on fevers and the nature of "the stone" to a broader critique of the academic and logical systems of his time, which he finds distracting from practical medical progress.</meta>

# TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

a 2

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...concerning the five voices of* **Porphyry** <term>Porphyry of Tyre: a 3rd-century philosopher whose "Introduction" to Aristotle's logic became a standard textbook for over a millennium.</term>*, which they call the* **predicables** <term>prædicabilia: the five concepts used in logic to categorize things (genus, species, difference, property, and accident).</term>*? What clever tricks have they not set in motion concerning empty things? chatter, I say, which serves as a witness to linguistic industry?* **Raymond [Lull]** <note>original: "Raymundus" — Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan polymath who developed a complex system of logic using diagrams and revolving wheels to combine concepts.</note>*, however, not content with these, found nine other "most universal" voices: and then to these he added another 28 less universal ones: and finally, at last, he subjected 72 other universal ones, by which any matter could be described, predicated, and distinguished seventeen thousand, four hundred and forty-six times. This is useless chatter, an immense* **husk** <note>original: "siliqua" — literally the pod of a bean or pea; here used to mean a shell with no nutritional value or "kernel" of truth.</note> *of the sciences, without a kernel. Certainly, human minds everywhere, even without being spurred on, are naturally prone to subtleties if the goals of those subtleties are empty. But in matters looking toward life and health, they have continually snored as if in a springtime slumber. Law also leans so much on subtleties concerning the explanation of decrees, that these "sharp points" of wit—by which the human mind is seized with such wonderful admiration and looks upon itself with its own delight—are called the "subtleties of the Law" by a singular prerogative. These are indeed less vain than the linguistic ones, inasmuch as they are designed for acquiring and defending Law. But in matters of* **Theology** <term>Theologicis: refers here to "Scholastic Theology," the medieval academic discipline that applied Aristotelian logic to religious doctrine.</term>*, what majesty do the professor’s chairs not hope for through the* **minute precision** <term>acribologias: from the Greek "akribologia," meaning hairsplitting or excessive attention to detail.</term> *of their questions? I wish that human necessity were free from all these things. Let "mine" and "thine" yield to each person without deceit! Let faith also stand without dispute (as it does in* **Islam** <note>original: "Mahumetismo" — Van Helmont is using the contemporary European perception that Islam did not allow for the complex, logical disputes common in Christian universities as a point of comparison.</note>*). Let every subtlety yield, for which no account will be held on the Last Day: for in this way,* **Apostolic sincerity** <note>original: "Apostolica sinceritas" — a reference to the simple, direct teachings of the early Apostles before they were complicated by medieval philosophy.</note> *would return; "Thus I received, and thus I handed down to you." At least those who, in life, were most foreign to such subtleties will undergo a milder judgment. But in medical matters (oh, the pain!) where investigation is most necessary, useful, and commended for the sake of charity, almost all things have remained untouched: because on both sides there is a supine laziness, prone to subscribing to the* **ancestral stupidities** <note>original: "avitis stoliditatibus" — the ancient, traditional errors passed down through generations of medical textbooks.</note> *of the ignorant; more harmful also among them...*

<summary>Van Helmont critiques the "useless chatter" of traditional logic and scholasticism (specifically Porphyry and Raymond Lull), arguing that while Law and Theology indulge in such hairsplitting, the medical field has stagnated by lazily following ancient errors instead of pursuing practical, charitable research.</summary>
<keywords>logic, Porphyry, Raymond Lull, scholasticism, legal subtleties, theology, apostolic sincerity, medical reform, ancestral stupidities</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader Who Loves Medicine," following the author's critique of useless logical hair-splitting. He now turns his attention to "street-corner" doctors and the rigid, unquestioning adherence to ancient Greek authorities in universities.</meta>

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...who wander through the streets, and run from house to house, in order to prostitute health for sale, and chase away a disease unknown to them. For it has not been doubted only once or in passing in the Academies until now, regarding the reliability of the theorems handed down by the* **Pagans** <note>original: "Gentilibus" — here referring to non-Christian ancient authorities like Galen and Aristotle whose teachings dominated medieval and Renaissance medicine.</note>*: which otherwise veil their futility by their mere ease, and at first sight, ought to excite suspicion of themselves: since in human affairs, nothing is harsher than shame and stupidity, nothing fuller of lies and fraud, and nothing more remarkable than cruelty and credulity—accepted through so many centuries now—than the profession which daily conducts experiments through the deaths of men, under a* **collective agreement** <note>original: "concentrica subscriptione" — implying a "conspiracy of silence" or a unified front among the medical establishment to stick to old ways.</note>*, according to the wishes of the Pagans. For nations living without a Physician admit this along with me, [seeing] what a [good] life they lead. More discerning physicians, I say, admit it too. For not so long ago, I asked a pious and modest man, yet a most famous Physician,* **Doctor Johannes vander Weghen**, *why, (since he lived in* **Louvain** <note>Lovanii: A major university city in modern-day Belgium, then part of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>*, and had friends at court and lords whom he treated, and was most fit for a University Chair) *he did not seek some teaching position: he answered frankly, that it was not permitted to offer any other kind of doctrine to the youth besides* **Galen** <term>Galen of Pergamon: A 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories on "humors" dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years. Van Helmont was a famous critic of his system.</term>*; and thus (he said) "I would knowingly damn my soul, knowing the better things, yet teaching the worse." Therefore, what I reveal that I know, others know as well, but they do not dare to speak. Good* **Jesus**, *how long will the* **torpor** <note>original: "torpor" — a state of mental or physical inactivity; sluggishness.</note> *of the Healers last? And such cruelty against the work of your hands? Allow, allow, O infinite Goodness, that the human race may recognize that the demon* **Moloch** <note>Moloch: A biblical reference to a deity associated with child sacrifice; Helmont uses it here to suggest that medical authorities are sacrificing human lives to their "idolatrous" ancient theories.</note> *envies no subtleties more than those which are tossed about concerning charity, and which regard and preserve the life of your image. For at first I grieved at such immense credulity toward ancient principles, and such great laziness of mortals concerning matters of such importance; and day by day, my pity for this matter grew within me. From here at last...*

<meta>catchword: last (tandem)</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont critiques the medical establishment for blindly following Galenic tradition, sharing an anecdote of a Louvain doctor who refused to teach because the system forced him to ignore better, modern truths. He laments the "cruelty and credulity" of a profession that prioritizes ancient dogma over the actual preservation of human life.</summary>
<keywords>medical reform, Galenism, Louvain, Johannes vander Weghen, medical ethics, academic corruption, Moloch metaphor</keywords>

<page-num>5</page-num>

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

<sig>A 3</sig>

*...indeed, having obtained such a small light, I recognized with great pain that the* **errors of the Schools** <note>original: "Scholarum errores" — referring to the established medical universities of the 17th century which relied on ancient Greek texts rather than experimental chemistry.</note> *must be brought to light through me. Truly, at the threshold, it seemed to me full of untamed arrogance that I, the least of all, should brand everyone before me with ignorance of* **Philosophical Truth**: *while attributing the* **Mastery of Healing** <note>original: "Adeptum Medendi" — an "Adept" was one who had achieved the highest level of alchemical and medical knowledge.</note> *to myself alone. Therefore I often asked the Lord to take back this* **talent** <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is punished for burying his master's money rather than investing it.</note> *of His from me, and deign to remove it entirely, and grant it to another more worthy. For I knew that "he who has hidden well, has lived well,"* <note>original: "qui bene latuerat, bene vixisset" — a variation of the Latin proverb "Bene qui latuit, bene vixit," suggesting that a private life is a happy one.</note> *at least morally, and especially in this* **ulcerous age**. *And so I resisted, and for a long time delayed proposing this ignorance of the principles of Healing to its world, until now, as an old man, forced by ultimate necessity and standing in the agony of death, I promised the Lord that I would openly reveal His talent: lest I be judged, perhaps at God's strict judgment, to have come into the world in vain and to have departed from here useless. For indeed, through a* **dream-vision** <note>original: "visionem somnialem" — Van Helmont frequently claimed that his insights came through divine dreams or intellectual visions.</note> *I understood that I feared reproaches more than God's indignation. That nature is cunning, so long as it uses pride as a pretext in purely obeying God, because of deceptive human considerations. Likewise, I would not perceive my own arrogance if I were to carry out less generously, through fear, what was required of me against those judgmental men who would rise up against me for so many centuries, rather than in purely obeying the most glorious Giver of Truth. Nay, I would not even pity my neighbor, and I would bury the talent in the ground, looking back at the world's uncertain judgments concerning me. I knew indeed that the doors of* **Medicine**, *had been locked for so many centuries, and the bars and bolts were covered with rust, yet I hesitated to open them: as if I presumed the office of the* **Gatekeeper** *to be merely mine, and not given from elsewhere. I therefore resolved within myself that I should do what charity, not arrogance, urged to be done: knowing that he who looks to the public good is not injurious, although it may be shameful to those who...*

<meta>catchword: rashly (original: temere)</meta>

<summary>The author describes his spiritual crisis and ultimate decision to publish his medical findings despite the "untamed arrogance" it might represent. He concludes that his duty to God and the public good outweighs his fear of being attacked by the medical establishment.</summary>

<header>TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.</header>

### TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...rashly subscribed to the trifles of the* **Pagans** <note>original: "Gentilium" — Van Helmont refers to ancient non-Christian authorities like Galen and Aristotle, whose "trifles" or "nugae" he believed misled medical science.</note>, *to the detriment of the human race. At last, therefore, I stood in the middle between shame and terror at the magnitude of the matter, and many times I put down my pen. And again, I seriously asked the Lord to deign to choose another more worthy than me. Wherefore the Lord, justly indignant, permitted this evil and useless servant to be* **sifted by Satan** <note>A reference to Luke 22:31, implying a period of intense trial and testing.</note>. *For the* **Order** <note>This likely refers to the ecclesiastical and medical authorities, such as the Medical Faculty of Louvain and the Spanish Inquisition, who placed Van Helmont under house arrest and interrogated him for years.</note>, *whose zenith is the house of Powers, and whose Nadir is the other Orders, began to persecute me without cause, with unworthy tricks. I knew immediately that the hand of the Lord had touched me. And so, in the full storm of persecutions, I wrote a volume, the title of which is* **The Dawn of Medicine** <note>original: "Ortus Medicinae" — This became Van Helmont’s most famous work, published posthumously by his son. It challenged the foundations of traditional medicine.</note>. *That is, unheard-of physical beginnings. In which I uncovered the* **errors of the Schools** <note>The established universities of the 17th century which still taught the "humoral" medicine of the ancients.</note>, *common in healing. I gave and demonstrated, I say, new principles of diseases, as well as theorems unheard of until now; so that, having abandoned the vanities of the Pagans, the Academies might henceforth become accustomed to the Truth. For from this I found in my soul a* **Sabbath-rest** <term>sabbatismum: a state of profound spiritual peace and divine rest, often associated with mystical experience or the completion of a divinely-mandated task.</term>, *such as I never had in my prosperity; so much so that, full of suspicion, I grieved that such a great storm did not disturb the peace of my soul or the sleep of my body in the least. In this I cannot sufficiently praise, O God, my protector, the abundance of your generosity, which did not permit my soul, even a little, to fall away from the full enjoyment of peace under such great distresses on all sides: fearing only this, lest I, a useless servant, should be buried with such a small* **talent** <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is punished for failing to use what God gave him.</note>. *Whoever, therefore, interprets my zeal as proud boasting, it is fine by me, provided you do not harm yourself. For I shall rejoice to bear all confusion for the good of my neighbor and posterity, and I shall enjoy my wish, whether in the meantime my daring turns out to be rashness for me, or not. For God the sower will water those things which He willed to grow. Truly, in the book on fevers, I recounted the beginnings of my* **repentance** <note>original: "poenitentiae" — here used to mean a "change of mind" or conversion away from his previous academic training.</note>, *and how I defected from* **Galen** *and* **Avicenna** <note>Galen (2nd century CE) and Avicenna (10th-11th century CE) were the two pillars of traditional European medical authority whom Van Helmont explicitly rejected.</note>, *because of the [errors] detected...*

He explains his departure from the traditional medical teachings of Galen and Avicenna in favor of newly discovered "physical beginnings."</summary>

# TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...certainly, the falsehoods of the pillars of Healing. From then on, while I was still a young man, a remarkable confidence grew in me, through which I willingly exposed myself to the injurious censures of everyone for the sake of my neighbor: and gradually, as the number of days passed, the Lord looked upon the sincerity of my zeal, and permitted me, now a man, to see that whatever is taught in the* **Medical Schools** <note>original: "Scholis Medicis" — the traditional universities that taught the theories of Galen and Avicenna, which Van Helmont vehemently opposed.</note> *is full of hardships and ruin, and would be a laughingstock to posterity. Good Jesus! How I then trembled at the greatness of your mercy, which reveals to little ones what has been denied for so many centuries to men otherwise most religious and ingenious. Furthermore, however much I was thereafter made* **more certain** <note>original: "factus" — here emphasizing his growing conviction.</note> *that the manifestation of the talent of Truth I had received was incumbent upon me: nevertheless, nature is easy at finding excuses, and deceives itself and its own sorrows through the supports of reason, its handmaid. Soon therefore (alas, I am ashamed of my inconstancy) I shook off the burden I had taken up from my shoulders again, and said: Who am I, Lord? For I lack the more solid things that I might substitute in the place of those to be suppressed. For the things I previously believed were commanded of me, I suspected again were suggested by the cunning of Satan: because the* <term>secret remedies</term> <note>original: "arcana remedia" — specialized, often chemically prepared medicines that Van Helmont believed were the "credentials" of a true physician.</note> *were lacking; namely the credentials, or signs of my mission. Wherefore in my youth, I had long since persuaded myself that the art of Healing itself was nothing but a mere* **imposture**, *devised by idle Greeks, originally constructed for the destruction of the Roman conquerors, and thereafter confirmed for the calamities of men; to which human credulity, because of the hope it had conceived, easily subscribed; and thus that* <term>medical religion</term> <note>original: "religio Medica" — a provocative term suggesting that the medical establishment of his time demanded a kind of blind, uncritical faith in its doctrines.</note> *brought forth authority for itself; because we usually believe too easily what we too greedily desire. I knew indeed from then on that the Medicine of the* **Universities** <note>original: "Academiarum"</note> *was a thing of nothing. To this end, both incurable diseases and, moreover, the vanity of experiments gave me their support: and finally, assistance flowed in from everywhere, because Healers, everywhere [are made a] mock-* <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ludi-", which will complete the word "ludibrio" (mockery/laughingstock) on the next page.</note>

<summary>Van Helmont reflects on his youthful discovery of the "falsehoods" of traditional medicine, describing his internal conflict between a divine mandate to reveal the truth and his fear of having no better remedies to offer in their place.</summary>

<header>TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.</header>

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...exposed to mockery. At that time, the very Philosophy taught to me in my youth supported this error of mine. To be sure, the Logic, Physics, and Metaphysics of the Schools are not that Philosophy for which Pythagoras once took up a few disciples—those of a better character—to be instructed, binding them to himself by a silence of several years and a secret oath that they would never reveal anything of what they had heard there. For I suspected that the foundations of Pyrotechny* <note>original: "Pyrotechniae" — Van Helmont’s term for chemistry or alchemy, literally "the art of fire."</note> *were delivered there, rather than the knowledge of that which Galen* <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was the Roman-era physician whose theories dominated European medicine for over a thousand years.</note> *spreads out with Greek wordiness. Indeed, long before Pythagoras, it was the custom for everyone to faithfully record in the temples, by means of Hieroglyphics, whatever had been of use to their own people. For such great necessity and such internal disaster had convinced them that these things should be handed down to posterity without envy* <note>original: "absque invidia" — meaning without the desire to hide knowledge from others, a common theme in alchemical literature where "envy" is the hoarding of secrets.</note>*. When, therefore, the art of Healing fell into contempt in my eyes, I happened upon a sacred text, often read but never understood: namely, that the Most High created the Physician, and commanded him to be honored because of the necessity for him.* <note>Van Helmont is referencing the Bible, specifically Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 38:1: "Honour the physician for the need thou hast of him: for the Most High hath created him."</note> *In this I immediately perceived:*

1.  *That He, who created all things, nevertheless singularly glories in being the Creator of the Physician.*
2.  *That for the sake of His glory and the outpouring of His goodness toward the needs, relief, and aid of the sick, the Most High wishes to be appeased through the Physician, by restoring lost health.*
3.  *That He, to whom all honor and glory are due, commanded that only parents and the Physicians created by Him be honored: as if the Physician had something of a father-like* <term>father-like: "patrizantis" — behaving like or resembling one's father; here implying the physician acts as a representative of God's paternal care on earth.</term> *quality.*
4.  *Then, in my manhood, being not a little anxious day and night, I sought out who that happy man might be, whom the Most High destined from eternity, chose, and created as a Physician, and hence also commanded to be honored? Could it perhaps be he who, having read through the Institutes and some classical authors, and having completed...*

<meta>catchword: que (part of "emensusque", meaning "and having traversed/measured out")</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont rejects university philosophy in favor of the secret traditions of Pythagoras and ancient Egypt. He reflects on the biblical mandate to honor physicians, questioning who truly qualifies as a physician created by God.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Pyrotechny, Galen, Hieroglyphics, divine physician, Ecclesiasticus 38, medical education reform, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of the medical establishment of his day. He contrasts the "true physician" created by God—who can cure all things—with the university-trained doctors who rely on pagan texts (Galen and Aristotle) and dismiss difficult cases as "incurable."</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.</header>

*...[ored]; who, in the Academy for a full three years, was at last sent out as a candidate through disputations and examinations by the Professors, admitted by both secular and ecclesiastical law? Or rather he, who already possessing that same title of Physician, had grown old under a foreign law, meanwhile full of years, experiments, and money? Then immediately I considered that the sick man needs a Physician whom the Most High created, fully equipped in all respects; and that the healthy man does not need a Physician standing by to preside over the kitchen, count his mouthfuls, and prescribe rules of diet. (You will find more concerning the honor due to the Physician hereafter under the history of Duelech <note>Van Helmont's specific term for the process of stone formation—like kidney stones—in the human body.</note>.) I considered on the other hand: That the apothecary <note>original: "unguentarius," a maker of ointments or perfumes</note> prepares the ointments of sweetness <note>original: "pigmenta suavitatis"</note>; and his works shall not be consumed; nor should there be a medicine of destruction upon the earth.* <note>Van Helmont is paraphrasing the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38, which discusses the divine role of the physician and the pharmacist.</note> *Which means that the true Physician mows down all diseases with an equal scythe, and that in his hands there are no incurable diseases. A notable distinction indeed between the Physician whom the Most High created, and him whom the Academies created through the doctrine of the Pagans <note>original: "Gentilium" — referring here to the reliance on non-Christian, ancient Greek and Roman authorities like Galen whose theories dominated the universities.</note>. Immediately an immense catalog of "incurables" presented itself to me: as if the Most High were not concerned for the righteous, or as if their fields <note>Van Helmont often uses agricultural metaphors for the human body</note> were not diseased. As if, for their necessity, He had created neither Physician nor Medicine in the Academies. Since those men do not merely cast the sick into despair in such cases; they even count among the "hopeless" all those diseases that do not fall silent of their own accord. Indeed, they regard the quartan fever <note>original: "quartanam" — a fever recurring every four days, often associated with malaria</note>, and those which keep stable roots for years and usually end only through nature’s own weariness, as incurable. And unless the remaining diseases run their course to the end, they would surely all be added equally to the incurables. For most of them do not know how to take away a toothache except by pulling it out. Thus perhaps, in an old case of Cephalalgia <note>A chronic, severe headache.</note>, they would command the removal of the head for the sake of health, if life could remain safe—*

<meta>catchword: ret.</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that true physicians are divinely ordained to cure all diseases, unlike university doctors who rely on pagan theories and dismiss many common ailments as incurable.</summary>

<header>->TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.<-</header>

*...returned. Therefore, after several years of significant labor, I felt ashamed that I knew nothing else, or had learned nothing but what was worth nothing. For although I believed that the Physician was created by God, just as simple medicines <note>original: "medicamina simplicia" — medicines made from a single natural ingredient, usually a plant, rather than complex chemical mixtures.</note> are; nevertheless, I remained entirely stuck in the knowledge of that Physician and the things that serve him. For I often wished: "Oh, would that I might finally one day become a disciple of such a Physician!" Meanwhile, I clearly recognized that the Art of Healing <note>original: "Artem Medendi"</note>, cultivated by both the Greeks and the Arabs—and which the Jews falsely claim was handed down to them from their Rabbis under the Kabbalah <note>original: "Kabala" — a tradition of Jewish mysticism and esoteric scriptural interpretation.</note>—is very far removed from the one which the Sacred Text describes. Finally, therefore, I convinced myself that the science of healing has a good beginning in its means, intention, and end: namely, that it is a "good gift, descending from the Father of Lights" <note>A reference to the New Testament, James 1:17.</note>. And I concluded that this gift had therefore never descended upon the Pagans or the Jews of old, however much they may be puffed up by our own gullibility; for the Lord did not create them as Physicians, nor for our necessity. Indeed, he commanded that they should not be honored, but strictly avoided. For the Physician created by God is not flawed, devoted to profit, or an enemy of Christians: but is full of charity. For I first noticed the rarity of that good gift in Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD. His work was the standard text on herbal medicine for 1,500 years.</note>. Who, from the days of Plato (during which he lived <note>Van Helmont is mistaken here; Plato lived in the 4th century BC, centuries before Dioscorides.</note>), indeed described the histories of herbs. Yet to this day, hardly anything has been added to his work, while much has been taken away. And so, in two thousand three hundred years, hardly any light from above has shone upon the study of herbs <note>original: "rem Herbariam"</note>, although it is most obvious and necessary. Therefore, I reasonably concluded that this light from above has fallen upon other ranks of more hidden knowledge. But least of all upon the Pagans, Atheists, and faithless Jews, who are shut out from Truth and charity, and for that reason abandoned by God. And the births of things, as they are deferred until the fullness of time, are hidden from us...*

<meta>catchword: So,</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont rejects the medical traditions of the Greeks, Arabs, and Jews as "worthless," arguing that true medicine is a divine gift from God that must be rooted in Christian charity rather than ancient pagan texts.</summary>
<keywords>divine physician, herbal medicine, Dioscorides, Kabbalah, Christian charity, medical history, Father of Lights</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of traditional medical education. Having rejected the "Pagan" foundations of university learning, he now argues that true healing is a divine gift that cannot be forced or bought, but is granted by God in His own time.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>->TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE. 11<-</header>

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*Thus the gift of the truth of* **Healing** <note>original: "Medendi"</note> *does not descend, except at a maturity established by God. For a gift of light will not shine at our whim. For He who made all things as He willed, does the same when He wills, and perfects them in those whom He shall will. For I have grown old, now for forty years and more, in the crowd of* **Healers** <note>original: "Medentûm"</note>; *and finally as an old man I have realized that the* **Theorems of the Scholars** <note>original: "Theoremata Scholarium" — the formal, academic doctrines taught in universities based on ancient texts rather than experience.</note> *must be overturned by me, as they will soon fall into ruins in the coming age, insofar as they do not hold together, being deprived of the mortar of truth. There was in me such certainty and venerable authority of that gift of the* **Adept** <note>original: "Adepti" — in alchemy and Hermetic philosophy, an "Adept" is one who has attained the ultimate secret of the art, often through divine revelation.</note>, *that I felt the Giver would also be the interpreter of His own gift, so that I might exclude every doubt thus far. And knowledge of this kind is far more certain than that which is formed through demonstration <note>scientific or logical proof</note>. Because words lack the power to make this certainty common to others. I know also that not everyone who reads my "Principles of Healing" will carry away equal fruit from them; for God will always remain the dispenser of His own gift. I have said these things so that you might also know that my own unworthiness will shroud the gift in shadows, so that whoever can may grasp the course of nature. For I have hoped that, since He has now increased the number and atrocity of diseases, He would inspire the gift of* **Healing** *in the little ones and the outcasts of this world. And since in the previous century He sent ahead* **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged traditional medicine.</note> *as a wealthy precursor in the analytical science of bodies and the brilliance of remedies, I hoped that He would now wish to add the science of the* **Adept** *(which that other man lacked). Furthermore, if it is pleasing and acceptable to inquire without sin into the reason for that divine decree, why the gift of the Adept of Healing has descended less upon* **Christians**: *I believe the* **Schools** <note>original: "Scholas" — the established universities.</note> *resist it, insofar as they stubbornly insist on the principles of the* **Pagans** <note>original: "Gentium" — literally "the Nations," referring here to the non-Christian Greek and Roman authorities like Galen and Aristotle.</note>. *Then likewise, because all* **Medicine** *is practiced for profit, almost from its very beginning; though it alone of the arts ought to be weighed mercifully out of compassion*...

<header>->TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.<-</header>

*it must be weighed; Not, however, as if one should live cheerfully and pompously or grow rich from the miseries of the wretched. Therefore, greed has snatched away the necessary disposition from men, and the falsehood of pagan doctrine* <note>Helmont frequently refers to the teachings of Aristotle and Galen as "pagan," contrasting them with Christian revelation.</note> *has turned away the* **attained gift of healing** <note>original: "Adeptum medendi donum." In the Hermetic tradition, an "Adept" is a practitioner who has achieved the highest level of spiritual and physical knowledge.</note>. *The pursuit of this gift, therefore, seemed to me to consist in compassion toward the sick, in unlearning false theories, and in putting on a profound humility of spirit. Which, just as it is then not puffed up by the letter* <note>The "letter" refers to book-learning or literal scholarship that lacks spiritual depth.</note> *nor depressed by disorder: so in the humble, intuitive knowledge of its own nothingness, the mind empties itself of all knowledge introduced through the inductions of reason. Then thereafter, then, I say, the* **Most High**, *scarcely leaves the empty mind alone without filling it with the fertile ray of His light. I have perhaps already offended some by saying that the* **attained gift of Healing** *descends from above: those who wish* **Medicine** *to be learned in the manner of other arts.* **Understanding** <note>original: "Intellectus"</note> *(they say) is a natural power, and any natural power is born to produce its own proper effect: moreover, the proper effect of the* **Understanding** *itself is the act of understanding.  **Therefore, man naturally understands all things that can be understood, as the proper object of the Understanding.*** *Furthermore, the faculty of Healing is something that can be understood, and therefore it does not descend from above.* **I respond: The Soul, and its understanding, are not the immediate works of nature, since they arise from a supernatural source.** *And so, regarding its beginning, although* **Understanding** *is a natural faculty of the soul, it is nonetheless not to be counted entirely among the natural faculties.* **It is a matter of faith that God created the Physician** <note>A reference to Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38:1: "Honor the physician... for the Lord created him."</note>, *and so the art of* **Healing** *speaks of something above the common rule of the Creator, such that its attainment does not happen in the manner of other arts. For* **Nebuchadnezzar** <note>The Babylonian king described in the Book of Daniel (chapter 4) who lost his human reason and lived as a beast until God restored his "understanding" to him.</note> *will testify to the taking away and the restoration of the* **Understanding**. **Likewise: Do not be like the horse and the mule, which have no Understanding** <note>A reference to Psalm 32:9.</note>, *which would have been said in vain [if understanding were purely a natural right],*

## TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.

*...if Understanding were given equally by Nature. Furthermore, the given Understanding (of which they preach here) does not exercise its own natural or intellectual act, except insofar as it draws some notions, by discourse, from observations which it has received from the perception of the senses, since it is entirely ignorant of causes* **from the first** <note>original: "à priori." A philosophical term meaning reasoning from causes to effects, or from principles already known.</note>. *For medical science, however, there is required a certain clarity of light which surpasses that knowledge obtained through the senses, and indeed even through the logical consequences of causes to effects, according to the (mostly fallacious) suppositions introduced to it by reason. For it is a matter of faith that the understanding, together with the totality of human nature, and so forth, however clear it may be, does not perceive propositions that are primarily true and surpassing the senses, except with an influx of* **celestial light** <note>original: "luminis superni." Van Helmont believes that true understanding is a divine gift rather than a result of human logic.</note>. *For instance, I often read a passage of a book attentively; and, although I understand the words, only once and unexpectedly do I grasp its true meaning, with wonder at my previous readings. Such knowledge, moreover, I call* **grace**. *Thus, indeed, the Understanding, however clear, does not always assent to the truth, because naturally it does not even perceive it. (Hence, for instance, the factions in the sciences and in religion.) Thus, in the gift of healing, there is something nobler and higher than that which is formed in the imagination from the pre-existing knowledge of the senses: something which is true, solid, and good, surpassing the authority of logical deductions; indeed, something that cannot properly be taught or demonstrated. Yet I would not have it understood that the* **attained gift of healing** <note>original: "adeptum medendi." Referring to the status of an "Adept," a master who has received spiritual and physical insight into nature.</note> *is an infused knowledge, such as once illuminated* **Bezalel** <note>original: "Bezeliel." The biblical craftsman filled with the Spirit of God to build the Tabernacle (Exodus 31:2).</note> *and* **Oholiab** <note>original: "Oolias." An artisan who worked alongside Bezalel.</note>; *much less is it like that which rained down in a heavy shower upon the Apostles on the day of* **Pentecost** <note>The Christian festival celebrating the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the disciples.</note>, *so that they might immediately speak in various languages; nor is the attained gift of healing, likewise, of purely* <term>sublunary<gloss>Pertaining to the world below the moon; the physical, earthly realm subject to change and decay.</gloss></term> *matters. For eternal Wisdom created His Physician in a singular way, above other created things; and thus something more majestic seems to be required for him than for other professions; which therefore [neither]...*

<summary>Van Helmont argues that true medical knowledge is a gift of divine grace rather than a product of sensory observation or logic. He distinguishes this "attained gift of healing" from biblical miracles while asserting the physician's unique and elevated status in the natural order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s argument that true medical knowledge is a supernatural gift ("donum") from God, rather than a purely human achievement reached through sensory observation or logical deduction.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>->TO THE READER WHO LOVES MEDICINE.<-</header>

...commanded to be honored. Otherwise, all our understanding in nature arises only by way of logical discourse, the observation of a premise, and the pursuit of consequences and investigation—and all of this is done **from experience** <note>original: "à posteriori." In logic, this refers to reasoning that works backward from effects to causes, or from observations to principles.</note>. Therefore, all knowledge of this kind is crowded with uncertainty. Thus, between the ordinary way of understanding and the infused sciences of the first degrees, there are certain expanses, each distinguished by a unique degree in the Intellect, originating supernaturally.

I prove this as follows: **Every good gift descends from the Father of Lights** <note>A reference to the Biblical passage James 1:17. Van Helmont uses this to argue that medical skill is a divine "light" granted to the soul.</note>. The **attainment of Healing** <note>original: "Adeptum Medendi."</note> is a good gift; therefore, it descends from the Father of Lights. The major premise is a matter of faith. The minor premise is established insofar as the Physician, as such, is created by the Father of Lights.

They <note>Van Helmont's intellectual opponents, likely the scholastic followers of Galen and Aristotle.</note> reply with a certain comparison—though it contributes nothing to the logical argument—in this way: "The knowledge of God is more difficult than the knowledge of Medicine. But the Gentiles <note>Here referring to non-Christian philosophers like Aristotle or Galen.</note> naturally discovered the existence of the Deity through the operations of the Intellect. Therefore, they achieved the natural science of Healing much more easily."

I respond by conceding the whole point, provided they have not introduced **four terms** <note>original: "quatuor terminos." A logical fallacy (quaternio terminorum) where a syllogism is invalid because it contains four terms instead of three.</note>. Therefore, just as no one can draw out the light of faith through nature, but only some shadowy knowledge; so too regarding the gift of Healing, I concede that a certain knowledge of Healing is acquired naturally through observations of what helps and what harms. But truly, that knowledge is so **shadowy** and blind that it plainly contradicts the text <note>Likely referring to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38:1, "Honor the physician... for the Lord created him."</note>; which would say in vain that God created the Physician as such, and that he is to be honored, unless some light shone forth in this created Physician that is above the common, ordinary, and natural power of the thinking soul.

Finally, the reason that neither Atheists, nor Gentiles, nor Jews ever received this gift of Healing is not to be sought elsewhere or further than from the fact that, in reality, disease, remedies, and the proper application of these are still unknown to mortals today. For...

## TO THE PHYSICIAN-READER.

*For the argument is inescapable: until now, the* **Attainment of Healing** <note>original: "Adeptum Medendi." Van Helmont uses this term to describe a state of perfect medical knowledge that is "attained" through divine grace rather than just study.</note> *has been unknown. Therefore, God did not grant it to paganism in past centuries, nor to the* **Schools** <note>Referring to the traditional universities that taught the theories of Galen and Aristotle.</note> *who follow blind leaders. The corollary of this is that whoever agrees with the doctrines of the pagan Schools is excluded from the true principles of Healing. I will demonstrate this premise, God willing, in a large volume. Namely, that the principles of knowing the causes and roots of diseases, remedies, and their* **specific applications** <note>original: "appropriationibus." This refers to how a specific remedy is "appropriated" or directed to a specific organ or disease.</note> *have remained unknown. The consequence is clear in itself: unless they can show that every good gift is derived from somewhere other than God. For to establish the gift of Healing, it should be enough that, although the Attainment of Healing is so close to the nature of the Intellect—because of the proximity and necessity of natural objects, it is customary for it to be attributed to the natural sciences grasped by the simple Intellect—nevertheless, insofar as it includes the gifts of prudence, counsel, etc., which are* **spiritual gifts** <note>original: "charismata."</note> *of the Holy Spirit, the gift of Healing must surely be brought and expected from a source that is clearly carried above the path of nature. Indeed, it is not rare that someone, submerged in the middle of dreams, suddenly conceives a knowledge which he had never achieved while awake.* **For night unto night shows knowledge** <note>A reference to Psalm 19:2. Van Helmont suggests that inspiration can come during sleep or contemplation.</note>. *Often, someone reads a passage they have read many times before without result, but from which they finally establish a more reformed life. Do these things not descend from the* **Father of Lights**? <note>original: "Patre luminum." A reference to James 1:17.</note> *Therefore, sciences of this kind are indeed infused, though not of the highest order. They are, I say, talents upon which a well-formed Intellect afterward builds useful doctrines. For the learned (as such) will shine (above the unlearned) in the kingdom of heaven, if, for the sake of doctrine, their souls have prepared themselves for greater spiritual gifts. For the Almighty was pleased with a diversity of mansions, choirs, brilliance, and the Intellect of Angels, and of men...*

<summary>Van Helmont argues that true medical knowledge is a divine gift (charisma) rather than a product of pagan Aristotelian logic. He asserts that insights often come through supernatural "infusion," such as in dreams or sudden inspirations, rather than through traditional schooling.</summary>

<header>->TO THE PHYSICIAN-READER.<-</header>

...and of the men accompanying them. At the very least, it supports the idea of the *Attainment of Healing,* that among the seven spirits standing closest to the throne of God, the name of one of them is the **Medicine of God** <note>original: "Medicina Dei." This refers to the Archangel Raphael, whose name in Hebrew means "God heals."</note>. For this office is ranked above the Principalities, Thrones, Powers, and Dominations. Yet, those who dwell in the heavens <note>original: "Cœlites."</note> do not fall ill, nor do they have any need for medicine. Nor should this **Medicine of God** be understood as a mere metaphor, for it possessed practical powers even within the gall of a fish <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Tobit, where the Archangel Raphael instructs Tobias to use fish gall to cure his father's blindness.</note>. In this section, however, I have undertaken to explain the origin of **Lithiasis** <note>The medical condition of forming stones in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>, as I promised. I will show how the stone involves a **metamorphosis** <note>A fundamental change in substance or state.</note> that cannot possibly originate from the **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that traditional medicine of the time blamed for all diseases.</note>, but arises instead from the waste products of the urine itself. Therefore, this treatise can easily do without the discussion regarding the **fiction of complexions and humors** <note>Van Helmont is famously critical of Galenic medicine, calling the theory of the four humors a "fiction" because it fails to explain the physical reality of chemical changes in the body.</note>.*

# T R E A T I S E
## O N
# L I T H I A S I S.
<note>original: "Lithiasi." The medical condition of forming stones in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>

->*Petrification.*<-
<note>The process of turning into stone.</note>

### C H A P T E R I.

1. **B**efore Paracelsus, the Medical Schools were already wavering. 2. *The opinion of the ancients regarding the causes of petrification.* 3. *The sounder doctrine of Paracelsus.* 4. *The flow of seeds toward stone.* 5. *The arrangement of minerals since the creation of the world.* 6. *What the Threefold Line is.* <note>original: "linea Trinalis." A geometric or metaphysical concept in early chemistry/mineralogy.</note> 7. *What a flinty mountain is.* 8. *The source of the diversity of stones.* 9. *Diamond dust is always yellow.* 10. *Rocks and stones: how they differ.* 11. *The seed of stone, and how it exceeds the vegetable seed.* 12. *Human Petrification, and why stone grows on the teeth.* 13. *Several noteworthy things.* 14. *Why certain insects do not petrify, while more perfect animals sometimes do so entirely.* 15. *That "Form" is not introduced from the power of matter.* <note>A rejection of the Aristotelian idea that the shape or nature of a thing emerges purely from its physical material.</note> 16. *How a human is petrified.* 17. *Rock has nothing in common with a calculus.* <note>The author distinguishes between geological stones and stones formed in the body.</note> 18. **Duelech** <term>Duelech: A term coined by Paracelsus specifically for the stone formed in the human body.</term> of Paracelsus. 19. *Praise of the Wild Carrot, etc.* <note>original: "Dauci." The wild carrot was frequently used as a remedy for stones.</note>

The more discerning physicians of the past century stood silent in amazement at how unfortunate and inconsistent with their own observations were the teachings of the Schools regarding the **Elements**, **Temperaments**, and **humors**. <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional university medical education based on Galen and Aristotle, which taught that health depended on the balance of four fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note> For they could not satisfy themselves with this "fourfold of humors" as an explanation for all diseases. Therefore, it was quite easy for Paracelsus (who, by the powerful evidence of his remedies, had drawn all of Germany into admiration of himself) to persuade those already wavering to believe in his "fiction" of **Tartar**. <term>Tartar: In Paracelsian medicine, this refers to stony precipitates or wastes from food and drink that "harden" in the body, much like the crust inside a wine barrel.</term> He claimed that this Tartar, entering treacherously through food and drink, was the true cause of any disease whatsoever. This reasoning of his gained such trust, and has now taken such stable root, that there is hardly anyone who [does not refer back] to **Tartar**...
