# ON LITHIASIS
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI." The medical condition of forming stones (calculi) within the body, such as in the kidneys or bladder.</note>

...of the neglected doctrines of Paracelsus, he may not flee. I indeed owed a unique treatise to Tartar <note>In the medical system of Paracelsus, "Tartar" refers to the stony precipitates from food and drink that settle in the body, which he blamed for many illnesses.</note>, I who was prepared for a history of the stone, had I not already written abundantly about it among my *Physical Beginnings* [early works on nature]. And therefore I would have left this volume truncated [incomplete] if I had transferred the treatise on Tartar from that work to this one. 

Since in that place I established the primeval integrity of nature within matter, the **Archeus** <term>Archeus: The internal "master-workman" or vital spirit that Van Helmont believed directed the functions of each organ.</term>, and life—or rather "form"—along with seminal beginnings [seeds] previously unheard of, and also **Ferments** <term>Ferments: Chemical agents or "yeasts" that Van Helmont believed were responsible for all biological transformations and digestion.</term>, the newly detected authors of all transmutations; while truly rejecting the [four] elements, qualities, complexions, and their battles, struggles, oppositions, and victories. 

Likewise, I relegated the fictions of **Humors** <note>The traditional "four humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) of Galenic medicine.</note> and **Catarrhs** <note>Traditional medicine taught that many illnesses were caused by waste fluids "dropping" from the brain into the body.</note> to a place outside of nature and medical consideration. Finally, having prescribed winds [gases], Tartars, and the three primary principles of the Chymists [Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury] to be outside the arena of diseases: gradually then, turning from theoretical matters to pathological ones, I explained the defects and "othernesses" of nature, and I revealed to the world the true cause of diseases from the very marrow [the core], previously unheard of. 

Therefore, for the **Calculus** [the stone], as a monster born at home [within the body], I have dedicated this book as if to an outlaw; and now, having abandoned the error of "Tartar" borrowed from Paracelsus, I come now to **Petrification** <note>The process of turning into stone.</note>, a process unknown to the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional academic universities that taught Aristotelian philosophy and Galenic medicine.</note>. 

Truly the Ancients, giving their names to **Aristotle** [following his authority], still judge according to his principles that stones and all minerals without distinction are made chiefly from earth, by means of heat and cold—external craftsmen—with the addition, however, of the three other elements [air, fire, and water]. Yet, since the weight of rock exceeds the weight of water, they conjectured from this that earth must be the proper material of all minerals. And although they might hesitate regarding the weight of gold—knowing that a mathematical demonstration sought from weight is stronger than any **syllogism** <note>A formal logical argument (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal) used extensively in medieval schooling.</note>—they did not, in the meantime, believe that gold was earth.

<summary>The author rejects traditional Greek theories of disease (the four humors) and Paracelsus's theory of "Tartar," proposing instead that stones form through a specific process of "petrification" driven by internal vital forces.</summary>
<keywords>Lithiasis, Archeus, Ferments, Paracelsus, Aristotle, Petrification, Calculus, Humors, Chymical principles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 18. The opening words "neglecting its own dimensions" complete the thought from the previous page regarding the errors of the Scholastics and Paracelsus in defining the nature of matter. The author is Jan Baptist van Helmont, a 17th-century chemist and physician, who is critiquing the Aristotelian theory of the four elements (earth, water, air, fire) and their role in the formation of metals and minerals.</meta>

### CHAPTER I.

...neglecting its own dimensions, many times penetrating itself. And much more now did they distrust their own theses; since they established that gold was composed not of earth alone—which is heavier than the other three elements—but of other lighter elements, mixed in a just or equal balance and proportion. 

Therefore, lacking a plan, they "hung the investigation of weight on a nail" <note>original: "indaginem ponderis, suspenderunt ad clavum." A Latin idiom meaning to abandon an inquiry or give up on a problem.</note> and, putting aside their disputes as if overcome by sleep, were content to say that metals—like things frozen by cold (since they flow again under the torture of fire)—remained as if dried out, once the superfluous water was dried away and air and fire were almost entirely excluded. This is what the dry philosophy of Aristotle brought forth. 

Moreover, they proved their thesis as I have said: for metals melt away by the contrary heat (as they believe). As if indeed a frozen thing would not melt except by the use of bellows! Or as if earth were liquefiable by fire and would return to the form of earth again at will whenever it felt the cold. 

Were they so forgetful of their own School? For not long ago, they said that the element of water is <term>intensely</term> <note>Scholastic philosophy graded the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) into degrees: "intense" meant the quality was at its highest strength, while "remiss" meant it was weak or diluted.</note> cold of itself and <term>mildly</term> wet; and thus metals ought to be "frozen" <note>original: "glaciari," literally "to be turned to ice." Here it refers to the solidification of molten metal.</note> from water, not from earth. Yet they say that earth of itself is <term>intensely</term> dry and only <term>mildly</term> cold—and thus incapable of being frozen? 

So that it follows from this: if metals were composed mostly of earth, they could never flow or be melted, since they could only be, at most, <term>mildly</term> cold. Nor could earth ever be converted into water, or a watery substance, by intense heat while melting within a metal. Indeed, they attribute an <term>intense</term> dryness to earth, which can only be strengthened by fire, not destroyed. Likewise, the <term>mild</term> cold of the prevailing earth could not—while it had been liquefied into water by the power of fire—convert it back into earth again. This is because they believe that the <term>mild</term> qualities of the elements do not possess enough activity that [the qualities] of another element...

b 2
<meta>catchword: fran-</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont critiques Aristotelian physics, arguing that the traditional theory of four elements cannot explain why metals melt and solidify, as earth is supposedly "dry" rather than "freezable." He highlights the contradictions in the Scholastic belief that metals are primarily composed of the element of earth.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian philosophy, four elements, metallurgy, melting points, Scholasticism, qualities of matter, gold composition, Jan Baptist van Helmont</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Baptist van Helmont’s critique of the Scholastic (Aristotelian) and Paracelsian views on the composition of matter. On the previous page, he dismissed the idea that metals are simply "frozen" or "dried" elements. Here, he extends this criticism to stones and minerals, arguing against the traditional four-element theory and introducing his own theory that all matter originates from water and a "seed."</meta>

-> ### ON CALCULUS <-

...break down their intense qualities. For with the same foolish step with which they began, they continue to say that rocks and stones are merely earth, hardened by heat and, as it were, dried out. They think they prove this by way of potter’s clay, which turns to stone by heat alone—or so they wish to believe. Indeed, because stone does not melt in fire as metals do, they guess at its nature by what it *lacks*, and believe they have untied the whole knot of the problem. 

This "coarse Minerva" <note>original: "crassa Minerva." A Latin idiom referring to plain, unrefined reasoning or a "dull-witted" approach to a complex problem.</note> should have been suspect to everyone long ago, if they were not all sleeping through the same deep lethargy. For what will they say of sulfur, which flows in fire? Did frozen water or earth give sulfur its beginning just because it melts? Or what about the hardening of glass, which is melted again by the same heat that formed it? Finally, what of salt, which is solidified and dried at one degree of heat, and melted at another—and is melted again by moisture? Truly, it is shameful to dwell any longer on Aristotelian trifles and the fables of elementary qualities, while the causes and origins of things must be investigated.

<margin>3</margin> Therefore, Paracelsus was the first to teach our grandfathers that all minerals come primarily from water. (Though he believed elsewhere they were made from the meeting of the four elements, and in other places from only his three <term>principles</term> <note>Paracelsus’s "Tria Prima": Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which he believed were the building blocks of all matter.</note> materially). He claimed they were the fruits of the element of water, just as plants are the fruits of the earth. 

But until my time, it was unknown that all bodies—which are believed to be "mixtures"—are materially made from water alone, without exception. However, their body is driven to take form by the necessity of a certain proper and specific <term>seed</term> <note>Van Helmont uses "seed" (*semen*) to describe an internal, spiritual blueprint or "Archeus" that directs water to become a specific substance like gold, stone, or a human organ.</note> toward ends known only to the Creator from the beginning. This proposition I have fully proved in my *Physical Principles*. 

<margin>4</margin> It has also been neglected until now how these seeds of things come into the light, cover themselves in the wrappings of bodies, and arrange them; and how the seeds themselves eventually must obey the requirements of those bodies. Wherefore...

<summary>Van Helmont rejects the Aristotelian idea that stones are just "dried earth," pointing out contradictions in how substances like sulfur and salt react to heat. He introduces his landmark theory that all physical matter is formed from water directed by a specific, divine "seed."</summary>

# CHAPTER I.

-> b 3 <-

<meta>The text begins mid-word, completing the word "therefore" [propter] from the previous page.</meta>...therefore, it will not be unwelcome to repeat here succinctly, from the doctrine I have more extensively taught elsewhere, the progress and flow of seeds <term>semen</term> toward their form, and their maturities within minerals. Indeed, if that which is not stone becomes stone, it is necessary that <term>petrification</term> <note>The process of turning into stone.</note> include the <term>generation</term> of some new entity. Moreover, every generation presupposes some kind of "seed," which disposes matter toward becoming an entity in potential. For nothing that lacks a vital principle can promote itself toward its own perfection. It would, therefore, be a foolish and accidental "perfection" that proceeded from a body without an internal leader and a goal established for itself. 

Thus, if a body is distinguished by its arrangement from an internal efficient cause, and flows forth in its production toward the ends proposed for it in nature, then the name "seed" belongs to it by right; for it proceeds from a beginning that is entirely incorporeal. It will be easily granted to me that this beginning flows forth in vital things from an image—or according to an "Idea" fabricated by the concept or thought of the generator (which is therefore called the <term>imaginative power</term>). 

But that even inanimate things have seminal gifts implanted in their first entities—which, according to the capacity of the receiver, correspond even analogically to a certain extent to "imagination"—is taught by the <term>antipathy</term> and the <term>sympathy</term> of inanimate things. <margin>5</margin> For it is necessary that non-sensitive things (take, for instance, the Magnet <note>The Lodestone.</note>) "feel" in some way the position of the pole, if it directs itself toward it of its own accord, rather than being pulled by the pole (as I teach with manifest arguments in my book *On the Plague* <note>original: "De Peste." Van Helmont’s work on the plague explored his theories of magnetism and spiritual "contagions."</note>); and likewise, that iron "feels," if, neglecting the pole, it inclines itself toward the magnet by choice. 

Lest these things be tediously repeated by me here, it suffices to have briefly assumed them as true. Furthermore, this "Idea" of the seed and the <term>entelechy</term> <note>original: "entelechia." A term from Aristotle meaning the realization of potential; here used as the vital force guiding development.</note> of the new entity—namely the seminal efficient cause—performs its duty even in non-sensitive things, just as if it were endowed with life and sense. This "Idea" clothes itself in the aura of its <term>Archeus</term> <note>A central concept for Van Helmont: the "Archeus" is the internal vital spirit or "master-workman" that governs the growth and functions of a body.</note>, and subsequently, by its means, [governs] the dispositions of the body and the organs...

...completes the **organs** <note>original: "organa"</note> at last, and finishes those things which were designed in the blueprint of their **seminal image**, toward ends known only to God. And thus far also, every creature originally depends on God. For here, He freely bestowed upon animals and plants the seminal fertility for building such an **Idea** <note>In van Helmont's philosophy, an "Idea" is a spiritual archetype or "mental" image within a seed that directs matter to take a specific shape.</note>—that is, for multiplying and raising up offspring—by the power of that voice (*Increase and multiply*) <note>Genesis 1:28</note> which shall endure for all time. These things, under the correction of the Church, I thus borrow from the Holy [Scriptures]. *The Earth in the beginning was empty and void.* <note>Genesis 1:2</note> It was, to be sure, hedged in by a double emptiness. Yet the same is not said of the element of water. For if the earth was "void," it did not yet have minerals within its foundation. The earth was, indeed, mere and pure sand <gloss>sabulum</gloss>, not yet distinguished by the numerous variety and classes of minerals. 

But *the Spirit of the Lord was moved over the abyss of the waters.* <note>Genesis 1:2</note> Truly, this "moving over" was not a hollow thing, a leisure lacking mystery, or a pleasant comfort of swimming; rather, it contained the mystery of blessing, through which water might fill the emptiness of the earth with fruits on one hand; and on the other hand, through plants and animals, it might satisfy the earth's emptiness and saturate its void. 

Therefore, before light was born, during the fluctuation of the divine Spirit, the metals and all minerals began at once. I am persuaded of this primarily by the **hidden lights of metals**, which rival the stars, and by the evidence of those lights which are often seen shining at night in ore-bearing mountains. <note>Van Helmont refers to the folk and alchemical belief that minerals "ripen" under the influence of stars and that "glowing" vapors or lights over mountains indicated the presence of metal veins.</note> At the very least, the Spirit of the Lord, who filled the world and was already eager to create, sealed within the bed of the abyss of waters—by His word—the fertile **Idea** of His desire. In an instant, this produced the entire opulent diversity of earths, stones, minerals, and metals, with which He filled the emptiness of the earth with a much greater bounty <gloss>foenore</gloss> than animals and plants could ever provide; for they [animals and plants] were not enough to adequately fill...

## CHAPTER I

<margin>6</margin>
...they were able. However, the layer, or "sole" of the earth, in which this most rich offspring of the waters was entrusted to fill the void, is called by **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist who heavily influenced van Helmont.</note> the **Trival Line** <note>original: "Linea Trivalis." In Paracelsian thought, this refers to a foundational boundary or "three-fold line" where minerals are formed.</note>, a womb heavy with the seeds of minerals, in which the Lord implanted the logical principles, the gifts, and the seeds that would be sufficient for all ages to come. Thus, indeed, the wealthy seed of rocks and minerals is implanted into the water so that it may receive its specific determination and its **ferment** <note>van Helmont uses "ferment" to describe a catalytic spiritual power that transforms raw matter into a specific substance, like yeast in dough.</note> within the womb of the earth. Moreover, I have demonstrated in my books on Physics what "virgin earth" is, outside and apart from minerals. However, the most rich seed of this storehouse and treasure seems to have been intentionally neglected by **Moses** <note>Referring to the author of Genesis</note>: lest **Israel**, by attributing divine and immortal powers to springs and mountains, might offer sacrifices to them. Otherwise, the sand, or the earth, which was uniform everywhere, once it had received the seed, rose up into hills and cliffs, and divided the strata of the rocks. For just as the origin of things began with a miracle, so now it clings to its secondary causes in such a way that the invisible **Archei** <term>Archei: the plural of Archeus, the internal "master sculptor" or vital spirit that governs the life and shape of an individual thing.</term> of things, and their hidden seeds, may testify that they are governed, at least, by the intelligible world. For this reason, the waters remained impregnated by the desire of the seeds, and the Almighty arranged the **Ideas** <note>original: "Ideas" - the spiritual blueprints or mental archetypes held by God and nature.</note> of His pleasure through the water. Yet these seminal desires of the water do not bear fruit through the successive propagation of one from another, in the manner of plants; instead, the seminal power, hiding in the treasures of the water, raises up its offspring from itself individually, and perfects them successively. For the seed—that is, the seminal and mineral **Idea**—is enclosed in the water, which it never leaves; instead, it bolts and shuts itself within that matter until finally, under the maturity of days, that which was born to be made from it is formed there. The operative **Icon** <note>original: "Icon" - an image or representation; here, the spiritual "signature" or blueprint acting upon the water.</note> in the waters therefore soon receives a sensible and ferment-like odor from the **Siliceous mountain**.

<page-num>24</page-num>

# ON LITHIASIS
<note>Lithiasis: The formation of stony concretions (stones) in the body, though here van Helmont begins by discussing the formation of stones in nature to establish his theory.</note>

...stones, and all minerals, take their origin from water. This is just as I have stated elsewhere regarding the origin of springs. Furthermore, that **odor** <note>In van Helmont's theory, an "odor" is an invisible, seminal vapor or spiritual essence that carries the blueprint of a substance.</note> is the **ferment** <note>A catalytic power that transforms raw matter into a specific substance, like yeast in dough.</note> from which, finally, the complete mineral seed flows out. From this also comes every rock. This seed in minerals is not a metaphor, nor is it something ambiguous or merely analogous through some loose allusion of similarity. For new flints and stones actually grow up in springs and rivers. Whatever is made, and for as long as it is being made—while it is not yet in the state of perfection intended by the **Archeus** <note>The "Archeus" is the internal master-builder or vital spirit that directs the growth and functions of a living or mineral body.</note>—it contains the seed within itself; thus I understand its nature to be uniform in its constituents. 

For indeed, purely transparent water, containing within itself the seed and the ferment of stone, turns into the crystal of all gems. If, moreover, the pure color of a certain metal or pyrite should coincide, a gemstone is formed, following the hardness and properties of its own coagulation. For just as tin—which provides painters with the yellow color they call **Masticot** <note>A historic yellow pigment made from lead tin oxide.</note>—renders every metal (except lead) brittle, so too does it tint even the hardest of gems. <margin>9</margin> Therefore, only the **Diamond** <note>original: "Adamas"</note> produces a yellow dust; the dust of other gems is white. 

But if the water, not being purely transparent, incorporates itself with a metallic color, it becomes an opaque stone, such as **Jasper, Agate, Flint, Porphyry, or Marble**, according to the requirements of the mixed seed. <margin>10</margin> Meanwhile, **rocks** <note>van Helmont distinguishes between "saxa" (softer, common rocks) and "lapides" (harder, precious stones).</note> are more easily dissolved than stones, and return again into water, either spontaneously or by art (*"who turns the rocks into pools of water"* <note>A reference to Psalm 114:8.</note>). By "rocks," I mean those that can be converted into lime; these perish spontaneously thereafter, turning into the richness of the fields and grain crops. In stone quarries also, a **nitrous salt** <note>likely saltpeter</note> often drips spontaneously in a perpetual oozing. This is because rocks return to their "first matter." Just as, in a reversed process, by the stony odor of the ferment, the whole water migrates into rock—or at least that part where the odor has taken root. And this happens both while the water is flowing and while it is standing still.

<summary>Van Helmont explains that all stones and gems grow from water via a "seed" and a "ferment." He distinguishes between transparent gems and opaque rocks, noting that stones can also dissolve back into water or "first matter."</summary>

## CHAPTER I.

...still. But it was not enough for Divine bounty to have made cliffs, rocks, and stones from the beginning, and to have bestowed a seed for the propagation of new offspring, by which stones and crags might thereafter be formed out of water: <margin>11</margin> nay, He willed moreover that the **petrifying seed** <note>In van Helmont's biology, this is the "semen petrificum," a formative power that directs matter to become stone.</note> should, in many instances, exceed its own vegetable properties, and that these seeds should prove themselves more powerful than the substances they inhabit. For the seed does not only turn to stone when water is subjected to it; rather, it also petrifies any body adjacent to it by the mere contact of the **odor** <note>As noted previously, "odor" refers to a spiritual vapor or subtle influence that carries the seed's instructions.</note> of the petrifying seed. Thus, for example, a **glove** <note>original: "chyrotheca"</note> of Emperor Frederick was petrified in one part—specifically, where it had been moistened for a while within a spring—while the other half, protected by a seal, remained leather. So much so that not only herbs, wood, bread, iron, eggs, fish, birds, and quadrupeds are petrified by a wondrous **metamorphosis** <note>A total transformation of substance.</note>. Furthermore, on the testimony of **Ambroise Paré** <note>A famous French surgeon (c. 1510–1590). The case described is a "lithopedion" or stone baby, a rare medical phenomenon where a fetus calcifies.</note>, a human fetus of adult size was cut out in Paris, having turned into stone. A toe of its foot was broken off, and inside the tendons and joints of the bones appeared. Likewise, a broken gum (for the mouth was indeed gaping as if wailing) revealed a tooth beneath in the socket of the jaw. This was attested to me by a friend who, in sharpening instruments and preparing tools dedicated to **Mathematics** <note>original: "Mathesi." In this era, this included the making of scientific and astronomical instruments.</note>, is often accustomed to use the back of this fetus as a **whetstone**. <margin>12</margin> In like manner, histories record that in the caves near Pergamon, certain petrified corpses—namely of those whom the fear of war had driven into hiding—were petrified from the surface all the way to the center. From which several things worthy of note arise:

1. That rocks are generated by their own specific seed, and that they consist moreover of another petrifying principle. That is, such seeds naturally transmute not only water, as their proper and immediate object, but also foreign bodies which have absorbed the aforementioned seed, at least by its odor.

2. And that therefore, those foreign bodies must [be reduced] to

That rocks, moreover, are not composed of **coagulable Tartar** <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) used the term "Tartar" to describe the stony precipitates from food and drink that he believed caused disease, comparing them to the crust in wine barrels.</note> as if it were their own and immediate matter; rather, they arise from their own **seed**, which is born to petrify anything—even matter not naturally predisposed to becoming stone. <margin>13</margin> 4. That the **petrifying seed**—by which herbs, birds, nests, leather, etc., turn to stone—is of more powerful efficacy than the seeds of plants, which otherwise require matter already prepared by a generating force. Therefore, **"not every land bears all things."** <note>original: "non omnis fert omnia tellus" — a quote from Virgil’s *Georgics*, meaning that specific environments are usually required for specific growths. Van Helmont argues the petrifying seed is so strong it ignores this rule.</note> But the stony seed seizes anything, even things far removed from its own nature. Furthermore, other seeds require the underlying matter to be reduced into a **compliant liquor** <note>original: "liquorem sequacem" — a fluid, workable state</note> that is receptive to the seed (which they call the "primary matter of generation"); they also require every shape and beauty of the preceding solid to be destroyed. Yet the petrifying seed, preserving the human shape, without intervening rot or the dissolution of the matter, petrifies the whole thing through and through—the bones as well as the skin. 5. That the petrifying seed consists solely in a **stony odor** <note>In van Helmont's chemistry, an "odor" is a spiritual, invisible vapor that carries the formative "image" or instructions of a seed.</note>, which is an incorporeal and invisible **ferment**. 6. That the matter of the tooth is not merely bony, but is a "neutral" substance between bone and rock. And therefore the tooth, by its contact, eventually petrifies whatever has stuck to it too stubbornly, whether that mush came from bread, meat, vegetables, fish, fruit, or legumes. That is to say, even though the food has no disposition toward petrification in or of itself. This seed, I say, notably deceived **Paracelsus** and his followers under the name of **tartar**. For dental stone does not dissolve in boiling water like [wine] tartar does;

...nor is its generation akin to the **tartar found in wine**; rather, it is a neutral animal petrification: arising, clearly, from the odor and the stone-forming seed, which the pulp adhering to the teeth absorbs through contact. Speculation on horns belongs here. For the **cow’s horn**, like the hooves—or shoes—of cattle and flocks, are simply and properly made of **horny matter**. The **stag's horn** <note>Antlers</note>, however, is partly made of **bony matter** and partly of **woody matter**; and for that reason, it mimics thorns and branches, falling off annually because it retains the properties of leaves and the woody part. **Ivory**, too, possesses a large portion of bone, and another portion that is stony, or tooth-like. 

8. Though many things may turn to stone in springs, this does not happen except through a remarkable **petrifying odor**. For this reason, whatever things are petrified are transformed by the odor of the place; it is not that the rock, of its own accord, produces a seed like a biological parent. 

9. And for that reason, the original seed of rock was created directly by the **Creator** and established in certain places in sufficient quantity to last until the end of the world. 

10. If petrification does not require the fabricated concept of **Tartar** <note>Van Helmont is arguing against the Paracelsian idea that all stones come from food residues ("tartar").</note>, then surely the generation of diseases requires it much less. 

11. <margin>14</margin> Certain insects (most notably the **toad**) may be born within the rocks themselves, yet they do not petrify, as almost anything else would; this is because they receive a vital <term>Archeus</term> <note>The "inner master" or spiritual life-force that governs the growth and form of a living being.</note> through a process of separation from the rocks themselves—just as **pyrite** or **metal** is separated from the rocky veins in which they are born, preserving their meltable matter untouched. Therefore, that separated Archeus remains unconquerable by the stony seed. 

12. <margin>15</margin> The axiom that "no form can be introduced except from a pre-existing disposition of matter" is false. For stone is formed immediately from subjects that are even diverse in kind, without any melting of the matter. Indeed, when **Pharaoh’s magicians** saw the <term>Cyniphes</term> <note>original: "Cyniphes" — referring to the gnats or lice produced from the dust of the earth in the third plague of Egypt (Exodus 8).</note> created immediately from the dust of the earth, which...

<header>ON THE FORMATION OF STONES</header>

# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"</note>

...they recognized the immediate offspring of water, and they exclaimed: *This is the finger of God* <note>A reference to Exodus 8:19, where Pharaoh’s magicians admit they cannot replicate the plague of gnats.</note>. This was because they were unable to imitate this effect. For while the return of earth into water is most difficult, they recognized that it was a far narrower thing for **gnats** <note>original: "Cyniphes"</note> to be made from the dust of the earth than for a **serpent** to come from a rod (and vice versa), or **frogs** and **blood** from water. Recognizing this difficulty, Satan did not say, "*Command that this bread turn to stone*" (*for this occurs in nature immediately*), "*but that these stones become bread*." From this, he would surely have tested the omnipotence of Christ. Indeed, just as shells and stony crusts are armored from the **decay** <note>original: "fracedine," referring to a type of damp fermentation or mushy state Helmont believes precedes generation.</note> of the sea floor when a petrifying seed arises—whose seed is propagated not so much by sexual union as by the decay of the floor itself, and thus their progeny grows onto the shells from the outside—so too there are other insects whose <term>Archeus</term> <note>The internal vital spirit or "master-governor" of a living thing.</note> could not be encrusted or overcome by the stony seed. For the same reason, the **toad** wards off all external petrification that would be troublesome to it. Nevertheless, worms of this kind are not sufficient for **curing the stone** <note>original: "medendo calculo," referring to kidney or bladder stones.</note>. This is because their "final life" (under which the resistance against the stony seed is hidden) has vanished before they can be taken up for medicinal use.

<margin>16</margin> Furthermore, if a decaying stony odor falls into the "vegetable juice" of the earth (which **Paracelsus** calls <term>Leffas</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the vital sap or juice of the earth that feeds plants.</note>), layers of stone arise beneath the earth. A **human being**, too, if confined in a place of such decay, is first suffocated by the stony odor; then, as that odor pervades the arteries and solid parts, it transforms the corpse into rock before it can rot. For in this way, the earth penetrates the vegetable juice with a stony odor, and a **stony plant** rises up, as if from a transplanted vegetable seed—as is evident in **coral** and its **moss**. But from where would the **fetus** mentioned by **Paré** <note>Ambroise Paré (c. 1510–1590), a famous French surgeon who described a "lithopaedion" or stone baby.</note> have drawn the odor of a stony seed? It did not happen primarily by the power of a stony seed; rather, the **transplantation** was caused by the power of the pregnant mother, who too intently admired a stone statue. Otherwise, once the fetus was established...

<meta>catchword: enecans</meta>

...fetus, and once it had been transmuted into stone, a stony odor then flowed out from it. By this process, it happened that the mother's womb, almost entirely, along with that fetus, was petrified. For just as smoke penetrates and stains meat soaked in salt and pressed together, from the outer surface even to the very center, so too does a stony odor penetrate flesh. <margin>17</margin> This occurs so that a true mineral stone is made from a corpse, having nothing at all in common with a <term>Calculus</term> <note>A stone formed inside the body, such as a kidney stone; Van Helmont distinguishes these from "true" mineral stones.</note>; and therefore, following <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>The famous 16th-century Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note>, I shall henceforth call this <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term Paracelsus used specifically for a painful, stony mass in the body that resists normal medical treatment.</note>, because of its singular nature and properties compared to other stones.

But smoke, although it stains flesh, does not transmute it. Therefore, it is clear enough from this that not every odor leads to the transmutation of a thing, but only that one to which a ferment is added, whence the odor becomes entirely pregnant with seed. <margin>18</margin> Things are therefore petrified naturally by their own seed, but they are petrified in a plainly monstrous way when they are substances of a foreign kind, acted upon by a "pilgrim" seed of the place <note>Van Helmont suggests that a "seed" belonging to the environment can "colonize" and transform a biological body into stone.</note>. 

In this way, stagnant waters encrust shells that touch the bottom because of the <note>original: "fracidum"</note> decayed odor of the floor. This does not happen to the insects swimming in the water. For this reason, running waters mostly lack such little creatures. Crabs, too, are found only in stony places, because other locations are destitute of the ferment of stone. 

Around the year 1320, within Russia and Tartary, at a latitude of 64 degrees, not far from the marsh of <note>original: "Kitaya," likely referring to Lake Kitay or the region of Cathay as understood by early explorers.</note> Cathay, it is read that the **Horde of the Bashkirs** was completely transmuted into stones, along with their whole herd, chariots, and weaponry. And even today, it is said that the men, camels, horses, flocks, and every accompanying kind of chariot and armament still stand there, turned to stone under the open sky—a horrific spectacle. If a miracle be absent from the cause, then certainly that whole region is nothing but a continuous rock, passable by cracks, from which—when the wind has been silent for many days and the air above is pressed down—a powerful, decayed, stony odor (such as is seen in certain tunnels or mines) [emanates]...

<summary>Van Helmont explains that petrification is caused by a "stony odor" or ferment that penetrates organic matter like smoke. He cites a historical account of an entire Mongolian horde in 1320 being turned to stone as evidence of this environmental "seed" at work.</summary>

<keywords>petrification, Duelech, Paracelsus, Bashkir Horde, transmutation, stony odor, mineral stone, ferments</keywords>

<header>ON LITHIASI <note>The medical condition of stone formation in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note></header>

(a killing odor) was able to breathe out, and its traveling vapors were able to kill in a single night; this odor finally, because of the coldness of the place which inhibited rot, transmuted the creatures it had killed long before. It is no different than those people of Pergamon in the crypts, or the glove found in the fountain. Therefore, the drinking of such springs is extremely unhealthy. This is because it inclines the <term>Archeum</term> <note>The internal "master workman" or vital force that Van Helmont believed governed the body's functions.</note> toward a stony nature, plagues it with *tormina* <note>Severe, twisting intestinal pains or colic.</note>, shortens life, and thus kills the heart-region [praecordia] before they are even changed into stone by the drinking. In the Red Monastery of the Sonian Forest near Brussels, and in the sanctuary of the church, certain springs breathe out [vapors] that deposit stones against the wall, contrary to the proverb:
<margin>19</margin> *A drop hollows out a stone by falling often.* <note>original: "Gutta cavat lapides saepe cadendo."</note> For with a hook and an axe, they frequently knock off the stones that have grown there. The monks, however, complain that they suffer from *Strophos* <note>A Greek term for twisting abdominal pain.</note>, or *tormina*, unless they use the seed of the wild carrot <note>Daucus carota.</note> boiled into their beer every day. Insofar as the odor of the wild carrot seed masters and suppresses the stony odor. Therefore, let the beginners [Tyrones] learn that stones have their own seeds no less than other things do in their "middle life," <note>Van Helmont's term for the state of a substance while it is actively governed by its internal vital principle.</note> under the cloak of a fermenting odor—and not in some tartaric coagulum of matter.

## CHAPTER II.

1. *The thoughtlessness of the Schools.* <note>The "Schools" refers to the established medical authorities of the time who followed traditional Galenic or Aristotelian teachings.</note> 2. *The material thought to be <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term coined by Paracelsus to describe a hard, stony mass formed within the body that is distinct from common sediment.</note>, and its effect.* 3. *The causes proposed by the Ancients are rejected.* 4. *A false calculation deceived the Schools, by which they believed the effect to be the cause itself.* 5. *The progress of human nature is the same everywhere.* 6. *The error of the Schools regarding the causes of Duelech is proven.* 7. *Some instances of the thoughtlessness, harshness, and laziness of the Schools.* 8. *A refutation of the Schools regarding the efficient cause of Duelech itself.* 9. *Arguments derived from the senses.* 10. *Duelech is formed from the urine itself, and not from things contained within it that are distinct from the urine.* 11. *The consequences of ignorance regarding the causes.* 12. *The author's weariness.* 13. *The author's mechanical demonstrations, rejecting the causes assigned to Duelech by the Schools.* 14. *An axiom opposed to the Schools.* 15. *The vanity of the theory of tartar in the stone [calculus].* <note>Van Helmont famously argued against the common theory that kidney stones were formed like the "tartar" (sediment) found in wine barrels.</note> 16. *Pray and it shall be given.*

<page-num>31</page-num>

-> # CHAPTER II. <-

<margin>1</margin>
Having long ago and elsewhere conquered and cleared away the **Horde of Tartars** <note>Paracelsus used the term "Tartar" to describe stony precipitates and crusts that form in the body, comparing them to the hard deposits found on the inside of wine barrels.</note>, which, under the leadership of Paracelsus, had invaded the realm of diseases, we must now do battle in this place with the dogmas of Galen regarding the causes of <term>Duelech</term> <note>A specific Paracelsian term for a stone formed within the human body, such as a kidney or bladder stone.</note>. Truly, the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities of the time, which strictly followed the teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note>, having forgotten the fourfold nature of natural causes <note>Aristotle's four causes: Material (what it's made of), Efficient (who/what makes it), Formal (its essence), and Final (its purpose).</note>, remember only two concerning the generation of Duelech. And so they agree with me at least in the name and the number of causes, but not in the reality of the thing itself. Since they teach that *matter* and the *efficient cause* are the parents of the stone. Thus, driven by their own conscience, they either deny the *form* and the *end* as causes, or they treat the stone insufficiently, or finally, they exclude Duelech from the series of natural things. Indeed, since they want every efficient cause to be external, they leave it to their students to conclude that Duelech is naturally constituted by and depends only upon an external efficient cause.

<margin>2</margin>
Therefore, the Schools call the matter of the stone a certain mucilage, which they call viscous or **mucous phlegm** <note>original: "mucous pituita"</note>. They claim, however, that the efficient cause of the stone is heat—both the external heat of the bed, etc., and the internal heat of the poorly affected organ itself. In this, right at the threshold, they desert their own patron <note>Galen</note>, who denies an internal efficient cause in the study of physics. Thus, [they claim] Duelech is produced by heat alone.

<margin>3</margin>
I, on the other hand, [say] the opposite. Through mechanics I have shown that no mucilage, as such, ever is, was, or will be the matter from which stones are made. But if that slime <note>original: "mucago"</note> is sometimes caught by the true matter of the stone and enclosed within it, it indeed **turns to stone** <note>original: "petrescit"</note> by the seed of Duelech, along with the proper matter of Duelech; yet it does not do so because of the mucilage itself, or because it is sticky or viscous.

<margin>4</margin>
For what deceived the Schools most of all was their indistinct observation of experience. For they saw the mucous urine of those who already carried a stone in the bladder; and immediately stopping further investigation there, victo- <note>The word continues on the next page as "victoriam" (victory).</note>

<summary>Van Helmont critiques the "Schools" of medicine for claiming that stones are formed simply from phlegm and heat. He argues that mucus is not the cause of stones, though it may be trapped within them during the petrification process.</summary>

<header>ON THE FORMATION OF STONES <note>original: "DE LITHIASI"</note></header>

...they shouted victory, and rejoiced as if they had found the next and immediate cause of the stone. Truly, the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities following Galenic tradition.</note> are wretched first; but the sick are much more wretched. For if they had looked behind them only once, they would have easily seen that when the stone is properly cut out, that mucus and the grit <note>original: "saburram," referring to the sediment or "ballast" often found in the urine of stone-sufferers.</note> which was usual before soon ceases to appear in the urine of that patient. From this, the Schools could have known for certain that if that mucus—which is excreted while the stone is still present—were some kind of cause or the material of the stone, then it would either be produced by the bladder itself, or by the stone, or be sent to the bladder from elsewhere. If it were sent from elsewhere, then even after the <term>Duelech</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for a stone formed within the body.</note> was cut out, it ought still to be produced, sent, and excreted daily; for the cutting and removal of the stone only affects the bladder: it in no way affects a distant part that would otherwise be breeding and sending continuous streams of mucus. If, however, this kind of mucus proceeded from the stone or from the bladder itself, it will by no means be the cause: but rather the *effect* of the stone, presupposing the stone's presence.

For the bladder is damaged in its own internal "digestion" by such a cruel and troublesome guest as the stone: on which account, being unable to endure it, the bladder continually "weeps" <note>Van Helmont uses the poetic "plorat" (weeps) to describe the discharge of fluids from an irritated organ.</note> a part of its nourishment which remains undigested, because it cannot complete or process it; and therefore it draws in new nourishment successively. Therefore, that mucus is not the *matter* from which the stone is made, but is a mournful effect of it. And for that reason, they wrongly accuse that mucilage of being the material of the stone. They see it, yet they do not know what they have seen. They call it **Phlegm** <note>original: "Pituitam." In traditional medicine, phlegm was one of the four humors; Helmont argues here it is simply failed nutrition.</note>, meaning one of the four primary humors arising in the "sanguification" <note>The process of turning food into blood.</note>: which is the final nourishment in digestion, intended for the solid parts and the "spermatic" <note>Meaning the basic structural or formative tissues of the body.</note> nutrients, but which has degenerated from the totally digested blood along the way, due to the poor condition of the part to be fed. For the stone has nothing vital in itself, nor does it have anything outside itself that it gives, or rouses from its own "seed," to create mucilage. And much

# CHAPTER II:

...even less does nature solicit or intend the increase of the stone, such that it would think of procreating from its own continuous nourishment, whereby it might increase and strengthen its own enemy and internal destruction: especially since the direction of nature depends on an intelligence that does not err. Indeed, the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities following the teachings of Galen and Avicenna.</note>, if they were ever moved by any charity toward their neighbor or a desire for knowledge, should have at least glanced at several similar examples. For instance, a speck in the eye provokes continuous tears against one's will. The ethmoid bone <note>A porous bone at the top of the nasal cavity that the ancients believed filtered waste from the brain.</note> when blocked with mucus, provokes a continuous flow in a cold <note>original: "coryza"</note> and pours out phlegm. **Angina** <note>A general term for a sore or inflamed throat.</note> also froths into a failing and slimy saliva; just as **dysentery** drips its own mucus from the intestine along with blood.

They would have easily seen then that mucus is formed by the bladder in this way, when it is occupied by a <term>Duelech</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for a stone formed within the body from "stony" salts.</note>, and it flows forth continuously. It is not, however, that the tear is the cause of the speck in the eye; or that the poured-out watery liquid blocks the spongy bone in the forehead; or that the slimy saliva creates **Angina**. For it is a perpetual rule of the whole body, that when a limb is injured, or by the power of its inhabitant <note>The "Archeus," or vital spirit that Van Helmont believed governed the functions of each organ.</note>, its functions go astray, and its **digestion** <note>In Helmontian medicine, "digestion" refers to the internal chemical processing of nutrients within a specific organ to maintain its health.</note> is immediately corrupted. Its nourishment—otherwise life-giving—usually degenerates so that, while it does not turn into a formative substance, it deflects at least into a mucous nature. 

Thus the bladder "weeps" the continuous mucus of its defiled nourishment while the stone is present; and it stops when the stone is absent. Therefore, by citing such mucus, they [the Schools] strive too frivolously to prove that the material cause of the stone is that which is effectively produced by accident and occasion *after* the stone is already formed. Finally, if such a slimy substance <note>original: "muccago"</note> born in the urine were the material from which stones are made, and heat were its proper efficient cause, and both these sufficient causes were present; then certainly, since when sufficient causes are...

<header>ON THE STONE DISEASE</header>

<meta>catchword: con-</meta>

...acting, the effect would follow of necessity and without excuse. Therefore, by necessity, all such **mucus** <note>original: "mucago"; a thick, slimy secretion.</note> would soon become a stone within the bladder. This would be no different than how milk is uniformly and entirely curdled by rennet. In this way, the bladder would be immediately filled with a single, solid stone. Otherwise, it would be false to say that once the causes required for a thing's constitution are present, it is necessary for that thing to be made or to exist. 

However, in the terms we are discussing, this mucus—which is continuously present (or at least appears in succession) under the heat of the bladder—does not turn entirely into a stone (as would otherwise be required) according to its similar, uniform, and homogeneous nature. Instead, it is all excreted. Therefore, the two constitutive causes of the stone assigned by the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical establishment following Galen and Avicenna.</note> can be neither true nor sufficient. 

<margin>7</margin> Wherefore I wonder greatly at such laziness in investigation, and that in so many passing centuries, no one has possessed the curiosity to take that mucus excreted from the bladder and dry it out even once with a degree of heat. For he would have learned, and known for certain, whether a stone would ever come of it, or rather some crumbly <term>tophus</term> <note>A porous, stony deposit, often associated with gout or mineral buildup; Van Helmont distinguishes this from a true "Duelech" or bladder stone.</note>, just as if nasal mucus were dried on a plate. 

It is therefore intolerable that none of the Professors until now has warmed **urine** <note>original: "lotium"</note>, together with the aforementioned mucus, at a proper temperature, so that he might have learned that the stone grows not from the mucus, but indeed from the urine itself, having condensed in the <term>chamber pots</term> <note>original: "matulis"; the vessels used for collecting and observing urine.</note>. 

I am rightly indignant that in matters of such importance—from which an almost hellish sentence <note>He refers to the "hellish" pain of the stone and the failure of doctors to cure it.</note> nevertheless hangs over the **Schools**—a lack of charity, indeed the very denial of science, is proven. They have never before considered that as long as one is alive, nothing can ever be dried out or parched within the bladder; nor is the action of heat ever required for the hardening of the stone, such that the watery parts are consumed while the thicker parts, by that same process, are more stubbornly condensed. For otherwise, if they suppose the necessity of an efficient heat, like that of lime, which by its own maturity,

### C H A P T E R II

...the stone might grow, fostered by heat. The **Academies** <note>The traditional medical universities of the time.</note> now confuse themselves, while they see clear and transparent **urine** <note>original: "urinam"</note> deposit its crusts in the cold and in **chamber pots** <note>original: "matulis"; vessels used for collecting urine for medical inspection.</note>. I say they see stones ripen without heat; they even see the urine of healthy people fix sands and scales to the chamber pots. Nor does this same thing happen if, in a closed vessel, the urine is kept warm very thickly all day long. Therefore, it is a sign of ignorance that, despite all the clarity of the **Physicians** <note>original: "Medentum"</note>, the difference has not yet been grasped between the coagulation of flint in a spring or river, and the drying of mud caused by heat. 

Learn therefore, **Schools** <note>The medical establishment following ancient authorities like Galen.</note>, from me—a useless and most minor novice—that the heat of the loins is merely an <margin>§</margin> occurrence: but it is not the triggering cause of the stone or the adhering sand. That is, the stone does not come from the heat, but the heat comes from the stone; just as in a finger, heat arises from an embedded thorn, but the thorn is not created there by the heat. For you have heard the wailing of **strangury** <term>strangury</term> <note>Painful, frequent, drop-by-drop urination, often caused by stones or irritation.</note>, but not of heat, in a stone of the bladder; just as you have heard other complaints of heat in the **stone disease of the kidneys** <term>lithiasis of the kidneys</term>. 

Wherefore if heat were the efficient cause of the stone, the complaints would be far greater in a stone of the bladder. <margin>9</margin> This is because here, on account of its greater hardness, the stone would be the offspring of a greater heat and drying than in the kidneys. And even more so because the stone swims in a nearly continuous liquid, where it does not hinder the urine gliding past at all. Truly, a bladder stone would have need of violent heat. For acrimony, burning, and pain are sought after as evidence. But these are not felt in the "nest" of the stone, as they are in the glans of the penis. 

Therefore, boys know how to distinguish the sense and location of acrimony and pain, but the Schools do not. Indeed, even if the urine seems biting and sharp, burning like fire as in **strangury**, yet when excreted, it is not at all hotter or sharper to the taste, or saltier than usual or proper. The burning and acrimony of the urine are only apparent; not indeed...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that heat is a symptom rather than the cause of bladder stones, noting that stones can form in cold chamber pots and that the heat felt by patients is an inflammatory response to the stone's presence.</summary>

-> ON THE STONE DISEASE <-
<note>original: "D E L I T H I A S I"</note>

<meta>catchword: se</meta>

not from true heat, or due to some acrimony of the **urine** <note>original: "lotii"</note>: but solely, on account of a foreign ferment of some tiny acidity committed to it. Which **strangury** <term>strangury</term> <note>Painful, frequent urination, often in drops.</note> teaches, when contracted from new beers that are still fermenting from acid. For that reason, when **Mace** or **saffron** is taken (which are sharp and warm, indeed reaching the place itself if they are to help, and thus testifying to their presence in the urine by their smell), the aforementioned burning usually ceases. For it is a **Philosophical principle** <note>Van Helmont refers to the "Chemical Philosophy," which sought to understand nature through laboratory observation rather than ancient texts.</note> that the stone increases by the same causes by which it originates, and vice versa. But the stones deposited in our **chamber pots** <note>original: "matulis"</note> confirm that the stone is formed naturally, at least without the actual heat of the pot or the surroundings; that is, heat is not required for its formation. Therefore, the stone is formed and increased materially from the urine: but not from vital mucus: nor does it require heat as an efficient cause: much less an excess of it. For **mucus** <note>original: "mucago"</note> is not seen to be cast out unless a stone is already present in the bladder; and thus the cause would have arrived late, occurring after its own effect.

For I have observed that if someone were to urinate through a thick cloth, and find no mucus in it: yet after a few hours, that same strained or filtered urine in a clean glass vessel yielded a thin, reddish **sand** <note>original: "sabulum"</note>, adhering equally to the sides, nor had it fallen more copiously toward the bottom than it adhered around the sides. And this occurred in a cold environment. Wherefore, even from this anyone ought to be certain that this sand did not exit with the urine at the start of urination (because it was not yet "born") nor was it actually present in the urine. For otherwise it would have remained caught in the cloth, however thin, like the atoms of **potter's clay** <note>original: "terrae figulinae"</note>. Or if the cloth, not being dense enough, had failed to catch it: at least it would have soon fallen to the bottom in the form of sand or sediment, and not in such grains, while forming, and with such equality of distance...

## CHAPTER II.

[it would have attached] itself to the sides of the vessel. Because it would have lacked the **glue** <note>original: "glutine"</note> by which it could have adhered to itself. Finally, since this **sand** <note>original: "sabulum" — referring to the gritty sediment found in urine</note> lacks glue across its entire surface, except for that part where it sticks to the **chamber pot** <note>original: "matulae"</note>: it is clear enough that in the very instant this sand is formed, it is also simultaneously attached. From this, anyone should be more certain (if they had ever labored to investigate the truth) that since this sand applied itself to the glass of its own accord—even long after **urination** <note>original: "emictionem"</note>—it was generated in the immediate instant before its attachment. Once attached, however tiny in itself, it later grew through additions. These additions occur by a "common nature" in the swelling **urine** <note>original: "lotio"</note>, and not by some particular atom of sand that attaches itself to the vessel. From this, it also withdrew evenly and simultaneously from all the urine. From this so ordinary and daily mechanism, if health were truly at the heart of the **Schools** <note>Van Helmont refers to the traditional medical universities of his time, which he believed were stuck in outdated Greek theories.</note>, they should have known several centuries ago (not indeed by some far-fetched argument from similarity, but strictly from the identity of the urine and the **calculous sand** <note>original: "calculosæ arenæ"</note>) that this sand had hardened from the matter of the urine—the same matter, clearly, from which the stone is made. This happens even in the absence of **mucus** <note>original: "mucilagine"</note> and the heat of the body (for the tin urinal stands in the cold air), and it is likewise beyond the suspicion of **Lithiasis** <term>lithiasis</term> <note>The medical condition of forming stones in the kidney or bladder.</note> or the weakness of the person urinating (for any blameless urine of healthy people generates this sand and coats the urinals). Therefore, by the same causes, the sand and the stone arise in us who suffer from the stone as they do outside of us: even in the healthy, the urine becomes granular in the glass.

<margin>II</margin>
Having seen this for myself, I sighed deeply, and knew for certain that the **Schools** had erred in their knowledge of causes, and continue to stumble to this day in the healing of the stone, which they nevertheless rashly assume and presume to treat. I grieved immensely at the stupors and false inventions of so many centuries; and even more, the unlucky...

He asserts that the stone forms through a spontaneous chemical hardening of the urine itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of the medical establishment. <page-num>38</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE</header>

# ON THE STONE

<margin>12</margin> ...the obedience of the sick, the strict ties of patients, the pains, the deaths, the premature destruction of families, and finally the plundering of widows and orphans, had occurred under the hands of untrustworthy and ignorant assistants who deceive people under the title of doctors <note>original: "medentûm"</note>. It was then that I seriously recognized that I knew nothing, for I had learned my lessons from those who knew nothing. Disgusted by my long-held ignorance born of blind presumption, I cast away my books—perhaps two hundred gold pieces' worth of books—and gave them away to students (though I wish I had burned them instead). I was completely resolved to abandon such an ignorant profession, especially since it was also full of deceit. 

Finally, one night, having been woken from sleep, I thought: *No disciple is above his master.* <note>original: "nullus discipulus, supra suum Magistrum esset" — a reference to the Gospel of Matthew 10:24.</note> Yet I turned over in my mind how many of my fellow students had surpassed their own teachers. But the truth of that scripture was brought before me: *That indeed he watches and builds in vain, unless the Lord works with him.* <note>original: "frustra vigilaret, & ædificaret, nisi Dominus cooperaretur" — a reference to Psalm 127:1.</note> Thus, I likewise knew that we teach anyone in vain, unless the Teacher of all truth <note>original: "Magister veritatis" — Van Helmont often refers to a divine or inner light as the only true source of medical knowledge.</note> teaches them from within at the same time—a Teacher whom no disciple has ever surpassed. 

I therefore sought, for a long time and in earnest, how I might acquire knowledge of the **stone** <note>original: "calculi"</note> from this Master. For I recognized most clearly that the Authors <note>Referring to classical medical authorities like Galen or Avicenna, whose theories Helmont rejected.</note> did not possess even the smallest spark of light, and consequently, they could not give knowledge to me. 

<margin>13</margin> I confessed that I was a great sea of ignorance and an abyss of manifold darkness, and that I almost lacked all light, except for a single spark left to me so that I might recognize that I knew nothing. And so, although I prayed frequently, I would immediately lose heart. 

Finally, weighing myself in the balance, I found that I myself was free from the **stone** <term>calculo</term> <note>Specifically, a kidney or bladder stone.</note>. For I had never felt pain in my kidneys, nor had I noticed even a single grain of grit. Nevertheless, I had repeatedly seen that clinging **sand** <term>sabulum</term> <note>The gritty sediment Van Helmont discusses as the precursor to a solid stone.</note> in the **chamber pot** <term>matula</term> <note>A vessel used for collecting urine for medical inspection.</note>, though without any stickiness, or the discomfort of heat or pain.

<summary>Van Helmont describes his radical break from traditional medicine, recounting how he gave away his library and sought divine inspiration to understand kidney stones after realizing that academic authorities were "ignorant" and "deceitful."</summary>
<keywords>medical education, spiritual medicine, kidney stones, urine sediment, chamber pot, religious revelation, medical ethics</keywords>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II.</header>

## CHAPTER II.

...of local color. <note>original: "loris topici" - likely the end of "coloris topici" from the previous page, referring to the "local heat" or "color" that traditional doctors blamed for ailments.</note> For I wondered why, once the <term>urine</term><note>original: "lotio", referring here to discharged urine.</note> had been poured out, <term>sand</term><note>original: "sabulum", the gritty sediment Van Helmont investigated as the precursor to kidney stones.</note> adhered to the <term>chamber pot</term><note>original: "matula", a vessel for collecting urine.</note>, and was so firmly attached at such regular intervals that it defied any idea that the matter had existed in that form before falling out of the body. 

It once happened that I was conversing with noblewomen, the wives of lords, and even with the Queen herself, from three in the afternoon until three the following morning in **London** at **Whitehall** palace. For it was the eve of the <term>revelries</term><note>original: "bacchanalium", referring to the festive celebrations and parties typical of the court.</note> during **Epiphany** <note>The feast of the Epiphany, January 6th.</note>. Now, I had the urge to urinate while these women were first leading me with them to the royal palace; therefore, for the sake of propriety, I held my urine for at least 12 hours. 

Then, having returned home, I could not find even an atom of sand in my urine, even with the most careful inspection. For I had feared that, because it had been held much longer than usual and "cooked" <note>Van Helmont is testing the Galenic theory that body heat "cooked" or "baked" fluids into stones (coction).</note> by my body heat, it would by then be grainy. Therefore, being curious, I urinated through a cloth. But the urine was free from all sand. 

Therefore, the next morning, I urinated new urine through a linen cloth: and I kept it in a glass chamber pot for the same number of hours (namely, 12). And at last, I saw clearly sand adhering, evenly dispersed wherever the urine had stood. Finally, after the urine was poured out, I touched that sand with my finger. 

And having been taught by my own experience, I concluded to myself: that because the urine I had held for 12 hours did not contain sand, nor had I expelled any myself; and yet at another time, in a shorter span of day, sand had condensed in my urine and attached to the glass vessel during the cold of **January**. 

I knew for a certain fact that the thickness of the matter was by no means required for that sand, and that the heat of the body part in no way caused the <term>stone</term><note>original: "calculi", referring to a kidney or bladder stone.</note> to harden. From then on, having gone home, I cast away from me the doctrine of the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional academic medical establishment based on ancient Greek theories.</note>, and immediately the truth took hold of me. For, being confirmed and no longer wavering in doubt, I believed with certain conviction that internal, se-

<summary>Van Helmont recounts a personal experiment at Whitehall Palace where he discovers that urine held inside the body does not form "sand" or sediment, debunking the contemporary theory that body heat "cooks" urine into kidney stones.</summary>

->ON THE DISEASE OF THE STONE<-

...the <term>seminal cause</term><note>original: "seminalis causa"; Van Helmont believed diseases were caused by "seeds" or spiritual blueprints rather than simple physical imbalances.</note> of stones would be unknown to mortals. 

Therefore, with great courage, having grown disgusted with all the books of writers, I cast them away once more and renounced them utterly. Nor did I decide to look for help in my calling from anywhere else than from the **Father of Lights**, the only Master of Truth. And immediately I rejected all accidental causes and the mockeries of <term>tartar</term><note>In early chemistry, tartar referred to the crusty deposits in wine barrels; Paracelsus used this as an analogy for deposits in the body.</note>: likewise, whatever relates more to human artifice than to the course of nature. 
<margin>14</margin> For I knew that nature never operates primarily through heat or cold in things such as <term>seminal transformations</term><note>The process by which a "seed" or life-force changes matter into a specific form, like a stone.</note>—even though she is not infrequently 
<margin>15</margin> forced to use them as stimulants or hindrances for internal agents. Thus I recognized the vain fictions of **Paracelsus** regarding Tartar, which he invented at least so that he might be considered the first to have introduced the generation of the <term>stone</term><note>original: "calculi"</note> into the entire nature of bodies and diseases, through a fictitious history of stones based on the likeness of wine-tartar. For even if he perfectly cured <term>Duelech</term><note>A specialized term used by Paracelsus to describe a specific stony deposit in the body.</note> (as his epitaph claims), he nevertheless did not attain a theoretical understanding equal to his powerful use of the <term>secret remedy</term><note>original: "arcani", referring to an 'arcanum,' a highly concentrated chemical medicine.</note>. For in this way many experiments wander among <term>idiots</term><note>original: "idiotas"; here meaning unlearned laypeople or practitioners without formal academic training who nevertheless have practical cures.</note>, who yet do not know their causes. 

Therefore, the help of books was lacking, and the voice of a living person to teach me face-to-face was lacking; yet I knew that *woe to the man who trusts in man* <note>original: "væ homini, qui in homine confidit", a reference to the biblical verse Jeremiah 17:5.</note>. Good God, comforter of the poor in spirit, closer to no one than to him who, with full freedom, resigns himself and his affairs to your most placid will; since You, O **Father of Lights**, illuminate no one more kindly than him who, recognizing the humility of his own nothingness, trusts in the good pleasure of Your mercy alone. Grant, O profound **Master of Sciences**, that I may rather become poor in spirit than heavy with much knowledge. Grant me, freely, an intellect purely seeking You, and a will clinging to You. Illuminate my darkness of nothingness as much as You will; and no more:

<header>ON THE FORMATION OF STONES</header>
<meta>catchword: -telligi (likely "intelligi," to be understood)</meta>

# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES

1. **I**n our furnaces <note>A reference to the laboratory; alchemists often referred to their work as "the philosophy of the furnace."</note> we have learned that there is no more certain way of knowing in nature—for understanding through the radical and constitutive causes of things—than when one knows what, and how much, is contained within each thing. Indeed, the knowledge and connection of causes do not stand more clearly than when you have unlocked the things themselves in such a way that they come forward in person and, as it were, speak with you. For since real entities—standing only in their primordial <note>original: "primordialibus," meaning the very first or original states.</note> and succeeding principles of seeds, and thus in true substantial being—give knowledge and provide the cause for knowing the nature of bodies, their middle states, and their extremes. Indeed, they are the cause of generation, existence, and transformation according to their own root. Since (as **Raymond** <note>Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull), a 13th-century philosopher and alchemist whose works were highly influential to Van Helmont.</note> testifies) <margin>Testament, ch. 26.</margin>

*however much a **Logician** may have a profound intellect, capable of argument or naturally gifted regarding external things, yet he will never, through any reason that reaches the senses, be able to directly know or judge by what nature or virtue, through an inner strength, the multiplication of grain has the power to grow upon the earth—except by way of an analogy drawn from observation. Nor will he ever know how a seed sprouts in the earth, grows, and gathers fruit, unless he has first entered into our **natural Philosophy** <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science.</note> through experimental doctrine, and not that sophisticated, talkative kind which is born to Logicians through various fantastical presumptions. Those men, with their predictions of consequences that go against the force of nature, cause many to err stubbornly in a sophistication of the mind. Because through our **mechanical science** <note>term: "mechanicam scientiam" — here referring to experimental laboratory work and manual chemistry rather than abstract theory.</note>, the intellect is corrected by the force of experience, with respect to the eye and true mental knowledge. Indeed, our experiences stand above the fantastical proofs of conclusions, and therefore they do not even tolerate them; rather, they show that all other sciences enter vividly into the intellect. From which point, hereafter, in—* <note>The text ends mid-sentence on the catchword "telligi," part of the word "intelligi" (to be understood).</note>

->Testament, ch. 26.<-

<summary>Van Helmont argues that true scientific knowledge comes from laboratory experimentation ("the furnace") rather than abstract logic, citing Ramon Llull to support the idea that only "mechanical science" can reveal the inner workings of nature.</summary>

<header>C A P U T  III.</header>
<meta>Catchword: ter (likely "tertius" or "tertio" following "Distillavi tum, cohobando qua-")</meta>

### CHAPTER III.

...we understand by nature, from within, that which it is, and what kind of thing it is. Because through such knowledge, the **Understanding** stands stripped of the excesses and errors which usually remove it from the truth, due to the presumptions and prejudices believed in [logical] conclusions. For hence our [philosophers] directed themselves to enter, through any branch of knowledge, into all experience by means of art, according to the course of nature, in its own corresponding principles. For **Spagyria** <term>Spagyria: An early term for chemistry, particularly the art of separating and recombining the components of a substance to find its essence.</term> alone is the mirror of true **Understanding**: and it demonstrates how to touch and see the truths of things in a clear light. Nor does it require logical arguments, because they are too remote and distant from that clear light. And therefore the **Emerald Tablet** <note>The *Tabula Smaragdina*, a foundational hermetic text attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, which describes the "as above, so below" principle of the universe.</note> states: *Through this kind of demonstration, all obscurity shall flee from you, and there shall be acquired for you the strength of all strength, overcoming every subtle thing and penetrating every solid thing. Therefore I am called Hermes Trismegistus, having the three (that is, all) parts of the philosophy of the whole world's perfection <note>original: "telesmon," a Greek-derived alchemical term for the completion or "talisman" of the work.</note>.* So much for him. Therefore, between "praying" and "knocking," a middle ground is placed in the study of nature: "seeking by fire." <note>A play on the biblical "Ask, Seek, Knock," suggesting that the chemist "seeks" the truth through the heat of the furnace.</note>

<margin>2</margin> I indeed was hoping, by investigating the contents of urine, to know visually <note>original: "opticè," meaning through direct observation.</note>—and certainly not otherwise than through a true analysis of the urine. Therefore, I first kept my own urine in a wooden vessel until at last it spontaneously conceived a ferment and bubbled up just like wine; so much so that the ear could perceive the bubbling. Near the end of this, a little **burning water** <note>original: "aquæ ardentis," usually referring to spirits or alcohol produced by fermentation.</note> was distilled from it. From the residue, however, I collected a very white salt, of a sharp and foul urinous smell. I do not know whether, in the whole nature of things, there exists anything more subtle.

<margin>3</margin> A noble remedy for **Jaundice** <note>original: "Icterum"</note> and other diseases. I attempted through this salt to dissolve **Duelech** <term>Duelech: A term used by Paracelsus and Van Helmont to describe the hard, stony tartar or calculus that forms inside the human body.</term> in a glass vessel: but the result did not respond [as hoped].

<margin>4</margin> Again, for thirteen days, I let my own urine putrefy anew in **horse dung** <note>A common method in early chemistry to provide a gentle, constant heat for digestion.</note>, so that the dissimilar parts might tend toward the final separation of life. I then distilled it by **cohobating** <term>Cohobation: The process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid remains and re-distilling it to concentrate its properties.</term>...
