# ON THE STONE DISEASE

<meta>The text begins with the completion of a sentence from the previous page, starting with the word "thrice."</meta>

...thrice according to the prescription of **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note>: and I found frequent yellow and pointed crystals. Although these might be useful against old blockages <note>original: "oppilationes," a traditional medical term for obstructions of the internal vessels or organs.</note> of excrement, they contributed nothing against **Lithiasis** <term>Lithiasis: The medical condition of forming stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</term>.

<margin>5</margin>
Third, I mixed spirit of urine with dephlegmed <note>distilled to remove excess water</note> water of life <note>original: "aqua vitæ," refers here to concentrated alcohol or spirit of wine.</note>: and in a moment, both were coagulated together into a white mass <note>original: "offam albam." This reaction between ammonia and alcohol to form a solid is a famous experiment in the history of chemistry, often called "Van Helmont’s offa alba."</note>: yet it was strangely fleeting and subtle. My eye, above all, taught me there:
1. That the spirit of urine was an unequal and mighty coagulant: namely, because it was capable of coagulating the water of life.
2. That in the process of coagulating, it had separated a sluggish and watery part, which floated above the aforementioned white mass; perhaps in a manner not unlike how, in the coagulation of **Duelech** <term>Duelech: A Paracelsian term specifically denoting the stony matter that forms within the human bladder.</term>, it separates from the rest of the body of the urine, and thus completes its solidification in the midst of the waves.
3. That the coagulant, or spirit of urine, had bound itself indissolubly to the spirit of wine.
4. It is not a perpetual rule (though the schools offer it as an axiom of **Chemistry**) that every sharp coagulant would, by the same action, dissolve its own counterpart.
5. That the spirit of urine had not coagulated in the glass because of the powder of crushed **Duelech**: but had merely mixed and co-coagulated with the other spirit—namely, that of wine.
6. That if it had therefore encountered an earthy spirit, it would have contracted a marriage even with that, so that from both spirits, it would have made a stony body.
7. I learned, at least, how the spirit of urine could coagulate another spirit within the urine.
8. That an association of this kind is not a mere mixing of parts: but a marriage of indissoluble unity, a certain substantial transmutation, the production of a new entity from the agent and the patient into a neutral body. This experiment gave me the entrance to investigating **Lithiasis**. Yet I still remained wandering.

<margin>6</margin>
For after giving thanks, I moved myself into meditation: in how many ways things in the universe consist...

<summary>Van Helmont describes a chemical experiment mixing spirit of urine and alcohol to create a solid mass, which he uses as a laboratory model to explain how kidney stones (Duelech) might form in the body through "chemical marriage" rather than simple sedimentation.</summary>

### CHAPTER III

...were thickened. For ice immediately presented itself to me first. In this, water—out of a dread of the cold and by a primary action—encrusts itself; it is not, however, actively coagulated by the cold, as I have argued elsewhere in my writings on the Elements <note>Van Helmont believed that water was the primary element of all things and that cold was a "privation" or a specific impulse rather than just a temperature.</note>. Yet other bodies, which are believed to be mixtures, are occasionally frozen by the cold with that same zeal and consistency, so that they might reveal themselves as true fruits of water. For in this way, bones and a sword are broken more easily in the cold of winter than in summer. 

2. When the mixtures of **Salts** are not sufficiently dried out and are left in the cold, they are separated from the water and form grains, each according to its own kind and inborn property. 

3. If salts have overcome anything by eroding <note>original: "rodendo," literally gnawing or corroding</note> it, they pass from their native condition into a neutral state and are coagulated. In this way, for instance, **tartar of wine** <note>Potassium bitartrate, the sediment found in wine casks.</note>, **soap**, **borax**, etc., are coagulated. 

4. Then **Mucilages**, thickened by the "marriage" of seeds <term>Seeds (Semina): In Helmont's theory, these are the invisible, formative "blueprints" or vital sparks that direct matter to take a specific shape, like a stone or a plant.</term> and released from their own body, become glues, gums, pastes, and so on. 

5. But if a mucilage contains a fatty mixture, it is coagulated for both reasons. Thus do **aloe**, **onion-juice** <note>original: "cepola"</note>, **pitch**, **resin**, **ammoniacum**, **Frankincense**, **Myrrh**, **Mastic**, **opopanax**, **sarcocolla**, **asafoetida**, **elemi**, etc. <note>This is a list of various plant resins and gums used in early modern pharmacy and alchemy.</note> 

6. **Earth**, by converting salt and mucilage, becomes condensed and hardened if it is dried out. 

7. **Mineral salt** innate to the earth turns into stone—forming rocks, shells, and pots—through the process of roasting <note>original: "ustulando," referring to the application of high heat or calcination.</note>. 

8. If these are urged by an even stronger degree of heat, they eventually turn into glass. 

9. **Lessas** <note>A term coined by Van Helmont to describe the "juice of the earth" or the watery substance that provides the matter for plants.</note>, the watery part of the earth, is hardened into wood, herbs, and so on, by the power of the seeds. 

10. In this same way, water is petrified <note>turned to stone</note> by the force of a seed. 

11. Mucilage, when associated with dust, creates **tufa** <note>original: "tophos," a porous rock formed from volcanic ash or calcium deposits.</note>; but when combined with dust and lime, it then mimics various marbles. 

12. Whatever dissolved **lime** <note>original: "calx"</note> seizes, it coagulates along with itself. This is because there are two salts in lime: one is a lixivial alkali <note>a salt obtained by leaching ashes</note>, and the other is an acid. While these two are destroying one another, they are coagulated at the same time. 

13. **Metals**, **marcasites** <note>Iron sulfide minerals, often called "fool's gold."</note>, **sulfurs**, etc., obtain their own specific coagulations by the power of their seeds. 

14. Also, by an innate glue, they spontaneously grow-
->together<-

<summary>Van Helmont categorizes fourteen different ways that substances—including water, salts, resins, and minerals—transition from liquid to solid states. He emphasizes the role of "seeds" (formative principles) and the chemical interactions of salts as the primary drivers of this hardening.</summary>

<page-num>46</page-num>

# ON LITHIASIS
<note>original title: DE LITHIASI, referring to the formation of stones in the body.</note>

...many things [become firm], which afterwards harden by drying. Such as blood, cheese, egg white, varnish, etc.

15. Glass is a crafted stone, consisting of **alkali salt**. When it is melted by fire, it liquefies sand—or the powder of a stone that cannot be calcined <note>calcining involves heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</note> or otherwise fused—by corroding it; and together they are turned into a transparent mass. Therefore, limestone, or rock, is unsuitable for glass because of its acid salt. Because its lime destroys the vitrifying <note>glass-making</note> alkali, and it becomes a certain opaque neutral substance. Lime, therefore, contrary to the opinion of **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamum (129–216 AD), the ancient Greek physician whose traditional theories Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note>,
<margin>8</margin> differs greatly from ash. For ash separates the lye <note>a liquid solution of salts</note> from itself; but lime contains an acidity that cannot be separated from the whole. When this lime is finally burned by excessive fire, it is vitrified through its own lye-like part, making it unfit for building. According to **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a legendary figure in Arabic alchemy.</note>:
<margin>9</margin> that all fixed things are eventually turned into glass along with other vitrifying substances. Cheese also, having been coagulated by a moderate acid, is dissolved by a strong acid. For cheese shavings melt when mixed with dry quicklime, but not with the alkali of ashes. From all these aforementioned points, I have gathered that the coagulation of <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech is a specific term used by Van Helmont and Paracelsus to describe the unique, stony material of a kidney or bladder stone.</note> is unique and anomalous. Even lime gradually turns to stone in the middle of water, as its aforementioned salts coagulate one another. But the human body does not coagulate rock, nor does it tolerate lime in the bladder. Indeed, that wonderful coagulate has always remained before my eyes, by which—faster than the blink of an eye—the **spirit of urine** <note>likely an ammonia-rich solution</note> had condensed the **spirit of wine** <note>alcohol</note> into a mass. Therefore, I have perceived that all other coagulations have nothing in common with <term>Duelech</term>. On this account, I decided to examine the spirits. I first distilled horse urine. But indeed, its spirit lacked that coagulation. Therefore, I noted the uniqueness of human urine <note>original: "lotii humani"</note> with the greatest wonder. I then observed that the acid spirit of sulfurs or salts became earthy when combined with an alkalized body.
<margin>10</margin> Thus, for instance, with iron it becomes dross, rust, or verdigris,

<meta>catchword: go, (completing the word "aerugo" or verdigris)</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the formation of the "Duelech" (kidney stone) is a unique chemical process that differs from the formation of glass, lime, or other minerals. He highlights a specific experiment where spirit of urine and alcohol instantly solidify, noting that this phenomenon is unique to human urine.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R III.</header>

# CHAPTER III.

...white lead, etc. And **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a pioneering but controversial physician whose theories Van Helmont both admired and frequently corrected.</note> wrongly judges these to be **tartars**, or the separated impurities of things, covered by their own internal coagulating agent. Whereas in fact they are nothing but the collapse of two mutually reacting substances, with their strengths on both sides exhausted. Next, for some time I have examined salts through every kind of analysis; and I have found that the spirits of all salts <margin>11</margin> are acidic, except for those that are alkalized, and the spirits of essential sulfurs <note>sulfur: in Helmontian chemistry, this refers to the oily, combustible principle of a plant, not just the mineral sulfur.</note> in vegetables. Their saline sharp qualities are indeed oily and sulfurous, and cannot be readily reduced into a salt, except through a tedious inversion of their principles. These salts then, as if refined into an elixir, <margin>12</margin> represent the true and highest constitution <note>original: craſim, a term referring to the specific temperament or "mixture" of qualities that defines a substance.</note> of the solid seeds <note>seeds: or "seminal beginnings," the internal blueprints Van Helmont believed directed the growth and form of all matter.</note>. However, the spirit of human urine <note>original: lotii, an archaic term for urine.</note> is neither acidic nor alkalized, but merely salty, just like that of beasts of burden. And this is because the volatile acid of the stomach's chyle <note>chyle: the milky fluid produced from digested food in the stomach.</note> is transformed by the power of another ferment <margin>13</margin> <note>ferment: a vital agent or catalyst that Van Helmont believed was responsible for all biological transformations.</note> into a volatile salt. As I have discussed elsewhere regarding the digestions of animals. I note here in passing that in things transformed, there is no immediate return to that from which they were changed—no more than there is a return from deprivation to <unclear>possession</unclear> <note>original: habitum. Van Helmont is likely referencing an Aristotelian logic concept where the loss of a quality (privation) cannot be simply reversed to regain the previous state (habit).</note>. This is because in the process of transformation, the "final life" of the thing perishes; for almost the entire disposition of the "middle life" of the former being is taken away at the same time, due to the extinction of the former seed. For this reason, transformed things maintain the essence of the new being, ignoring the nature of the previous solid. Therefore, I have found useless any remedy that I had previously believed—likely because of its prior solid form—would dissolve a stone <note>original: calculi, referring to bladder or kidney stones.</note>. It is nevertheless true that the spirit of urine, at the fundamental point of its birth, is salty; and by reason of that salt, it coagulates other spirits more readily than any acid coagulates milk. Yet the spirit of urine does not coagulate milk or gore <note>original: cruorem, specifically thick or clotted blood.</note>, because the spirit of the blood—indeed, even our vital spirit—is salty, in the manner of urines. Hence, indeed, the spirit of urine behaves by way of
<meta>modum:</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that bladder stones are not "tartar" as Paracelsus claimed, but are formed through chemical transformations where the "seed" of a substance is fundamentally changed by biological ferments.</summary>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>ON LITHIASI <note>Lithiasi: From the Greek 'lithiasis,' meaning the formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note></header>

...like an excremental spirit separated from the blood; and thus, because of their shared affinity, it dwells alongside it, and they do not react against one another. Next, I also observed that the spirit of urine does not further solidify substances that have already been coagulated. For a lump of clay, mud, or stone, when treated with the spirit of urine, gradually degenerates into a **nitrous salt** <note>nitrous salt: likely referring to a reactive salt like saltpeter (potassium nitrate), which Van Helmont viewed as a cooling or dissolving agent.</note> and is dissolved instead. Since, therefore, the spirit of urine does not solidify what is already solid (such as clay or mud), nor substances that are capable of being solidified (such as milk and blood), but it *does* solidify the **spirit of wine** <note>spirit of wine: ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note> or similar substances that reside within the urine (for as stated above: after the fermentation of urine, the liquid also contains the spirit of wine, or **water of life** <note>original: aqua vitae</note>), I did not cease to investigate seriously how the stone <term>calculus|A hard mass or "stone" formed within the body from mineral salts.</term> is formed within us and within our urine.

<margin>14</margin> 1. First, it is certain that **Duelech** <note>Duelech: A term coined by the physician Paracelsus to specifically describe the stone formed in human organs.</note> is not of a calcined <note>calcined: the process of reducing a substance to powder or lime by heat.</note> or lime-like nature, despite what **Paracelsus** <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine, though Van Helmont often disagreed with his specific chemical theories.</note> argues to the contrary.
1. Because we lack the degree of heat required for calcination. 
2. Furthermore, every **alkali** <note>alkali: a basic salt; Van Helmont argues here that basic substances tend to break stones down rather than build them up.</note> is destructive to stone rather than something that binds it together. 
3. Because lime <note>original: calx</note> presupposes the existence of a stone; therefore, *Duelech* would have to be burnt into lime before it even became a stone. 
4. From the components that make up *Duelech*, it will soon be clear that lime cannot exist in it; indeed, *Duelech* itself cannot be calcined, nor can it produce lye <note>original: lixivium, a caustic alkaline solution made from ashes.</note>. 

Likewise, *Duelech* is not of the same nature as **gouty chalk** <note>gouty chalk: the "tophi" or chalk-like deposits of urate crystals that form in the joints of people suffering from gout.</note>, because it forms in the midst of the liquid urine, whereas that chalk is solidified from **synovia**. 

<margin>15</margin> Synovia <note>synovia: the lubricating fluid found in the joints; Van Helmont uses it here to describe a life-giving fluid that has gone wrong.</note> is a binding and "seminal" <note>original: spermatica, referring to the generative or life-directing principles Van Helmont believed existed in all bodily fluids.</note> mucilage which, during the process of true nourishment, has degenerated. It has moved from being transparent and crystalline to an opaque, white, and viscous matter (as I discuss elsewhere regarding gouty patients). It has been transformed, I say, from tasteless to acidic; it is by this acidity that it acquires its opacity. Indeed, it is then unfit for its own complete **evaporation** <note>original: difflationem, a Helmontian term for the "blowing away" or the body's process of turning nourishment into spirit and dispersing it.</note>, by which the nutritive liquid is normally consumed entirely without any residue. But Synovia, once infected by acidity...

<meta>catchword: ...acidity</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney and bladder stones (Duelech) are not formed by heat or lime-like processes as Paracelsus claimed, but are distinct from the chalky deposits found in gout. He emphasizes that the "spirit of urine" dissolves rather than creates these stones, pointing toward a more complex chemical origin involving fermented fluids.</summary>

## C H A P T E R  III.

[mani]fest, its watery parts are blown away, while the residue gradually dries out into the ultimate dryness and hardness of a **tophus** <note>tophus: A porous deposit of crystalline salts, common in gout or around joints.</note>. But **Duelech** <note>Duelech: A term coined by the physician Paracelsus to specifically describe a stony mass formed within the human body, such as a kidney or bladder stone.</note> arrives at its ultimate hardness in a single instant. Therefore, **gouty chalk** <note>original: creta podagrica</note> differs from Duelech in its entire matter and its efficient cause <note>efficient cause: the primary agent or force that produces an effect.</note>. For chalk of this kind is hardened outside of water—namely, through drying. Nor does it emulate the hardness of a rock for this reason, but only that of a tophus. I have said these things so that it might be clear that Duelech—being irregular <note>original: heteroclitis</note> in both its agent and its matter—differs from any other coagulated substances. 

And yet, while I did not yet know the manner or process of the origin of Duelech, I did know in the meantime that bodies do not undergo the determination of hardening except through the actions, destinations, and properties of their own **seeds** <term>seeds|In Van Helmont’s chemistry, 'seeds' (semina) are internal spiritual blueprints or vital forces that direct the growth and development of all matter.</term>. I knew, finally, that whatever things act corporeally are sluggish, inert, and utterly idle when compared to the coagulation of Duelech. 

<margin>16</margin> Therefore, I investigated fermental tastes and odors, as if they were the authors of many seeds. Thus, I did indeed encounter the tastes and illustrious actions of salts, but they in no way attained the power of the **salt of urine**. Then also, I observed weaker salts, which followed the lineage of **sulfurs**. 

<margin>17</margin> But as for **Mercury**, although according to **Paracelsus** <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a revolutionary Swiss physician who proposed that all matter was composed of three principles: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> it alone contained the entire perfection of the matter, I found it nevertheless to be lazy and weak. For as often as I distinguished the salts and sulfurs from the Mercuries, I marveled at the idleness of the latter, and yet the dignities of those former two principles. For this reason, I focused on the salts for the investigation into the birth of Duelech. 

I confess, indeed, that **Mercury** <note>original: Mercurium</note>—the flowing metal—is never sufficiently understood in its nature and properties. But this deceived Paracelsus by the likeness of an analogy. He thought—because his own invention had pleased him, whereby he had bestowed the name "Mercury" upon the watery matter of things—that the properties of **quicksilver** <note>original: argenti vivi</note> na-

-># ON THE FORMATION OF STONES<-

<meta>catchword: my [nature]</meta>

...[na]ture without equal, never sufficiently investigated, would agree adequately with all liquids that can be extracted from simple substances. For all the Philosophers of previous centuries admit that nothing in the universe, not even remotely, is similar to **quicksilver** <note>original: vivo argento; mercury</note>: yet it has not yet been sufficiently explained before now that quicksilver is a simple substance existing in actuality, and not a constitutive part of things. And thus, the name is nothing but a mere abusive misnomer.

Until now, I had persuaded myself of this: that since **Duelech** <term>Duelech|A term borrowed from Paracelsus referring specifically to a stone formed inside the human body, distinct from common rocks or gouty deposits.</term> was made from non-Duelech, it must happen through the action of an agent upon a prepared matter. And although I knew these things and many more like them, I discovered that I still knew nothing more. For this reason, I detested even more the <term>discursive philosophy|original: Philosophiam sermocinalem; the traditional academic approach based on logic and verbal debate rather than physical experimentation.</term>, seeing as it left me standing at the threshold of nature; and with my very guide herself, I was shut out. I returned to myself again, and in a simple manner, first considered that Duelech is not born except from the principles of **urine** <note>original: urinæ</note>. And since I knew that urine was salty, I turned back again to the varieties of salts.

<margin>18</margin> And I was astonished that everywhere, both by the Schools <note>The established medical universities of the 17th century.</note> and by healers, the use of salt was forbidden to **those with stones** <term>calculosis|Patients suffering from kidney or bladder stones.</term> without any controversy. Yet I found that the foundation for that prohibition was unknown to them—especially since the frequent and heavy use of sea salt has never, in real experience, harmed anyone suffering from stones. On the contrary, I have carefully noted many who, by the abundant use of salt, cut off the recurrence of fresh and growing stones. For I had seen the sea cliffs, like rocks, eaten away in the harbor <note>Van Helmont uses the observation of sea water eroding stone to argue that salt might break down stones rather than create them.</note>. Meanwhile, it always came back to my mind with wonder how, in a single instant, the **spirit of urine** <note>Ammonia</note> had coagulated the **spirit of wine** <note>original: spiritum vini; concentrated alcohol or ethanol</note>, with the separation of the watery part.

<margin>19</margin> I therefore decided first to <term>anatomize|In this chemical context, to "anatomize" means to break a substance down into its component parts through laboratory processes like distillation.</term> the salt of urine to its furthest veins. Wherefore, in the foul [smell] of urine...

### C H A P T E R III.

...of my own body, after putrefaction under manure <note>original: sub simo; manure piles were frequently used as a source of gentle, steady heat for "digesting" substances in alchemy</note>, I began this dissection: and immediately by distilling, I discovered, besides the aforementioned spirit of urine, two more fixed salts, and no more: 
<margin>20</margin> 
regardless of how much **Thurneisser** <note>Leonhard Thurneisser (1531–1596) was an influential alchemist known for "uronoscopy," the practice of diagnosing every possible disease by distilling urine in a specially marked glass vessel.</note> may babble at his pleasure about the various species found by him in urine <note>original: lotio</note>, which are almost as many as the simple substances in the universe. He wishes not only for man to be a **microcosm** <term>microcosm|The "little world"; the ancient theory that the human body reflects the structure and composition of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</term>: but that his urine also should enjoy the same prerogative. For to this end, in his audacity, he distinguishes twenty-four parts on the distilling vessel, and marks them on the outside with his own lines, and divides the human body into as many leaves. He claims, finally, that the vapors raised from the urine by distilling must strike a particular region of the glass, and condense into drops in that specific part where the business of the disease is carried out in the man whose urine it is. He likewise feigns that as many diversities of vapors rise from urine as there can be diseases in the universe. So that from there we might learn visually <note>original: optice</note> not only the affected locations but also the disease-causing matter: for in this way, he busies himself with imposing on the world his **uronoscopies** <term>uronoscopies|The diagnostic inspection of urine.</term>—which are suspected of wickedness and magical vanity, regardless of how he excuses them—through his **hydromantic** <term>hydromantic|Refers to hydromancy, or divination by water; here used as a biting insult against Thurneisser’s chemical methods.</term> distilling vessels. That is to say: in these trifles, he wanted to seem altogether serious; but I have never distilled so as to drive others mad along with me. Farewell then to the useless invention of **Thurneisser**, introduced into **Medicine** at least triflingly, if not even impiously.

<margin>21</margin> 
Therefore, in the dregs <note>original: fece</note> of urine, I distinguished two salts: one indeed is marine <note>sea salt</note>, consumed not long ago and still intact and unchanged (as I have shown elsewhere, concerning the boiling of **saltpeter** <note>original: salispetræ</note>). The other, however, belongs to the urine itself, born in our digestion and transmuted from what was not salt.

<margin>22</margin> 
Now it differs from the **Marine salt**. 1. If for several days no sea salt has been used, the urine's own salt is nevertheless not lacking. 2. Sea salt **coagulates** <note>original: coagulat; turns from liquid to solid</note> itself into grains with pointed tips: but the salt of urine will condense into dice and **gem-like** cubes. 3. Sea salt always [retains] its former...

# ON STONE-FORMATION
<meta>catchword: osten-</meta>

...by taste, it also recalls substances from latrines, when prepared with saltpeter <note>original: sale petræ; potassium nitrate, commonly found in areas where organic waste decomposes</note>. However, the salt of urine always tastes of urine.

4. Sea salt <note>original: Sal marinum</note> sticks to a wooden vessel during its cooling, just as when it is separated from saltpeter. 

<margin>23</margin>
But the salt of urine crystallizes at the bottom of the liquid. Furthermore, the fixed salt of urine distinguishes itself from the volatile salt: 1. By fire and flight <note>original: fugam; refers to evaporation or sublimation</note>. For one flies away while the other remains behind. 2. The fixed salt is separated from the dregs by extraction in liquid; but the volatile salt is sequestered by fire. For although foods and drinks generally turn sour in the stomach equally: nevertheless, the salts born from them are not equally volatile. This is because that stomach ferment is received according to the manner of the receiver, and thus it varies. Just as <term>chrysulca</term> <note>original: chrysulca; literally "gold-puller," referring to *aqua regia*, a powerful acid capable of dissolving gold</note>, while dissolving a whole and homogeneous metal, does not therefore make the whole of it equally volatile. And this diversity does not break forth from essential traits, but according to the participation of the properties of the "middle life" of things <note>Van Helmont believed substances had stages of "life" or vital identity as they were processed by the body</note>. 

<margin>24</margin>
Nor, finally, is that ferment of the stomach merely a bare acidity: but a vital and specific gift, whose purpose is to transmute food into the <term>chyle</term> <note>the milky fluid produced in the small intestine during digestion</note> of human digestion. Since many more fixed <note>solid or non-evaporating</note> things are taken in with food, which must nevertheless undergo the same condition as the volatile juices if they are to migrate more deeply to the root of life. At which point the energy of nature especially shines forth: which, from a completely similar mass, builds bones and the fibers of flesh, and so on. Finally, in a dog's stomach, it reduces bones to chyle, so that from there <term>blood</term> <note>original: cruor; typically refers to thick or clotted blood, but here signifies the vital fluid formed from digestion</note> might again be made. 

<margin>25</margin>
There are, therefore, the two aforementioned more fixed salts. But there is one highly volatile salt in the urine. Yet never from urine did an alkali or anything like <term>lye</term> <note>original: lixivio; a caustic alkaline solution made from ashes</note> appear to me. For if any lye-like substance were taken internally, it is immediately saturated by acids so that it puts aside its alkaline nature. 

<margin>26</margin>
Indeed, I saw a peasant who cured huge wounds with a drink of <term>lye</term> prepared from the Lime tree <note>original: Tilia; the Linden tree</note>. Yet the urine of those patients contained nothing lye-like at all...

# CHAPTER III.

...revealed. I have accordingly taught elsewhere that all alkali is created through the liquefying expression of fire; it was not present in the solid bodies before, just as the fixed salt was not. <margin>27</margin> If, however, some volatile spirit reflects the property of alkali (as is the case with the healing spirit of any wound-herbs), it is indeed cleansing and diuretic; yet by its own power, during the second digestion <note>In Helmontian medicine, "digestions" were stages of metabolic transformation; the second usually refers to the transformation of nutrients into blood</note>, it takes on the nature of the salt of urine. It then checks the acidic corruptions which would otherwise spontaneously arise in wounded patients thereafter. 

Since, therefore, there is no alkali in the urine <note>original: lotio; a common medical term for urine</note>, I have considered it an error to drink alkali as a stone-breaker <term>saxifragium</term> <note>literally "stone-breaker," a medicine intended to dissolve kidney or bladder stones</note>; for it does not reach the urinary passages. For even the <term>Ludus of Paracelsus</term> <note>a specific medicinal stone or preparation described by Paracelsus, often identified as a type of fossil or calcareous spar used to treat the "stone"</note> does not produce a lye <note>lixivium; a strong alkaline solution</note>, but rather a bitter salt derived from an acid. <margin>28</margin> For this reason, Paracelsus calls his *Ludus* the "gall of the earth." It is indeed a flinty stone, yet softer, and one which almost entirely flies away <note>original: avolat; meaning it evaporates or sublimes into a vapor</note> through a continuous fire of two days. With saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, however, it does so much more quickly. 

Furthermore, stones, gems, sands, marbles, flints, and the like are turned into glass <term>vitrificantur</term> when alkali is added; but if they are cooked with even more alkali, they are indeed dissolved into a liquid state. Once dissolved, they are easily separated from the alkali by the action of acidic spirits, returning to the weight of the original stone powder. But these substances never reach the urine, and so they are not useful as stone-breakers. 

However, rocks that contain "inflammable sulfur" <term>sulfur phlogiston</term> <note>an early chemical term for the principle of combustibility or "fire-stuff" within a substance</note> are indeed calcined <note>reduced to powder by high heat</note>, but they do not easily turn to glass, because the residual salt of the sulfur and the acid consume the vitrifying alkali. Metals also—on account of the total and unconquerable simplicity of their mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury was one of the three prime principles; here it represents the indestructible metallic core</note>, and the impossibility of penetrating them—either mock the labor of the fire by remaining unchanged, or they flee away entirely. Yet they do so in such a way that although they fly away as smoke, that smoke can nevertheless be reduced back into the nature of the original metal. For this reason, metals have never yielded any alkali; much less do they reach the chambers of the urine. <term>Marcasites</term> <note>a term then used for various metallic sulfides like pyrite</note>, however, since they contain a combustible sulfur that devours alkali; yet the mercuries of those... <note>original: refi-; catchword for the next page</note>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that alkaline salts are products of fire rather than natural components of the body, and therefore alkaline remedies cannot reach the bladder to dissolve stones. He explores the chemical reactions of various minerals, flints, and metals under heat to prove their medicinal limits.</summary>

-># ON THE STONE DISEASE<-

...they resist, so that they do not reach the hostels <note>hostels: a metaphorical term for the bladder and kidneys where urine resides</note> of the urine. Blood also, although it possesses a wonderful salt for healing—both a volatile <note>volatile: easily evaporated</note> and a fixed one—I have nonetheless observed is not profitable in <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>Lithiasis: the formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney stones</note>. Furthermore, the shells of snails, whether terrestrial or from aquatic shellfish, are indeed useful to those suffering from stones who require a "cleansing" <note>abstergent: a medicine that thins and carries away phlegm or impurities</note>, insofar as they contain an acid and calcareous <note>lime-like</note> salt. However, they contain a struggle within themselves regarding their lye-like <note>original: lixivium; an alkaline solution</note> portion, to the extent that they never actually reach the urine. 

Burnt bones of animals retain no fixed salt for themselves, <margin>29</margin> but only a tasteless, residual earth. It is therefore a mark of notable stupidity that ivory and hartshorn <note>hartshorn: the antler of a male deer, a common source of ammonia and calcium in early medicine</note> are calcined <note>calcined: reduced to ash by high heat</note> into protections against diseases; for by doing so, doctors command patients to purchase a powder deprived of all its strength. In this way, they impose upon both the purse and the hope of the sick, they let the opportunity for doing good pass by, and they make themselves ridiculous. 

The <term>minters</term> <note>original: Monetarii; workers at a mint who assay or refine metals</note> stand as witnesses for me in this matter. Indeed, the testing vessels (which they call <term>cupels</term> <note>original: capellas; small, porous bowls used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note>) must consist of ash that is deprived of all salt. For this reason, ash made from bones is best, <margin>30</margin> and those made from ivory and hartshorn are by far the most excellent. 

Truly, in my early years, the traditions of the <term>Schools</term> <note>original: Scholarum; the established medical universities of the time, which followed Galenic and Aristotelian teachings</note> were as so many oracles to me. But once I was taught by fire <note>Helmont calls himself a "philosopher by fire," meaning his knowledge comes from laboratory chemistry rather than old textbooks</note>, all the theorems of the Schools were deleted by fire. They had persuaded me, among other things, that sea salt was harmful to those with stones, both because it provided the material for the salt found in urine, and because it supposedly precipitated a mucous phlegm to form <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: a term coined by Paracelsus and used by Van Helmont to describe the specific "stony" matter or tartar that forms calculi in the body</note>. 

Examination of salt by fire taught me otherwise. Most notably, I kept a sixty-year-old man (my assistant in distillations) free from kidney stones for sixteen years through the generous use of sea salt—though he had otherwise been quite prone to them. I have since confirmed this in many others. For the Schools, when they see that in a sharp brine <note>original: myria; a concentrated salt solution</note> of salt that has been cooled, every salt is coagulated in its own manner, they believe that brine does not become pure except through the evaporation of a part...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that burnt bone and ivory are useless for treating kidney stones because the "healing salts" are lost in the fire. He also rejects the traditional medical warning against sea salt, claiming his own chemical experiments and clinical observations prove it prevents stone formation.</summary>

<header>C A P U T III. 55</header>

...watery [parts]: immediately they <note>the "Schools" or traditional physicians</note> suspected that the **stone** is **coagulated** by salt and a drying heat. And so they thought that even the salts in the said mass do not form grains <note>original: "granulari"</note> except through the innate heat of the salt—a heat which, therefore, is not able to manifest its effect as long as plenty of water is present in it. Therefore, while they tasted their own mucus as salty—and indeed with the flavor of sea salt, but not the saltiness of urine <note>original: "lotii"</note>—and they wished to connect the efficient cause <note>the active agent that produces an effect</note> to the matter, they reckoned that in the same mucus there is a viscous and tenacious matter joined to salt. They believed the salt itself was salty by its nature. Finally, by a perpetual decree, they ordained that the stone is generated from salty **phlegm**, and therefore also from an actual heat; and consequently, salty things are harmful to those suffering from stones. Indeed, they held that as long as phlegm remains such, once its qualities and proper passions are changed, it migrates into a stone through heat and a viscous dryness—just as glues and pastes, as the watery part gradually departs, bring about their own thick tenacity.

->Good God, how tasteless <note>tasteless: Van Helmont uses the word "insulsæ" as a pun; in Latin it means both "unsalted" and "foolish/absurd."</note> the Schools are, and how tasteless they command us to be!<- 

As if You, everywhere caring for mortals and providing for salts, had designed it by Your own effort so that people would become afflicted with stones. How great is their laziness, that they never tried to sprinkle a single handful of salt into the urinal <note>original: "urinali"</note> of a stone-sufferer to explore whether sea salt would coagulate the future sands—which otherwise would stick to the chamber pots <note>original: "matulis"</note>—or whether, I say, the saltiness of the urine is so great that no more salt could be dissolved in it. For if urine is meant for dissolving salt, then that salt cannot be the cause of granulation. Finally, they would have easily found that sea salt, when added to urine, hinders **coagulation**; it does not cause it. I say that sea salt dissolves the matter prepared for the clot <note>original: "coagulum"</note> and does not allow for a clot. Whatever, moreover, aims at the destruction of that clot will also necessarily disturb the coagulation proceeding from it. For indeed...

Just as the Schools laugh at our "coagulations" <note>Van Helmont uses "coagulum" to describe the chemical process by which a liquid becomes a solid, similar to how milk curdles into cheese.</note> in the nature of things, so in turn, I laugh at their absurdities—specifically, that they believe a stone or flint could grow or dry out in a watery environment through the power of heat. Against this idea of stony coagulation, one need only look to springs and rivers; their beds are stone, yet they utterly reject the "rules" of heat and dryness. Finally, our own flesh, blood, milk, and mucus bear witness to how liquids can solidify. Indeed, if it were established that phlegm <note>original: "pituita"</note> was the material from which stones were made, and that salt—having been "re-boiled" many thousands of times—had scraped away and dissolved the mucus of salted meats, eventually rejecting those thickened foams through boiling, they would surely have known that the use of salt is by no means to be avoided or forbidden.

But such a laziness in investigation has beset the Schools that, content with their shameful small profit, they have neglected everything by which they might benefit their neighbor, if not even themselves. For if a stone were dissolved in urine (even if it were boiled), or if urine were incapable of dissolving salt added to it, then perhaps, upon seeing that, they might have proscribed salt entirely from human use. 

But the common people laugh at the Schools, and the use of salt has increased in defiance of their rules. It has come to the point where, with the authority of the Schools despised, a diet without salt is considered not only unpleasant but even unhealthy. If the seasoning of salt pleases the palate, it is favored for no other reason than that salt dissolves the waste products that weigh down the stomach with their mucus. 

Furthermore, if salt is placed in the mouth of <term>catechumens</term> <note>original: "cathecamenis," referring to those being prepared for Christian baptism. In the traditional rite, a grain of salt—the "Salt of Wisdom"—was placed on the tongue.</note> as a symbol of Wisdom in the first hours after birth, yet the Schools with great effort forbid all eating of salt, what else is to be predicted except that these "pagan" Schools do not admit Wisdom, whose very symbol they take care to exclude? Do they imply that the Church, from the very start, intends the destruction of infants? For salt is even separated from latrines by the boilers of <term>saltpeter</term> <note>original: "salispetræ," or potassium nitrate. In this era, it was commonly harvested from nitrogen-rich earth in cellars and stables.</note>, such as was once taken together with food...

### C H A P T E R III.

...was then. Therefore, it ought to remain equally in the *Duelech* <note>Duelech is Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony matter that forms in the human body, which he distinguishes from ordinary rocks.</note> itself. But not even the smallest amount of sea salt is found in the *Duelech* by chemical means <note>original: "per artem", literally "by art," referring to laboratory analysis or alchemical procedures.</note>. The *Schools* <note>The "Schools" refers to the academic medical establishment of the 17th century, which relied on ancient Greek and Roman texts rather than experimental chemistry.</note> would have learned this at no expense if any eagerness for learning, or charity toward their neighbor, had driven them. At least the boilers of saltpeter have been more inquisitive than so many thousands of *Healers* <note>original: "Medentum"</note>. Consequently, they deserve reproach, so that even the common people now know how unrefined the decrees of the *Schools* are, 
<margin>31</margin>
since they do not know in *Dietetics* <note>original: "Diaetetica", the branch of medicine concerning diet and lifestyle.</note> why they forbid salted things. Nor indeed was it to be feared that if not salt itself, then at least its spirit <note>In alchemy, a "spirit" is a volatile substance or acidic vapor extracted from a solid; the "spirit of salt" is what we now call hydrochloric acid.</note> would harm those suffering from stones <note>original: "calculosis"</note>. 1. Because salt was forbidden by the *Schools* for ages, while the *Schools* were still ignorant of whether any spirit could be found in salt, or what its nature was. 2. Power is in vain for acting if it is never brought into action. For it is clear enough that from the sea salt consumed, the extraction of its own spirit is never possible within us. 3. For it is extremely acidic, and has no equal remedy for extinguishing the burning of urine, even when a stone is present in the bladder, and even in the painful urination <note>original: "stranguriis"</note> of the elderly: it prevents decay, dissolves mucus <note>original: "mucagines"</note>, and expels sand. Therefore salt is useful to those with stones, both in its body and in its spirit. Truly, spring salt <note>original: "sal fontale", salt obtained from inland saline springs rather than the sea.</note> was given by the providence of *Divine* generosity for the needs of mortals, so that where the continent recedes far from the sea, salty springs may supply the lack of the sea. But the *Schools*, far from returning thanks to *God* for this benefit, actually accuse *God*—not only in vain, but to the ruin of men—of having given salt. Meanwhile, it is notable that although salt flows into the sea from many abundant springs, the saltiness of the sea does not increase, because some salt gradually rises from the surface more truly as a vapor; and although it is converted into the primary matter of water in the clouds, it nonetheless retains some kind of constancy within itself. Hence it is no wonder that rainwater is not corrupted by itself throughout the ages.

## ON THE STONE DISEASE

It is clear, moreover, that in a warmer zone, salt also evaporates from the sea; for there is no one who does not smell whether meat is being boiled in a pot with salt or without it. For sea salt, since it works toward the preservation of the element <note>The "element" here likely refers to the Water element or the integrity of the flesh being preserved.</note>, evaporates with difficulty. Therefore, Spanish salt is almost devoid of this vaporous salt; it is stronger and more effectively prevents putrefaction and <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony matter that forms in the human body, distinct from common geological stones.</note>. On the other hand, salt from Lorraine abounds in vaporous salt, and it partly turns sour in the stomach. This vaporous spirit of salt (which is like a sublimated "flower of salt") also differs from the distilled spirit of salt <note>In 17th-century chemistry, a "spirit" was a volatile substance extracted from a solid; the distilled spirit of salt is what we now call hydrochloric acid.</note>, just as olive oil differs from oil of bricks <note>Oil of bricks (Oleum Laterinum) was a traditional remedy made by soaking heated bricks in oil and distilling the result.</note>. For the spirit of olive oil—the oily part that emerges in the first half of distillation—leaves behind a silver thread, in my experience, and finally dissolves in the flask. Now, olive oil protects iron from rust. But that residue of the oil, from which the aforementioned first half was removed, protects armor from corrosion far more powerfully.

<margin>32</margin>
Therefore, it should be noted that there is no salt purer or more refined than that which has been re-boiled from pig brine <note>original: "muria suilla", the salty liquid used to cure pork.</note>. Indeed, by the act of salting or working upon the pork, the salt has lost its more vaporous spirit through a process of coagulation. And so the remaining salt, being almost "fixed" <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand fire without evaporating.</note> and freed from its earthiness, is found to be clear and most fit for seasoning. When thrown into a cask of beer that is turning sour, it preserves it, which no other salt does. Wherefore, within us, it absorbs and transforms those superfluous acid spirits—which are the authors of all corruption if they exist outside the "workshops" of the first digestion <note>Van Helmont believed digestion occurred in specific stages or "workshops," the first being in the stomach. Acidity in the wrong place was seen as a cause of disease.</note>. Salt that has first been melted in a glowing-hot pot is nearly equal in quality to this Spanish salt. 

Since, therefore, sea salt is not part of the composition of urine, but is entirely foreign to it and remains so—indeed, sea salt, when retained in the urine, preserves its own unmixed and unchanged nature right until the final reduction of things and the separation of saltpeter <note>original: "salispetræ". Van Helmont is noting that even after urine decomposes or is processed to make saltpeter, the original sea salt remains a distinct entity.</note>—it is certain that no difficulty is [posed] to it— <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "tii" suggests the next page begins with "negotii" (difficulty or business).</note>

<summary>Van Helmont distinguishes between different types of salt, arguing that purified or "fixed" salts (like Spanish salt or salt used in meat curing) actually prevent the "Duelech" stone from forming by neutralizing harmful acids in the body.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

...calculi, with <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony matter that forms in the human body, distinct from common geological stones.</note>. And therefore, not even the slightest bit of sea salt is ever found in its composition, since it destroys the birth of Duelech rather than promoting it. Therefore, without foundation, sea salt is forbidden to those suffering from the stone by the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical academic authorities of the time, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized for following ancient theories over experimental evidence.</note>. So much for salts. I had learned thus far, however, that Duelech is an anomalous coagulum born from urinary salt, which has no equal in the whole universe: because human urine is found nowhere outside of man. <margin>32</margin> For that reason, I did not mind distilling urine many times. I decided to <term>putrefy</term> <note>In early chemistry, "putrefaction" was a process of slow fermentation used to break down substances, often seen as a necessary step for transformation.</note> my own urine for 40 full days in a horse's belly <note>original: "ventre equino"; a common laboratory technique where a vessel was buried in warm horse manure to provide a constant, gentle heat.</note>, so that through the preceding ferment of putrefaction, the dissimilar parts might break apart. Then I distilled about half of it. But having been called away from the work by family business, and then hindered by the Pentecost holidays, I ceased for fifteen days. Now, the receiving vessel was very large, clear, crystalline, and precious; I had already separated it from the beak of the <term>alembic</term> <note>Alembic: An early chemical apparatus used for distillation, consisting of a gourd-like container and a cap with a long "beak" or tube.</note> during that whole interval. Therefore, when I returned to the work, I first poured what had been distilled into another flask, because I saw that the receiving vessel (which otherwise easily held three <term>congii</term> <note>A "congius" was an ancient Roman liquid measure, roughly equivalent to 0.9 US gallons or 3.4 liters.</note>) was covered with a brownish whiteness wherever the distilled liquor of the urine had touched it. I grieved that such precious glass was stained by so foul a thing. Then I was even more indignant when I saw that the blemish contracted could be removed by no ash <note>Potash or wood ash was commonly used as a cleaning agent for laboratory glassware.</note>. I truly regretted my curiosity, which had proven so costly and was so often repeated. Therefore, I poured the distillate out. But immediately, I became suspicious of this impulse of laziness and unusual indignation. I marveled at myself—a man who previously spared no labor or expense—now being indignant over the ruin of a single vessel. I examined the receiving vessel, then, to see whether the blemish was an external stain, or whether something had been lost from the concave surface being corroded. At last, I knew for certain, the truth...

# ON LITHIASI <note>Lithiasi: The medical condition of forming stones in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>

A brown <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony matter that forms in the human body, distinct from common geological stones.</note>, from a very clear watery distillate, was deposited on the vessel. For when I was filled with wonder, I praised the Lord that He had taken care of me: for what I thought I had committed through my own neglect, I recognized had happened through the arrangement of divine goodness. Truly, He turns all things to good for those whom He wills. Indeed, I had already long ago observed in my <term>urine</term> <note>original: "lotio"</note> the presence of such a powerful coagulator. Now at last I had also learned this: that the very clear liquid separated from the urine—which was now more thoroughly putrefied <note>putrefied: In this context, fermented or broken down by long standing in heat.</note>—actually contained the <term>Duelech</term> which it had deposited. From this, it became especially certain to me that no slimy or viscous matter contributed at all to the composition of <term>Duelech</term>. 

This vessel, therefore, although precious and now ruined by its own stain, was dear to me because it had paid a <term>minervale</term> <note>minervale: Literally a "gift to Minerva" (the goddess of wisdom); here it means a "fee" paid in exchange for a great scientific insight or a "reward of learning."</note>. Therefore, I placed it back again as a receiver, thinking that eventually I would scrape away that <term>Duelech</term> using <term>aqua regia</term> <note>original: "chrysulcam," a potent acid mixture capable of dissolving gold.</note>. I then proceeded with the distillation of what had remained for me from those fifteen days. Behold, struck then by a new favor of divine bounty, I saw that as individual drops ran down, they dissolved the adhering brown <term>Duelech</term> wherever they touched it, and the vessel was soon restored to its former brilliance. Furthermore, I found that this second liquid, although it had the odor of the first, when poured into <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "aquæ vitæ," or "water of life," a term for concentrated alcohol.</note>, would no longer cause it to coagulate. 

And thus, led by a divine sign (which others might consider a chance occurrence), I found a part of that which I had long sought anxiously and at great expense. I therefore praised the Lord, because He had given understanding to one so small and poor. For if He had not ordered me to be called away from the work, and if the holidays had not detained me until the <term>Duelech</term> had solidified in the receiver, and if the vessel had not been so clear and precious—or rather, if I had completed the entire operation in one continuous flow—I would certainly have worked in vain and attempted everything to no avail. Therefore, the Lord looked upon the needs of mortals, and did not despise the prayers of the lowly. Wherefore

->### CHAPTER III.<-

...gave me the knowledge of the different parts of healthy **urine** <note>original: "lotii"</note>. That is, my own. One part was in a way lighter and swifter, containing a hidden **Duelech** <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s term for the stony matter that forms in the human body.</note> beneath a clear distilled liquid, without dregs, mucus, or sediment. However, it formed in a very clear drop and in the cold of the surroundings. The other part was equally wonderful, which would dissolve the **Duelech** that had arisen in the cold, and absorb it back into itself, also in the cold. For in a single **volatile salt** <note>volatile salt: A substance, like ammonium carbonate, extracted from organic matter through distillation that easily turns into vapor.</note> of urine, I seriously marveled at such opposite powers. 

I considered then that whoever's urine had more of the former spirit would be liable to the **stone** <note>original: "calculo"</note>; and another would be immune, whose urine contained more of the latter. That first concept pleased me. But that verbal knowledge was vain, and useless for actually getting things done. Since urine does not undergo those preliminary stages of **putrefaction** <note>putrefaction: Here meaning a controlled fermentation or chemical breakdown that Van Helmont believed was necessary to "awaken" the active properties of a substance.</note> within us, without which those entities would sleep forever. Furthermore, it was not clear from that speculation by what means more of the latter spirit could always be produced, or by what method the production of the former spirit could be inhibited. Much less how help could be given once the **Duelech** was already formed. 

Nevertheless, lest the speculation turn out fruitless, I wondered if that latter spirit could be permitted to be injected via a **syringe** <note>Van Helmont is contemplating a medical procedure to dissolve bladder stones by injecting a chemical solvent directly into the patient.</note> into a stone-filled bladder (after the urine was first evacuated). Thus, the knowledge of mortals seemed sterile to me, which, rejoicing in speculation alone, keeps its hand away from **Pyrotechnia** <note>Pyrotechnia: "The art of fire"; Van Helmont’s term for laboratory chemistry or alchemy.</note>. For urine, properly putrefied, gives forth a coagulating spirit that coagulates **spirits of wine** <note>original: "aquam vitæ." Literally "water of life," referring to concentrated alcohol or brandy.</note>. And likewise afterward, a spirit which is entirely transparent and volatile contains the potential for **Duelech** because it produces it; and finally, one that immediately absorbs the **Duelech** once it is born. 

But soon, with the **Devil** <note>Van Helmont often attributed his scientific doubts or intellectual blocks to spiritual interference or the "tempter."</note> dissuading me, I began to waver in doubt, and I considered that the things I had discovered and valued so highly were vain. Especially since urine does not putrefy within us, to...

<page-num>62</page-num>

# ON THE STONE
<meta>catchword: ciis</meta>

...that limit to which he had given these spirits. I therefore detested my own curiosities. For although they suggested a remedy in some way, they nevertheless left behind my previous ignorance regarding the causes and the way **Duelech** <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony matter that forms in the body, which he distinguishes from ordinary stones found in nature.</note> is formed. Wherefore I began to disregard everything seen before, as if it had not happened, and I felt a numbness strike me. From this I was confirmed in what I have explained at length in my preface: that no labor is more tedious, and no kind of knowing is more pushed aside by the urging of an **evil spirit** <note>original: "cacodaemone"</note>, than that from which the human race might one day reap fruit. It therefore befits a Christian to be of a constant mind in good work. 

Finally, therefore, through repeated **distillations** <note>distillations: The process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then condensing it back into liquid to purify it or separate its components.</note> and a more exact period of waiting, through prayer, I achieved the knowledge of the causes, the way of forming, and of dissolving **Duelech**. Good God, I admire your magnificence, which has led the most inept of physicians to the disclosure of such a great matter, neglected for so many centuries and by great minds. I return thanks, therefore, to your infinitely glorious name—not because you led me, above others, to these secrets (for can the clay boast and say "Aha!" to the other vessels because it was shaped into a vessel of a more choice form under the potter's wheel?), but because you have done your will, which alone is good. I return therefore to you all glory, you who in this age have revealed this knowledge through the most lowly and little ones of this world. For that is according to your custom, and for the greater glory of your name. 

For I have known that a single laziness stood in the way of both the ancients and the moderns: they were disdainful of distilling a thing so foul and filthy, being deceived by the sweet scent of profit. For **Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientia"</note> scorns those who have refused to learn the matter from which it comes, the dispositions, contents, properties, progress, and meanings of **urine** <note>original: "lotii"</note> **through fire** <note>original: "per ignem"; here referring to chemical analysis via heat.</note>. <margin>34</margin> Nor indeed has there been any less stumbling regarding the matter, the content, and the judgments...

<summary>Van Helmont attributes his discovery of the causes and dissolution of the "stone" (Duelech) to divine revelation through prayer and chemical distillation, rather than academic tradition. He criticizes other physicians for avoiding the study of urine due to its foul nature, suggesting their greed and laziness have hindered medical progress.</summary>

# CHAPTER III.

...of urines, as has been the case until now regarding their petrification <note>petrification: the process of turning into stone.</note>. For this reason, the <term>diagnostics</term> <note>diagnostics: the art of identifying a disease from its signs.</note> as well as the judicial <term>prognosis</term> <note>prognosis: a forecast of the likely course of a disease.</note> of urines remained hidden, as we have demonstrated from first principles in our <term>uronoscopy</term> <note>original: "vronoscopia"; the study or examination of urine for medical diagnosis.</note>. I wish that more earnest judgments would refine this study hereafter. Indeed, I prepare myself for the tomb in the hope that my labors will not be 
<margin>35</margin> useless to human miseries. I shall proceed now to recount my follies and the weariness of my experiments. For first of all, from <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: Van Helmont’s specific term for the stony material formed in the kidneys or bladder, distinct from common rocks.</note> when it is broken apart and distilled by itself; likewise from the scrapings of the <term>urinal</term> <note>original: "matulae"; the flask or chamber pot used to collect urine.</note>; and thirdly from urine distilled to the thickness of an <term>electuary</term> <note>original: "eclegmatis"; a medicinal paste or thickened liquid.</note>, exactly the same oil and the same crystals of liquid dung arise. For from Duelech, there remains an earthy dross—black, crumbly, and burnt—which is no longer stony and retains hardly any of the more fixed salt of urine. This is because the volatile spirit, entirely and in every part within the Duelech, has finally been changed into earth, along with the other parts of the composition joined to it. This is a certain sign that the fixed salt of urine does not have the power of an active coagulant, but is merely coagulated passively. Furthermore, that earth left behind from the distilled Duelech never 
<margin>36</margin> previously descended to the bladder in the form of powder or mud; rather, it was a liquid while it was in the urine, which then hardened there through the spirit of the urine <note>original: "spiritum lotii"; likely referring to the volatile ammonia or salts found in urine.</note>. For I had long meditated on the fact that earth or powder, however skillfully it might be connected to the spirit of urine, would nevertheless never grow together into Duelech. Consequently, the discovery of <term>Tartar</term> <note>Tartar: A reference to the theory of Paracelsus, who believed "Tartar" from food and drink formed stones; Van Helmont is arguing against this idea.</note> as the cause for Duelech would be in vain. Indeed, I had already seen in the glass that Duelech is made from spirits (namely, clear and distilled liquids), being formed by the same matter and the same efficient cause by which Duelech arises within us. I therefore concluded from what has now been 
<margin>37</margin> mechanically proven: if urine, with its spirit of salt, possesses a spirit of volatile earth, then Duelech will necessarily be generated from those two, unless by the dross...

(which I call "liquid dung" <note>original: "stercus liquidum"; Van Helmont's term for a specific waste product in the body that he believed could be transformed into stones or other materials.</note> in my book on fevers), the salt of the urine becomes saturated, and to that extent it is prevented from coagulating. For I have observed not rarely that a person suffering from the stone, if later afflicted with <term>jaundice</term> <note>original: "ictero"; a yellowing of the skin and eyes caused by liver or gallbladder issues.</note>, remained free from the stone for as long as the jaundice prevailed. And thus it was not asserted by me without reason in my book on fevers that the aforementioned dross is mixed into the urine as a foreign substance, like a useful excrement. 

<margin>38</margin>
Furthermore, these "sands" <note>arenae: small stony particles or grains found in urine.</note> granulate—both within us and in the urinals—at the very moment of their granulation; and once they have granulated, they simultaneously acquire their final hardness. 

<margin>39</margin>
It is not the case that they harden gradually, more and more. Therefore, whatever the <term>Schools</term> <note>original: "Scholae"; referring to the traditional academic medical authorities of the time, often followers of Galen or Avicenna, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note> devise regarding a "confirmed" versus a "not yet confirmed" stone is mythical—merely an excuse for their own confirmed ignorance and laziness. For a fresh grain of sand, whether excreted from our bodies or wiped from the urinals, is just as hard as it will be ten years later. Let the same judgment be held regarding <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: the specific stony material formed in the body.</note>. I also stated above that unless the sand which fixes itself to the urinal were to coagulate in an instant, it would have all fallen headlong to the bottom; it would not be fixed to the sides, nor would it be so proportionally distributed. 

<margin>40</margin>
For instance, a heretical preacher near Berkeley <note>original: "Barclayam"; likely Berkeley in Gloucestershire, England.</note> in England, in the year 1629, while healthy and sound, was attempting after dinner to pull down a book from a high shelf toward himself; he was suddenly struck in the lower belly by a great weight and pain. From certain signs four days later, he knew he was burdened with a stone. Then, eight days later in London, he died under the knife of the <term>lithotomist</term> <note>lithotomist: a surgeon who specializes in the surgical removal of bladder stones.</note>. The stone weighed one English pound and two drachms besides. I do not remember ever seeing a similar stone. (Note that 100 pounds in Antwerp weigh 104 in London). For <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Paracelsus: a famous 16th-century Swiss-German physician and alchemist who proposed that diseases were caused by external chemical imbalances.</note>, marvelling at this sudden appearance of a stone, 

<margin>41</margin>
and lest a figment be missing from his <term>Microcosm</term> <note>Microcosm: the philosophical concept that the human body is a miniature reflection of the universe (the Macrocosm).</note>, calls it a "thunderbolt stone" <note>original: "calculum fulminis"; Paracelsus likened the sudden formation of a kidney stone to the sudden strike of a lightning bolt.</note> and thinks it solidified while falling. But his error is manifold. 1. For...

## C H A P T E R III.

...no room is given for chance. Indeed, the bladder contains either urine, or none at all. If it contains none, it is folded together like a wet linen cloth; but if it is distended by urine, then since the bladder is situated below <note>The author is describing the anatomical position of the bladder relative to the kidneys and the rest of the abdomen.</note>, the <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech"; a technical term used by Van Helmont for a stone formed in the bladder or kidneys from the "spirit" of the urine.</note> cannot be formed outside the urine, or without the urine, as if it were made without matter and then fell down into the urine, being created by the very act of falling.

2. He is mistaken, believing that a stone cast down in a thunderstroke <note>Referring to "thunderstones" or meteorites, which early modern thinkers often compared to biological stones.</note> is generated by ordinary and usual causes: rather than by portentous ones. Otherwise, if the matter of natural thunder were naturally coagulated in an instant, stones of this sort ought to be common in every thunderclap. Nor would there be a reason why a small stone of about three pounds would drill through the earth to a depth of nine feet by the mere, naked chance of its own weight, unless it were thrust down by a more robust force, as I have discussed elsewhere concerning anomalous <term>meteors</term> <note>original: "meteoro"; in this period, a "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, lightning, or falling stones.</note>.

3. Finally, the *Duelech* revealing itself by a sudden tyranny <note>original: "subita tyrannide"; likely referring to the sudden, agonizing onset of symptoms when a stone blocks the urinary passage.</note> does not prove its instantaneous generation. For nothing prevents it from having been attached to the bladder by its own "foot," and once its <term>stalk</term> <note>original: "stolo"; a botanical term for a shoot or runner, used here to describe a small attachment point between the stone and the bladder wall.</note> was broken off, falling into the open space of the bladder.

4. Whatever is ever condensed into a true *Duelech*, whether it be a central nucleus descending from the kidney, or finally increasing as far as the outer crust: any generation of it always happens in an instant. Indeed, <margin>42</margin> I have learned from my mechanical experiments <note>original: "mechanicis"; refers to Van Helmont's practical laboratory work and chemical observations.</note> that the whole *Duelech*, as far as its essence is concerned, is composed of mere volatile substances. However, a *Duelech* of one pound cannot be generated from only three or four ounces of urine (the amount a person might pass at once). Furthermore, although I knew human urine in our species alone, and that only the spirit of human urine resides in man: nevertheless, being curious, I examined horse urine (under the category of herd animals) to see if perhaps there might be another similar coagulating spirit which, on account of innate impediments, might not obtain the power of coagulation everywhere. But however much I labored, I did not find that coagulating spirit in horse urine. Nor the spirit of ferment...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that bladder stones (Duelech) are formed instantly from volatile spirits in the urine rather than by chance or gradual hardening. He notes that while human urine contains this unique "coagulating spirit," his experiments show it is absent in the urine of animals like horses.</summary>

<margin>43</margin> ...to the mind, or to the water of life <note>original: "aquæ vitæ"; while this often refers to distilled alcohol, Van Helmont uses it here to describe a vital, reactive component he believes exists within the urine.</note>. It is of the highest importance to note that the spirit of urine does not coagulate except through its marriage with the water of life; a fact I have frequently proven through distillation. Therefore, three things are present in human urine that must necessarily come together: and they do so more powerfully in anyone suffering from stones, who already carries no small principle of corruption in his urine (as will be shown in its proper place). <margin>44</margin> the water of life itself are extremely volatile <note>original: "fugaces"; literally "fleeing" or "evanescent," referring to substances that easily evaporate or escape during chemical processes.</note>, and thus hardly suited to the stubborn hardness of the <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term coined by Paracelsus and used by Van Helmont to describe the specific matter of a kidney or bladder stone.</note>. For it is certain that the most volatile spirit of vitriol <note>original: "spiritum vitrioli"; an early name for sulfuric acid, though here likely referring to its more volatile vapors.</note> is immediately fixed <note>original: "fixari"; in alchemy, "fixation" is the process of turning a volatile substance into a solid that can withstand heat.</note> by its volatile companion—indeed, by <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "salem armeniacum"; ammonium chloride, a salt used frequently in alchemical experiments.</note>, which is also volatile—to such an extent that it can withstand melting. Having been taught these things, our students cease to wonder at things that otherwise leave the ignorant in a state of stupor.

## C H A P T E R IV.

*22. Second question. 23. Third. 24. Fourth. 25. Bladder catarrhs <note>original: "catharri vesicales"; refers to the old medical theory that stones were caused by "mucus" or "catarrh" flowing into the bladder.</note>, ridiculous. 26. Fifth. 27. Sixth. 28. Astrologers are criticized. 29. Paracelsus is criticized, as is Galen <note>Galen was the ancient Greek authority on medicine; Van Helmont frequently challenges both him and the more recent rebel Paracelsus.</note>. 30. Solution to the proposed question. 31. Galen's oversight.*

The **SPIRIT** of urine, seizing the volatile earth produced by the seed and a foul, putrefying ferment, awakens the spirit of wine <note>original: "spiritum vini"; Van Helmont believed a volatile, alcohol-like principle existed hidden within urine.</note>—previously hidden in potential—which dwells within the urine.
<margin>1</margin> When these two meet, like two sexes coming together, the aforementioned earthly spirit drinks in that unique coagulator. Because of this mutual exchange, a most inward connection arises between the two as they act upon one another: for they meet at the level of their spirits, through their smallest parts <note>original: "per illorum minima"; a reference to "minima naturalia," the smallest possible particles of a substance in early modern science.</note>. Thus, the coagulator instantly solidifies the spirit of wine, which was potentially excited in the putrefying ferment. When the foul mass contributes its
<margin>2</margin> matter, they are condensed together into the true **Duelech** <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term coined by Paracelsus for a stone formed within the human body.</note>. This is truly a monster, a new thing coagulated in the middle of water, and therefore not capable of being resolved back into water. It is a stony, animal-like entity, unlike
<margin>3</margin> any other, which is why Paracelsus calls it *Duelech*. This concept will enter the mind more easily through an example that the **Springs of Spa** <note>original: "Spadani fontes"; the famous mineral springs in Belgium near Van Helmont's home.</note> show us daily. For they possess a sulfurous spirit that is clearly acidic (which is why they are called acid springs) and a "vein" of iron. Both are contained in the water in an immature, embryonic, and liquid state. Soon, however, both begin to exert their reciprocal powers upon each other. At last, when their strength is exhausted and their action ceases, they condense into a stony body that attaches itself to the bottles in the form of ochre <note>original: "ocræ"; iron oxide deposits.</note>. Thus, the water returns to its original element, stripped of every foreign quality. If
<margin>4</margin> Paracelsus had sufficiently contemplated these acidic springs, he would have ignored the history of the **tartar of wine** <note>original: "Tartari vini"; the crusty deposit in wine barrels. Paracelsus famously argued that kidney stones were "tartar" from food and drink, a theory Van Helmont rejects here.</note> borrowed from Basil Valentine; for he would have known that the history of ochre and
