<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE <note>original: "DE LITHIASI"</note></header>

...the origin of wine tartar is not the same. At least it would have been more difficult to refute. For certainly, tartar is dissolved back into water; but **ochre** <note>original: "ocra"; refers to the mineral deposits left by mineral springs</note> is not, and neither is the **stone** <note>original: "calculus"</note>. Indeed, I have never tried to deny that solid bodies are formed from liquids. But I reject **tartareous liquors** as the causes of diseases, as they have been dragged in by force (as I discuss in my *Treatise on Tartars*); on the other hand, I have reverently admired the activities of **spirits acting upon spirits**. Truly, since ochre—or a stony crust—grows out of the waters of **Spa** <note>original: "Spadanis"; referring to the famous mineral springs of Spa in modern-day Belgium, famous for their healing properties</note>, coating the flasks throughout their entire hollow, it would be a great sin, first of all, to prescribe Spa water if we believed that tartars were formed in the same way as the ochre in Spa water. That is, if we believed that tartar resided in Spa water, which would soon coagulate in the person drinking it, then whoever prescribes Spa water commits a crime. For while
<margin>5</margin> the acid salt of wine corrodes the dregs <note>original: "faecem"; the sediment or lees of wine</note>, the salt—which was previously acid and not yet coagulated—remains acid even when it is coagulated; it does not change the essence of the salt, even if it is compressed into a solid, though it was previously fluid. Conversely, even if the dregs absorb the acid spirits and coagulate them within themselves, it nevertheless remains a solid body, while the spirit of the acid salt is coagulated into the solid body of wine tartar. Indeed, before it is fully coagulated, it attaches itself to the vessel. For in the generation of wine tartar, the spirit acts upon the body; but it is a far different action entirely when **two spirits act upon each other**. For in this latter case (as occurs in Spa water, in **Duelech** <term>Duelech</term> <note>The Paracelsian term for the stony material formed in the human body</note>, etc.), a new and neutral entity is established, such as ochre, formed from the **spirit of sulfur** and the **volatile vein** <term>volatile vein</term> <note>original: "venae volatilis"; an early chemical term for a volatile mineral or metallic principle</note>. But in the tartar of wine, only the acid spirit, or the vinegary liquor of the wine, is changed into salt, and the dregs remain as they were before. Therefore, what is formed from it is again resolvable <note>i.e., it can be dissolved back into a liquid state</note>. For a metal, stone, or solid body is not "un-bodied," changed, or turned into a gas because of the corrosion of spirits. This is clear. For silver, pearls, coral, the stones of sponges, or the "stones" of crabs and 
->with<-

<meta>The catchword "with" (Latin: cum) transitions to the list of substances like snails (limacum) that can be dissolved by acids without losing their essential nature.</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the urinary stone (Duelech) is not made of "tartar" because tartar is re-soluble in water, whereas stones and mineral ochre are permanent solids created by the interaction of two spirits. He uses the mineral deposits in Spa water to prove that if stones were like tartar, medicinal waters would be poisonous, which they are not.</summary>

## CHAPTER IV.

...with etc. although through **Chrysulca** <note>original: "Chryſulcam"; an alchemical term for *aqua regia* or nitric acid, literally "gold-washer"</note>, or other acid liquors, they may vanish from our eyes: they are nevertheless stones, which the **Spirit** <note>In Helmontian theory, the *spiritus* is the internal formative power that directs matter</note> previously created as much as it could (just as was said concerning fevers). It worked upon the body as if in vain, while by corroding the body, it coagulated itself. There is certainly in the whole nature of the universe only a single fire, the **blazing Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcanus ardens"; the mythological god of fire, used here as a metaphor for a specific, transformative alchemical heat</note>; so too, there is only a single liquor that dissolves all solids into their primary matter, without any change to itself or loss of its powers (as those who have achieved it <note>Refers to the "adepts" or successful alchemists</note> know and will testify). In other activities of liquors, however, a solid body can never radically mix itself with the solvent liquor. Therefore, it is indeed corroded, but not intimately dissolved, as is otherwise required for a **formal transmutation** <note>A change in the very essence or "form" of a substance, not just its appearance</note>. 
<margin>6</margin>
For every **corrosive acid spirit** <note>original: "ſpiritus acidus rodens"</note>, by corroding another body, is coagulated and almost fixed, and migrates into the form of a thickened salt: yet the body, which endured the will of the corroding spirit, does not act upon that spirit, which coagulated itself by its own action while corroding. This indeed does not happen when two active spirits run into each other. For then, there is a double action, by which each acts upon the other in turn. 
<margin>7</margin>
For that reason, an action of that kind happens with a radical mixing, and there is constituted from both an offspring of inseparable mixture; and this transmuted thing is a product that is neither of the two original parts. If **Paracelsus** <note>A famous 16th-century physician who theorized that all "stony" diseases were caused by "tartar" similar to wine-crust</note> had maturely contemplated this, he would have taken the **ochre** <note>original: "ocram"; the mineral sediment left by springs</note> of the **Spa water** <note>Mineral springs in Belgium</note> instead of his **tartar of wine**, and he would have said something more probable than his claim that liquors were present in all things which were coagulated in the manner of tartar in wine, and were the "common mother and matter" of all diseases whatsoever. Indeed, the ochre—the daughter of the Spa—is not dissolved again like the tartar of wine: and yet it differs from **Duelech** <note>original: "Duelech"; a specific Paracelsian term for the stone formed inside the human body, such as a kidney stone</note>:

<summary>Van Helmont argues that stones form through a unique "radical mixing" of active spirits, rather than simple precipitation like wine tartar. He asserts there is a unique universal solvent (the Alkahest) and critiques Paracelsus for conflating different types of mineral formations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s discussion from page 69, further distinguishing between how minerals form in nature and how stones (calculi) form in the human body. He specifically contrasts his concept of "wild gas" with the behavior of wine dregs and medicinal spring waters.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"; lithiasis is the medical term for the formation of stony concretions, such as kidney stones, in the body.</note>

...as much as mineral petrification differs from a stone <note>original: "calculo"; referring here to a kidney or bladder stone</note>. In this latter case, the **coagulating spirit**, existing in the urine, works by the power of its own unique salt upon the weak and putrefying spirit of the earth, without any boiling or the belching of **wild Gas** <term>Gas silvestris; a term coined by Van Helmont to describe the carbon dioxide released during chemical reactions, which he believed was a "wild" or untamable spirit of matter</term>: and so, in a moment, it completes its operation and coagulates itself with the spirit of wine <note>alcohol</note> proper to urine—just as I described above in the *Mechanics of the Spirit of Urine and Wine*, or "burning water." 

But the **acid spirits of Spa** <note>original: "Spadanae"; referring to the famous mineral springs of Spa in modern-day Belgium, which Van Helmont studied extensively</note>, born from embryonated sulfur, first work through a long process; they stir up bubbles and "wild gas," and finally fix themselves to the vessel. Otherwise, if that gas cannot be belched out, the Spa waters remain safe and fit for healing. For if the gas is prevented from exiting, it hinders what follows, and the spirits are rendered exhausted in their action. 

But the **dregs of wine**, since they have their own proper coagulation, do not need to acquire it from elsewhere. When the acid spirit of wine has corroded the dregs, it is impossible for it to give through that corrosion what it does not possess within itself. Therefore, in the generation of the **Tartar of wine** <note>The potassium bitartrate crystals that deposit in wine casks, which Paracelsus used as a universal metaphor for disease-causing deposits in the body</note>, that acid saline spirit is indeed coagulated because of the earth: but it will remain in the form of a soluble salt, and not in the form of a stone.

<margin>§ 8</margin>
This is because of the rule that a **transmutation of essence** presupposes a **transmutation of matter**. Thus, an earthy body—whether it is dissolved by a corrosive or not—preserves its original being. This is because the solvent does not penetrate the dissolved substance in its **radical bond of connection** <note>The "radical bond" was thought to be the deepest level of chemical union; Helmont is arguing that most acids only break things apart superficially</note>. This penetration, however, is absolutely necessary in transmuting essential natures. 

Let the **novices of Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiae tyrones"; common phrasing in early modern textbooks for students or "beginners" in the art</note> learn, therefore, that bodies are not truly resolved by the calcinations of corrosives, even if they are repeated many times—unless a **fermental impression** <note>Van Helmont believed "ferments" were the spiritual agents that directed all biological and chemical transformations</note> intervenes through **putrefaction**, which must precede any radical solution. **Camphor**, for instance, in **strong water** <note>original: "aqua forti"; nitric acid</note>, assumes the nature of a floating oil: but when the acid is washed away by water...

<meta>catchword: water (aquam)</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones form instantly without releasing gas, unlike mineral deposits or "Spa" waters. He critiques chemical "novices" for thinking acids truly transform matter, asserting that true change requires a biological-style ferment.</summary>
<keywords>lithiasis, wild gas, Spa waters, tartar, transmutation of essence, radical bond, fermental impression, camphor, chemical novices</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s exploration of the chemical nature of matter. He transitions from discussing the behavior of minerals and "wild gas" to a critique of how substances change—or fail to change—when combined. He uses his early alchemical experiments with gold to illustrate that physical changes are often temporary unless a deeper "transmutation" occurs.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV.</header>
<sig>e 4</sig>

# CHAPTER IV.

...common water with a corrosive, it is immediately camphor, just as it was before, whether this is done once or even a thousand times. For in my apprenticeship <note>original: "tyrociniis"; referring to his early years of study and experimentation</note> I used to rejoice because I had passed gold, in the form of a pomegranate-red oil <note>original: "specie olei punicei"</note>, through a retort seven times. I was mindful that he who knows how to destroy <margin>9</margin> gold likewise knows how to construct it. But once the accompanying corrosive was removed, the gold returned to its former self, and my empty joy ceased. It is a vain labor to try to extract from a thing that which is not in it. Those who do not work through the proper means also labor in vain. Therefore, the generation <margin>10</margin> of **Duelech** <term>Duelech; a term borrowed from Paracelsus referring to the stony, marble-like matter that forms kidney stones</term> is not an imaginary petrification of "cooked mucus" or "artificial phlegm" <note>Van Helmont is arguing against the common medical theory of his time that stones were just dried-out slime</note>, dried out by local heat or hardened by drying: (for in that case, it would only be a resolvable lump <note>original: "bolus"</note>, and not Duelech). Rather, it is the transmigration of three spirits together <margin>11</margin> into Duelech, by a true and essential transmutation. 

Truly, bodies do not act upon other bodies through the natural action of their composition: but whatever bodies do to one another is done by reason of weight, size, hardness, shape, and motion. For these things serve Mathematics <note>original: "Mathesi"</note>, and hardly Physics <note>original: "Physicæ"; in this era, "Physics" referred to the study of the inner nature or "soul" of things, while "Mathematics" dealt with mere external measurements</note>. <margin>12</margin> If, however, corporeal salts do work, it happens either because they contain a volatile spirit in some way, or because they find that spirit within the body. Let the apprentices at least remember: bodies, in whatever way they have been mixed together by melting, nonetheless remain in their original state of being, unless they are transmuted by fire or by a **ferment** <term>fermentum; a vital power or agent of change that Van Helmont believed was responsible for all biological and chemical transformations</term>. Finally, bodies do nothing to spirits; they only limit the spirits by being acted upon <note>original: "patiendo"; literally "by suffering"</note>. Therefore, this operation of bodies is not a true reaction, but rather a mere effect of the spirits, resulting from their own activity. For this reason, once their faculties are drained and exhausted, the spirits collapse of their own accord at the end of their movement. And although this action of the spirits occurs with "suffering" and the loss of their own powers, they do not, for that reason, transmute bodies into their own nature.

<summary>Van Helmont argues that true chemical change (transmutation) requires a "ferment" or "spirit," using his failed attempt to permanently alter gold as proof that physical mixing is temporary. He defines the formation of kidney stones (Duelech) as a spiritual transmutation rather than simple drying of mucus.</summary>
<keywords>gold, alchemy, Duelech, kidney stones, transmutation, ferment, spirits vs bodies, Mathesis vs Physica</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s argument that chemical reactions often involve substances acting upon one another without losing their underlying essence. He moves from his previous example of gold to experiments involving iron, mercury, and sulfuric acid to demonstrate that what looks like a total transformation is often just a temporary change in state or a "tiring out" of the chemical "spirits" involved.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

-># ON LITHIASIS.<-

...[but]. For they only gnaw at it, and grind it into powder; this they also interpret as a "calcination by water." <margin>13</margin> For example: mix a pound of **Saffron of Mars** <term>Crocus Martis; a reddish-brown powder, usually iron oxide, used in both alchemy and medicine</term> with six times as much **oil of vitriol** <term>Oleum vitrioli; concentrated sulfuric acid</term>, then distill away whatever is watery. You will find "vitriol of iron." Remove the iron from it, and you have the vitriol of iron—a salt, I say, resembling vitriol, which has the taste of iron. Yet it retains nothing of the actual matter of **Mars** <note>original: "Marte"; the alchemical name for Iron</note>. For you have the "determination" <note>the specific qualities or instructional power</note> from the iron regarding its medicinal efficacy, but not its physical matter. The resulting substance will be the former spirit of vitriol (or oil from the **vitriol of Venus** <note>original: "vitriolo veneris"; copper sulfate</note>) fixed into a certain salt, possessing only the scent of iron. 

Let us look at the same thing again, even more clearly. Combine a pound of running **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurii"; liquid quicksilver</note> with four times as much oil of vitriol; remove the **phlegm** <note>the watery, non-active component of a distilled liquid</note> by distilling it, and a white precipitate, like snow, will remain at the bottom. Likewise, if you pour in more oil than is proper, the Mercury will pass over imperceptibly along with the oil during distillation. Furthermore, if you wash that "snow" to remove its sharpness, a lemon-yellow powder will remain at the bottom; if this powder is "revived" <note>reduced back to its metallic state</note>, it will weigh exactly the same as the original pound of Mercury. 

Yet the water used for washing, which absorbed the salt into itself, yields true **alum** <term>Alumen; a type of mineral salt used as an astringent</term>. In this way, by "touch" alone, a single pound of quicksilver—without any loss of its own substance—could gradually change many thousands of pounds of very acidic oil of vitriol into alum. Similarly, that same oil, by the touch of iron, is changed into a "vitriolized salt of Mars," which is a noble substance for healing. 

Note well this action of Mercury without any essential **reaction** <note>original: "repassione"; a philosophical term meaning the agent is not affected or "suffered upon" by the object it is changing</note> upon its own substance. <margin>14</margin> This is a contemplation of great importance. Indeed, a great crowd of **Spagyrists** <term>Spagyrorum; alchemists, specifically those following the tradition of Paracelsus who focused on separating and recombining substances for medicine</term> is deceived by their own hopes; they think that "fixed" bodies, once dissolved in corrosives, have granted their own properties to those corrosives—provided the solvents have coagulated into a solid form through their own activity. They do not know, I say, that the "spirits" of these chemicals, becoming exhausted by the act of processing, simply degenerate into a new being <note>original: "ens"</note> when they reach the limit of their power in acting. 

Next, it must be known...

<summary>Van Helmont uses experiments with mercury, iron, and acid to prove that a substance (like mercury) can trigger a chemical change in another without being consumed or altered itself. He argues that many alchemists mistake the "exhaustion" of chemical solvents for a successful transmutation of the base material.</summary>

<page-num>73</page-num>
->CHAPTER IV.<-

# CHAPTER IV.

...[it follows] that every operation tending toward the transformation of both (namely, the agent and the patient) consists only between pure spirits. However, the operation of a body with a spirit in non-living things begins from a spiritual odor of some <term>fracedo</term> <note>original: "fracedinis"; a state of ripening or transformative decay that Van Helmont believed preceded the generation of new forms</note>; for the seeds and fermental dispositions depend on this, and by their own command, they rule over the liquids destined for generation. Wherefore the ancients noted, not without reason, that the origin and continuation of the visible world come from the invisible and incorporeal (such as odors and ferments); and in our terms, <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term borrowed from Paracelsus referring specifically to the stony matter formed within the human body</note> grows together through an incredible coagulating spirit and an invisible beginning. For it did not require the birth of an externally brought-in **tartar** <note>original: "tartari"; mineral deposits, which other physicians blamed for stone formation</note>, the inner child of <term>mucago</term> <note>a slippery, mucous-like substance that acts as a precursor to the stone</note>, nor did it need imagined clots formed by drying. It is far more calamitous that we carry the very <term>Vulcan of the stone</term> <note>original: "calculi Vulcanum"; Vulcan was the Roman god of fire and the forge, used here to represent the internal "artificer" or heat that actively forges the stone within the body</note> within us in our urine; at his troublesome command, the properties of the wandering spirit obey.

<margin>15</margin>
Indeed, God would seem to have loved the brute beasts more than us if he had not directed diseases toward merit, and thus toward good—for which temporal punishment is not sufficient. However, where a foreseen end to punishment exists, out of the gift of His munificence, He has raised up the powers of medicines.

In wars too, their own stones arise—though these are given neither for punishment nor for merit—which grow for us into medicines. And for that reason, they arise from a far different

<margin>16</margin>
root. Furthermore, before I proceed to the history of the stone, I shall put forward a few exercises. First, therefore: at the Salt Works in **Burgundy** <note>specifically the town of Salins-les-Bains, famous for its brine springs</note>, there are today no more than two wells: the **Myriæ** and the **well of Gray**. But if a hundred measures from each well are boiled separately, they yield far less salt than if the same quantity were boiled together in connection. The inhabitants marveled at this experiment, and therefore they subsequently mix both "myriads" <note>original: "myriam"; likely a pun on the name of the well and the Greek word for a vast number</note>. For truly, one of them contains more of a breathing or volatile salt, which singularly...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones (Duelech) are formed by invisible internal "spirits" and "ferments" rather than external debris. He also discusses the theological purpose of disease and provides a chemical example from the salt mines of Burgundy to show how different substances interact to produce greater yields.</summary>

<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"; On the formation of stones in the body</note>

...thoroughly cooked by a flaming fire, it flies away before its coagulation. However, meeting another more fixed substance, it is imbibed and bound into a solid salt. This example teaches that the bodies of salts drink in their own spirits, and the spirits, in turn, gnaw at the bodies. Indeed, Burgundy brine <note>original: "Myria Burgundica"; likely referring to a type of mineral salt or alum found in the Burgundy region</note>, clearer than crystal, yet by its own vaporous spirit, it drinks a large part of the rocky stone into itself. For this reason, during the cooking—while the spirits are coagulated into the more solid body of the salt—it settles and is skimmed off with difficulty. Therefore, that spirit of salt, even if it were to dissolve the stone, did not for that reason enter into a marriage with the earth, so that either the spirit should become stone, or the earth should become salt. 

Indeed, it is evident from this that even if sea salt had vaporous parts, it would not contribute to the stone <note>original: "calculo"; the kidney or bladder stone</note>, nor would it join the spirit of urine for its growth. This is because it consists of far different principles (as I have discussed elsewhere regarding digestions); rather, as much sea salt as travels with the urine will actually encourage the dissolution of <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for the stony matter formed in the human body</note>. For whatever dissolves a rock and hides it invisibly within itself: that, at least, will not suggest the origin of the stone. 

Thus far we have spoken of fixed earth dissolved by the spirit of salt, and of the vaporous and coagulated spirit of salt. Now let us speak of the volatile spirit breaking down into a solid body. 

<margin>17</margin> Sublime <term>stibium</term> <note>antimony</note> with an equal part of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride</note> over a modest fire, and you will see the salt rise up, tinged with various colors. Separate the color from the salt by water, and you will have a powder which, with <term>saltpeter</term> <note>potassium nitrate</note>, flies away almost entirely into flame. But if, left with the <term>sal ammoniac</term>, it is sublimed twice more by itself and freed from the salt, you will have a powder of <term>stibium</term> that is devoid of salt. If you then mix <term>saltpeter</term> into this, it will no longer be inflamed; instead, however much <term>saltpeter</term> you have mixed in is changed into earth, and it ignores the nature of salt. 

For the odor of sulfur penetrates the <term>saltpeter</term>. In the same way, the odor of the salt of urine and its volatile spirit [transform] the earthy spirit in the urine...

<summary>Van Helmont uses alchemical experiments with antimony and various salts to demonstrate how "spirits" can transform substances into solid, earthy matter. He argues that while some salts dissolve stones, the specific "spirit of urine" acts to coagulate and create the stone known as Duelech.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s argument that kidney stones (calculi) are not formed by simple heat or sedimentation, but by a process of "putrefaction" or chemical change within the urine. He begins by challenging the prevailing medical theories of his day regarding why certain age groups are more susceptible to stones.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
# CHAPTER IV.

<margin>18</margin>
...urine, stirred up by some degree of putrefaction <note>In 17th-century chemistry, "putrefaction" referred to a transformative breakdown or fermentation process, rather than just rotting.</note>, immediately transforms it into a stone <term>calculus</term>. Therefore, the urine is not turned into grains immediately upon urination, but only after it has taken on the beginnings of this putrefaction. This leads to a question: Why are children and the elderly more prone to stones than adult men? Is it because they are warmer? What if the medical Schools—lacking any real strength in their arguments—blame the "coldness" of children and the elderly in this matter? In doing so, they forget their own shame, for by their own decree, **Stone-formation** <note>original: "Lithiaſis"</note> is supposed to solidify through heat alone. What good does it do to call upon an abundance of phlegm <note>one of the four traditional bodily humors, thought to be cold and moist</note> if the only efficient cause—heat—is missing? If, I say, this phlegm (which they claim petrifies as such) is actually lacking in nature? Nor can they devise the same temperament or complexion for both children and the elderly without bring disgrace and confusion upon their own dogmas. Furthermore, they will find no similarity in the urine of the two groups. For the urine of children is thicker, while that of the elderly is watery and diluted. The urine of those suffering from stones <margin>*</margin> is also watery. I have seen elderly men in the past, troubled even unto death by continual **strangury** <note>original: "ſtranguria"; a condition characterized by painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination</note>, for whom diuretic remedies (that is, medicines to promote urination) made from saffron, mace, etc., as well as soothing remedies made from mallow and marsh-mallow, were useless and vanished without effect. This continued until the Physicians pronounced them to be besieged by a stone; yet, upon dissection, they were proven to be free of any stone. (Michael de Montaigne <note>The famous French essayist Michel de Montaigne, who wrote extensively about his own suffering from kidney stones.</note> says his uncle, the Bishop of Paris, was cut open in a fruitless surgery.) Thus, physicians have not yet learned how to divine the presence of a stone from the urine. To explain these cases of strangury, **Paracelsus** <margin>19</margin> invented his "frosty fiction" of **Tartar** <note>original: "Tartari"; Paracelsus believed that "Tartar"—like the crust in a wine barrel—formed in the body from food and drink, causing various diseases.</note>, which has never yet been found in those who have been dissected. Indeed, I later recognized that whenever the gall <note>bile</note> is weaker than it should be (as occurs in the elderly), it cannot transform the acidic **chyle** <note>original: "chylum"; the milky fluid of digested food</note> of the stomach into a salty salt. Therefore, because of a small amount of leftover acid, the strangury of the elderly persists daily, even when no stone is present. Similarly, new beers <margin>20</margin> provoke strangury in many people because of the residual and inherent...

<meta>catchword: ...inherent</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that stone formation is caused by chemical transformation rather than heat, debunking the Galenic "Schools" and Paracelsus's theory of Tartar. He uses the example of "phantom stones" and the chemistry of stomach acid to explain urinary pain in the elderly.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the chemical formation of stones, focusing on the "gall ferment" (bile) and why kidneys are more prone to stones than the bladder.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

-># ON THE STONE DISEASE<-

...and the sharpness of a more recent ferment. Under this heading—namely, the deficiency of the gall ferment—the urine of the elderly and children is less colored. From this, the following notable points follow:

<margin>21</margin> 1. Because of a lack of dross <term>scoria</term> <note>In Van Helmont's chemistry, this refers to waste material or pigment that normally prevents stony particles from sticking together</note>, or liquid waste, in the urine, the stone disease <term>Lithiasis</term> hardens more easily. 
2. The primary remedy is to strengthen the ferment of the gall. 
3. The urine seems sharper in cases of **strangury** <note>painful, drop-by-drop urination</note> and dribbling, insofar as it contains something acidic. 
4. The usefulness of the dross becomes clear, as does its beneficial connection within the urine.

<margin>22</margin> Next, the question was asked: Why is the stone frequent in the kidneys, but rarer in the bladder? I have answered elsewhere: because as long as the urine is in the veins, it is not yet perfected. For it does not yet smell like urine, nor does it have the properties of urine <note>original: "lotium"</note>, since it is not fitting for the blood to be imbued with the smell of waste. Therefore, the determination of urine comes from the Kidney; but the odor belongs to the putrefactive ferment, because it is waste. And thus it makes the "earth" <note>the solid mineral component</note> of the urine volatile. 

Furthermore, although the mellowed <note>original: "fracida," referring to a state of partial fermentation or softening, like ripening fruit</note> rot of the urine makes its earth smelly and putrefying, it does not remain in the person long enough to fully rot. Therefore, its mellowed earth needs the spirit of the urine in order to rot. For in the Kidney (where the fermental mellowing of the urine begins), the fresh and volatile earth easily joins with the spirit of the urine and becomes granulated—especially when the dross, the preservative against stones, has not yet arrived. It turns yellow, however, no less from its location than from the aforementioned dross. Thus, the Kidney pays the penalty for being the primary author of these conditions. 

Elsewhere, I will teach concerning the "womb of Duelech" <note>Duelech is a technical term for the stony matter formed in the body; its "womb" is the specific environment that allows it to grow</note>: that a disposition toward Duelech precedes the kidney. Because this disposition is vital and not merely excremental (as it is in the bladder), it also coagulates more effectively in the kidney than in the bladder. For the bladder, because it is a mere **sewer** <note>original: "cloaca"</note>, is utterly destitute of any ferment. But the intestines—insofar as they are the "stomach of the gall" (as discussed elsewhere regarding digestions)—are a vital and "cooking" receptacle. The bladder, however, is for mere excrement alone...

<summary>Van Helmont explains that kidney stones form when a lack of "gall ferment" prevents waste products (dross) from keeping urine components liquid. He argues that kidneys are more prone to stones than the bladder because the kidneys are active, fermenting organs, whereas the bladder is a simple "sewer" for waste.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont's chemical analysis of how stones form in the urinary tract. He is moving from the general theory of "mellowed earth" to the specific differences between stones found in the kidneys versus the bladder.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

### CHAPTER IV.

...restraint of the mind. Finally, the question is asked: Why is a **kidney stone** usually yellowish-tawny, while a **bladder stone** is whitish? For the kidney possesses a ferment for the creation of waste; it therefore requires a liquid and coloring <term>scoria</term> <note>original: "scoria" - refers to dross or the pigment-rich waste products of metabolism</note>. Furthermore, the kidney has blood nearby and its own tinted <term>parenchyma</term> <note>the functional tissue of the organ</note>. 

<margin>23</margin> The bladder, however, takes the "glue" from its own nearby nourishment and joins it to the "mellowed" earth <note>original: "terrae fracidae" - earth that has undergone a softening or ripening process</note> and the coagulating spirit within the body of the urine. Consequently, it is clear from this why <term>Duelech</term> <note>a specialized term used by Van Helmont and Paracelsus to refer to a stony accretion or "calculus"</note> formed in the bladder is harder. 

<margin>24</margin> Indeed, a large part of the bladder’s nourishment turns into **mucus**, which, together with the stony beginnings of coagulation, produces a harder and tougher <term>Duelech</term>. This is just as lime mixed with flour creates a cement that is far more durable. Even the kidneys—if they are eventually damaged in their solid fibers by the presence of <term>Duelech</term>—will thereafter eject white and quite hard stones. 

I have taught elsewhere that the nourishment of the bladder, before its digestion is complete, may be separated from the solid parts due to a stone or other irritation. It is then "wept out" like a mucous tear and mixed with the urine. I maintain that this is a waste product of the "final digestion" <note>the last stage of nutrient processing at the tissue level</note> that has gone astray and been hindered in the bladder. It is, in fact, sometimes an *effect* caused by the stone, rather than the cause of the stone itself—though it is often mistakenly assumed to be the cause. 

<margin>25</margin> For some physicians, marveling at such a great and continuous quantity of mixed mucus, and knowing it was not <term>purulent</term> <note>containing pus</note> (since they could not find an ulcer large enough to drip such a vast amount of pus), were at last forced by their own limited logic to claim that **catarrhs** <note>original: "catharros" - traditional medicine often blamed "catarrhs" or fluids dripping from the brain for various internal ailments</note> were flowing into the bladder—a ridiculous idea! Such miserable stupidities. 

Others, while not daring to suggest such empty excuses to a sane brain, yet still fearing pus, declare that ulcers of the kidney are the sources of this great "gluttony" or excess. They do this to such an extent that even if that mucus fills half the chamber pot along with the urine, without any previous abscess, they still believe...

<summary>Van Helmont explains that kidney stones are colored by blood and organ tissue, while bladder stones are whiter and harder because they are reinforced by mucus. He argues that this mucus is a result of irritation caused by the stone, mocking contemporary doctors who blame the fluid on "catarrhs" from the head.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont's investigation into why some people develop stones while others do not. He is transitioning from a discussion of how mucus and "mellowed earth" form stones to a critique of contemporary medical and astrological explanations for the disease.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE-DISEASE <note>original: "DE LITHIASI"</note></header>

### ON THE STONE-DISEASE

...from a painless kidney; to rain down just as much pus daily. Specifically, I have noted many people cured at **Spa** <note>original: "Spadæ" — the town in modern-day Belgium famous for its mineral springs and healing waters</note>, whom the shameful disputes of **Physicians** <note>original: "Medentum"</note>, regarding pus, ulcers, kidney consumption, and catarrhs, had already condemned to death. These people, when they returned healthy beyond the hope of the Physicians, would boast that they were cured by themselves, thanks to the beneficial advice of traveling.

<margin>26</margin> But why does my healthy urine deposit sand on the chamber pot in the cold, yet not in the heat while it is briefly held inside the body? I have said that by an **innate balsam** <note>In Van Helmont’s chemistry, a "balsam" is a natural preservative force that keeps the body's fluids from decaying or solidifying</note>, the urine is preserved so that it does not turn to stone or rot—both being equally avoided. Furthermore, the chamber pot is a vessel well-suited for such attachment, whereas the bladder is not. And finally, because earth is made volatile through the process of decay.

<margin>27</margin> There is also a doubt as to why, of twins nourished on the same milk, one suddenly becomes so prone to **stones** <note>original: "calculosus"</note>? To this doubt, the Schools <note>the traditional university authorities</note>, women, simpletons, and rustics alike think they have satisfied the question with a single response: if they name a "bad distemper" <note>original: "intemperiem" — a medical term for an imbalance of the four humors</note> of indisposition or an "inclination," and blame the humors.

<margin>28</margin> This "Inclination" is something the **Astrologers**—though they do not distinguish it by conception or by **animation** <note>original: "empsychosi" — the moment the soul enters the body</note>—nevertheless discriminate by the moment of birth; and thus far they cast the twins into various different fates.

<margin>29</margin> But at the very least, the root meaning of an "inclination" toward the stone makes **Paracelsus** <note>A famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note> suspect regarding his theory of **tartar** <note>Paracelsus believed that "tartar" (like the crust in wine barrels) precipitated out of food and drink to cause stones</note>. Indeed, the Schools have even deserted **Galen** <note>The ancient Greek physician whose theories dominated medicine for 1,500 years</note> on this point, being without light. Galen, content with an "unequal distemper" in the seminal constituents (even though they are of the same kind), thinks he has abundantly satisfied the inquiry, provided that this distemper has turned into a "second nature." He then follows this with despair, claiming that the condition is inseparable from the person from birth. He takes away the common name of "Inclination," but the previous darkness remains, if it is not even thicker; for he resolves one dispute with another dispute, and with his despair, he cuts off the desire for further inquiry.

<margin>30</margin> Meanwhile, it is certain that this dual nature of the question arises only from a **disorder** <note>original: "ataxia" — a state of confusion or lack of order in the matter of the body</note> of matter...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that stones do not form inside a healthy body due to a protective "balsam," and he critiques Astrologers and traditional physicians like Galen and Paracelsus for their vague and discouraging explanations of why certain individuals are prone to stones.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R IV.</header>

# C H A P T E R IV.

...must be taken. Since this [cause] is not found within such a simple homogeneity of the seed, it must necessarily be determined by the location, or rather, the great influence of the **uterine climate** <note>original: "climatis uterini" — Van Helmont suggests that the environment within the womb, not just genetics, shapes the health of the child</note>. For in this way the sides of women differ, so that we are each like two people, distinctly separated side-to-side; and other internal organs lie alongside the womb on either side. For **hereditary defects** <note>original: "hæreditariæ labes"</note> are indeed drawn from the first constituents and are evenly distributed throughout the entire conception if they descend from the parent or progenitor; but those found in a specific place remain adjacent to those locations and invade the parts closest to them.

It is a wonder! How easily our most tender beginnings obey foreign impressions, how easily, once received, they grow, and how stubbornly they finally persist. Also, how comfortably those "seed-beds" of diseases acquired soon after through one's own errors in living are hosted and ruled by the same powers as hereditary ones. And they enter more powerfully and are branded in more unusually, the more their union defiles the **Archeus** <note>the inner vital spirit or "master craftsman" that Van Helmont believed governed the functions of each organ</note> while we are still young. For as long as we are growing, the seeds of diseases—even if their influence is only inhaled like a scent—are incorporated into our fundamental beginnings, and the stony **entelechies** <note>original: "entelechiæ" — an Aristotelian term Van Helmont uses for the internal "blueprints" or active forces that drive the growth of a substance, in this case, the stone</note> of the seeds grow up with us as one single thing. I have discussed elsewhere more extensively how the ferments of seeds establish these, even through a mere scent. 

For from an intact nature, every human should be healthy and of a single inclination, were it not for the properties of the "middle life"—food, emotional disturbances, and climates—that caused **disorders** <note>original: "ataxiæ"</note> to creep into the **sons of Adam** <note>original: "filios Adæ" — a biblical reference to all of humanity</note>. Those things which sneak in with the mother's blood and the milk of nurses possess the treasures of life like domestic thieves, and do not easily depart except through the aids of renewal.

But coming closer to the heart of the matter, I say that in the kidney there is a **stercoraceous ferment** <note>original: "fermentum stercorarium" — a "dung-like" or digestive ferment. Van Helmont believed "ferments" were the chemical agents responsible for all bodily transformations</note> which causes the urine to break down; once this goes astray, and once...

<meta>catchword: aber- (likely "aberrante," meaning "wandering" or "straying")</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE</header>

marked by a deviant sign, the urine proceeds, and then by a spontaneous flow, gradually tends toward its ultimate **rot** <note>original: "putredinem" — in Helmontian medicine, "rot" or "putrefaction" is a transformative process that releases hidden powers within a substance</note>, under which lies the power of **volatilizing earth** <note>turning solid matter into a vaporous or spiritual state</note>. Since this power of **fermenting** <note>original: "fermentandi" — the vital process by which one substance is transformed into another through an internal agent</note> resides in the Kidney, the question of why one of two twins might have stronger **dung-like ferments** <note>original: "fermento stercoreo" — the waste-processing power of the organ</note> in their kidneys is resolved by the chapter on the **Unequal Strength of Parts**. Specifically, just as one twin's stomach may be easily upset while the other’s is robust, so a kidney richer in rotting ferment is more prone to the **fabrication of the stone** <note>original: "calculi"</note>. Furthermore, if the father, while procreating, has a bladder filled with urine, he is likely to sire offspring prone to the calamity of <term>Duelech</term> <note>a term used by Paracelsus and Van Helmont to refer specifically to the stony matter formed in the body</note>. Indeed, the rottenness of urine held too long attaches itself fermentally to the neighboring seed as it passes by. In this regard, <margin>31</margin> **Galen** errs quite ridiculously, claiming that a bit of urine is naturally and always added to every seed for the sake of "tickling" <note>Galen suggested urine provided a stimulus for ejaculation</note>. He was ignorant of the fact that not even a foreign hair can be mixed into the beginnings of generation without causing total destruction. However, as the things mentioned above serve our purpose, note that the **unequal strength of parts** is, as it were, necessary to the inner nature of things. For you cannot draw a wire from homogeneous gold that does not break in one part sooner than another; thus, it becomes weaker than itself. **Ataxia** <note>disorder or lack of coordination</note>, disparity, and **heterogeneity** <note>having diverse or different parts</note> are now part of the intimate essence of things, even if they are entirely foreign to their original essences. Hence, twins born from a single, individual seed cannot escape heterogeneity, especially that which is induced by such easy **contagion** at the very beginnings of things.

<margin>1</margin>
**G**od did not make death, nor is there a medicine of destruction, nor is the kingdom of the underworld upon the earth <note>original: "Deus non fecit mortem, nec est medicamentum exterminii, nec inferorum regnum in terra" — This is a quote from the Book of Wisdom 1:13-14</note>. For this reason, I have believed that no defect encountered in healing has flowed from the treasuries of Him who did not make death, but rather made remedies. Just as He does not err in His foresight, He would not leave the one He chose and created to be a Physician destitute and lacking in many respects.

<margin>"</margin> Therefore, a Physician is not meant to merely thrust a palliative <note>a remedy that masks symptoms rather than curing the cause</note> and useless remedy upon those suffering from the stone, like an impostor. For the sick man is in need of a Physician who might testify through good works that he was created by God. But after Medicine was erected into a profession, every wicked kind of man thrust himself into the practice of healing out of a greed for profit—an error the Magistrates, by right, ought to punish severely. Consequently, the **Schools** <note>the "Schools" refers to the established university medical authorities of the time, who followed the ancient Greek teachings of Galen and Aristotle</note> took the selection of those practicing medicine unto themselves; they judged as worthy whoever would subscribe to the ignorance of the Pagans, so that the University chairs and human lives might be entrusted to them. For several centuries now, this has passed through the hands of those in possession of power in Europe; charity has grown cold, and laziness has been introduced under a seal of safety. Long-standing custom has confirmed this acquired ignorance, pretending a right of prescription. Because of this, God has withdrawn His gifts and withheld what He otherwise would have given. Indeed, St. Paul...

<header>ON LITHIASIS</header>

[Pau]l <note>the text begins mid-sentence, likely referring to the Apostle Paul’s instructions in 1 Timothy 5:3</note> wishes that widows be honored, who are truly widows in good works: following the imitation of the commandment which established that the Physician must be honored, who is truly a Physician in good works, and would thus testify that he was created and chosen by God. For indeed, the works, diplomas <note>original: *diplomata*, referring to licenses or formal certifications</note>, signs, and merits of his honor follow worthily after his calling. 

<margin>3</margin> Ruminating on this passage <note>Ecclesiasticus 38:1, "Honor the physician for the need thou hast of him"</note> with myself, I find that honor is shown to the Physician because of necessity: which necessity presupposes a resulting fruit <note>the outcome or successful cure</note>. Otherwise, it is in vain. It is not, certainly, that the force of the commandment flows into the necessity—as if when a healthy person does not need a physician, the physician is not then to be honored. For a Judge, a Praetor, a Lawyer, a Soldier, a Lictor <note>a civil servant who attended magistrates</note>, a Torturer, a Potter, a Weaver, etc., would be prescribed for honor by the same right of necessity. 

Nevertheless, in mental matters, the whole context of words is always nothing but the concept of a single thing. Those who have perhaps tasted something intellectually and in an abstract manner will testify to this. And this can be demonstrated in a certain way. For by the same concept through which I consider a sword <note>original: *ensem*</note>, I first conceive a long, flat, polished, and sharp figure; likewise its material as hard, metallic, and not flexible in its line of straightness; and finally its purpose, not for cutting bread or wood etc., but for wounding. All these things, indeed, are represented to me simultaneously in a single mental concept of a sword. 

But in abstract mental matters, not only the accompanying conditions, but furthermore whatever can be said (indeed, cannot even be expressed) in many hours, is infused in a single concept—as if of one word—in an intellectual rapture <note>original: *intellectuali raptu*, a flash of intuitive or divine understanding</note>. 

Honor, however, is prescribed for the Physician, [by the creation/appointment] <unclear>creati-</unclear> of the Most High, on account of the necessity [for] <unclear>ad</unclear> healing them. Which surely in m[any cases?] <unclear>ultis</unclear> [carries] the simple significance of a Soldier, a Judge, a Tortur[er] <unclear>is</unclear>...

# CHAPTER V.

[Physicians] are valued as if they were chosen by the Most High <note>original: *Altissimo*, a common Latin epithet for God</note>: but rather as if they were promoted by men to perform duties required by the malice of men. Elsewhere, I have demonstrated that a fourfold darkness of ignorance, driven by the greed for possession, entered the medical profession together and left it stripped of honor. Namely: ignorance of causes, of the manner of process, of the remedy, and of the proper application. Indeed, just as **Pyrotechny** <note>original: *Pyrotechnia*, referring here to "the art of fire" or chemistry, which Van Helmont championed as the basis of medicine</note> brings physical bodies before the very eyes, so it opens the door to **natural philosophy** <note>the study of nature and the physical universe</note>. Under the ignorance and laziness of the Schools, therefore, true **Pharmacy** has lain hidden and depressed; that which ought to have brought light to the Physician was considered a entirely mechanical trade, and was handed over to the **Pharmacist** <note>original: *Pharmacopaeum*, or apothecary</note> and his wife. 

Indulge my freedom, Reader, whenever I dispute about God, about life, about diseases, about the Commonwealth, about one's neighbor, about my calling, about the true and the good, about the harmful, and about matters so serious and of such great moment for the sake of mortals. For I do not set out to hunt for the favor of any man. I have shown thus far the blind descriptions arising from ignorance of causes and remedies, or from laziness in investigation and the ease of agreeing with false principles. For this reason, we consequently predict unfortunate treatments of the stone <term>calculi curationes<gloss>medical treatments for kidney or bladder stones, the primary subject of this book</gloss></term>, deceptive healings, and desperate aids, as well as the miserable obedience of the sick. I shall proceed now.

<page-num>84</page-num>

-># ON THE FORMATION OF THE STONE<-

...existing in potential, it somehow pre-exists, so that it adapts the underlying mass of a prior being into a subsequent being for itself. Indeed, the pre-existence of such a being consists in a certain seminal spirit, in which are contained the patterns and shadowy foreknowledges of the dramas that things must perform. This spirit, I call the <term>archeus</term> <note>Van Helmont's term for the "master workman," the internal vital spirit that directs the functions of an organ and governs the formation of matter</note>; call it what you will. Let it suffice to know that nothing new arises in nature without a seed. Finally, every seed works through dispositions that serve it, which it propagates in matter according to its intended desires. But the intermediate instruments by which seeds organize matter, I call ferments. For just as the sour smell of a jar curdles milk, or the smell of leaven infects dough, or the foul smell of a cask turns wine into flat vinegar, etc. So in <term>urine</term> <note>original: *lotio*</note>, there is a seed for <term>Duelech</term> <note>a technical term used by Van Helmont and Paracelsus to denote a stone formed within the body, such as a kidney or bladder stone</note> (for I distinguish a stone from sand only by size, and signify both by the single name <term>Duelech</term>). It also has its own organizing ferment, often situated in the mere odor. Indeed, urine turns to stone more quickly in an old, smelly chamber pot than it does in a clean one. Yet that fermenting odor is not inherent to the urine, but is a foreign visitor which sometimes takes root in the kidney in such a way that, like a <term>Gorgon</term> <note>a reference to the mythological Medusa, whose gaze turned onlookers to stone</note>, it toils continually and incessantly in manufacturing <term>Duelech</term>, as if working toward its own perfection. Thus, the internal vital spirit of the parts is drawn against its will toward a foreign goal, and is led away to where it would not go <margin>6</margin> by the persistence of a foreign ferment. Therefore <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note> is in error, who notes a certain "tartaric mucilage" as if it were the primary and "betrothed" material of the stone, dispersed through the veins; and he urges that it be removed through certain laxatives. But I have dealt with these trifles regarding both the causes of the stone according to the ancients and in my treatise on <term>Tartar</term> <note>original: *Tartaro*; in Paracelsian medicine, "Tartar" was the name for stony precipitates or wastes that formed in the body, analogous to the crusts in wine barrels</note>. For it is well established that in human urine (even in healthy people) there is always a proximate, invisible matter, and a seed for <term>Duelech</term>; whether or not the stone actually breaks out into reality,

<summary>Van Helmont explains that kidney and bladder stones (Duelech) are formed not by "tartar" in the blood as Paracelsus thought, but by a specific "seminal spirit" and "ferment" that acts upon the urine, comparing the process to the petrifying gaze of a Gorgon.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>
<sig>f 3</sig>

# CHAPTER V.

...for as long as urine is ripened within us, or after it has flowed out of us. Urine indeed contains the essential beginnings for the <term>Duelech</term> <note>a technical term used by alchemists like Paracelsus and Van Helmont to denote a stone formed within the body, such as a kidney or bladder stone</note>; yet it is accidental to the urine whether these beginnings ripen or not. And although urine contains within itself the seed and matter of the stone, it is not the <term>uterus</term> <note>womb or breeding ground</note> of the stone, but only the matrix of the stony seed, which seeks and finds a womb for itself, whether outside or inside the body. For just as a <term>being in act</term> <note>original: *ens in actu*; a thing that has fully realized its form</note> must perish so that a <term>being in potential</term> <note>original: *ens in potentia*; a thing that exists as a possibility or seed</note>—pre-existing in a seminal way—may arise from it, it is necessary that the essence and matter of the urine from which the stone is made should first break down <note>original: *fatiscere*; to gape, crack, or dissolve</note> before the stone can be formed. In this process, however, only a small delay occurs. In the urine, there is suitable matter and a seed for the stone; it only lacks an actuating and exciting ferment to cause the seed to germinate. This is because the transformation of a thing implies its own falling away through a neutral state, mediated by its own specific ferment. Therefore, the corruptive ferment of the urine is what awakens the seed.

For this reason, it is shown through mechanical experiment that uncontaminated urine is preserved longer under the "balsam" of our internal economy <note>the body's natural preservative forces</note> and under its steaming heat, than that which otherwise, within a few hours, yields to corruption in a cold urinal, and consequently fouls the chamber pot with <term>sand</term> <note>original: *sabulo*; the gritty, sandy precursor to a solid stone</note>. For kidneys that are in any way polluted have already conceived a corruptive ferment for the urine. To the kidney, indeed, belongs its own excremental ferment by nature; but that is not yet sufficient for the propagation of a stone. Thus, the stone exists in the urine as a *being in potential*, which bursts forth into *act* while the corruption of the urine—the prior state of being—is imminent.

Meanwhile, it is true that certain provinces provide no small help toward the frequency of the stone. For <term>Illyria</term> <note>a region in the western Balkans</note> was once populous, but today <margin>7</margin> is almost deserted because its foul breath <note>original: *tetro halitu*; referring to miasmas or unhealthy air</note> cuts off life. For there are places prone to <term>scurvy</term> <note>original: *Scorbuto*</note>, <term>asthma</term> <note>original: *Asthmati*</note>...

<summary>Van Helmont explains that while urine contains the "seed" of a stone, a specific ferment is required to trigger its physical formation. He observes that stones form more quickly in cold urinals than in the body, suggesting that kidney health and regional environments (like Illyria) play a role in this "ripening" process.</summary>

<header>ON THE STONE DISEASE</header>

<meta>catchword: dregs,</meta>

...or led as if harmful. It is not indeed as Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals in medicine</note> thinks, that such places are fertile with <term>tartars</term> <note>original: *tartarorum*; Paracelsus used "tartar" to describe the stony precipitates or sediments found in the body, comparing them to the crust left by wine in a barrel</note>. This is evident since Europeans, carried on the same ship and eating our usual foods, are still afflicted by foreign and local diseases. For the seeds of some diseases are present in certain locations, and they forge materials suitable for themselves. If they do not find materials already compliant or "wedded" to them, then away with those trifles which assume and require a bare allusion of pre-existing tartaric matter—and thus a <term>mucilage</term> <note>original: *mucaginem*; a thick, gluey substance</note>—to stand in for <term>Duelech</term> <note>a kidney or bladder stone</note>, and which ground their theory in a fictional allegory of artificial crafts.

As if the consistency of a stone were no different from that of dried mucus? As if slimy saliva were not generated from a drink that is not slimy? As if every generation and hardening of stone were ascribed simply to mucus and heat? Indeed, if the heart—being hotter than the bones—were also harder, perhaps their theses would deserve credit. But nature scorns similarities fetched from far-off artificial crafts. 

Therefore, I understand that the <term>stercoraceous ferment</term> <note>original: *stercoreum fermentum*; literally "dung-like" or excremental, referring here to the waste-processing nature of the kidneys</note> of the kidney, when excessively exalted, then disposes the "coagulating spirit" of the urine and the matter of <term>volatile earth</term> <note>in alchemy, "volatile earth" refers to solid matter that has been refined or made subtle enough to move through the body's fluids</note> so that they grow together into the seed of Duelech. For there is no transmutative principle in nature outside of innate or acquired ferments (as I have mentioned elsewhere regarding ferments), except for those artificial ones established through fire. 

<margin>§</margin> Whence also every similarity deduced from those artificial processes is inappropriate. For in one way a potter's clay is burned into stone without a "seed," but in another way does every <term>petrification</term> <note>stone-formation</note> happen when it is derived from a seminal beginning. Moreover, that "earth" is present in urine is proved first by the mechanical distillation of urine <note>original: *lotii*; an older term for urine</note>. Then, from the distilled ash, much earth finally remains, which otherwise during nutrition exhales entirely as a volatile substance, is consumed, and leaves no dregs of itself behind. But the volatile earth in urine comes from what has been putrefied. Therefore, as a stercoraceous putrefaction grows in the urine, being brought in, for instance...

<summary>The author argues against the idea that body stones are formed simply by heat drying out mucus. Instead, he proposes that a specific kidney "ferment" acts upon the urine to create a "seed" that grows into a stone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s investigation into the chemical composition of kidney and bladder stones. <page-num>87</page-num>

-> ### CHAPTER V. <-

...dross, or liquid dung, it immediately obeys the coagulating spirit: namely, as often as the mutual action of both is stirred up by the ferment of putrefaction. 
<margin>9</margin>
I distilled an extracted <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for a kidney or bladder stone</note> by itself, and I drew nothing from it except a foul spirit of urine, a yellow crystal, and at the same time an oil, such as is drawn from dried urine. What remained at the bottom for me was a black earth, burnt, crumbly, and tasteless. Therefore, the authors of the *Chemical Apprenticeship* <note>original: *Tyrocinii chimiatri*; likely referring to Jean Beguin’s famous 1610 textbook *Tyrocinium Chymicum*, which was a standard manual for beginners</note> deceive their readers, as many as boast that the <term>Ludus</term> of Paracelsus <note>original: *Ludum Paracelsi*; a legendary "stone" or remedy described by Paracelsus as the "prince of stone-breakers" for its supposed ability to dissolve internal calculi</note>—the foremost of stone-breakers—is found through the distillations and preparations of the human stone. For they write mere trifles and colossal lies. I cannot be indignant enough at their impudence and rashness in a matter of such easy experimentation, especially where anyone can conveniently learn this from the scrapings of a chamber pot. Not even the smallest amount is drawn from the human stone of those things which those <term>pedotribes</term> <note>original: *Pędotribę*; literally "youth-trainers" or "gymnastic masters," here used as a derogatory term for medical practitioners who merely "rehearse" or "rub in" old theories without understanding</note> rashly write. 

It is certain meanwhile that through the means of putrefaction, not a few things become volatile which were previously bound more tightly when their gates were not yet opened; for even plants, when distilled, give off more potent waters if they have not yet putrefied. Therefore, we do not perish struck by a single weapon, since individual things which are mild, if they deviate but a little, create new calamities for us. Indeed, the <term>parenchyma</term> <note>the functional tissue</note> of the kidneys, the hardest of all the internal organs and devoid of nerves and arteries, was most suitable for the dross-like ferment of the urine. If to this is added even a hint of the ferment of urinal putrefaction, a swift inclination toward <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>the formation of stones in the body</note> is impressed upon the passing urine. Since the odor alone of the aforementioned putrefaction in the urine stirs up a heterogeneity <note>a state where different, incompatible substances begin to separate or react</note> that was previously hidden. For urine that falls into a dirty chamber pot immediately smells foul, and it gives forth the sand hidden within it much more quickly than that which has been collected in a clean glass. For I have shown by an indubitable experiment, that...

<page-num>88</page-num>

-> ## ON LITHIASIS <-

...even the urine of healthy people, to attach <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for a kidney or bladder stone</note> to chamber pots, in the form of grains or scales. And this does not happen immediately upon urination, so that they settle straight away: but some hours thereafter, they are attached. Namely, while the urine, unfolding the <term>ferment</term> <note>a chemical or vital agent that transforms one substance into another, similar to how yeast transforms dough</note> conceived from the kidney, enters the path of corruption. In <term>Nephritics</term> <note>patients suffering from kidney disease or kidney stones</note> indeed, the urine takes on that putrefactive ferment which is otherwise not communicated to the urine of the healthy, whose urine is otherwise sufficiently characterized by its own stercoral [fecal] ferment. Furthermore, it is not necessary for actual rot to be present in the kidney to stir up sand inside. Just as urine in chamber pots does not yet stink while it has already freed itself of gravel: but a threatened, or incipient, putrefaction is enough, so that the spirits, by their own right, may freely take control and act in turn upon each other once the barriers have been cast down. I consider this "threatened" putrefaction to be that which is present only seminally in the <term>Archeus</term> <note>in Van Helmont's system, the vital governing spirit or internal "chemist" of an organ that directs its functions</note> of the kidneys, although not yet unfolded. For otherwise, if there were even a tiny bit of actual rot in the kidney, a slow fever would accompany it; however small it might be, it easily takes root in the urine, into which, evidently, the urinary ferment has already penetrated, which in itself is both stercoral and putrid. For the association of rot and excrement is easy in the symbolic ferment. Whoever, therefore, wishes to ensure that their urine does not turn to stone inside them: let them look ahead in good time, lest it turn to stone inside them unseasonably. For there are things that, with a pleasant smell, tint the urine and the kidneys and thus soothe the organs. Insofar as they are <term>Diuretics</term> <note>substances that promote the production of urine</note>, or urine-movers, they gain entry to the kidneys and mix themselves with the urine. For whatever things, by hidden or manifest quality, have earned the nomenclature of <term>stone-breakers</term> <note>original: *saxifragi*; plants or medicines believed to break up or dissolve urinary stones</note>; they certainly clean and wipe away, and thus far they comfort kidneys threatened by putrefaction: but truly they do not melt or dissolve the sand at all. Such as are the tart [acidic] springs, stones, and diuretic herbs, which by washing and scouring—

<summary>Van Helmont explains that kidney stones form when a "ferment" from the kidneys triggers a process of incipient putrefaction, causing urine to solidify into "Duelech." He argues that while many "stone-breaking" remedies can cleanse the kidneys, they often fail to actually dissolve stones that have already solidified.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R  V.  89</header>

## CHAPTER V.

...and cast out sand and finer clumps; however, they do not dissolve them, much less do they inhibit the new beginnings of a <term>calculus</term> <note>a medical stone, such as a kidney or bladder stone</note>. This is because, being devoid of <term>balsam</term> <note>in Helmontian medicine, a vital, preservative substance that protects the body from decay</note> and the seasoning of a pleasant odor, they do not appease the filth of the putrefactive <term>ferment</term>, even if it is still dormant. For just as a plant's regrowth is not prevented by lopping off branches, but by uprooting, so also <term>nephritis</term> <note>inflammation or disease of the kidneys</note> is not cured by merely driving out the stone. Nothing worthy of reward has been achieved if an epileptic is raised from a fall, but not also protected from a recurrence in the future.

Yet the <term>Schools</term> <note>the traditional academic medical establishments that followed the ancient teachings of Galen and Aristotle</note> have touched this peak of healing in vain; indeed, they have rather despaired of it, because they saw the stain of <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>the disease of stone formation</note> contracted and often passed down to descendants by hereditary right, growing ever more fierce.

<margin>10</margin> For since they had seen patients who, after being cut for a stone in the bladder <note>referring to lithotomy, the surgical removal of bladder stones</note>, often remained free of it for the rest of their lives, the Physicians—promising themselves the same outcome for kidney stones and not worrying at all about a recurrence—persuaded their patients to hope for the best. These doctors hoped for the best too: namely, that they would receive more money at the next "market day" of the disease's return. Relying, of course, on foolish principles, they think they should only use milder **laxatives** from time to time to sweep away the preceding mass of the stone. They actually believe they are wiping away all the material of the stone from the stomach. To speak seriously, they boast through their "blessed" laxatives that they have provided a palliative cure, provided the patient is obedient in repeated bowel movements and observes dietary rules. But I have sufficiently dealt with these trifles in my book on fevers. For we are nourished by the same things of which we consist.

<margin>11</margin> Nor are the solid parts nourished except by a related, spermatic, and viscous liquid. Therefore, the simplicity of <term>Galen</term> <note>an ancient Greek physician whose humoral theories Van Helmont frequently attacked</note> is all the more laughable; he forbade eating membranes, nerves, and tendons—the very parts of our primary constitution—in foods, lest **mucilage** or <term>pituita</term> <note>original Latin: "pituita," referring to phlegm or mucus, one of the four humors</note> should grow from them. He was surely forgetful that the similar parts within us are immediately [nourished] by that vital mucilage alone...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that traditional doctors fail to cure kidney stones because they only remove the stone itself without addressing the "ferment" that causes it to form. He mocks the Galenic focus on laxatives and dietary restrictions, particularly Galen's avoidance of "mucilaginous" foods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s argument that kidney stones are caused by a specific local "ferment" within the kidney itself, rather than by a general bodily imbalance or dietary "tartar." He highlights the clinical observation that one kidney can be diseased while the other remains perfectly healthy.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
->### ON THE STONE DISEASE<-

...are nourished. Meanwhile, the <term>Schools</term> <note>the traditional academic medical authorities following the teachings of Galen</note> do not notice that often only one of the kidneys, on just one side, produces stones and subsequently becomes entirely encrusted with stone, <margin>12</margin> while the other kidney, in the meantime, performs its duty correctly for the rest of the patient's life. From this, at the very least, it must be admitted that the urine does not turn to stone in its own foundation because of some fault of its own, or because of mucus (which has already been soundly rejected), nor finally from the diet or some imagined **tartar** <note>a term popularized by Paracelsus to describe stony deposits from food and drink</note> in food and drink. Rather, it is because the kidneys, through their own defect, stir up a faulty **ferment** <note>in Helmontian medicine, a "ferment" is a specific vital power or catalyst that directs how matter is transformed</note>, and finally give birth to this unusual monster.

For often one of the kidneys has long flowed with much **sand** <note>fine gravelly deposits in the urine</note>, and then is completely blocked by a stone. The patient feels a dull pain on that side, but subsequently no sand is passed for the rest of life. Eventually, the Schools do indeed notice this through *Anatomy* <note>original: "Anatomen"; the practice of dissection</note>; yet they do not cease to condemn the innocent stomach for producing "stony phlegm" for only one kidney and not the other. 

Therefore, let the stomach be flogged for the crime of this *Lithiasis* <note>the disease of stone formation</note>; let it be swept out with brooms; let it endure "minorative" <note>mildly laxative</note> and common evacuations for three days; and let it tolerate even harsher punishments from purgatives during the fixed changes of the year. Indeed, let the stomach endure the torture of having **cauteries** <note>medical branding or chemical burning of the skin to create a permanent sore, intended to "drain" bad humors</note> impressed under the shoulders and the backs of the knees to prevent, divert, or pull away the "flowing phlegm," and other such animal fictions. The Doctors rage all the more cruelly against the stomach for a crime it did not commit, acting as Torturers, because the stomach allows its cautery wound to be closed with a scar, while the healers keep it forced open. 

As if this fictional phlegm, rushing down from the level of the head, remaining unchanged in the stomach, would glide through the intestines, be sucked up again by the **mesenteric vessels** <note>original: "Mezaraicis"; the veins that drain the intestines</note> without any harmful distinction, and from there be carried again to the liver and kidneys—unless it were revealed and diverted from its intended journey to the outside by opening the skin below the shoulder or the knee. Good Jesus! Wisdom of the Father! Are these your Schools, which [teach] such things...?

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones are a localized disease caused by a specific "ferment" in the kidney, mocking traditional doctors who blame the stomach and use painful cauteries and purges as ineffective treatments.</summary>

# CHAPTER V.

...sorts of delusions do they propose to the gullible common folk? And how do they deceive their own with mere trifles? Do they torment mortals with so many butcheries? Far be it. Far be it to believe that this is the doctrine of truth: that is, your doctrine. But the enemy of the primary <margin>13</margin> truth, the foe of mankind, brought forth these nonsense-songs and continues to protect them. Furthermore, some prescribe <term>diuretics</term> <note>remedies that encourage the flow of urine</note>, while others, fearing in the meantime, are held back, lest perhaps a <term>stone</term> <note>original: "calculus"</note> driven out of the kidney should get stuck in the passage. 

<margin>14</margin> For thus an Abbess, oppressed by a descending <term>stone</term>, during the <term>Dog Days</term> <note>the hottest period of summer, from the Latin "Canicula"</note>, at the persuasion of a circle of <term>physicians</term> <note>original: "Medentum"</note>, abstained from a diuretic remedy; she feared that perhaps by the heat of the season and the diuretic, the <term>stone</term> might grow larger and harder. She waited, therefore, through four sleepless days with immense wailing, until, of its own accord, the little stone reached the bladder. Then the physicians' counsel was exulted: they claimed that if she had not followed their rule, she surely would not have saved her life. 

A certain noblewoman, infested with a <term>stone</term> and a fever, after bloodletting <note>original: "phlebotomiam"</note> repeated four times, along with <term>enemas</term>, <term>minoratives</term> <note>mild laxatives</note>, <term>laxatives</term>, <term>blister-inducers</term> <note>original: "vesicatoria"; substances applied to the skin to cause blistering, intended to draw out "bad humors"</note>, and other things of that kind, survived for ten whole days with cries, becoming a spectacle for the <term>physicians</term>: because they could not find an hour free from fever in which they could have administered a drug against the stone, nor would her strength have sufficed for what was essentially a new butchery. 

Indeed, what I have seen perpetrated by <term>physicians</term> under the pretext of "heat" while treating patients, I could scarcely describe in a whole volume without horror and pity. For I remember a Jesuit in Antwerp in the year 1606, whose both kidneys were besieged by stones and denied him the passage of urine; he finally breathed his last after a two-day struggle under the <term>physicians</term>. For they were debating over <note>a proverbial expression for a trivial or useless dispute</note> the "shadow of Apuleius’s donkey." Namely, whether a <term>suppository</term> or indeed an <term>enema</term> should be applied to him. <term>Diuretics</term>, or stone-expelling agents, were dreaded by everyone. 

Meanwhile, a certain merchant, Joannes Vermieren, having suffered from stagnant urine for eight days and being now near death, took a diuretic made from the juice of mil-
