<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s discussion on the treatment of kidney and bladder stones. The previous page ended with a list of ineffective or dangerous remedies prescribed by the medical establishment of his time, concluding with "laxatives."</meta>

# ON THE STONE

...of woodlice, and the juice of black shellfish, in which he had boiled one grain of <term>Spanish Fly</term> <note>original: "cantharidum"; a toxic beetle used historically as a diuretic and irritant</note> drunk in a single draft. These things I dismiss. 

<margin>15</margin> I settle the dispute over <term>diuretics</term> <note>medicines that promote urination</note> in this way: Every stone is either larger than its <term>ureter</term> <note>the tube passing urine from the kidney to the bladder</note>, or smaller, or equal to it. If it is smaller, diuretics will be appropriate and not to be feared. If, however, it is larger than it should be, diuretics will be plainly useless and in vain. But if it is equal, corresponding to the size of the ureter, it is better for it to be expelled as quickly as possible, lest it increase in size through delay. 

<margin>16</margin> Nevertheless, because while a stone is passing, the ureter—contracted from pain—is generally <term>convulsed</term> <note>original: "crispatur," meaning curled or shriveled up</note>, diuretics given during a <term>paroxysm</term> <note>a sudden attack or fit of pain</note> must be administered with precaution. Specifically, one must provide remedies that inhibit the pain and the resulting contraction. From the neglect or ignorance of this single point alone, it occasionally happens that stones have stood still in mid-passage and killed patients with miserable wailings. And this occurs not so much from the "insolence" of the diuretics as from the error of the **Physicians**. For it must not be thought that the canal of the ureter is of unequal tightness, such that a stone, which descended through the ureter at the first impulse, should finally get stuck, squeezed by the narrowness of the path. Rather, those later compressions are morbid <term>shrivelings</term> <note>original: "crispaturæ"</note> and <term>spasmodic</term> <note>original: "spasmodēs"</note>, caused by pain, as I have discussed elsewhere in my writings on *Sense and Sensation*. 

Therefore, I highly encourage and judge as necessary both external poultices and internal remedies 
<margin>17</margin> which soothe those convulsive movements. Why then should I not distinguish between diuretics, whose purposes and selections were hardly known before now? 
1. Indeed, some sharpen the urine with a corrosive poison, such as **Spanish Fly**. 
2. Others provoke acidity and leave it in the urine, and excite <term>strangury</term> <note>painful, frequent, and "drop-by-drop" urination</note>. Such are fresh **beers**. 
3. There are those that make the urine <term>abstersive</term> <note>cleansing or scouring</note>, such as acidic springs, **vitriol of Mars** <note>iron sulfate</note>, **crab stones** <note>original: "lapis cancrorum"; calcium deposits found in the stomach of crayfish</note>: and likewise herbs everywhere enjoying the name of diuretics. Each contains a volatile alkali, or at least that which is to be digested below...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that stones get stuck in the ureter due to pain-induced spasms (shriveling) rather than just the narrowness of the passage. He categorizes diuretics into poisonous, acid-provoking, and cleansing types, emphasizing the need to treat the underlying contraction.</summary>
<keywords>diuretics, kidney stones, ureter, Spanish Fly, strangury, vitriol of Mars, crab stones, spasms, medical error</keywords>

<header>C H A P T E R V.</header>

# CHAPTER V.

...acquire an impulse to be carried forward. For thus far, <term>diuretics</term> <note>medicines that promote the production of urine</note> generally contribute to a wound-healing drink. This is because in every wound, an acidity—which is the indicator and companion of all rot in the flesh—soon arises; <term>alkalis</term> <note>substances that neutralize acids</note> easily absorb and consume this acidity. For this reason, it was a simple countryman who used to heal the wounded with a <term>lye</term> <note>original: "lixivio"; a solution made from wood ashes</note> of linden wood. Likewise, "crab stones" <note>original: "lapis cancrorum"; calcium deposits found in the stomachs of crayfish, used as an antacid</note>, when boiled or steeped in wine, remarkably represent the taste of lye. 

4. There are also certain things that provoke urine and stimulate the expulsive faculty insofar as they generate a "putridity" of the urine. Of this kind are radishes, asparagus, etc. For I saw a **Lawyer** who had never been afflicted with <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>the formation of stony concretions (stones) in the body</note> until after he had returned home from a rather large meal of asparagus; thereafter, he lay bedridden with excruciating pain—not so much from stones as from the finest sands—returning every fortnight or so for several years. From this I learned that the error of a single evening induced a "bad habit" in the kidneys which could hardly be removed afterward. I also recognized from this the proneness of our nature, which listens so quickly to its own ruin, and having once fallen, rises again slowly and with difficulty, even with the favor of medicines. 

<margin>18</margin> Finally, I saw that this kind of habit, though it had persisted for several years, was nonetheless corrected. Thus, those inclinations which they call <term>Intemperies</term> <note>a Galenic term for an imbalance of the "humors" or bodily qualities</note>, even when they have turned into a second nature, are changeable and removable—contrary to the despairs of the medical **Schools**. 

5. There are also diuretics which, by their pleasant odor, refresh the urine and the kidneys. Such as mace, nutmeg, turpentine, mastic, juniper, etc. It is as if the kidneys, comforted by the scent, become mindful of their duty. 

6. Then there are also those which, from a lye-like alkali, change during digestion into a sharpness that cleanses the urinary passages like soap; they stimulate the excretory power and cut through clinging filth. Of this kind are those things collected from shells, stones, and the ashes of appropriate things, which alone seem worthy of the name <term>saxifrages</term> <note>literally "stone-breakers"</note>.

-> # ON LITHIASIS <-
<note>Lithiasis: the medical condition of forming stones in the kidneys or bladder</note>

they seem to be, especially if they have been brought to a degree of **volatility** <note>the state of being easily evaporated, which Van Helmont associated with higher medicinal potency</note>. 

7. Finally, there is a type of <term>diuretics</term> <note>medicines that promote the production of urine</note> which, even in a small quantity, pours out an abundance of urine from the whole body, such as <term>woodlice</term> <note>original: "millepedes"; these insects were commonly dried and powdered in early modern medicine</note>, and species of <term>Brooklime</term> <note>original: "Becapunga"</note>. Likewise, there is the juice of black, oblong sea mollusks, and whatever contains <term>volatile nitre</term> <note>a term for saltpeter or similar nitrogenous salts believed to have active spiritual properties</note>, and those things which, by their own nature, awaken sluggish kidneys. 

8. There is also a type which is useful for soothing kidney pains and which strengthens those that are failing. Such a quality is found in saffron, rhubarb, and <term>cassia</term> <note>a legume used as a laxative</note> when they are "inverted"—that is, when they have first been deprived of their laxative power. 

9. <term>Spirit of sea salt</term> <note>original: "Spiritus salis marini"; an early chemical term for hydrochloric acid</note> is not only a diuretic and simultaneously a relief for <term>strangury</term> <note>painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination</note> in those for whom a <term>stone</term> <note>original: "calculus"</note> is rolling in the bladder; it furthermore reduces stones in the kidneys, provided it is distilled by the final fire of a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>original: "reverberii"; a furnace that reflects heat back onto the substance being heated, used for intense chemical transformations</note>. 

It is not enough, therefore, to say that diuretics "create urine." Rather, one must also determine whether they do so by stimulating the attractive faculty, by the liquefaction of the urine, by irritation, by the summoning of putrefaction, or finally by some other title. Nor is it enough to say that milk whey contains something of a nitrous nature; for it also contains a residue of the former blood, from which it becomes <term>cadaverous</term> <note>corpse-like or foul-smelling</note>, and thus it maintains the same character as **Asparagus**. 

Indeed, many things provoke urine by comforting the kidneys and suffuse the urine with a pleasant odor; others are more troublesome due to their sharpness, such as those which accelerate a putrid urinary ferment. 

<margin>19</margin> These are entirely harmful to those suffering from stones. Therefore, it is a great error of the Physicians that <term>Asparagus</term> is boiled into almost any <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "apozematibus"; medicinal drinks made by boiling herbs</note>. 

Furthermore, I have sometimes feared the habit of lying on only one side. This is because a kidney lying flat (on the back) might be blocked by the weight of the intestines resting upon it, and the urine stagnating within it would become sandy if it were closed off there daily for several hours. This is especially true because a kidney lying on the back is separated by at least ten fingers from the <term>vena cava</term> <note>the large vein that carries blood to the heart</note>, and the bladder is situated in the middle between each kidney. I therefore persuaded myself that a kidney lying on its back would not...

He also proposes a mechanical theory that sleeping posture can lead to urine stagnation and "sandy" deposits by compressing the kidneys.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s discussion of the physical causes of kidney stones, moving from the properties of diuretics to the mechanical and spiritual causes of stone formation, specifically addressing posture and his chemical theories on the "spirit of urine."</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

### CHAPTER 5

...that it could empty itself upward, into the bladder. But afterwards I realized that this fear of mine was vain, and that in respect to the <term>Archeus</term> <note>the internal vital force or "master-chemist" that Van Helmont believed directed all bodily functions</note>, nothing is truly "below." Nor was it enough to have investigated this theoretically. Therefore, a man became known to me at an opportune time who, from childhood, had never lain on his left side. Even as an old man, he had not yet suffered from <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>stone formation; original: "Lithiasim"</note>. I observed another, who never slept except on his back or his right side; yet, despite this, he ended up <term>calculous</term> <note>afflicted with stones</note> in his left and prone kidney. I repeat here: that the clear, distilled liquor of my urine carried its own "earth" upward with it through the <term>alembic</term> <note>a vessel used in distillation</note>, shaped it into true <term>Duelech</term> <note>Van Helmont's specific term for the stony matter formed in the body</note> on the sides of the vessel, and fixed it there. This hardening was caused by the <term>spirit of urine</term> <note>likely an ammoniacal solution derived from urine</note>, which can coagulate many things in various ways. For it condensed <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>alcohol; original: "spiritum vini"</note> into a <term>volatile mass</term> <note>original: "ossam volatilem"; a reference to the white precipitate formed when ammonia and alcohol meet</note>. 

<margin>20</margin> 

But if it finds a fixed object of the nature of salt, it is turned into salt, just as happens to the spirit of wine when it meets <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>potassium carbonate; original: "sale tartari"</note>. Or, when the spirit of spring salt is drunk up by a related salt, it becomes salt. If, however, the spirit of urine finds a fixed earth which it may corrode, and it lacks a coagulable object, it is absorbed by the earth and is subdued by it; thus, the author of coagulation is itself coagulated passively. But where the spirit of urine finds a volatile substance that is not yet coagulated, but is nonetheless coagulable (because it is of an earthy nature), it exerts its power of coagulation and binds that same <term>effluvia</term> <note>original: "apporrheam"; refers to invisible emanations or particles</note> into earth. By the joined forces of both sides, a "new creature" is made, which is the birth of **Duelech**. 

<margin>21</margin> 

However, the <term>Schools</term> <note>the traditional medical universities following the ancient theories of Galen and Aristotle</note>, insisting on their principles of bodily heats, prescribe that one should not sleep on their back, and that the loins should be rubbed with cooling ointments; they even suggest a thin plate of lead should be worn topically. They command a wool mattress instead of a feather bed, so that the kidneys do not grow too warm. Furthermore, they spread a hide of leather between the blankets and the mattress. To be sure, the Schools are only occupied with the "defeat of the symptoms," and they look...

<meta>...toward</meta>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of contemporary medical schools and their misunderstanding of the relationship between heat and the formation of kidney stones. He specifically disputes the idea that external heat from bedding is a primary cause of the ailment.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

# ON LITHIASI
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"; the study of stone formation in the body</note>

...which is merely a byproduct, or a later consequence, and by no means a cause—not even an incidental one. For by obsessing over trifles, the physicians follow one another in succession without any sign of providing relief. Indeed, these "waking dreamers" are so earnest that they flatter the sick. For stones are not born because the loins are hot; rather, the loins are hot because stones are being born.

<margin>22</margin> They choose wool over feathers, therefore, because of the heat of the latter (so they say). They are unaware that feathers actually produce less heat than wool, because they do not exclude the surrounding air as thoroughly. 

<margin>23</margin> The sense of touch can judge this. Furthermore, even granted that a feather bed might heat the body lying upon it and wrapped within it more than wool would: nevertheless, all that heat is halted if a linen sheet is placed between the feathers or the wool. Indeed, the heat that emerges from feathers or wool is not the heat of these simple materials themselves; rather, it is the **reflected heat** <note>original: "calor reflexus"</note> of the person lying on them, which is then received into the feathers or wool. From there, having been deposited in the middle (the linen), it returns—not stronger than it was before, but almost equally tempered to the same degree of heat as the body itself possesses. 

This dispute is visually decided by the **glass instrument** <note>original: "organum vitreum"; an early version of the thermometer which Van Helmont used to prove that "heat" was a measurable quantity rather than just a philosophical quality</note> constructed to measure the temperature of the surrounding air, about which I have written in my *Elemental Principles*. 

Nor does it prove the contrary that a **kidney-stone sufferer** <note>original: "calculosus nephriticus"</note> feels warmer in a feather bed than on a wool mattress. For that does not happen because of any greater heat in the feathers; rather, it is because the patient sinks deeper into a feather bed, whereas on a wool **mattress** <note>original: "culcitro"</note> he merely lies on top, and cooling air blows in from the sides. 

Will the **Schools** <note>original: "Scholæ"; referring to the traditional academic institutions that followed the ancient Greek medical teachings of Galen</note> never distinguish anything from its foundation, cause, and root? Will they always think like peasants based on the **pagan dogma** <note>original: "dogmate gentilitio"; Van Helmont frequently criticized "pagan" Greek philosophy for leading medicine astray</note> of heat and cold? I beg you, for the love of God—by which everyone might hope for a clear view of themselves once this age is over—cast off your stubborn...

### CHAPTER VI.

...arrogance, presumption, and laziness, and do not despise a better doctrine.

# CHAPTER VII.

->The Womb of Duelech<-
<note>Duelech: A term coined by the Swiss physician Paracelsus to describe a stony, tartar-like deposit that forms in the human body, specifically a "calculus."</note>

1. Why the womb of the Stone must be investigated. 2. The bladder also generates a stone of a different sort than the kidney. 3. Prognoses. 4. Heat in urine does not coagulate anything. 5. The necessity of one or the other. 6. The location of this womb. 7. Mechanics. 8. Observations gathered therefrom. 9. The extension of this womb is conjectured. 10. The reason for the marvels in kidney patients. 11. Why the kidney disease recurs. 12. Kidney disease obeys the "Meteors" <note>Van Helmont often used "meteors" to refer to internal chemical vapors or "weather" within the body's microclimate.</note>. 13. The manner of its becoming. 14. Why the urine is turbid. 15. Kidney pain from contraction. 16. Those who blame the narrowing of the ureter for the severity of the pain are mistaken about the cause. 17. Ignorance of the womb caused healing to be neglected. 18. The fabulous persuasion of the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical academic authorities of the time, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note>. 19. Another necessity of recurrences. 20. Elucidation of a certain doubt. 21. The story of a madman. 22. Separation of urine from blood. 23. The disordered generation of a foreign stone.

Now that the seed, the matter, and the process of the stone's formation have been revealed—and urine has been recognized in its contents insofar as it is a seminal vessel carrying the seed of the stone—not enough has yet been said. For indeed, while one kidney remains healthy, often only the other is afflicted with stones. Therefore, it is not enough to have blamed the common origin of urine. Because of this, we must imagine even more powerfully that everything created presupposes a certain **womb** <note>original: "uterum"; Van Helmont uses "womb" or "matrix" to describe the specific environment or organ where a chemical process or disease is birthed.</note>, from which the product subsequently receives a nature that is not inactive. For it is necessary that there be places where things are made before they are born, and this is so by the priority of both locations and movements. 

Indeed, in the **liver** <note>original: "hepate"</note>, urine already exists materially, and even in the **mesentery** <note>original: "mezenterio"; the membrane attaching the abdominal organs</note>, before it is in the kidneys. Nor would the kidneys, by separation, sequester the **urine** <note>original: "lotium"</note> from the **blood** <note>original: "cruore"</note> unless the urine and blood were already really distinct. If indeed urine exists before it reaches the kidney or the **renal veins** <note>original: "emulgentes venas"</note>, it is necessary that the stone also be prepared in some way before reaching the "guest-houses" of the kidneys. And—

->**ON LITHIASI**<-
<note>Lithiasi: From the Greek *lithiasis*, meaning the formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>

...carrying <note>completing the thought from the previous page</note> it. For if feces begin to be prepared right from the beginning of the duodenum <note>the first part of the small intestine</note>, why shouldn't the same happen with urine? Therefore, it seemed to me that urine alone could not fulfill the requirement of the "calculous womb" <note>the specific environment or "matrix" where a stone is formed</note>, and much less the kidneys themselves, through which the passage of urine is so swift, as if through syringes <note>tubes or narrow channels</note>. Therefore, I had to be the first to attend to the "womb" of this monstrous offspring, especially because the Schools <note>the traditional medical authorities</note> have until now jumped over this peak of knowledge, content with the judgment of the common people, and knowing no further than the simple country-folk, who only look at the kidneys and the bladder. But certainly the "mine," or the womb, causes a great diversity in the thing being produced in both places, just as it mostly determines the fertility or sterility of a generation. For if a specific nature resides in the soil: surely a nature must reside in the womb; this is especially clear if one of the kidneys turns to stone while the other remains healthy. And this is best contemplated from the same material and within the same material—the urine of a single person. Therefore, the cause of the infirmity must be sought from the womb and nowhere else.

<margin>2</margin> For both the bladder and the very same urine produce a different sort of <term>Duelech</term> <note>Duelech: A Paracelsian term for a stony, tartar-like deposit or stone</note> than what is formed in the kidney—or at least what used to form nowhere else before. Indeed, I am not accustomed to subscribe to the mere pleasures of my predecessors, nor to their arbitrary judgments, because I have become certain that the very power of <term>Healing</term> <note>original: Medendi</note> languishes under their sluggishness. Therefore, I had to explore the womb of <term>Duelech</term>.

<margin>3</margin> First, I examined <term>Nephritics</term> <note>sufferers of kidney disease</note>, who are often accustomed to predict the coming evil and the future pains by seeing watery urine that is not tinted <note>pale or colorless urine</note>. But then, when the pain is present, that same urine is very abundant and turbid, like yellow beer that has not yet been cleared <note>original: defæcatæ; referring to the process of sediment settling out of a liquid</note>. And when a thinner urine, mixed with sand, flows out later, they testify that the pain is clearly relieved for them, or almost gone. Indeed, even if

<margin>4</margin> for several days the urine flowed continuously, it was larger than both kidneys at once <note>referring to the volume of fluid exceeding the capacity of the organs</note>. I then observed also—

and that a continuous and copious <term>mass</term> <note>original: "bolum." I concluded, therefore, within myself, that this entire birth of the monster <note>Van Helmont often refers to the kidney stone as a "monstrous" or unnatural byproduct of the body.</note>, and its preparation, is not completed in such a hurried passage through the kidneys: especially since that mass sometimes fills a third part of the <term>urinal</term> <note>original: "matulae." <margin>6</margin>

Indeed, the foundations are forged for those things which soon appear to be manifest. But so that I might cast away all doubt from myself, and not believe that in a momentary passage through the kidneys, the urine—swifter than the blink of an eye—turns into a mass, sand, or larger stones, and is then precipitated through the <term>ureters</term> <note>The tubes that carry urine from the kidneys to the bladder.</note>: I collected the urine of a man who 
<margin>7</margin>

<margin>8</margin> than if it had come out of the <term>chamber pot</term> <note>original: "matulae." A vessel used for collecting urine for medical inspection.</note> without the <term>mass</term> <note>original: "bolo." A soft, semi-solid lump of matter.</note> and sand. On the other hand, I also discovered that urine which had once deposited its sand in the chamber pot deposited no further sand in a new chamber pot for thirty hours thereafter; rather, it only left behind a sediment that could be washed away. Therefore, I was made certain that all that <term>gravel</term> <note>original: "sabulum." Coarse, sandy sediment.</note> ejected with the urine of a person suffering from stones did not actually belong to that specific batch of urine in which it had sunk to the bottom. Consequently, the sand passed in successive and continuous turns was not the product of that single urination, nor was it created or completed during the time that urine was being collected. Indeed, otherwise the kidney—which is not even four times larger than the mass itself—would not be able to host the gravel flowing down during that <term>paroxysm</term> <note>A sudden attack or increase in the symptoms of a disease, such as a "fit" of kidney stone pain.</note>. Therefore, I learned that watery and colorless urine is a harbinger of future sands and a paroxysm, and a predictor of future pain: even then, it has already deposited the foundations of these conditions in some cavity, perhaps larger than the folds of a single kidney.

<margin>9</margin> Therefore, I conjectured that there was a "womb" more capable of holding sands, masses, and little stones than the internal folds of the kidneys were. Namely, there is one central cavity bordering the ureter, and another winding one, curved through the body of the kidney.

<margin>10</margin> For I wondered greatly why, in the first days, the urine did not "glow" <note>The author uses "flaveret," referring to the healthy golden-yellow color of urine.</note>, yet it flowed abundantly. Nor was the "dross"—the <term>tincture</term> <note>The pigment or coloring matter of the urine.</note> of the urine—then drawn out as it usually is: it was as if its entire color were consumed in the construction of the urine's sediment; or as if the sands were made from the mere dross of that color. Thus, I thought that the mixture of liquid waste with urine is a morbid state contrary to nature, even if it is common. This meditation, indeed, troubled me during my first steps in this study. At least from this I knew more clearly that the "material cause" of <term>Duelech</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for a kidney stone.</note> assigned by the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional university medical authorities who followed Galen and Avicenna.</note> is entirely vain and stupid. They claim a whitish and phlegmatic slime is "cooked" into a stone by the heat of the kidneys; but if that were so, the sand of the kidneys would not be more yellow than a stone of the bladder, but equally...

# CHAPTER VI.

...so that both stones would turn equally pale. Since the "cooking" <note>original: "coctio." A traditional medical term for the body's processing of fluids via internal heat.</note> and drying out of <term>mucilage</term> <note>A thick, glue-like substance (mucus) which many doctors of the time believed was the primary material of kidney stones.</note> cannot turn its pale heat into a lemon-yellow color; otherwise, if left in the bladder for a long time, it would turn entirely red. Unless, perhaps, the <term>Schools</term> <note>The "Schools" refers to the established university authorities and traditional Galenic medicine, which Van Helmont often challenged.</note> have demonstrated that the mucilage of the kidneys is yellow and that of the bladder is white. Otherwise, they truly teach nothing but old wives' tales concerning the mucilaginous material of the stone.

Furthermore, I have observed that urine is clear, copious, and watery, and not colored before a <term>paroxysm</term> <note>A sudden attack of intense pain from a kidney stone.</note>; then it becomes turbid with pain, and sandy after the pains have passed. It seems to me that while the "sand" is in the process of being made, this happens in the <term>vena cava</term> <note>The large vein carrying deoxygenated blood into the heart; Van Helmont believed stones could begin to form in the blood vessels before reaching the kidneys.</note> and the renal vessels themselves. At that stage, it is not yet in the form of sand or a stone, but rather like a <term>mass</term> <note>original: "boli." A soft, semi-solid lump.</note>, a thinner mud, or a kind of sediment. 

And so, in that "kitchen" <note>original: "culina." A metaphor for the metabolic or digestive processing areas of the body.</note>, the urine is not then rightly prepared. For this reason, it is watery, and the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The "Archeus" is Van Helmont’s term for the internal "vital director" or spiritual governor of an organ that manages its functions.</note> of those parts is primarily in a diseased state. By the "cooking" or digestion of the urine, I mean its advancement toward its final urinary perfection. For the sand is not yet there, but rather the most minute "atom" of a mass. This is because a corruptive <term>ferment</term> <note>An active agent, similar to an enzyme or yeast, which Van Helmont believed caused chemical transformations in the body.</note>—one that is contrary to nature and established in those required places—is present there. 

But the further the fluid recedes from there toward the kidney, or toward the final "sewer" of the urine <note>The bladder.</note>, the more it is weighed down by its own urinary ferment. Thus, the <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term borrowed from Paracelsus to specifically describe the stony material formed in the body from urine.</note> increases in size almost every moment and gradually hardens into larger grains. 

For I argued: if a vein preserves the blood from clotting and corruption even after death, it would not be unfitting for a similar preservation from stony clotting to exist in the urinary passages or veins—unless this preservation is powerfully ruined by the "vicious ferment" of the neighboring kidney. Once this has seriously occurred, so that the veins have moved away from their natural goodness, the same thing happens to them as happens to any impure vessels infested with a nearby stinking ferment: some of the residue of that impure contagion always stubbornly sticks to them. 

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones form not from the "cooking" of mucus, but from a chemical "ferment" that corrupts the urine, beginning even in the blood vessels and hardening as it moves toward the bladder.</summary>

<margin>11</margin>
Indeed, there is a continuous succession of recurrences in kidney stone sufferers because not all the adhering <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "amurca." Literally the bitter sediment of olive oil; Van Helmont uses it here to describe the waste products or impurities that stick to the vessel walls.</note> are wiped away from the fullness of the vessel, and that same character of "bad disposition" remains nearby, which prepared the previous calamities. It works in the same way that a hen carries mature eggs, less mature ones, and others like tiny grains in her loins, which are the pledges of future births to follow successively over several months.

<margin>12</margin>
This is surely the necessity by which kidney stone sufferers mostly obey the disturbances of the <term>heavens</term> <note>original: "meteori." In the 17th century, this referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including weather changes, which were believed to trigger bodily humors.</note>; they even foresee future storms, and their pains ascend from the loins into the back. Because while a little more of those impurities is already attached to those veins—or rather to those "wombs"—they become distressed and contracted, at least on the side where they are infested, or even on both sides through the loins equally.

<margin>13</margin>
Under this contraction of the veins and wrinkled shriveling, a stinging, piercing, and as it were, tearing pain emerges. And in that shriveling, coarser atoms collected in the <term>renal vessels</term> <note>original: "emulgentibus [venis]." The old medical term for the veins and arteries of the kidneys, thought to "milk" or "strain" the blood.</note> fall down to the kidneys. Meanwhile, a <term>lump</term> <note>original: "bolo." A mass or medicinal ball; here referring to the physical matter of the stone.</note> remains behind as a pledge, like a seedbed for the next attack, until it reaches its own mature growth.

<margin>14</margin>
In which at last, as the contraction of the veins causes pain, a <term>fluid</term> <note>original: "latex." Van Helmont's specific term for a clear, nutritive fluid in the body that he believed was distinct from both blood and urine.</note> from afar runs quickly from the veins to the kidneys as a reinforcement, or is drawn there, and flows together in obedience. Hence the turbid <term>urine</term> <note>original: "lotium."</note>. But as for what that fluid is—which, while it is not urine, is nevertheless mixed with it—I have spoken about it at sufficient length in its own treatise. After the fragments of sand and stones are ejected, the pain ceases, because the contraction of the veins ceases.

<margin>15</margin>
Therefore, that immense kidney pain comes from the contraction, and not from the passage of the lump or the grit.

<margin>16</margin>
For often a large <term>stone</term> <note>original: "calculus."</note> later hurts less than a tiny one did at first, which was extremely painful. This is not because the <term>ureter</term> has become wider than itself (as the <term>Schools</term> <note>The "Schools" refers to the established medical authorities of the day whose theories Van Helmont frequently contested.</note> otherwise believe), but because the contraction was greater due to the unaccustomed malady. # CHAPTER VI.

would undergo three times as much expansion, they contradict themselves when they forbid <term>diuretics</term> <note>Medicines that promote the production of urine.</note> because of the constriction of the vessels. I further considered that at the beginning of an attack, urine is excreted clear, watery, and in abundance. For since urine is tinted by <term>dross</term> <note>original: "scoria." Here referring to the waste products or "liquid excrement" that provides urine with its color.</note>, or liquid excrement; and since that dross is not drawn out except near the end of the <term>ileum</term> <note>The final part of the small intestine.</note> and near the source of the fecal ferment, it follows that this dross is not drawn from so far away during the confusion of an impending <term>paroxysm</term> <note>A sudden attack or violent expression of a disease, such as a kidney stone "fit."</note>. This is because the economy of that "kitchen" <note>A metaphor for the digestive system.</note> is confusedly disturbed and interrupted.

Indeed, the stomach, along with the entire abdomen, is disturbed; it senses in a premonition and fear the impending storm, no less than it suffers and endures it once it is present. It seems to sense the <term>sand</term> <note>original: "arenam." Fine gravel or mineral sediment.</note> not yet seen; but truly, it is already present in its "womb," and while it is being sensed, the beginning of that contraction starts from then on. Therefore, the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The internal "vital director" or spirit that Van Helmont believed governed the life processes and chemical changes within the body.</note>, wishing to wash away the enemy and excuse the impending paroxysm, summons every fluid from everywhere and sends it down to flush the kidneys.

Therefore, the veins in kidneys prone to stones are contracted, and the intestines sympathize; they mimic <term>colic pain</term> <note>Abdominal pain usually associated with the intestines.</note> for that reason, because of this sympathy. For this cause, <term>nephritic pain</term> <note>Pain originating in the kidneys.</note> is not yet sufficiently distinguished from colic by the <term>Schools</term> <note>original: "Scholas." The traditional university medical authorities who followed Galenic and Aristotelian teachings.</note>. It is no wonder that when the veins go into spasm, the intestines themselves are also convulsed, since contractions of the limbs follow—due to sympathy and kinship—both the terrible pains of colic and those of the kidneys.

Therefore, the Schools deviate far from the truth in thinking that the "dross" is drawn or sent to the factory of the stone; rather, its tinting happens by accident while the <term>coagulating spirit</term> <note>The force that causes liquid matter to harden into a solid stone.</note> exerts its power upon the <term>volatile earth</term> <note>Mineral matter that is suspended in the blood before it solidifies.</note>. For all other things being equal, a tinted stone is always more crumbly than pale ones. This clearly proves that the tint of the urine, if it were possible, would totally prevent the formation of <term>Duelech</term> <note>A specific Paracelsian term for a stony deposit or calculus formed from "tartar" in the body.</note>. <header>ON THE STONE</header>

...and for that reason, <term>jaundiced patients</term> <note>original: "Icterici." Those suffering from yellowing of the skin and eyes, often linked to bile and the liver.</note>, though they might otherwise be subject to <term>the stone</term> <note>original: "calculo."</note>, are hardly ever seen with stones while they have jaundice. For that reason, during the formation of the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "Lithiasis."</note>, the urine turns yellow at the first conception of the <term>sand</term> <note>original: "arenarum." Fine mineral sediment or grit.</note>, and it is pale at the beginning of an attack. This is because at that stage it is still <term>raw fluid</term> <note>original: "latex." A term Helmont uses for fluid that has not yet been fully transformed into urine.</note>, not yet pure urine. I knew for certain, therefore, that if all the sand being excreted were formed solely in the hollow of the kidney, the pain would be greater while it is being excreted than before the sand appears. Yet this contradicts experience. Furthermore, because once the <term>gravel</term> <note>original: "sabulo." A coarser sediment than sand.</note> has been formed, the urine is more disturbed than usual and becomes thicker—since the turbid confusion suggests it contains more powder (namely, in a thicker

<margin>17</margin> consistency, it contains more powder than it previously did when clear and watery)—this clearly proves that the <term>womb</term> <note>Van Helmont uses "uterus" metaphorically for the internal cavity or vessel where the stone is produced.</note> of the Stone is already filled up and can no longer host that thicker <term>sediment</term> <note>original: "saburram." Literally "ballast," referring to the accumulated waste in the kidney.</note>. Therefore, this unknown variety of the "womb" has not only led to the neglect of a cure for <term>Kidney Disease</term> <note>original: "Nephritidis."</note>, but has also introduced different ideas about causes and healing. That is to say, it is one thing for sand floating from the kidney to be knocked down by a succeeding drop of urine; and it is quite another thing to shake off adhering sand, not by a washing wave, but by a coordinated contraction and <term>shriveling</term> <note>original: "crispatione." A rippling or curling contraction of the organic tissues.</note> of the parts.

<margin>18</margin> For they [the medical Schools] claim that <term>Marshmallow</term> <note>original: "Althea."</note>, <term>Mallow</term> <note>original: "Malva."</note>, <term>Almond oil</term> <note>original: "Amygdalino."</note>, and similar things soothe pains, moisten the pathways, dilate them, and lubricate them. And so in this, as in all things everywhere, the <term>Schools</term> <note>The academic medical establishment of the 17th century, which Van Helmont frequently criticized for being too theoretical and following ancient Greek authorities.</note> are either only focused on the aftermath, or they propose something ridiculous. While they ought to sweep out the entire "womb" of sand and <term>clay-like mass</term> <note>original: "bolo." The sticky, earth-like material Van Helmont believed was the precursor that hardened into a stone.</note> with a cleansing power, they instead focus entirely on loosening the pathways and moistening the membranes—which by their nature are always very wet anyway.

<margin>19</margin> For even if the gravel is expelled, the "womb" is not necessarily safe; indeed, the wall of the veins remains defiled by that clayey mass, at least for the future punishment of the stone. To this, the Schools have hitherto given no reflection.

<margin>20</margin> For I used to wonder why a kidney-sufferer, long after he has sent the stone down into the <term>bladder</term> <note>original: "vesicam."</note>...

<meta>Catchword: more rarely (rarius)</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones form in a specific "womb" within the renal vessels and criticizes traditional medicine for using lubricants like marshmallow, which fail to clear the underlying "clay" that causes the disease.</summary>
<keywords>jaundice, lithiasis, kidney stones, marshmallow, bolo, medical schools, renal anatomy, urine color</keywords>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>C H A P T E R VII.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

...it rarely triggers new little stones in the kidney as long as another stone infests the bladder. However, a <term>kidney-stone sufferer</term> <note>original: "Nephriticus."</note>, if he is also simultaneously <term>gouty</term> <note>original: "podagricus." Gout and kidney stones were frequently linked in early modern medicine as "calculous" diseases.</note>, may admit gout as a companion during an onset of kidney disease or a stone attack. From this, I learned that just as pain in a wound summons <term>serous fluid</term> <note>original: "aquam glacealem." Literally "icy water," likely referring to the clear, cold discharge from an injury.</note>, so too does pain change the urine itself, which prevents <term>petrification</term> <note>The process of turning organic matter into stone.</note> in the kidney's former womb. For this reason, turbid urine is seen in stone patients even without <term>sand</term> <note>original: "arenas." Fine mineral grit or sediment.</note>. For pain is the <term>trumpet-call</term> <note>original: "classica." A military signal for assembly.</note> that occasionally summons the <term>fluid</term> <note>original: "laticem." Helmont's term for the raw, unrefined liquid in the body.</note> from everywhere; it ignites and even disturbs the urine once a foreign guest has been admitted. In such a great confusion of functions, however, nothing is thought of the confusion itself. Indeed, pain has often placed an image of <term>feverish heat</term> <note>original: "fervoris."</note> before my eyes. For after the boiling of such heat, the water is usually turbid and confused. In this way, a <term>lump</term> <note>original: "bolus."</note> is formed from the volatile earth of the urine, which is not yet sufficiently seasoned with salt because of a lack of the <term>urinary ferment</term> <note>In Helmontian theory, a "ferment" is a vitalizing agent that directs the chemical transformation of substances.</note> stationed in the kidney. Therefore, this lump melts by fire and is not forced into harder or larger grains of sand as long as it does not experience the power of the renal ferment. Furthermore, this lump is deeply stained, not indeed by the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "scoria." Literally "slag" or "refuse."</note> that arrive later and color the sand (for that lump is redder than the yellowness of the dregs), but by diluted <term>blood</term> <note>original: "cruore."</note> which is erroneously transferred into the veins—the womb of the lump—to the ends of the disturbance. And thus far in <term>uroscopy</term> <note>original: "Vronomantia." The practice of diagnosing disease by examining the urine.</note>, the lump testifies to the liver and blood, but the sand shows none of these things.

<margin>21</margin>
It is therefore clear that urine is salty of its own accord, even if a person does not eat salt; you will find the cause of this in the section on <term>Digestions</term> <note>Van Helmont proposed a system of six "digestions" or stages of transformation in the body.</note>. A certain patient in our city spent 17 whole days out of his mind, without any food or drink, before his death. Yet, daily urine was never entirely lacking for him, although it came out more sparingly and became gradually redder. From this I gather that there is a <term>transformative power</term> <note>original: "vim commutativam."</note> in the kidney that changes blood into urine.

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain acts as a physiological signal that alters the chemical state of urine, often preventing stone formation temporarily by creating a turbid "lump" instead of hard sand. He also concludes that the kidneys possess a vital power to transform blood into urine, independent of food or salt intake.</summary>

<page-num>106</page-num>

<header>ON STONE-FORMING DISEASE</header>

(which I also pursue with study elsewhere in the treatise on <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydrope." A condition of swelling caused by excess fluid, now known as edema.</note>) no differently than a wound, it prepares a swift and abundant <term>Synovia</term> <note>original: "Synoviam." While modern medicine uses this for joint fluid, Van Helmont uses it here to describe a clear, serous discharge.</note>, or gravelly water, from the blood. Therefore, the urine, for its final determination, requires and borrows its power from a <term>ferment</term> <note>In Helmontian medicine, a "ferment" is a vital spiritual force that directs chemical transformations in the body.</note>, which the kidneys breathe into the womb of the urine. It is no different than how the liver breathes the power of <term>making blood</term> <note>original: "sanguificandi."</note> into the portal veins and the connections of the <term>Mesentery</term> <note>The membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall.</note>. For this reason, in that very place, all the <term>chyle</term> <note>The milky fluid produced during digestion.</note> of the stomach immediately takes on the appearance of blood in its color. However, I pursue the power of the kidneys over the veins—which is clearly <term>despotic</term> <note>original: "despoticam." Meaning absolute or controlling.</note>—elsewhere in the discussion on dropsy.

But although the ferment of the kidney, serving the ministry of the whole integrated body, is like a digestion of a certain organ, it is nevertheless not numbered among the "digestions" because it concerns the "cooking" of <term>waste</term> <note>original: "recrementi." A term for metabolic byproducts that the body must expel.</note>, rather than nourishment. For since every transformation <margin>22</margin> that proceeds through digestions has its own means, or its own proper ferments suitable for a new generation; so the kidney begins to impress its own ferment into the <term>cremor</term> <note>original: "cremorem." A refined, creamy stage of food as it is processed by the body.</note> as soon as it is stationed at the gates of the liver. By the power of this ferment, the urine separates itself from the blood by its native properties. And although that blood is not yet capable of coagulating, nevertheless, liquid separates from liquid there.

The mystery of blood-making is indeed homogeneous, simple, and entirely consistent, and defined only by the act of making blood itself; nevertheless, it was necessary that, right at the threshold of the portal vein, a separable "deformity" be born into the <term>cremor</term>. For otherwise the blood, while it acquired its vital condition in the liver, would be unsuitably polluted by the breath of the kidney’s ferment.

Moreover, that urine is naturally salty, and where that saltiness comes from, you will find elsewhere in the discussion on digestions. Here, however, it shall be enough to note that however much <term>acid salt</term> <note>original: "salis acidi." Helmont believed specific salts governed different bodily functions.</note> is born in the chyle during the first digestion, just as much migrates in the second substantial transformation into [salt...]

<summary>Van Helmont explains that the kidney possesses a "despotic" ferment that separates urine from blood at the liver's entrance. This process ensures the vital blood remains pure while waste products are diverted for excretion.</summary>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VI. 107</header>

# CHAPTER VI.

<margin>23</margin>
...[a false] digestion. I have already designated the <term>womb</term> <note>original: "uterum." In Helmontian medicine, this refers to the place or environment where a substance is generated.</note> where urine and stones begin to form. I have also declared the wondrous property of the **spirit of urine** in its ability to coagulate and turn things to stone. From this, it is now sufficiently clear that if the spirit of urine happens to flow in a backward motion through the liver into the **portal vein**, and is then expelled from there as a foreign and unusual substance through the **mesentery** into the intestines, it will easily coagulate unusual stones there as well. These are what <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>A famous 16th-century Swiss-German physician and alchemist whose theories Van Helmont both built upon and critiqued.</note> calls "congealed" rather than "coagulated" things, because they do not reach the hardness of the <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech." A Paracelsian term for a specific, painful type of stone or tartar formed in the body.</note> of the urine, which are solidified from a "mother material" of mucilage.

But if the spirit of urine is carried through the **vena cava**, upwards or downwards, it alters—by a power specific to itself—the fluid-like and mucilaginous nourishment into a harder structure. From this, eventually, <term>scirrhus</term> <note>original: "Schirrhi." Hard, painless tumors or swellings.</note>, **quartan fevers**, and various **obstructions** arise. Physicians try in vain to ward these off using <term>juleps</term> <note>original: "Julapia." Sweetened medicinal drinks.</note> or <term>apozems</term> <note>original: "Apozemata." Herbal decoctions or infusions.</note>. 

Finally, the bile is nourished by the blood near it; if the spirit of urine deviates from its own "womb" into this bile, stones are immediately formed in the gallbladder as well. Indeed, whatever enters another's "harvest" becomes foreign and hostile. Thus, extraordinary effects arise from similar causes. Nor did I teach inappropriately that the watery serum mixed with the blood—which is lost through sweat or other invisible ways—is not urine, nor does it have the same properties as urine. It is not the kind of serum found in milk, much less is it bile or yellow gall; rather, it is part of the <term>Latex</term> <note>original: "Laticis." Van Helmont's specific term for the "neutral" clear fluid of the body, distinct from blood or urine.</note>, about which I have written in its own treatise.

<margin>1</margin> I have spoken of the <term>womb</term> <note>original: "uterum." In this context, it refers to the internal environment or "nest" where the disease is generated.</note> of the Stone disease; now, however, we must think seriously about the remedies. Indeed, the common people laugh at the Schools, which have been brought into disgrace because they have searched in vain for the true causes or the healing of this disease until now. I have recounted the origin of Medicine elsewhere, and its power of mind, though still in its youth even to this day, has been driven in a circle like an exhausted thing, without any progress—because they preferred to stand by foreign, Greek, Barbarian, and Pagan inventions, and considered it an honor to have refined the works of others. Meanwhile, new diseases arise, and even old, obsolete ones reappear in masks; thus they appear "illegitimate," no longer corresponding to the descriptions of the ancients. Indeed, everything stands still without progress, while our health greatly needs an increase in the art of healing. To such an extent that a lazy and ungenerous race of Healers stands in the way, who wished to be wise only from someone else’s commentary, and they deny that the art can grow beyond what they already know. And therefore, whatever they are ignorant of, they cast into the catalog of incurable things, with a certain despairing adi- <note>The text ends mid-word on the catchword "adi-", likely "adjudicant" (they judge) or "adscribunt" (they assign).</note>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

## CHAPTER VII.

...they drive them. As if the inventions of our grandfathers had blocked the path of our own industry, locked up the treasures of Wisdom, and all modern mental power had become sterile; as if the power of divine Wisdom were exhausted, and there were nothing more that could further demonstrate the truth to us. Truly, the cup of sloth has driven the age mad with lethargy even from its very beginning: which is why everyone prefers to agree with others rather than to investigate for themselves.

So great, indeed, is the sweetness of profit that everyone looks with fond admiration upon their own drug combinations—or rather, their confusions and medleys (which they call **magisterial recipes**). Those secrets that once made diligent physicians famous have today, due to the sluggishness of healers, trickled down into the hands of apothecaries and old women. Hence every barber, bath-keeper, nun, executioner, or even a procuress fleeing the brothels of the flute-girls <note>original: "ambubajarum," a term for Syrian flute-players who were often associated with street-walking and brothels in ancient Rome.</note>, boasts of practicing medicine; I shall describe their ranks here.

<margin>2</margin> First among those we encounter are those who wish to heal, though they are not trained for it. Driven by their own nature and daring anything, they give to the sick whatever they have heard helped others, without any knowledge of the underlying causes or any discernment; thus, they cast their patients into danger. Indeed, almost all the "experiments" of the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical universities that relied on the ancient teachings of Galen and Avicenna.</note> have flowed from this source. Even <term>Galen</term> <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> confirmed this by following the example of his teacher, Quintus. For by conducting experiments through the deaths of men, the Schools immediately call their graduates "most experienced."

Other common folk prefer to treat only commoners, and offer them their advice; some also do so only out of favor and when asked. Many also have an innate vice of ambition for honor, wanting to be thought of as wise. Such deceivers want to appear wealthy, and therefore perform all their services for free—services that lead either to death or to a recovery that is merely accidental. Foremost among these are those who, in Rome, thrust <term>Theriaca</term> <note>A complex, ancient "universal" medicine containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, famously used as an antidote to poison.</note> upon the Cardinals and Nobles,

<summary>Van Helmont critiques the medical profession for its stagnation and laziness, arguing that true medical knowledge has been lost to profit-seeking and "magisterial recipes." He lists various unqualified people—from barbers to executioners—who now claim medical authority, and accuses university-trained doctors of learning through the "deaths of men."</summary>

...they provide, composed of the best natural ingredients—or <term>simples</term> <note>Individual medicinal plants or minerals used as drugs, as opposed to complex "magisterial" mixtures.</note>—that God created in nature. For in this way, we have deceived the people in the City and appeared as holy pharmacists. Next come those who seek gifts, yet absolutely refuse coins—lest they be seen as abandoning their noble status or their vow of poverty. Consequently, there are those who claim they earn nothing for themselves, but only for the sake of the poor community. Similar to these are the apostates, who admit they are not physicians, yet claim to possess secrets received from a Queen or an Emperor. They usually employ intermediaries to talk up the price of their medicine. Then follow those who wear clothes and purses full of holes like a sieve, yet they are never slow to practice their deception. They claim they were once very wealthy but have been impoverished—often by a wine barrel <note>A sarcastic reference to drinking away one's fortune.</note>—through the <term>chemical art</term> <note>Alchemy</note>, through wars, or through steadfastness in their religion. There are also those who once flourished in a company of soldiers; but in war (the "conflict for money"), they spent all their resources. They show off their scars—perhaps the only "reward" they actually earned. Some have even left behind wives and children, their hearths and altars, and the sweet fields of their homeland, all for the sake of their religious devotion. Many others are "voluntarily" poor simply because no one is willing to give them anything. Nor are there lacking those who fake religious orders, change their clothes, walk in wooden clogs, and falsely claim to be hermits—hiding their hypocrisy—asserting that God has inspired them with the knowledge of the virtues of simples. There are even those who everywhere mix in <term>Astronomy</term> <note>At this time, astrology was considered a vital branch of astronomy for determining the timing of treatments.</note> and <term>Chiromancy</term> <note>Palm reading.</note>. Finally, there are the <term>field-wanderers</term> <note>original: "campivagi," itinerant quacks or vagabonds.</note> who learned their craft in the "Mountain of Venus" <note>A folkloric place of carnal temptation and forbidden knowledge; Helmont implies they learned their "medicine" in brothels or through witchcraft.</note>; they claim to know how to cure beasts as well as men. Likewise, there are those who claim to predict the future and dig up buried treasures. And then there are those who, lacking books, write "harmful" words of Solomon <note>Referring to the "Key of Solomon" or similar grimoires; Solomon was historically associated with the magical control of demons and disease.</note> on scraps of paper, by which they claim diseases are put to flight just like demons,

### CHAPTER VII.

...before and after, they draw crosses, lest the devil carry away the powerful words as they are being written. There are those who are skilled in various languages; among the Belgians, they pretend to speak Chaldean, Arabic, or Dalmatian. Burdened with many "arts," they finally boast of <term>Mathematics</term> <note>In this context, "Mathematics" refers not just to calculation, but to astrology and the occult sciences used to predict the future.</note> or histories. Most of these know nothing less than how to construct the <term>Gold-making Stone</term> <note>original: "lapidem Chrysopejum"; better known as the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note>, yet they carry around threats that spread like a perpetual ferment. There are also Saracens, and there are baptized Jews (usually more wicked than those who are not baptized) who have learned from the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>A Jewish mystical tradition involving the study of divine secrets and the symbolic nature of Hebrew letters.</note> how to "mortify" <term>Mercury</term> <note>A chemical process of "killing" or fixing mercury to change its state, often to make it usable in medicine or alchemy.</note> in various ways, and likewise to prepare poisons in different fashions, which they claim are effective against all diseases—and many more besides. They boast that the <term>Hebrew</term> language contains the foundations of all sciences and the great secrets of republics; and they claim to be pregnant with foreknowledge of the future. They often cite their <term>Rabbis</term> <note>Jewish religious teachers.</note>, the book *Nebolohu* <note>Likely an obscure or corrupted reference to an occult or pseudo-epigraphical text.</note>, along with the <term>Key of Solomon</term> <note>A famous grimoire or book of magic spells attributed to King Solomon.</note>; from which they claim they can read the past as well as the future. 

Others also claim that the medical art is hereditary within their lineage alone, even if they are all fools or scoundrels. But if they are not accepted among men, they at least boast of <term>Cosmetics</term> <note>The art of beautifying the face and body, often viewed with suspicion by moralists of the time.</note> among women. For they are covered by the same skin as the Greeks and Jews, although they mock each other in turn. Indeed, being naturally quick, they learn to lie from their own nature. There is also a fugitive class from the household of <term>chemists</term> <note>Here meaning alchemical assistants or laborers.</note> which, while it boasts of choice secrets, sells nothing but poisons to the <term>perfumers</term> <note>original: "Seplasiis"; the Seplasiarii were sellers of ointments, pigments, and drugs, often accused of selling adulterated goods.</note>. For they seize every freedom to lie before the ignorant, with deceit growing through constant practice. For they are idiots, fugitive apostates from the chemical furnaces. 

The Schools <note>The established medical universities of the era.</note>, however, with greater security and the freest authority of all, impose themselves upon mortals; for since I show, by the inevitable decree of truth, that until now they have been utterly ignorant of the essences, causes, and remedies of diseases, and with a great volume, and

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of the medical establishment. Having previously discussed mystical and fraudulent practitioners, he now focuses on the "official" medical schools and their reliance on pedigree and abstract theory over clinical results.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>

-># ON THE STONE DISEASE<-

I shall establish this by <term>a priori</term> <note>Reasoning from theoretical principles or causes rather than observation of effects.</note> reasoning. In the meantime, the schools promote their students: one because he knows Latin and has a father who is a surgeon or an **apothecary**; another because, having been made a master of arts, he has attended several lectures by the professors; and yet another because he boasts of knowing a portion of **Euclid** <note>The Greek mathematician, whose work was a staple of classical education but, in Van Helmont's view, irrelevant to curing the sick.</note>, or has learned to dispute according to **Aristotle**.

But I pity the human race, subject to so many internal calamities and exposed to so many external exploiters. When these men, under the ill-fated rules of the Schools, have slaughtered some high-ranking person, they claim the right to appeal to the "incurability" of the disease, and they have their patrons and accomplices everywhere. Thus, they alone range about with impunity against the lives of princes, as I have shown in my book on fevers. Mortals trust them all the more miserably because, through their graduation and their oath, they hide their ignorance from the common people. Indeed, they swear that they will faithfully cure illnesses which, as I have shown, they do not understand at all. 

Nay, even their leader **Galen** <note>The 2nd-century physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over 1,400 years.</note> did not demonstrate a single remedy that was not borrowed from the <term>empirics</term> <note>original: "empyricis"; practitioners who relied on experience and observation rather than the abstract theories of the medical schools.</note>, however much his laughable theory of <term>complexions</term> <note>The balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile).</note> and <term>degrees</term> <note>The measurement of a medicine's primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) on a scale of one to four.</note>—both by kinds and by locations—may triumph in their books. For **Quintus**, Galen's teacher, who was entirely an empiric, is invoked everywhere by his student. 

Princes and Magistrates ought to divert this license for killing with impunity away from their subjects, and they are bound to do so by conscience. I do not think this has been neglected through carelessness, but rather set aside until now because of an ignorance of the proper remedy. I, however, propose a remedy: Let them decree that every Physician is bound to visit any sick person who has requested them at least three times, under penalty of exile and the loss of their office. For otherwise, the number of practitioners has increased enough. Second, let no payment be owed to the Physician if he has not cured the patient. By this twofold statute, namely, practitioners would become more vigilant, and

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that university-trained doctors rely on social status and abstract theories like those of Galen to hide their clinical failures. He proposes legal reforms, including a "no cure, no pay" rule, to hold physicians accountable for their patients' outcomes.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

...and the matter would go more rightly for the sick, and the Prince would preserve his subjects. But these statutes must be observed seriously. For they are equated to the **Cornelian Law on Assassins** <note>original: "Legi Corneliae de sicariis"; a famous Roman law established by Sulla that punished murderers, poisoners, and those who carried weapons with intent to kill.</note>. Now, I return to where I digressed. There are even those who, while they pretend to have read my book on fevers, object that I merely show off <term>chemical remedies</term> <note>original: "remedia chymica"; medications derived from alchemy or laboratory processing rather than raw herbs, a hallmark of Van Helmont's "iatrochemical" approach.</note> and unusual secrets, in order to summon every sick person to myself, while despising the safest doctrines of the ancients. Far be it from me. For I neither visit the sick nor practice medicine for the hope of profit. All the good men of our entire country testify to this. Truly, I call no one for the purpose of prostituting or selling my medicines. I live willingly in obscurity, sought after only by the poor. 

This one thing I profess openly and freely: namely, that victories over difficult diseases demand other physicians than the <term>Humorists</term> <note>original: "Humoristas"; those who follow the traditional Galenic theory that health is a balance of four fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note>, and remedies far different from those which the <term>Seplasia</term> <note>original: "Seplasia"; originally a famous marketplace for perfumes and drugs in ancient Capua, here used as a derogatory term for commercial apothecaries who sell standard, unrefined drugs.</note> sells. This is because the endowed powers of the most perfect bodies are most longingly desired, so that poisons may be separated from the <term>balsams</term> <note>Natural healing or preservative principles within the body.</note> within us. Indeed, where poisons are not openly visible, let the confusions of the <term>Archeus</term> <note>original: "Archeus"; in Van Helmont’s system, the internal "vital director" or spirit that governs the body's functions and health.</note> be overcome, let impurities be driven away in secret, and let the proportions of remedies be inverted so that their true properties may emerge—the works of which the Archeus has need. 

Indeed, let the impressions of remedies be turned inward, the tyranny of which our nature cannot bear without disaster. For in this sin, and in this poverty, many centuries have already unhappily passed away, because with the <term>disease-causing</term> <note>original: "morbificis"</note> causes ignored, the powers of remedies unknown, and higher laboratory preparations despised, whatever disease did not depart under its initial heathen treatments remained dedicated to the "desperate" or "incurable." Truly, no disease in particular is incurable. *For as God did not make death, so He does not rejoice in the destruction of the living. He made the nations of the earth healthy, and there is no medicine of destruction, nor is the kingdom of the underworld on earth.* <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 1:13–14.</note>

-> ## ON THE STONE DISEASE <-

Therefore, I urge that lazy breed of men—who are so quick to sign their names to things they are ignorant of—to contemplate with me, in the presence of God who presides everywhere, how certain diseases related to nourishment are sometimes healed by accident through the remedies of the <term>apothecary shops</term> <note>original: "officinarum"; referring to the standard prepared medicines sold by pharmacists of the day</note>, namely those which allow for spontaneous consumption and easy resolution. But in more serious cases, where there are fixed or chronic roots, the use of those remedies has done more harm than good. **Hippocrates** <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine."</note> indeed, without envy, left the investigation of deeper remedies to posterity, because those early leaders lived in more fortunate ages. The Schools, however, have not looked toward the greater needs of mortals, nor to the nature of the patient sitting or lying [in sickness]; but content with **Galen** <note>The 2nd-century physician whose theories dominated European medicine for 1,500 years.</note> and their own **Quintius**, they did not perceive the defects of mortals, as they saw themselves reaping profit regardless of the outcome. For they never once considered how to prevent <term>nephritic</term> <note>related to the kidneys</note> recurrences, and much less how to dissolve a stone, because they had given their names to deceived authors and false causes. Therefore, concerning the true cause of stones, and the true healing and remedies, nothing has been heard until now.

<margin>6</margin> Indeed, a remedy of this kind was desired: one that would prevent the future offspring of the growing **Duelech** <note>A term coined by Paracelsus and used by Van Helmont to describe the specific "stony matter" or tartar that forms in the body, distinct from ordinary sand.</note> through the preparation of the urine itself. Then also, one that would restore the <term>Gorgonic</term> <note>original: "Gorgonicam"; a reference to the Gorgon Medusa, whose gaze turned men to stone. Van Helmont uses it to describe the "petrifying" power of the body's internal ferments.</note> falling of the stone-producing womb and the power of the petrifying ferment; and finally, one that would dissolve whatever the "coagulating spirit" had perpetrated. About each of these, nothing has been heard until now. The Schools were intent only on pushing out the stone and relaxing the urinary passages. And so, in the healing of **Lithiasis** <note>The formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>, a double concern presents itself. One, surely, which removes the inclination and the fear of relapse. <margin>7</margin> The other, however, which would demolish the **Duelech** already formed. I shall show that neither of these was even dreamed of in the Schools; at most, they devised the expulsion of sand and small stones...

## CHAPTER VII:

...to have been born: but they did not think about a calming of such atrocious pain from the root. They indeed praise, and extol to the highest degree, **Mallows**, **Marshmallow**, **Almond oil**, and whatever they name as "moistening by softening" <note>These were standard Galenic treatments intended to "lubricate" the body's internal passages.</note>: then they join various poultices, both moistening as well as cleansing, and likewise cooling ones, lest the pains be intensified or the stones grow larger. Indeed, they even recommend **oil of Scorpions**, as if, when rubbed on as an ointment, it could break the small stones from the outside! As if, I say, they could loosen the fat, the fleshy membrane, and the <term>peritoneum</term> <note>the lining of the abdominal cavity</note>, so that by their dilation, the <term>ureter</term> <note>the tube that carries urine from the kidney to the bladder</note> would necessarily be softened and widened. The common people indeed discovered and promoted these helps for themselves, first at the suggestion of an **old woman** <note>Van Helmont often mocks the "Schools" for simply adopting folk remedies without understanding the underlying science.</note>. These the Schools first admired, and then soon <margin>g</margin> embraced. They did this, obviously, lest they should appear useless by despising them (since they have nothing else to offer). But these treatments were not undertaken for the sake of the pain alone: rather, while investigating the cause of relief only superficially, and being anxious only about the journey of the little stone, they decided by a final decree: that the ureters are to be widened only through moistening agents, and that there is no other hope of healing. This widening, however, was not intended for the length of the ureter, but only for what they hoped would be the width: as if nature were obliged to conform itself to the efforts of the healers. And so, they judged the remedies for the pains to be merely accidental: to which they added <term>enemas</term>, lest the ureter, compressed by the weight of leaning dung, should be obstructed by a barrier against the sliding stone, and thus close the way. And so the chief remedy of the Schools was focused around excrement and the hindquarters; it was by no means directed toward the causes, roots, and sources of the disease. Whence the Satire says: *Dung and urine are the primary dishes of the Physicians.* <note>original: "Stercus & urina, Medicorum fercula prima." A biting contemporary joke suggesting doctors only care about waste products.</note> But how vain and childish these helps of the Schools are, the afflicted themselves testify, along with their widows and offspring. First of all, the mucilages of Mallow do not pass from the mouth to the <margin>9</margin> ure-

...[process]ing in the form of a soothing, relaxing, and softening agent, without those substances first undergoing several formal transmutations <note>In Helmontian chemistry, a "formal transmutation" is a change in the essential nature or "seed" of a substance as it is processed by the body's internal "ferments."</note> along the way. Indeed, if the **ureter** <term>the tube carrying urine from the kidney to the bladder</term> is already blocked by a **stone** <term>calculus</term>—for as long as it is not blocked, it has not yet filled the entire width of the ureter, and thus any widening of it would be requested in vain—and the ureter supports the urine pressing from behind, how, I ask, can this waste matter give way in such a narrow passage? How could it rise up and make room for the incoming urine prepared from **Mallow**? <note>Mallow (*Malva*) was a common herbal remedy prized for its slimy "mucilage," which doctors believed would lubricate the internal passages to help stones slide out.</note> I at least confess that I do not understand any of these promises.

Finally, even if we grant the case of that old wives' fiction, and suppose that the moistening mucilage could arrive safely at the narrow alleyway of the urine: nevertheless, it would not thereby draw apart the already-wet canal of the ureters. Aside from its own current moisture, the canal does not desire or expect to be enlarged or dilated by a foreign mucus. Nor, if it were ever dilated once, would it thereafter desire or admit a repeated stretching of itself during relapses. Therefore, that supposed and deceitful remedy of the **Schools** <note>Van Helmont uses "The Schools" to refer to the university-trained medical establishment that followed the ancient teachings of Galen and Avicenna.</note> would be useful only a single time. 

Unless, of course, the doctor prefers to believe that the ureter is dilated by the flow and arrival of the aforementioned mucilages, and then narrowed back to its original state once they are gone by chance. This would be to grant the power of expansion—according to the **Healer's** <note>original: Medentis</note> wish—beyond the habit and nature of a solid passage and its original constitution. For naturally, they would afterwards return again to their original and birth-given narrowness. Indeed, if the Schools are speaking seriously about these things, they are either mad, or they are mocking the sick and behaving wantonly while applauding themselves. Tell me, if they believe these things to be true, why do they forbid **diuretics** <term>medicines that increase the production of urine</term>? If diuretics are effective for driving out the stone, and if, by using moistening agents, they can dilate the narrow passages? Why do they not join the moistening agents with the diuretics, so that both...
