<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>
<sig>h 3</sig>

# CHAPTER VII.

...which simultaneously satisfy the clinical indication? For I have already shown before that if death follows from a **stone** <term>calculus</term> stuck in the passage, it does not occur through the fault of **diuretics** <term>medicines that promote the flow of urine</term>. Nor does it happen because the **ureter** <term>the tube from the kidney to the bladder</term>—unless it were perhaps monstrously deformed—is narrower anywhere else than it is at its very beginning. Therefore, once a stone has departed from the kidney, if it is halted in its descent because the passages are too tight, this happens because of the intensity of the pain, which has contracted the ureter **spasmodically** <note>original: spasmodes</note>. And therefore, this does not happen through the "sin" of the diuretics, but through that of the Physician, who never heard a word about this spasm in the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical universities that followed the ancient teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note>, and consequently never sought a remedy for it. Here, indeed, one must note the logical blunder of the Schools, and the fallacy of citing "that which is not a cause, as a cause." <margin>10</margin> For the aforementioned moistening agents—**Marshmallow** <note>original: Althea</note>, **Mallow** <note>original: Malva</note>, **Almond oil** <note>original: Amygdalinum</note>, and so on—are helpful, not insofar as they widen the ureters (which is a ridiculous idea in itself), but insofar as they soothe the **spasm of crisping** <note>original: spasmum crispationis; a term describing the shriveling or tightening of the tissue due to irritation</note>, just as some external poultices do. Likewise, **lemon juice** <note>original: succus citri</note> does not help by the cleansing and cutting power of its acidity (for otherwise, vinegar and other acids would perform the same task), because lemon juice, in the first digestion of heat, loses its sharpness; therefore, it is not even mixed into sauces that are meant to be boiled. Instead, there remains in it a residual power suitable for soothing the spasm—namely, while it is being converted into urine, it still retains some character of its former intermediate life. <margin>11</margin> What if the Schools fear the use of diuretics, lest perhaps several **little stones** <note>original: lapilli</note> should fall together into the ureter while descending, and the one that is thicker at the rear should, like a **wedge** <note>original: cuneus</note>, block the passage? But even so, this accidental fault is not to be attributed to the diuretic. Furthermore, this is a fiction sold as truth. For whatever once falls into the ureter at the beginning (unless it happens to be hooked) proceeds downward and is expelled in that manner. For little stones do not play or frolic in such a narrow passage. No stone—nor several together—larger than the exit, migrates from the kidney: so that neither...

<meta>catchword: neither</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stone blockages are caused by spasmodic contractions of the ureter due to pain, rather than the fault of diuretic drugs. He critiques the medical "Schools" for failing to recognize this spasm and clarifies how plants like Mallow and lemon juice actually work by soothing these contractions.</summary>

<header>ON THE STONE</header>
<meta>catchword: sick-</meta>

...and once they have fallen from there, those which are behind sustain the weight of the **urine** <note>original: lotii</note>. This would indeed be a reasonable suspicion if the **ureter** <term>the tube connecting the kidney to the bladder</term> were not a soft and loose membrane, but rather a dry and inflexible reed. For a wet membrane, because of its "dread of a vacuum," necessarily collapses unless it is dilated from behind by the falling urine. However, the **diuretic** <term>a medicine that promotes the flow of urine</term> is not yet the source of the problem. For the occurrence of multiple and poorly-shaped little stones does not take its blame from the diuretic, but from a fatal rushing event which would have happened just as much without the diuretic. Therefore, one should not fear or avoid a diuretic for dread of an irregular or monstrous occurrence; it would be wrong to forbid what is normally useful in itself out of fear of an unusual and very rare situation. 

If they say that several little stones fall out glued together with slime, this primarily destroys the theory of the "material cause" of **Duelech** <term>a term used by the chemist Paracelsus to describe the specific "stony matter" or tartar that forms in the body</term> as taught by the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities following Galenic tradition.</note>. Since that phlegmy glue should have already turned into stone, yet the small stones neither found nor took on that kind of glue in the ureter. Therefore, whether a single stone or several stuck together slip from the kidney, it is all the same: since in slipping out, they were not larger than the kidney's exit. 

So, even if diuretics do not dissolve that glue or separate the stones, it would at least be very useful—with more abundant and swift urine pressing down—to have driven forward the troublesome burden of the **stone** <term>calculus</term> as quickly as possible by the weight of the urine itself. For naturally, the ureter is not wet with any internal mucilage, as the urine of healthy people proves. Therefore, if any mucilage from medicines were to reach there, it could not be anything but unnatural and unusual for the ureter. What if the ureter, blocked by a wedged stone, is said to produce mucilage? First, the urine of **nephritics** <term>those suffering from kidney disease</term> contradicts this. Then also, the Schools will be shown to be heedless, as they derive **phlegm** <note>original: pituita</note>—the supposed material cause of the stone—from above, even from the stomach, to explain the stones; whereas that which is at hand, even in the ureter...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the ureter is a flexible tube that requires the weight of urine to stay open, justifying the use of diuretics. <page-num>119</page-num>
<header>C H A P T E R VII.</header>

### CHAPTER VII.

...found in the sick person. And the mucilages <note>Thick, gelatinous extracts from plants.</note> of "simple" foreign medicines are foolishly prescribed, if mucilage were indeed the native cause of the evil. Then the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical authorities of the university system, primarily following Galen.</note> speak as if **diuretics** <term>medicines that promote the flow of urine</term> propelled the **stone** <note>original: calculum</note>—and even the urine—with a hammer, or pushed it from behind with a stick: in this way, through such artificial explanations, they make a public spectacle of themselves in the manner of the common people. They do not consider that in diuretics, a specific property remains, derived from the "middle life" <note>A term in Helmontian medicine referring to the vital state of a substance after it has been partially digested or transformed.</note> of the **simple** <term>a medicinal herb or individual ingredient</term>, or acquired in the transformation of digestion, by which diuretics draw out the urine. However, no diuretics, by themselves, are responsible for the "offspring" or creation of the stone.

Just as an honest **Physician** does not focus on effects that occur accidentally by chance, so as to neglect the primary effects themselves. Yet this is exactly what happens when they forbid a diuretic. For a healing indication mandates the swiftest removal of a harmful thing, and even more so of something which, through delay, subsequently grows larger. Therefore, I praise diuretics in **Nephritis** <term>inflammation or disease of the kidney</term>, provided they simultaneously soothe and calm the **spasm** of the **ureters** <term>the tubes connecting the kidneys to the bladder</term>. 

A certain Countess, and likewise another nun, ended their days in immense pain. For each displayed a stone shaped like a hook, with one point of the triangle in the kidney and the other ending within the ureter; and both died from a cruel spasm. They do not indeed die because of the suppression of urine, where the other kidney provides enough urine; but they perish from the atrocious spasm alone, which spasm is relaxed again at the moment of death. Therefore, the dissection <note>An autopsy.</note> showed nothing that brought about death except for the hook-shaped stone. 

In the beginning, I said that the healing of **Duelech** <term>a term for the "stony matter" or tartar that forms in the body</term> consists as much in the abolition of the "inclination" <note>The body's internal tendency or "disposition" to form stones.</note> as in the melting or dissolution of the stone: both of which the Schools deny are possible. And so, we stand at opposite poles. Therefore, we must come to reasons, to witnesses (that is, facts), and to legal proofs; and once my right has been proved, let the ignorance of the Schools be exposed. First of all, since it is not
<margin>12</margin>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones cause death through excruciating spasms rather than simple blockage and asserts that stones can be dissolved, contrary to traditional medical teaching.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s critique of the traditional medical Schools. He presents their arguments against the possibility of dissolving a kidney stone—arguments he considers futile and based on a misunderstanding of nature—before mocking their reliance on Galenic tradition.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

<header>ON LITHIASIS</header>

...of a being, or of the impossible, there is no positive concept, and therefore no science of it. Thus the **Schools** <note>The traditional university medical authorities who followed the teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note> confess they have no science, and they deny those things are possible which they confess they do not know.

<margin>33</margin> The reasons which drove the Schools to conclude it is impossible are these—and they are quite futile: "Our experience, the mistress of things, has not yet revealed to us that a bad inclination can be removed." Since according to **Galen** <note>The 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated medicine for over a thousand years.</note>, a **disharmony** <term>intemperies; a lack of balance between the bodily humors</term> that has turned into one's very nature cannot be cured, according to the proverb: "You may drive out nature with a pitchfork, yet she will ever hurry back." <note>original: *Naturam expellas furca tamen usque recurret.* (Horace, Epistles 1.10.24)</note>

Especially in a part of the body continuously filled with waste matter, it is utterly impossible to remove a "confirmed" disharmony. As for a confirmed **stone** <note>original: *calculum*</note>, however much the flashy boasting of "stone-breakers" <note>Practitioners or medicines claiming to break stones.</note> makes a noise, it is nothing but the vain attempt of **empirics** <term>unlicensed practitioners relying on practical experience rather than university theory</term>. It is nothing but the boasting of idiots.

For a **Physician** <note>original: *Medicus*</note> can only summon and propel the stone through **diuretics** <term>medicines that promote the flow of urine</term>, and loosen the passages through moistening softeners. Indeed, since diuretics are full of danger, nothing is more suitable for the Healer in **Lithiasis** <term>stone-disease</term> than to widen the **ureters** <note>The tubes leading from the kidneys to the bladder.</note> by moistening them, and to remove the pressing filth from the intestines through **enemas** <note>original: *enemata*</note>.

But the "smoke-selling" **chemists** <note>original: *chymistæ*; a derogatory term for alchemists or those following the new chemical medicine of Paracelsus.</note> attempt to dissolve a confirmed stone in the bladder through a "backward resolution" <note>The chemical idea of reversing the process of solidification to turn a stone back into liquid.</note>, and by their own words they bring nothing but disgrace upon themselves. "But our **Philosophy** <note>Van Helmont is here presenting the viewpoint of his opponents in the Schools.</note> promises nothing beyond the powers of nature, and therefore it remains venerated among the learned and has struck roots fixed for so many centuries. For who does not see that the stomach itself must necessarily be broken and liquefied sooner than a stone—which is a hundred times harder than the stomach and located far from the mouth?"

But because the **chemist** <note>original: *chymicus*</note> is mostly ignorant of Philosophy, he boldly promises anything so that he may swindle money from the wretched and the credulous—money he does not know how to obtain through **gold-making** <note>original: *chrysopejan*; the alchemical art of transmuting base metals into gold.</note>. For if there were anything in nature that...

->C A P U T VII.<-

# CHAPTER VII.

...were to dissolve a confirmed **Duelech** <term>A term coined by the physician Paracelsus to describe a specific type of stony deposit or "tartar" that forms in the human body, distinct from common gravel</term>, so many Princes and Great Men, and so many rare medical geniuses, would not have lacked such a happy remedy until now. <margin>14</margin> These are the haughty brows, decrees, and slanders of the **Schools** <note>The established university medical faculties of the 17th century</note>; however, when these are well weighed, the true privileges of their opponents are discovered. For first of all, if someone contracts a disease by sinning, why should they not, by healing well, remove the same fundamentally? And why not utterly root out the characters once impressed upon the part of the body?

For I have freed many from **Lithiasis** <term>The clinical condition of having stones in the kidney or bladder</term>, who had been subject to it for several years, so that in the future they lived entirely free. You will soon find their remedies, under penalty of my own infamy <note>Van Helmont is so confident in his cures that he stakes his professional reputation on them</note>. For I easily forgive the Schools because they speak according to their own experiences and ignorance of causes, and they deny that an "impression"—transferred into the powers of the membranes—can be taken away: since, clearly, they have hitherto recognized nothing but crude and sluggish remedies.

But meanwhile they wallow in inexcusable error, despairing that anyone might be wiser than themselves. While they meanwhile cease to investigate, and dedicate the whole span of their lives to profit alone. The judgments of the Schools look back to the writings of ancestors who followed pagan ways; our judgments, however, look at the **first beings** <note>original: *entia prima*; the fundamental, essential components of a substance in its most potent, purified state</note> of bodies, freed from the wrappings by which they are hindered from progressing to the first constituents of ourselves: in which they are able to stifle harmful impressions introduced into the middle of life, and to that extent remove inclinations which seem to have turned into nature.

Regarding the removal of the inclination, therefore, the first is the medicine **Aroph of Paracelsus** <term>A chemical medicine; the name is a contraction used in the Paracelsian tradition</term> (which signifies <gloss>the Aroma of the Philosophers</gloss>, so called because of its golden tincture). Prepared under dung <note>A method of providing gentle, steady heat for fermentation or digestion by burying the vessel in a warm dung-heap</note>, with a mixture of rye bread, and afterwards extracted with spirit of wine, it cures an old inclination toward **kidney stones** <note>original: *calculum renum*</note>. A certain Baron of our country, while for several years on his embassy to England, [suffered] several times from kidney stones with the greatest pain...

# ON LITHIASIS
<meta>catchword: medi-</meta>

...had been manifested, and at my suggestion he used the aforementioned liquor **Aroph** <term>A chemical remedy from the Paracelsian tradition, likely "Aroma Philosophorum," often containing saffron and processed mineral salts</term> twice a week. For eighteen years thereafter he remained free from **Lithiasis** <note>Kidney or bladder stones</note>, until he finally departed this life at the age of 83. When his body was dissected, it showed not even a grain of sand or a tiny stone. Yet previously, while he suffered from the stone, he had always passed bloody urine, whether he was carried in a carriage or even walking at a modest pace. His heirs still survive today as witnesses to this. I also remember a counselor whom I mentioned previously regarding the eating of asparagus. For he, when he used to lie in bed miserably every fifteen days, subsequently used a beer in which the seed of **Daucus** <term>Wild carrot (*Daucus carota*), historically used as a diuretic and to treat stones</term> was cooked. He lived for several years thereafter, free from Lithiasis. The experiences and testimonies of these men bring a blush to the cheeks of the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical faculties of the time</note>, since the truth is found in the mouths of these witnesses. 

**Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous Swiss physician and pioneer of the chemical revolution in medicine</note> also called upon the "essences" of gems and of **saxifrages** <note>Plants named for their supposed ability to break stones (from the Latin *saxum*, stone, and *frangere*, to break)</note> for help; and finally, by the unique remedy of **Ludus** <term>A specific calcareous stone or fossil (often septarian nodules) which Van Helmont believed could dissolve kidney stones through chemical sympathy</term>, he promised—and achieved—both ends of the healing process. Let the Schools deny as "impossible" what they themselves cannot do; their testimony is full of arrogance and stupidity. Indeed, whenever they witness the weak power of saxifrages (attempted with their own crude remedies) and see the effect vanish, they arch their eyebrows in astonishment, shrug their shoulders, and remain silent when questioned. But when forced to answer, they retreat to the claim of "impossibility." They prefer to accuse God—who is the source of mercy and goodness—of being forgetful, rather than admitting that He provided remedies in nature for a "formed" stone (as they call it) and for many other diseases. Indeed, they more willingly accuse God of forgetfulness than admit any mark of ignorance in their own **pagan doctrine** <note>Van Helmont frequently attacked the university medical curriculum for being based on the "pagan" philosophies of Galen and Aristotle rather than Christian or chemical principles</note>. 

The Princes, however, circumvented by the Schools, have signed off on these impostures. The liberality of their piety has erected **Xenodochia** <term>Hospitals or almshouses for the poor and infirm</term> for "incurables," being seduced by the deceptions of the Schools, which have argued that the text of **Wisdom** is a lie: *God made all the nations of the earth healthy, nor is there...* <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 1:14: "For he created all things, that they might have their being: and the nations of the world were healthful; and there is no poison of destruction in them."</note>

<summary>Van Helmont provides evidence of successful cures for kidney stones using chemical remedies and herbs, using these cases to attack the medical establishment's claim that stones are incurable. He argues that the Schools' pessimism contradicts both nature and the biblical assertion that God provided a cure for every ailment.</summary>

## CHAPTER VII.

*The medicine of extermination* <note>original: *Medicamentum exterminii*</note>. For the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical faculties rooted in Galenic and Aristotelian teachings</note> have made their own excessively thick ignorance reciprocal and interchangeable with the "impotence of nature," as if they knew everything possible and were only ignorant of the impossible. And they do this not just negatively, but entirely by way of deprivation—as if their lack of knowledge did not depend on the defects of the **Academies**, but rather on a shortage of goodness or a lack of divine providence.

Therefore, since the denial of the possibility of healing seemed to me to contain a hidden crime, I have always—even from my youth—hoped for the best and argued against them in this way: If, according to faith, every disease began from a transgression [against God]; and if any sin whatever may be fully forgiven, then it is entirely to be hoped that any disease may be removed in its specific kind, provided the punishment is equal to the sin in its remission.

This is especially true because the same One who forgives sins also heals diseases, has given remedies, and created the **Physician** out of the abundance of His goodness—which surpasses all His other actions. Indeed, His mercy is infinitely greater than all the sins of men. For could He perhaps not have created an adequate and victorious remedy for any disease? Or did He not know how? Or was He unwilling—He who gave the remedy for eternal death? For He does not rejoice in the destruction of the living, He who made all the nations of the earth healthy <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Wisdom 1:13-14, suggesting that God did not create death or disease as permanent, unfixable fixtures of the world</note>.

Regarding the authorities of writers: **Cardanus** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a famous Italian polymath and physician</note> writes that in his time, a man wandered through **Lombardy** who, in a few days, with a certain drink, commonly, safely, surely, and briefly cured as many as had a **stone in the bladder** <term>Calculus; a hard mass of minerals forming in the urinary bladder, a source of extreme pain in the early modern period</term>. He adds his judgment that he does not doubt this man is in hell because, as he was dying, he begrudged mortals his art <note>Meaning the man refused to share his secret medical recipe before he died, taking it to the grave</note>.

In the face of such a paradox, and against the shouts of the Schools, a single witness is not enough. The epitaph of **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>The influential and controversial Swiss physician (1493–1541) who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note>, which can be seen on the wall near St. Sebastian’s in the hospital...

<meta>catchword: is seen,</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that no disease is inherently incurable, suggesting that the "impotence of nature" claimed by medical schools is actually a mask for their own ignorance. He provides a theological defense for the existence of remedies for all ailments and cites Cardanus's account of a secret cure for bladder stones.</summary>

<header>ON THE STONE <note>original: DE LITHIASI</note></header>

# ON THE STONE

The monument erected by the Bishop of Salzburg sounds forth that the same miracle has happened many times, even if the critics <note>original: *Momo* — referring to Momus, the Greek god of mockery and satire; "burst their sides with complaining"</note> may grumble. His words [on the tomb] state:

->Here is buried **Philippus Aureolus Paracelsus**, Distinguished Doctor of Medicine, who with wonderful skill removed those dire diseases, **Leprosy**, **Gout**, **Dropsy**, and other incurable infections of the body, and honored his legacy by arranging for his goods to be distributed to the poor. In the year of our Lord 1541, on the 24th day of September, he exchanged life for death.<-

The **Schools** <note>The established medical universities of the time</note> themselves teach us that by "incurable diseases," the Prince-Bishop of Salzburg understood **Consumption** <note>original: *Tabem*</note>, **Asthma**, the **Stone** <note>original: *Calculum*</note>, and all such things. For they [the Schools] are always loudly proclaiming these kinds of diseases to be primarily among the incurable. Furthermore, the broad phrasing of the epitaph refers to the books published by Paracelsus about the **Stone**, written with a far more accurate pen than his works on other diseases. Therefore, at the very least, whatever has happened once or multiple times in nature is not impossible to happen again. Consequently, whoever asserts as impossible what has occasionally occurred in nature through divine goodness according to a **Physician’s** prayer, is a liar before God, the craftsman of nature.

For in Law, witnesses themselves point out what is a matter of fact, and arguments brought against witnesses are considered useless bickering. Thus, the **Schools**—who call us deceivers—mock the human race with a logical fallacy <note>original: *elencho*</note>. They say: "The **Stone**, located far from the mouth [in the bladder or kidneys], is much harder than the membrane of the stomach; therefore, whatever would corrode or diminish a **Stone** located far away would have long before consumed the stomach itself into which it had recently fallen. Therefore, the **Stone** is necessarily an incurable evil."

If, however, the Holy Scriptures say otherwise, they seek a way to excuse them from falsehood through some "moderate interpretation." But truly, the keys of wisdom are held by force in the **Schools**, so that while they themselves do not enter, they still strive to block others who wish to enter— <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 11:52: "Woe to you lawyers! For you have taken away the key of knowledge; you did not enter yourselves, and you hindered those who were entering."</note>

<summary>Van Helmont cites the epitaph of the famous physician Paracelsus as evidence that diseases labeled "incurable" by traditional medicine—specifically the Stone—can indeed be cured, accusing the medical establishment of blocking progress.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER VIII.</header>

# CHAPTER VIII.

...they strive. For to a **Chemist** <term>Chymico; a physician-alchemist who seeks to understand nature’s hidden powers</term>, it is not desirable to remove **Duelech** <term>Duelech; a term used by the Paracelsians to describe the specific stony matter formed in the body from a mixture of urine and earthy spirit</term> through corrosives, but through proper and specific resolving agents. For neither does the stomach of a dove dissolve pearls, nor that of an ostrich <note>original: *struthiocameli*</note> dissolve iron or flints by means of corrosives, but rather through an appropriate ferment of digestion. Or, if you were to give corrosives to the stomachs of animals, they are at least such that they bring no damage to the stomach itself. Indeed, if you look at the most powerful corrosives, **nitric acid** <term>aqua chrysulca; literally "gold-washing water," a strong acid used to separate metals</term> dissolves iron, bronze, and silver, but it does not even penetrate wax. Through a failure to consider this fact, the **Schools** <note>The established medical universities of the time</note> have frightened their students, the unlearned commoners, and even the great lords away from the investigation of things—driven to this by their own ignorance, a sluggishness for inquiry, and a hope for profit. Therefore, for the stone and diseases in the bladder, as if the **Doctors** <note>original: *Medentes*</note> intended a "diversion" <note>An old medical theory where one bodily fluid is diverted to another area</note>, they ridiculously turned to the anus and approached the matter with enemas, as if to have emptied the bowels were the same as having expelled a kidney stone.

<margin>17</margin>
For they saw that **lemon juice** <note>original: *succus citri*</note> reduced human **Duelech** <note>stony deposit</note> in a glass vessel, and they hoped the same ought to happen within us. If it did not, they could at least boast that by providing lemon juice, they had performed as much as was possible for nature. They are ignorant, first of all, that the acidity of the lemon turns sweet under heat, like an unripe fruit that is acidic at first and then becomes sweet after being ripened by the Sun. Finally, if a single drop of more acidic wine is sent into the bladder, it brings more pain than a huge stone. Therefore, those who boast that they have done something useful toward the reduction of a stone by administering lemon acid **by mouth** <note>original: *oretenùs*</note> are imagining something ridiculous. They err equally who offer distilled liquids made from salts to resolve the stone in the bladder. For acid, as such, cannot pass into the veins without significant harm. And although there may be relief for stone-sufferers in lemon juice, and much more in **spirit of sea salt** <term>spiritu salis marini; an early term for hydrochloric acid</term>, yet these benefits do not occur unless first saying farewell to <note>The sentence cuts off as it reaches the next page's transition; Van Helmont is about to explain that the "acidity" itself is not the curative agent once inside the body.</note>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones should be dissolved by specific "ferments" rather than harsh corrosives, mocking traditional doctors for using irrelevant enemas and misunderstood acids. He explains that acids like lemon juice change their nature within the body and cannot reach the bladder in their raw, corrosive state without causing harm.</summary>

...earlier by acidity. Because in truth, the healing or the dissolving of **Duelech** <term>Duelech; a Paracelsian term for the hardened, stony matter formed within the body from a mixture of urine and an "earthy" spirit</term> is not achieved by acids, nor by corrosives. Acidic drinks never reach the bladder, nor are they even allowed to be led there. While the **Schools** <note>The established medical universities of the time</note> took notice of the family of **Saxifrages** <note>original: *Saxifragorum*; plants whose name literally means "stone-breakers"</note>, they were paralyzed with fear regarding the promised outcome. 

<margin>II</margin>
Then, once they were made more certain and saw that they were frustrated in their hope, they nonetheless played with "human leather" <note>an idiomatic expression meaning they experimented recklessly with human lives</note> and administered crushed fruits and powders made from stones. They knew, I say, that they were destitute of a remedy, yet they did not stop prescribing things they knew were useless. In this, they deceived the sick, bypassing the opportunity for healing with vain and ridiculous remedies, just so they might excuse themselves of the death of their patients to future generations—as if they had faithfully offered everything possible in nature and everything praised by true Physicians. When they heard of hard seeds, such as **Gromwell** <term>Lithospermon; from the Greek for "stone-seed." Helmont mocks the Schools for assuming a plant can break stones just because its seeds are hard</term>—which is called by such a ridiculous name in the Schools—they immediately took an omen for themselves from the name alone. 

<margin>0I</margin>
Next, they added "stones" found in crabs, snails, fish, and the burnt shells of shellfish; they even added the hardest gems ground in **brass** <term>aurichalcum; a golden-colored alloy of copper and zinc</term>. Finally, they prescribed **Rock Crystal** <term>Crystallum</term> that had been heated in fire, quenched, and ground up—a ridiculous invention with a matching result. At last, when they saw they had become objects of laughter and contempt, and lest they should seem to expect their fees and the pay for their repeated visits for free without having done anything—even though they despaired inwardly, taught by manifold experience—they claimed that what Europe and Asia denied them must be sought as a subsidy from the **Indies** <note>A reference to the 17th-century trade in exotic, expensive, and often ineffective "miracle cures" from the colonies</note>. They did this at least so that the doctor's counsel, through his knowledge of such foreign **simples** <note>A "simple" is a medicinal herb or a single ingredient in a medicine</note>, might be held in admiration by those standing by. As if God were not the creator of remedies and the merciful helper of the sick, and had refused remedies to sick Europeans and Asians for so many thousands of years! But if, in the meantime, a **stone** <term>Calculus; the physical accretion in the kidney or bladder</term> should of its own accord from the kidney [fall]...

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

...fall headlong (for they have long since despaired of the bladder stone)—aha!—how much is made of the administration of **Saxifrage** <note>original: *Saxifraga*; a group of plants whose name literally means "stone-breaker," traditionally used to treat stones</note>? And those bold little liars claim that powders command the kidneys no differently than masters command slaves, and that they crush the stones as if under a hammer. But if the business goes less happily <note>meaning, if the patient does not recover or the treatment fails</note>, they lament that they were called too late, and that the passages could have been loosened more <margin>18</margin> opportunely with baths and **clysters** <term>enemas used to deliver medicine or clear the bowels</term>. They deplore the sin of having given too few enemas or simple remedies, and they accuse the relatives who were acting as healers. They especially grieve that they were called when the **stone** <note>original: *calculo*</note> was already "confirmed" <note>meaning fully hardened or matured</note>, even though a "diploma" <note>an official certificate or guideline</note> has been omitted by the **Schools** <note>the traditional medical universities</note> until now, which might show the exact time between a stone being "confirmed" and "unconfirmed." Perhaps this is not due to the negligence of the Schools, but because that diploma, written on parchment, was devoured by mice. Truly, a equally ridiculous excuse of the Schools!

For indeed, **Duelech** <term>a Paracelsian term for the hardened, stony matter formed within the body</term> <margin>19</margin> gains its hardness through stages; for it is hardened by its own **coagulum** <note>a chemical curdling or thickening agent</note> in the midst of the fluids. And the Schools are deceived because they thought the stone gradually hardened and dried out. In truth, it is as hard in its outer crust today as it is in its innermost core; it is just as hard when recently cut out and wet as it will be after being kept for a century. And the sand newly adhering to a **chamber pot** <note>original: *matulæ*</note> is as hard as it will be after it is scraped off a year later. I say, a flint at the bottom of a river is as hard today as it will be in a hundred years. Who is there, then, who would not marvel with me at the dense ignorance of the Schools everywhere? Not only have they deceived themselves with their own reasoning—saying that the stone in the bladder is generated, dried, and gradually more hardened or "confirmed" by heat—but they have not even considered in passing that urine, in a glass and in the cold of the surroundings, deposits sands—without the help of heat, dryness, or "cooking"—which are as hard today as they will be after they have dried on paper? These are surely the studies, decrees, and

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>ON THE DISEASE OF THE STONE</header>

The remedies of the Schools, through their own shortcomings, deny that nature provides any possible help for those suffering from the stone. Therefore, they decree that a stone in the bladder, when it is larger than its point of exit, has no remedy provided by Divine Goodness within nature, except for the razor <note>original: *novaculam*; refers to the surgical knife used in lithotomy, the operation to remove stones</note>. They admit a woman’s bladder can be widened through <term>dioptrismum</term> <note>the use of a dilating instrument to expand a passage</note> to emit its stone; but we are dealing now with the male. *Horatius Augenius*, a primary physician of Padua <note>Orazio Augenio (1527–1603), a famous Italian professor of medicine</note>, even rejoices that the cruelty of the razor was increased by his own wit—specifically, that he was the first to dictate that such a deed be performed with a red-hot blade. *Paracelsus* the younger, however, although he had also placed all hope against <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for the hardened, stony matter formed within the body</note> in the knife, later silently corrected his error. He did this through **Ludus** <note>a specific type of stone (often a septarian nodule) that Van Helmont used as a remedy</note>, resolving marble-like and flint-like "tartars" as easily as other shell-like deposits with that single remedy. I know well that Divine Goodness has created stones within the vegetable and animal families that are useless and purposeless to their own species—almost monstrous, and indeed burdensome to the individuals themselves—unless they were created for some benefit to us. Thus, since all things
<margin>20</margin> were created for the use of ungrateful man, and many things barely benefit the creatures they grow in, they would never benefit mortals either unless they were first resolved into a "milky juice" or a "first essence" <note>original: *ens primum*; the fundamental, medicinal state of a substance</note>. They certainly received their purpose from their use, despite their strange, stone-like formation. For a stone does not grow within an animal or plant as a punishment; rather, it shows the "signature" of its own gift, even in the hardness of its coagulation. Although a mineral stone might be a "stone-breaker" <note>original: *saxifragus*; refers both to minerals and the Saxifrage plant family</note> suited for dissolving **Duelech**—if it is prepared so that it reaches the kidneys with its powers intact—nevertheless, stones of an animal or vegetable nature are generally more "civilized." Thus, like domestic citizens, they are more easily admitted into the "republic" <note>a metaphor for the integrated systems of the human body</note> of our own nature. For their appearance gives hope of their "signature" <note>referring to the *Doctrine of Signatures*, the belief that the appearance of a natural object indicates its medicinal use</note>: so that as often as they depart from their stony character, they also possess the power to carry out the gifts promised in their signature, [the power...]

<meta>The catchword "tem" at the bottom of the page indicates the next word is "potestatem" (power/authority).</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont rejects the surgical "Schools" who claim only the knife can cure bladder stones, arguing instead that nature provides medicinal stones (like Ludus) whose "signatures" indicate they can dissolve human calculi.</summary>

### CHAPTER VII.

<margin>21</margin> ...that they might obtain [dominion], enter into marriage with us, and communicate their intimately betrothed, promised powers to us. This cannot happen except through their full resolution into a **first being** <note>original: *ens primum*; in alchemical philosophy, the primordial state of a substance where its medicinal power is most potent</note>. For I have made the stones of fruits—such as medlars, dates, peaches, etc.—volatile, without any **dead head** <note>original: *capite mortuo*; the useless, burnt residue left over after an alchemical distillation</note>, after they had returned into a milky juice. And this, truly, was done without any separative distillation. For I found that this kind of remedy **restores**, just as <term>Aroph</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for a remedy that "takes away" (from the Greek *aphairesis*) or prevents disease</note> **preserves**. I make this distinction between the two: **restoration** is the cutting off of an acquired inclination <note>the actual state of being sick</note>; **preservation**, however, is a precaution against the future, by blocking the available matter. <margin>22</margin> In true healing, however, both are included. Furthermore, for the true resolution and liquefaction of generated <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for the hardened, stony matter formed within the body</note>, the **Ludus of Paracelsus** <note>a type of stone, often septarian nodules found in clay, which Van Helmont believed could dissolve kidney stones</note> holds the first place. It is so named not because it is a flint stone and boys "play" with it <note>the Latin word *ludus* means "game" or "play"</note>, as some have interpreted its etymology. But because *Ludus* is always extracted in the form of a knucklebone, a die, or a cube. The description of its preparation is found in the Author <note>referring to Paracelsus</note>: *Ludus*, thoroughly crushed, calcined, and boiled until it takes the form of an oil; which he calls, in nearly a single word, **gall of the earth**, and **corrected Altholizoi**. Which sounds like <gloss>*Al. tho. oli. gesotten*</gloss> <note>a play on German: "All to oil boiled"</note> or, "the whole converted into oil through boiling." This most excellent preparation of *Ludus* has remained known to few mortals until now, hidden under this brief cloak. And although the world is worthy of pity, so that the preparation might be described in a clearer sense, nevertheless, the many despisers of secret things are unworthy for those things to be manifested now—things which God, for the weightiest reasons, willed to remain within the treasures of His dispensation among the few and the "little ones" of this age, until nothing remains so hidden that it shall not be revealed in its fullness of days. In which time, woe to the world and its confusion! Di-

<summary>Van Helmont distinguishes between "restoration" (curing current illness) and "preservation" (preventing future illness), highlighting the "Ludus of Paracelsus" as a supreme remedy for dissolving kidney stones. He argues that true alchemical secrets are intentionally kept from the unworthy and will only be fully revealed in God's chosen time.</summary>

-> # ON LITHIASIS <-
<note>lithiasis: the formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney stones</note>

...I will describe it a bit more clearly, so that only those skilled in pyrotechnic philosophy <note>original: *Philosophiæ pyrotechnicæ*; this refers to alchemy or the chemistry of fire and heat</note> may understand me. Let the <term>Ludus</term> <note>a specific type of stone, often septarian nodules found in clay, which Van Helmont believed could dissolve biological stones</note> be ground into a powder in a mortar and under a pestle. Then on a whetstone, then upon flint, again. Then afterwards, let it be calcined <note>reduced to a powder or "calx" through the application of heat</note>. Not indeed by a parching fire, but let <term>circulated salt</term> <note>original: *sal circulatum*; an alchemical solvent mentioned by Paracelsus, believed to be a highly refined and "circulated" form of salt</note> be added to it—about which Paracelsus speaks in his book *On Renovation and Restoration*—and once the salt has been distilled from it, it is called "calcined Ludus." Because with little labor—of about two hours—it will be entirely converted into salt. Therefore, the Ludus thus calcined and reduced into salt, and being of equal weight to itself, 
<margin>23</margin>
should flow of its own accord in a humid place <note>this refers to "deliquescence," where a salt absorbs enough moisture from the air to turn into a liquid</note>. This resolved salt should then be enclosed with a hermetic seal in a long-necked "egg" <note>a round-bottomed alchemical flask</note> and, in a sand bath, boiled with a continuous fire of the second degree <note>a moderate heat, hotter than a water bath but cooler than a direct flame</note> until the whole Ludus, in its equal weight, stands like a thicker oil upon the water which it drew from the air of the cellar. For then the whole Ludus is a <term>volatile salt</term>, in the form of a resolved oily salt, and it has something of the taste of urine. And therefore, through the urine, it travels with its full powers alongside the drink and dissolves every stone <note>original: *calculum*</note> wherever it may lurk in the body. Because it is a volatile salt, it is resolved in moisture and is not separated in the "workshops" of the digestions <note>the stomach and other metabolic "offices" of the body</note>; but because it represents the taste of urine to some extent and possesses properties friendly to our nature, it is willingly received and sent at once to the kidneys. Its dose is 14 grains, up to twenty, with a small amount of simple distilled water. And a stone <note>original: *calculus*</note> of average size in the bladder is resolved to the size of a pine nut, mostly within two weeks. The liquor of Ludus prepared in this way is called by Paracelsus the <term>gall of the earth</term> <note>original: *fel terrae*</note>, because if it is spread on paper, it is a dark citron-yellow, declining not a little toward green. For it is a truly marvelous stone, uniquely corresponding to the descriptions of Paracelsus—namely, a birth from the salt of the urine of the earth's liquids. In the depths of the earth, at the "depth" of the bladder in the human body. I found

<page-num>131</page-num>

### CHAPTER VII.

...it is found at the bank of the Scheldt <note>original: *Scaldis*; the major river flowing through Antwerp</note>, near Antwerp, where bricks are baked. It is situated more or less 40 feet below the horizon, according to the depth of the river. For I compare the bottom of the river to the bottom of the bladder. However, it is not found in the very bed of the river; instead, it extends in a single, simple chamber beneath the bottom of the bank, in the neighboring field, along the sides near the banks, and it does so for several miles. And that chamber of <term>Ludus</term> <note>a type of septarian limestone nodule found in clay, which Van Helmont believed had the power to dissolve kidney stones</note> scarcely exceeds a foot in thickness. Nor does it extend further above or below the aforementioned chamber, nor is it found anywhere else nearby. 

In the aforementioned field, <term>Pyrite</term> <note>iron disulfide, or "fool's gold"</note> is also frequent, rich in sulfur and vitriol <note>metallic sulfates</note>. Although it is very hard while underground, it soon becomes crumbly when exposed to the air, as the vitriol gradually decays. But <term>Ludus</term> is a pale stone, sometimes covered with a transparent crust along its joints; for the most part, it is volatile <note>it evaporates or transforms into gas</note> in a potter's kiln. This, surely, is the ultimate stone-breaking remedy and the great desire of those suffering from calculi <note>internal stones</note>. Happy is he who can calcine <note>reduce to a powder through heat</note> it, as I have already advised. But the labor of it requires not only reading and careful selection, but full knowledge, doubly confirmed. 

Because this is the labor of Wisdom <note>original: *Sophia*</note>, the hope of the Adepts <note>highly skilled alchemists who have achieved the "Great Work"</note>. Therefore, in this wretched age, very rare is the man whom God has led to this goal. It is not enough to have known the Ludus, or the <term>Cevilla of Paracelsus</term> <note>another name for the Ludus stone used by the physician Paracelsus</note>, and its birth and origin, and that it ought to be reduced into a volatile oily salt without the loss of its qualities. But since there is no more laborious part in all of <term>Chemistry</term> <note>original: *Chemeia*</note> (as Paracelsus often declares in the preparation of the tincture of sulfur, which enhances the natural colors of gems—for in both cases, the same circulated salt <note>a universal solvent</note> is required, though it is quietly suppressed in both places), I will add a few things, as much as one brother can communicate to another. For although the remedy against <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for a stony deposit formed in the body from a mixture of "tartar" and "sulfur"</note> ought to be made known before the whole world out of compassion and human charity...

<summary>Van Helmont identifies the specific location near Antwerp where the medicinal Ludus stone is mined and explains that while it resembles pyrite, it has unique volatile properties essential for alchemy. He emphasizes that transforming this stone into a remedy is a divine task reserved for true masters of chemistry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s discussion on the "Ludus" stone and the secret chemical processes required to transform it into a remedy for kidney stones. He contrasts his experimental and divinely-inspired "Adept" knowledge with the sensory-based logic of Aristotle and the traditional herbalism of the university "Schools."</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>

-> # ON THE STONE DISEASE <-

...shouted through trumpets: yet for reasons known only to God, it must be kept among the secrets, of which He Himself willed to remain the dispenser. Take one pound of powdered <term>Ludus</term> <note>a specific septarian stone Van Helmont believed could dissolve kidney stones</note> and an equal amount of the liquor <term>Alkahest</term> <note>original: Albahest; a theoretical universal solvent</note>, distill this from it, and at the first turn, the entire Ludus will be changed into a salt. This salt, when placed in a glass dish in a damp place, flows down without any remaining earthy matter; the resulting liquor is of a tawny color. When closed with a hermetic seal <note>an airtight glass seal</note> and boiled, it all floats on top like foam, in the form of liquefied <term>green fat</term> <note>original: axungiae viridis</note>. This is the "corrected Altholizoim" of Paracelsus, and the "gall of the earth." 

But whoever thinks they can achieve this mastery by adding saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> or similar things: let him know that though such salts be exquisitely and repeatedly mixed with the Ludus, only those salts themselves will flow down, leaving the earth behind like dregs in the dish. The Ludus, however, must be entirely transmuted into a tinted volatile salt, retaining nothing of the added Alkahest. For both this liquor and the Ludus retain their original weight. And the Ludus itself preserves the mineral gifts which Almighty Goodness gave to it. 

However, this work is by far the most difficult—not indeed regarding the preparation of the Ludus, but of the Alkahest itself. I know that I speak the truth, and among our <term>Adepts</term> <note>those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy</note> there is a proof that surpasses all logical demonstration. For Aristotle recognized no other science than that which sprouts from pre-existing knowledge of the senses. But there is another science, indemonstrable, in which the Giver Himself remains the interpreter of His own light, beyond all scope of the <term>Syllogism</term> <note>a form of logical argument used in medieval universities</note>: yet it is so certain that the whole world cannot stir the slightest doubt in the one who knows it. 

I have openly confirmed and made manifest this point in my treatise, *On the Hunt for the Sciences* <note>original: "De Venatione scientiarum"</note>. I have revealed, meanwhile, how a spirit may be drawn from putrid urine <note>original: lotio</note> which, when introduced into the bladder through a painless catheter, dissolves the <term>Duelech</term> <note>a Paracelsian term for a stone or calculus formed in the body</note>. Meanwhile, the Schools <note>the traditional medical universities</note> recommend their herbalists, and these recommend their <term>stone-breakers</term> <note>original: saxifragia; plants traditionally used to treat kidney stones</note>...

<summary>Van Helmont describes the alchemical transformation of the Ludus stone using the Alkahest solvent and argues that true chemical knowledge is a gift from God that transcends Aristotelian logic.</summary>

<page-num>133</page-num>

### CHAPTER VII.

...gery: yet they hesitate, and cross swords with one another without hope. If not for a settled resolution, they might at least, for the sake of a hope cast to the wretched, wish a prize were settled for themselves.
<margin>24</margin> In the meantime, there are those who promise **masterly** compounds, by which the stone, as if crushed under a hammer or a millstone, is ground into sand or flour. But if, in the meantime, some sand should happen to appear, they demand that it all be credited to their <term>stone-breaker</term> <note>original: saxifrago; a type of medicine or plant believed to break stones in the bladder or kidneys</note>. But the outcome has, until now, mocked the rashness of those whom the knowledge of the "root" has deceived. Indeed, it is established that <term>Duelech</term> <note>a term used by Paracelsus and Van Helmont to describe the specific material of a stone formed within the human body</note> is composed of a completely homogeneous material, derived from the single and constant liquid of urine. A <term>stone</term> <note>original: calculus</note>, to be sure, is a stone everywhere and in every part of itself. Sand does not differ from a stone in its material; nor is a stone merely a collection of sand joined together by glue or mucilage, such that only the dissolving of the glue is required for the grains of sand to fall away from each other. It is therefore frivolous to claim that a stone must be split into sands to dissolve the more stubborn parts. For <term>Paracelsus</term>, although he was successful in healing once he obtained the remedy, nevertheless reveals in this that he—along with the <term>Humorists</term> <note>traditional physicians who followed the ancient theory that health was governed by the balance of four "humors" or bodily fluids</note>—was ignorant of the nature of <term>Duelech</term>. This is because he promised that by taking the <term>Ludus</term> <note>the septarian stone remedy discussed on the previous page</note> remedy, the <term>Duelech</term> would be ejected in the form of sand. 
<margin>25</margin> Then again, he teaches that the size of the <term>Duelech</term> can be divined and weighed by boiling and drying all the urine preserved throughout the entire interval of the healing. Both of these claims, however, are a mere dream of rashness. For whatever remedy dissolves <term>Duelech</term>, it would first, and far more easily, liquefy the sands and small fragments, if any were to fall off. Indeed, whatever is coagulated from a homogeneous urine is necessarily homogeneous in itself. Furthermore, if the <term>Duelech</term> were dissolved by the <term>Ludus</term>, and if all the urines collected and preserved throughout the entire healing interval were dried out, they would perhaps exceed the weight of the stone forty times over.

<sig>i 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: weight.</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that kidney stones (Duelech) are uniform solids rather than clusters of sand held by glue. He critiques Paracelsus for claiming the stone would pass as sand and for suggesting its size could be measured by drying the patient's urine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of previous medical theories regarding kidney and bladder stones. While the previous page addressed the failure of "stone-breaking" medicines and Paracelsus's views on the homogeneous nature of stones (Duelech), this page examines Paracelsus's own practical limitations and the "new frauds" of the traditional Medical Schools.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>

<header>ON LITHIASIS</header>

...stones. For who does not know that even the dried urine of healthy people leaves behind a <term>dead head</term> <note>original: "caput mortuum"; a term from early chemistry referring to the useless, dry residue left after a liquid has been evaporated or distilled</note>? Therefore, understand that **Paracelsus** indeed frequently dissolved a stone of the bladder by offering **Ludus** <note>original: "Ludo"; a specific mineral remedy described by Paracelsus and Van Helmont, often identified as a type of fossilized stone believed to have the power to dissolve urinary calculi</note>; but he neither preserved the foul-smelling urine, nor likewise dried it out. This was because it would have been too tedious for him. For it pleased the Most High to send a precursor before the **Elias of the Arts** <note>a prophetic figure in alchemical tradition who was expected to arrive and reveal all the hidden secrets of nature and medicine</note>, one teaching the <term>composition</term> <note>original: "crasim"; the specific mixture or balance of components in a medicine</note> and preparations of medicines: to whom, so that the world might trust him, signs were given establishing his doctrine. Illustrious indeed for him is the preparation of great **secrets** <note>original: "arcanorum"; in this context, "arcana" are powerful, hidden medicinal essences</note>, which could only be confirmed through <term>attainment</term> <note>original: "adeptione"; referring to the status of an "Adept," a master who has successfully uncovered the secrets of alchemy</note>. Then followed those who, adding to the discoveries of Paracelsus, were the illustrators of the theory of the truth that had been found, so that at last there might follow one who has attained the Art of Healing, teaching by word and work those things which God announced through those who came before. <margin>26</margin>

Lastly, the **Schools** <note>referring to the traditional university-trained medical establishment of the time</note>, distrusting themselves, have gained credit among the gullible through a new fraud. They have boasted that through the rules of **Diet**, they knew how to dispose the body of a sufferer of the stone so that every preceding phlegmatic mass of the stone—which otherwise, without such institutions of theirs, would immediately and spontaneously depart into clusters of the stone—might be removed by continuous and successive repetition. 

That is: they promise they can snatch away all the <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "pituitam"; one of the four traditional humors, which the "Schools" blamed for forming the sticky material that binds stones together</note> which in any way forms the stone. Indeed, they claim that the phlegm which is required for the necessary elemental composition of the blood (according to the **Pagans** <note>original: "Ethnicos"; a derogatory reference to ancient Greek and Roman physicians like Galen, whose theories the author considers outdated or un-Christian</note>) will be so meager and small that it will scarcely suffice for that necessity of its own, and much less for the exuberance required to create a stone. To this end, they clearly promise certain "masterly" and repeated little purges; they call them "familiar" and "diminishing" for this reason. They promise, moreover, that they will show to the eye that phlegm truly cast down (once their <term>phlegm-drawing medicines</term> <note>original: "phlegmagogis"; specific laxatives or emetics intended to purge phlegm from the body</note> have been taken, of course), and from this, they will soon promise a favorable health to the sick.

<summary>The author critiques both Paracelsus for his lack of thoroughness and the traditional medical "Schools" for their "fraudulent" claims that diet and purges can prevent stones by removing excess phlegm. The text highlights a transition from the early alchemical "precursors" to a more complete understanding of medical science.</summary>

<page-num>135</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

### CHAPTER VII.

...health (at least a palliative one) because of the expelled precursor matter of the stone. In this way, however, the sick become gradually more infirm; they continue to live **medically and miserably**, as long as they submit themselves obediently to such "helpers." But I have already sufficiently responded to such trifles where I removed phlegm and mucus from the list of causes of <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech"; a specific term used by Paracelsus to describe a stony deposit or calculus formed in the human body</note>. <margin>27</margin> Likewise, **Paracelsus** trifles just as foolishly regarding the primary matter of tartar and stone; forgetful of his own previous doctrine, he defects to the "phlegmatic mucus" of the **humorists**—a name he was the first to give them, albeit in mockery. I, however, since this name is now widely accepted, call them such out of compassion rather than irony. Elsewhere, however, the careless **Paracelsus** attacks these ideas again, promising that within fifteen days of the healing of <term>Duelech</term>, its magnitude can be seen if all the daily urine is dried out. This clearly contradicts his previous statements regarding the matter of <term>Tartar</term> <note>original: "Tartari"; in early modern medicine, "tartar" referred to the stony precipitates from food and drink that were thought to cause various diseases</note>: and such a drying out of the urine would be a mere fraud and imposture, if the preceding calculous matter of tartar were excreted along with the dissolved <term>Duelech</term>. For he promises that whatever dissolves <term>Duelech</term> will dissolve much more quickly the precursor material of <term>Tartar</term> which grows daily into a stone. I certainly hate laziness and stupidity in **Healing**, persistence in a **Teacher**, ignorance in a **Professor**, lying in a **Writer**, as well as contradiction in one who aspires to leadership. Since all these things involve deceit and lack of skill in the teacher—if not also malice and ignorant recklessness in such a leader—they render that entire school of thought highly suspicious and stained with many faults. I, at least, from my youth upward, have grieved even to tears for the fate of the sick, who, in uncertain hope and full of trust, confided their life, family, wife and children, and even their fortune and resources...

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s investigation into the treatment of bladder stones. The text begins by completing a sentence from the previous page: "diss-[olving]... to govern the faculties" (facultates gubernandas). He shifts from criticizing Paracelsus's theories to recounting his own failed experiments with herbal and chemical remedies.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>

<header>ON LITHIASIS</header>

...to govern the faculties, through one who dares anything, a boaster. <margin>28</margin> Therefore, I first ran through the vegetable monarchy <note>The "vegetable monarchy" refers to the entire kingdom of plants and herbal medicine.</note>, but I did not find anything that could resolve <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech"; a term coined by Paracelsus to describe a stony deposit or calculus in the body</note> in the bladder. For whatever among them might liquefy <term>Duelech</term> in a glass vessel was either hostile to the body, or at least, because of those very qualities, it did not reach the bladder. If, however, it seemed that it should be injected by a <term>syringe</term> <note>original: "fyringam"; early medical syringes were used to inject fluids directly into the bladder via the urethra</note>, it was not tolerable by the bladder. Therefore, I learned that distilled and decocted vegetables, as well as ashes, limes, powders, and all extracts, were useless and meager against such a great enemy. Some more acidic things indeed reduced the <term>Duelech</term> in its wholeness; but when taken by mouth, they did not enter the bladder with that same power. If injected from the outside, even though they might seem mild like wine, yet in the sensation of pain, they rivaled a glowing hot iron.

I wondered, therefore, at <term>Paracelsus</term> and others, because they recommended distilled liquors made from honey, sugar, dew, etc.; since no mortal ever tolerated those things when injected through a syringe. Indeed, I observed by experiment that a pigeon, by its own acidic stomach ferment, could resolve an excised piece of <term>Duelech</term> into juice, just as it does fragments of brick. Consequently, the inner parts of the stomachs of pigeons and hens were <term>administered</term> <note>original: "Propinata"; specifically meaning "given as a drink" or "prescribed for consumption"</note> by the elders; but truly with a very deceptive hope. This is because the <term>fermental</term> power of the internal organs is extinguished along with the life of the animal. Furthermore, even granted that such a powder might preserve from death that primitive and pristine power which it possessed in life: yet in the "kitchens" of our own digestions, it would reach the bladder entirely stripped of those virtues; although in reality, powders are hardly ever turned into the watery flow of urine. Yet, despite a constant ignorance on both sides, many such things are prescribed to the sick.

Nevertheless, I learned this: that the spirit of salt of <term>Seville</term> <note>original: "Hifpalenfis"; from Hispalis, the Latin name for Seville, Spain. This likely refers to hydrochloric acid produced from Spanish sea salt.</note>, distilled with potter's earth in the extreme fire of a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>A furnace that reflects heat from the roof onto the material being treated, used here for high-heat chemical distillation.</note>, if drunk every morning...

<summary>Van Helmont recounts his failed attempts to find a herbal or chemical "stone-breaker" that can reach the bladder without losing its potency or causing the patient agonizing pain. He critiques the use of animal stomach linings as remedies, arguing that their biological "ferment" dies with the animal.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>
<sig>i 5</sig>

### CHAPTER VII.

[...spirit], taken with white wine drawn from the vessel the day before, not only removes the deadly <term>stranguries</term> <note>original: "strangurias"; a condition characterized by painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination</note> of the elderly and, being entirely diuretic, has healed several people; but furthermore, it has helped those in whom a <term>calculus</term> <note>original: "calculus"; a stone or pebble formed in the body</note> larger than normal, falling from the kidney and remaining in the bladder for several months, was finally diminished and expelled through urination. Previously, however, that same stone had to be pushed back into the bladder with a <term>catheter</term> <note>original: "cathetera"; a long, thin tube used since antiquity to clear blockages in the urinary tract</note> many times whenever it repeatedly entered the neck of the bladder.

It is prepared, moreover, from salt first melted and freed from stray impurities by fire; then, the salt having been crushed and dissolved between slices of radish, and finally dried again, it is distilled with an equal part of potter's clay using a sharp reverberatory fire <note>A furnace designed to reflect heat onto the substance being treated</note> in the proper manner so that nothing escapes, just as I will teach below regarding vitriol. For you have here a balsam of salt, the value of which you can never sufficiently weigh.

In my apprenticeship, however, I had seen the ruins of houses pouring out saltpeter, and that the dust of bricks—once freed from saltpeter and then kept under a roof for several years—would "mature" <note>original: "fracesceret"; literally to rot or become soft, referring to the chemical weathering and transformation of the material</note> and produce saltpeter anew; and thus all the dust, except for the grit, could eventually be turned into salt. I had also seen a nitrous brick, enclosed in the middle of a wider wall, display its saltpeter outwardly through all the stones neighboring it on both sides, and thus it was a destroyer of the surrounding rocks and bricks.

Mindful, therefore, of the name <note>original: "salpetræ"; literally "salt of the rock"</note>, I recognized that this salt is the very brick or rock itself, dissolved. This is especially clear because in the caves of rocks, it drips spontaneously, and elsewhere it hangs down in long icicles. Wherefore I prepared saltpeter in various ways for <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>original: "Lithiasi"; the medical condition of forming stones in the body</note>: but in vain. This was because at that time I was still unaware that <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech"; a term coined by Paracelsus to describe a stony deposit or calculus in the body</note> consists of far different principles than mineral rocks. I likewise saw <term>woodlice</term> <note>original: "millepedes"; often used in historical medicine for their supposed diuretic properties</note> and insects, born and living in nitrous places, applied for use against a bladder stone, but in vain. But after I realized that so many centuries had been dreaming, in...

He concludes that stones formed in the human body (Duelech) are fundamentally different from mineral rocks found in nature.</summary>

...understanding the causes of <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>The formation of stony concretions, such as kidney or bladder stones, in the body</note>; I confidently believed that every error lay within our own ignorance. At last, God, taking pity on the anxieties of the seeker, granted me the knowledge of <term>Ludum</term> <note>original: "Ludum"; a Paracelsian term for a specific mineral or "stone-breaking" substance, often associated with fossils or specific salts</note> with the preparation of Paracelsus. Those who understand me congratulate me in public studies, for they know that I write the truth. Seek, brothers, and to a diligent soul, an immense joy will come. For first, learn to dissolve <term>Duelech</term> <note>original: "Duelech"; a term used by Paracelsus to describe the stony, tartaric matter that forms within the human body</note> in a glass vessel using a lukewarm liquor—one that is not harmful to the stomach or the bladder—so that the Duelech is gradually diminished without bubbles or agitation. Rejoice then, for you are close. Then learn to turn Ludum
<margin>29</margin>
into a salt, without any residue of the transmuter. Moreover, remedies against Duelech should be taken on a fasting stomach, without having eaten supper the night before. But if you are frustrated by a successful internal remedy, let the [medicinal] extract be injected through a <term>catheter</term> <note>A thin tube used to drain the bladder or deliver medicine directly into it</note> at any hour, though the urine should 
<margin>30</margin>
first be discharged. While I was examining this urinary <term>stalagma</term> <note>original: "stalagma"; a drop or a deposit that has settled out of the liquid</note>, the story of a neighboring baker’s daughter came to mind. For he had a little daughter who often urinated blood, and the urine hung in a thread-like consistency. It was pale, with much sediment, and was sticky; she had lived this way into her seventh year. A certain village woman told the baker that the same thing had once happened to her. At last, I ordered the bladder of a bull embryo, not yet born, to be brought (for cows that are not pregnant are hardly ever fattened). That little bladder is usually filled with a liquor whose taste is not indeed like urine, but quite foreign. She drank about two ounces of this every morning, with an equal amount of white wine. She was later married in her 19th year, and in the year 1643, she remains alive and healthy, knowing nothing of stones. This same remedy later helped several poor girls. And since this had been tried several times, the same was
<margin>31</margin>
attempted with a goat embryo, and it succeeded more happily still. I will subjoin in this place my observations concerning the stones of cra-

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>### CHAPTER VII.</header>

...of <term>crabs</term> <note>original: "cancrorum"; referring here to freshwater crayfish, which were the primary source of medicinal "crab's eyes"</note>, which I have never seen noted by anyone else. First of all, they are foolishly called <term>eyes</term> <note>original: "oculi"; these are actually gastroliths, calcium carbonate deposits found in the stomachs of crustaceans before they molt</note>, since they do not perform the function of eyes, nor do they protrude outward, nor
<margin>32</margin>
do they last throughout the whole year. Furthermore, these stones are not an "extracted essence" from the entire shell of the creature (even though <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist whom Van Helmont often critiques despite being influenced by him</note> commanded it to be so), since through many uniform dissections of crabs, I have discovered the following.

First, the stomach of the crab is in its head, near the crown. The males begin to fall ill every year starting from the middle of June; the females then follow in July, before the <term>shedding of their shells</term> <note>original: "exuvium"; the process of molting or ecdysis</note>. For nine days or more, they are as if half-dead and motionless. During that time, a new little membrane is drawn over the outside of their stomach; between this and the old stomach, there is a certain milky fluid. This fluid gradually contracts on both sides into a hollow shape on the convex outer surface of the stomach where it touches, and it immediately <term>turns to stone</term> <note>original: "lapidescit"</note>. 

Meanwhile, neither then nor for a long time afterward does the crab eat anything. Therefore—and this is almost incredible—the old or inner stomach is gradually consumed into a <term>nutritional mucilage</term> <note>original: "mucilaginem alimentariam"; a jelly-like substance the crab uses for food while it cannot eat</note>, and the outer, new second stomach succeeds in the place of the one consumed. Indeed, a thin film is immediately stretched over that milk produced at the curve of the old stomach, in the same manner as usually happens with warm milk. This "milk" grows between the two aforementioned membranes of both stomachs. 

I observed all these things daily with a pleasant wonder, perhaps after dissecting two hundred crabs. Finally, the remaining part of the milk serves the crab as food. Lastly, each stone is also gradually dissolved again and little by little passes into nourishment. The crab eats nothing, nor is anything found in its stomach, for as long as these stones are within its stomach. It lives for about 27 days both from its old stomach, which is gradually consumed, and from the use of <term>refreshing...</term> <note>The text breaks here at the catchword "refo-", likely "refocillamento" meaning refreshment or revival</note>

<meta>catchword: refreshing- [refo-]</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont describes his discovery of how "crab's eyes" (gastroliths) actually form during the molting process, debunking Paracelsus's theories by showing they are temporary calcium deposits used for nutrition and shell regeneration.</summary>

# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES.

...of the stones that have been washed clean. How excellent and powerful this is can hardly be persuaded to anyone except the most expert. Nothing is more suitable for the wounded, or for mothers after childbirth, than the remedy made from these little stones. For it holds a remedy against the harshness of many vegetables that are infamous for their laxative power, provided it is so resolved that both are mixed together down to their smallest parts.

<margin>33</margin> ->Finally, in the **Margraviate of Brandenburg**, there is a most abundant fishing of crabs.<- The transporters, however, are forced to keep watch by night, lest by chance some pig should run, even lightly, beneath the wagon. For if that should happen, in the morning, as many crabs as were in the wagon are found dead. So fatal is the pig to the crab <note>This reflects 17th-century folk belief regarding the biological antipathy between certain animals</note>.

<margin>34</margin> To the end that any substance may be injected into the bladder without pain, I have invented a new **catheter**. Because the silver "little horn" with which <term>surgeons</term> <note>original: "Chyrurgi"; in this era, surgeons were often craft-trained individuals who performed manual operations, distinct from academic physicians</note> extract urine with the greatest of tortures, is cruel and bloody, and therefore entirely displeased me. Among the many types I have tested, the most suitable and harmless one I found was one made of thin leather. Indeed, on this leather, or fine suede, I painted the inside with a white color made from <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "cerussa"; a lead-based pigment common in the 1600s, though now known to be toxic</note> and linseed oil. When it was nearly dry, I ordered a tube to be fashioned from it by sewing, into which a brass wire was inserted along its length; its seam was flat so that it would not protrude at all. At one end of this tube, the opening is wide enough so that the nozzle of a syringe may be inserted whenever desired, and both fit together perfectly, so that by this path a liquid can be injected into the bladder. Furthermore, the entire leather tube is reinforced on the outside with diluted glue, so that once this has dried, it may then be painted with some color and linseed oil. This is done for the greater strength of the tube, and also so that it does not become soaked through by the liquid being injected, and
