<page-num>141</page-num>

...let it be added. Therefore, once the brass wire is drawn out, another substitute should enter its place, prepared from the gum of a **Whale** <note>original: "gingiva Balænæ"; referring here to baleen or whalebone, prized for its flexibility and strength</note>. Thus you have a thin, flexible tube, which causes no pain upon insertion, even if it is pushed into the bladder forty times in a single day. In the first few instances, it may hurt around the unaccustomed sphincter, but soon the fear of that contraction ceases.

Urine <note>original: "lotium"</note> is drawn off as often as desired. And once the bladder is emptied, whatever one wishes can finally be injected into the bladder through a syringe fitted to the back of the tube. Provided the liquid to be introduced is painless and not unpleasant. Now, the Syringe is the instrument to whose siphon I said the protruding end of the Catheter should be adapted. Eternal praise be to God in the highest, and may it please Him to bedew and make fruitful my services and desires offered for the help of mortals!

### ON STONE-FORMING DISEASE

...men, seeking clarification regarding the remedies presented there. Indeed, they confess to recognizing that within the boldness of my promise, the truth of the remedies for any fevers whatsoever lies beneath: but they grieve at the excessive obscurity of my writing. First, therefore, they inquire: what is <term>Horizontal Gold</term> <note>original: "Aurum horizontale"; likely a specific alchemical gold preparation intended for medicinal use</note>? Second, they ask for the composition of the element <term>Fire of Venus</term> <note>original: "ignis Veneris"; Venus is the alchemical symbol for copper, implying a potent medicine derived from that metal</note>. Third: could it perhaps be <term>Rectified Spirit of Vitriol</term> <note>original: "spiritus Vitrioli rectificatus"; vitriol refers to sulfuric acid or sulfates, "rectified" meaning purified through distillation</note>? Some even add threats: unless I satisfy their wishes publicly, my book on fevers is to be prohibited, like another *Prince* by <term>Machiavelli</term> <note>The author compares the potential banning of his medical work to the controversial political treatise by Niccolò Machiavelli</note>. This is because, if my book stands, the <term>Medical Academies</term> <note>The traditional university-based medical schools of the 17th century</note> would otherwise necessarily fall into ruins, and <term>Galen</term> <note>The 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories on the four humors dominated European medicine for 1,500 years</note> would soon be driven into exile, begging for alms from door to door. They do indeed think well of the things I have brought forward for the sake of compassion for my fellow man—concerning the unheard-of doctrine of fevers and the detestable abuses of <term>Bloodletting</term> <note>The common practice of "bleeding" patients to balance humors</note>, purges, and remedies—but they say that explanations are lacking and I must speak more openly. They wish that, even if I remain silent, all these things might be confirmed for the future through the experiences of others and the acclaim of the wretched, as if by a sealed decree.

<margin>2</margin> I first responded that the secret of the <term>Liquor Alkahest</term> <note>The "universal solvent" of Paracelsian alchemy, believed to be able to dissolve any substance into its primary components</note> of <term>Paracelsus</term> stands in the way; the Almighty teacher has surely decreed that He shall remain its dispenser until the confusion of the world’s end, for reasons known, in part, to the <term>Adepts</term> <note>The "Adepti" were individuals believed to have attained the ultimate secrets of alchemy</note>. Therefore, I shall leave the manifestation of that <term>Secret</term> <note>original: "Arcanum"</note> to the treasures of divine goodwill. As for the judgments to be held about me in the meantime, I care <margin>3</margin> little. For I will not be the first, nor the last, to be a victim of their reproach; I have never looked to the world’s censures regarding myself, nor do I value my evaluators based on their status (as many others do). For I love all equally in God: and I love no one at all simply because they flatter me. For I know that I have God as my protector, who deserts...

### C H A P T E R VIII.

...no one who invokes him. Indeed, the snares of tribulations rained upon my head. I stood firm, for they did not in any way crush my soul. They fell to the earth; I trampled them with contempt, and immediately, like dung, they rotted of their own accord. Their authors, however, were disturbed and shamed. May God forgive them! I know, finally, that God, with heavenly dew, will cherish the seeds which He has planted, and which He willed to grow. Nor did He allow me to be anxious for the whim of the world, the consensus of the <term>Schools</term> <note>The traditional medical universities and academic authorities of the 17th century</note>, or the acclaim of the common people. For He Himself can do all things that He wills, and shall do them according to His pleasure, until the World shall deserve to be consoled in its illnesses through true Medicine. Ah, how like a swollen bubble is ambition, which, always suspended by the judgments of others, hangs there? How poorly, finally, do the judgments of others always judge? Especially those which are ruled by continuous prejudice?

<margin>4</margin>
Regarding the proposed questions, I say: That just as the Sun is considered to be born on the horizon of the hemisphere, so <term>Mercury</term>, once it has been made <term>Diaphoretic</term> <note>original: "Diaphoreticus"; a substance that induces sweating to expel toxins or break a fever</note>, sweet as honey, and fixed in the likeness of gold, is gold on its own horizon. In medical matters, it is as much nobler than gold as an oriental pearl is to a Scottish one. For as long as <term>Mercury</term> remains in its metallic state, it is similar to and very near the "first entity" <note>The primary, unrefined state of matter in alchemical theory</note> of metals. But when it is melted with gold, all its medicinal power is enclosed and sealed; indeed, it is turned inward so that it denies the dowry which it owes to human nature for its illnesses. For the <term>Sulfur</term> of Venus <note>original: "Sulfur Veneris"; Venus is the alchemical symbol for copper. "Sulfur" here refers to the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of the metal</note>, after its separation from its own body and rising again, becomes a sort of "glorious sulfur." Thus, it immediately tints the <term>Sulfur</term> of <term>Mercury</term> (which in the powder of <term>John de Vigo</term> <note>original: "Joannis de Vigo"; an influential 16th-century surgeon known for his "red precipitate" mercury powder used to treat wounds</note> is turned outward through corrosive mineral sulfurs); they embrace each other in an inseparable marriage-bed. And so the power of both sulfurs remains on the surface. Hence, by the joining of their powers, <term>Diaphoretic Mercury</term>...

# ON THE STONE DISEASE
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"; Lithiasis refers to the formation of stones (calculi) in the kidneys or bladder.</note>

->...resulting from this, it completes a harmony of healing in all things that both a **Physician** <note>original: "Physicus"; a doctor of internal medicine</note> and a **Surgeon** could wish for: whether it is applied in respect to acute diseases, or finally in relation to chronic ones.<-

Therefore, the **Fire of Venus** <note>original: "ignis Veneris"; in alchemy, "Venus" is the name for Copper</note> is not the spirit of vitriol <note>original: "spiritus vitrioli"; an early name for sulfuric acid</note>, however exquisitely rectified; rather, that fire is the **volatile sulfur of copper**, in the form of a green oil, sweeter than honey, and completely separated from the mercurial body of its own copper. The remaining copper, however, stays white, and never turns green with verdigris, so that it is no longer numbered among the **seven metals** <note>The traditional seven metals known since antiquity: gold, silver, mercury, copper, iron, tin, and lead</note>. This is because it has turned into a new and anonymous metal. 

But the **Fire of Venus** cannot be obtained except with the full destruction of copper and the volatilization of the mercurial body of copper itself. 
<margin>•</margin> And no matter how volatile it becomes in the form of oil, it is nevertheless easily reduced afterward back into an unknown white metal, which can be extended under the hammer. But the Fire, or **Sulfur of Venus**, is no longer reduced into metal by itself. For just as no sulfur is a metal, so every metallic **Mercury** <note>In alchemical theory, Mercury was the "mother" or liquid principle of all metals, not just the liquid metal we know today</note> is a true metal. 

The <term>Adepts</term> <note>original: "Adepti"; those who have mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy</note> teach, moreover, that the 
<margin>6</margin> sulfurous part of a metal cannot be separated from its mercurial and metallic body except through its total destruction, which they therefore (albeit incorrectly) call "elemental." This is because, of course, there are two sulfurs in metals. One they rightly call the **external**, and the other the **internal**. 

In the proposed case of copper, consider that **internal** sulfur which fixes or coagulates the body into a white, anonymous, and mercurial metal, making it ductile <note>capable of being hammered thin without breaking</note> under the hammer; whereas otherwise, Mercury without sulfur could never be coagulated into a metal. But let the **external** sulfur of copper be that sweet green oil, which, once separated, cannot be forced back into a metal. Accordingly, the **Fellow-Initiates** <gloss>mystæ</gloss> <note>original: "Philosophiæ Symmystae"; those who share the secrets of alchemical philosophy</note> of this Philosophy unanimously testify—

### CHAPTER VIII.

...of mixed substances, the external sulfur cannot be separated from its own body—not even by fire—in imperfect metals <note>Imperfect metals are those like lead, tin, copper, and iron, which were believed to corrode or "burn" in fire, unlike the "perfect" metals, gold and silver.</note>, without its **Mercurial** part perishing equally at the same time. 
<margin>7</margin> For I saw **lead** <note>original: "plumbum"</note>, after being sublimated three times, return to the exact same lead, in number, as it was before. Therefore, since that external sulfur (of the kind drawn from copper) is not necessary for a perfect metal: <margin>8</margin> but because that sulfur was added by God to **Copper** <note>original: "Veneri"; Venus is the alchemical name for copper</note> for the sake of **Venus**: it is therefore necessary that this sulfur of Venus has its own purposes, serving the needs of ungrateful man—specifically for human infirmities—ranking above all dignity of metallic perfection. The **Stoics** <note>Ancient philosophers who believed the universe was designed by a rational, divine providence for the benefit of humanity.</note> themselves conjectured that all the world's created things were directed toward this very use. 

<margin>9</margin> Therefore, the writers of **Chemical Primers** <note>original: "Tyrocinii chymici Scriptores"; likely a reference to authors of introductory textbooks like Jean Beguin’s *Tyrocinium Chymicum* (1610).</note> are mistaken, as many as lie with various fables about the **metamorphosis of Mercury** into salt, water, or oil for various medicinal uses. They even dare to stabilize their inventions with this argument: "If indeed **gold**, the most constant of bodies, can vanish into vitriol <note>A sulfate mineral or the acid derived from it.</note>, and thus even fly away into smoke, why should **Mercury** not do the same much more freely?" 

<margin>10</margin> But I, in responding, will propose at the threshold two opinions of the Philosophers, which are by far the truest, yet shameful for these arguers: so that from here even those unskilled in **Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiæ"</note> may be able to point a finger at the vanity of that argument, and so that the authors of the *Primers*, covered in shame, may come to their senses. The first of these is: 

**That it is far easier to construct gold from non-gold, than it is to destroy natural gold.** 

Let those be ashamed first, therefore, who teach the destruction of gold, yet testify by their poverty that they do not know how to construct it! Therefore, either the **Adept Philosophers** <note>The "Adepts" were those who claimed to have successfully discovered the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> lie and are deceived, or the writers of the *Primers* are. 

The second is: If I had not seen **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; liquid mercury</note> elude every effort of the craftsmen, to such an extent that it either flies away entirely whole, or entirely in the fire...

<summary>The author argues that the sulfur found in copper is a divine gift for medicine and refutes textbook claims that gold can be easily destroyed or mercury permanently transformed, citing the fundamental alchemical principle that destroying gold is harder than making it.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, sulfur of copper, Venus, lead sublimation, chemical primers, transmutability of gold, mercury, Stoic philosophy, medicinal metals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of contemporary chemical textbooks. He argues that the fundamental "identity" of metals—specifically gold and mercury—cannot be permanently altered or destroyed by chemical processes, even when they appear to change form.</meta>

# ON THE STONE
<note>original: "DE LITHIASI"; this treatise discusses the formation of "stones" or calculi in the human body, using alchemical principles to explain their origin.</note>

...remains, and in either way, preserves its immutable and primitive identity, and the proportionate homogeneity of that identity. I would say that the "art" <note>Alchemy was often referred to simply as "The Art."</note> is not true—which in fact is true, without lie, and by far most true. So much so, that **what is above is like what is below, and vice versa.** <note>This is a famous quote from the "Emerald Tablet," a foundational text of Hermetic philosophy and alchemy, suggesting a correspondence between the celestial/spiritual world and the material world.</note> Therefore, those who teach the aforementioned metamorphoses of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"; also known as quicksilver.</note> and **gold** reveal themselves to be ignorant of metallic matters. For however much these metals—through certain minerals joined to them—are driven through a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</note> in the form of oil, salt, or sulfur, and assume the "mask" of these things: yet when these additions are removed, the gold always remains the same, and the Mercury is the same as it was before, and they return to their original bodies. Indeed, even if gold allowed itself to be radically separated into heterogeneous parts—namely salt, sulfur, and Mercury (which is in no way possible for nature, except through the single **constructive liquor** <note>likely a reference to the "Alkahest," a hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any substance to its first matter</note>), this remains impossible for nature and art regarding the homogeneity of Mercury. For Mercury is simpler than gold, constructed with a greater and more proportionate identity. Because in Mercury, there is no diversity to be found, such as exists between the "tincture" <note>the red, "soul-like" quality of gold</note> of gold and its whiteness. I have already distinguished this regarding the sulfur and mercury of **Copper** <note>original: "Cupri"</note>. For even if the mercury of copper is made entirely volatile, because it is not thereby stripped of its internal sulfur, <margin>11</margin> it is therefore reduced again into a white and malleable metal.

Furthermore, regarding the question they ask—whether the <term>fire of Venus</term> <note>original: "ignis Veneris"; a chemical preparation, usually an acidic spirit or "oil" derived from copper or its vitriol</note> is rectified spirit of vitriol—I will reveal certain things about the nature of vitriol and its distillation that have been handed down by no one before me. Truly, nature produced a certain acidic mineral salt, which the Greeks call <term>Chalcantum</term> <note>original: "Chalcantum"; specifically blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note>, and which the Latins call by the inept name <margin>12</margin> <term>Shoemaker's ink</term> <note>original: "Atramentum sutorium"; iron or copper sulfates were used to make black dyes for leather</note>. Chemists, however, call it <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "vitriolum"; a group of glassy-looking metal sulfates</note>, because it is translucent like glass. This salt is composed of an embryonic sulfur, imma-
<note>catchword: "imma-" for "immaturi" (immature)</note>

<summary>The author argues that Mercury and Gold have an immutable identity that resists permanent chemical transformation, and begins an explanation of the nature of vitriol (sulfates).</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII.

...an immature birth, which, while it licks the vein of copper, corrodes it, and is therefore called *cuperosum* <note>original: "cuperosum"; a term for copperas or vitriol, derived here by the author from "corroded copper."</note>, or "corroded copper." If, however, it has gnawed a vein of iron, or other minerals, it produces acidic and diverse springs, according to the nature of the eroded vein. This is a subject I have followed at length in my little book on the springs of Spa <note>original: "fontibus Spadanis"; referring to the famous mineral springs in Belgium known for their healing properties.</note>.

<margin>13</margin>
Furthermore, whether that water—which contains within itself the salt of embryonic sulfur <note>Sulfur in a formative or crude state, not yet a "mature" metal.</note> and carries with it the eroded vein of copper—drips out by itself and is boiled by fire into the consistency of vitriol <note>A term for sulfate minerals, often appearing as glassy crystals.</note>, or whether it finally coagulates elsewhere of its own accord: this in no way distinguishes its type or its quality. Indeed, nothing of its essence is lost by boiling. For once the watery liquid has sufficiently evaporated through boiling, the remainder then finally coagulates of its own accord in the cold. Only the price of the vitriol varies based on the diversity of the veins from which it came.

For near Antwerp, while sulfur is melted out of **Pyrite**, the remainder is exposed to the open air and, for the most part, gradually liquefies. For through the burning and the soot of the sulfur, it conceives a verdigris <note>a green pigment or crust</note>, which is gnawed by the remaining salt and flows down into pits via the rain. Thus also the neighboring Eburones <note>An ancient people of the Gallia Belgica region, near modern-day Liège and Spa.</note> prepare their vitriol from a richer vein. Elsewhere, indeed, the vein of copper itself is rich with the coagulated salt of sulfur, and it drips, flows down, and coagulates spontaneously—which would otherwise be diluted by the moisture of a nearby spring. The difference in quality, therefore, consists in the purity of the salt:

<margin>14</margin>
not, however, in the abundance and saturation of copper (for I speak not as a merchant, but as a Physician). It differs also because of the mixture of some foreign substance. If, for instance, a vein of lead is mixed into the Pyrite or the vein of copper (which is frequently encountered), or perhaps if there is a malignant participation of **Arsenic** (for Arsenic, because it usually lies in wait for and accompanies metals, is therefore called by the customary name, "smoke of metals" <note>original: "fumus metallorum"; arsenic was called this because it often appears as a vapor or "smoke" during the smelting of metallic ores.</note>). And so, what otherwise would be a legitimate vi- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with "triol."</note>

...triol, it becomes harmful for healing. Furthermore, the azure <note>deep blue</note> color of vitriol is generally preferred over the green: perhaps because it is more pleasing to the eye. Indeed, with very little effort, those who trade in fraudulent simple medicines <note>a "simple" is a medicinal herb or mineral used on its own</note> forge the azure variety out of green vitriol. <margin>15</margin>

<margin>16</margin>

Some, therefore, sprinkle sulfur upon glowing or molten copper, and with great labor they thus draw out its verdigris <note>a green crust or "rust" on copper</note>, and so on. Paracelsus, however, prepares the best vitriol for healing by "cementing" <note>a process of heating solids together</note> plates of copper layered with common salt and sulfur. Recent practitioners, led astray by this, repeatedly distill plates of copper with the spirit of sulfur or common vitriol until they are completely black and brittle; these finally melt in water, which becomes blue, and by boiling it is thickened, and in the cold it congeals into vitriol. This is certainly how that which is sold as **Cyprian** <note>vitriol from Cyprus was historically considered the highest quality</note> is adulterated today. With all due respect to **Paracelsus**, I know and have discovered that vitriol made from **Venus** <note>the alchemical name for copper</note> is far more sluggish in healing than the common variety, which is free from the suspicion of being a manufactured mixture. Furthermore, its spirit is nothing but pure mineral vinegar, deprived of the outflow <note>original: "aporrheâ"; a Greek term for an emanation or discharge</note> of copper sulfur. I have found, in fact, that vitriol made from **Venus** is far poorer than the fossil <note>natural or mined</note> variety. Likewise, the fossil variety that contains the most copper is slower in healing 
<margin>17</margin>
and in distilling than the common type. For I have distilled vitriol prepared by art, azure in color and in no way distinguishable by sight from **Cyprian** vitriol: it yielded only a little sluggish acidic spirit, and soon ceased...

## CHAPTER VIII.

Within a few hours every spirit <note>referring to volatile vapors or gasses</note> exhaled; and the whole remaining body, condensed into a black dreg, remained, and yielded back to me its copper as desired. For indeed, copper is already a complete metal, not easily destructible, nor receding back to its own first principles <note>the basic elemental building blocks in alchemy</note>. Thus, although it may be diminished by the ferocity of fire, nevertheless whatever of it has evaporated is still truly metal, for the reasons mentioned above. Indeed, among metallic veins, none is brought to metallic perfection with as much difficulty as copper itself (as **Georg Agricola** <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555), the German "Father of Mineralogy" and author of the famous mining text *De Re Metallica*</note> testifies), for it requires being smelted at least nine times before copper emerges from it. Whereas, meanwhile, the veins of other metals pour out their treasure at the first melting. Therefore, the vein of copper acquires its perfection through the separation of parts mixed with it from its birth. 

These parts are those which are still bound to its <term>first being</term> <note>original: "primo enti"; the raw, primordial state of a substance before it becomes a specific metal</note>: wherefore copper, once perfected, rejects and resists most stubbornly the dissolution of its body. Consequently, a perfect **medicinal vitriol** <note>a copper-based sulfate used in healing</note> cannot be obtained from it which possesses the power of the **Sulfur of Venus** <note>original: "sulfure Veneris"; in alchemy, Venus is the symbol for copper, and its "sulfur" is its active medicinal or combustible principle</note>. For this is not separable from the copper except through the total destruction of the metallic body, as I have taught before.

<margin>18</margin> Therefore, those parts of the copper vein which are far removed from the metallic nature and closer to the <term>first being</term> grant to the vitriol a medicinal power that is denied to manufactured <term>chalcantum</term> <note>original: "chalcantho"; blue vitriol or copper sulfate, often produced artificially for industrial use</note>. For common and low-grade vitriol breathes out its exhalations for at least eight full days, no matter how much it is pressed by the most blazing flames of a **reverberatory furnace** <note>a furnace that reflects or "reverberates" heat onto the material being processed</note>. Therefore, the freer a vein is from foreign impurity, and the closer it is to the **first being of Venus**, and the more distant it is from the metallic constitution of copper, the more blameless a vitriol its salt produces—naturally born and dissolved within it—and the richer the spirits it supplies, and the more fit for healing...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for extracting medicinal substances from minerals. The previous page concluded with a discussion on the "Sulfur of Venus" (copper) and its resistance to dissolution; this page details the specific, "unusual" distillation process required to break down vitriol.</meta>

# ON THE STONE DISEASE <note>Latin: *De Lithiasi*. This refers to the formation of stones in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones, which the author aims to treat with these chemical preparations.</note>

19. However, the unusual method of distillation is this.

Take **common vitriol** <term>vitriolum commune</term> <note>Sulfate minerals, usually of iron or copper, used in alchemy and medicine.</note>, not suspected of any foreign corruption; let it melt by boiling in a wide jar, and let it be cooked until it reaches dryness. Once the jar is broken, the vitriol, hardened like a stone, should be ground into a powder. The distillation should be performed with at least 6 **retorts** <term>retortis</term> <note>Glass vessels with long, downward-pointing necks used for distilling liquids.</note> at once. And let the **Retorts** be made of glass. For all stone vessels are porous: because all earth retains pores; for this reason, after drying, it necessarily breathes out something that is not fixed. Furthermore, the retorts should be coated <note>original: "loricentur"; a process called "luting" where a protective layer of clay or paste is applied to glassware to protect it from high heat</note> with a crust that neither gapes open, nor develops cracks, nor falls off on its own, but becomes thoroughly glass-like. It is also best if the protruding neck of the retort is connected to a wide receiving vessel, so that not even the slightest bit of vapor can escape. The receiver, moreover, should be placed in wet sand; likewise, its curved part should be covered with a bag half-filled with wet sand: which bag should be renewed several times, having been dipped in very cold water. 

Half of the retort should be filled with the powdered vitriol. Distill by degrees of heat, and finally let it be urged by charcoal as much as possible in a **wind furnace** <term>furno venti</term> <note>A furnace designed to create a strong draft of air to reach the very high temperatures needed for smelting or specialized distillation.</note>, which is blown by its own grate. When the wind furnace has ceased to send spirit into the receiving vessel, let a side portal be opened, through which path the **reverberation** <term>reverberium</term> <note>A "reverberatory" fire where the flame is deflected or reflected down onto the material being heated, rather than heating it directly.</note> of the flame from wood may penetrate beneath the retort; let this continue for 5 or 6 nights at the highest heat possible for nature. The retort will perhaps seem to you to melt in such a storm of fire: but nevertheless it will endure steadily: because the exterior earthen coating holds the glass together and absorbs it: and thus it becomes glass-coated as much as is sufficient for the work. Finally, remember to separate the receiving vessel from the neck of the retort while the fire is still most burning. Otherwise, in a cooler state, you will see the spirits return into the dregs, which spit them out. Then at last take the **Colcothar** <term>Colcotar</term> <note>The red oxide of iron (or sometimes copper) residue left over after the distillation of vitriol.</note>, or the dregs remaining from the distillation, which comes from true **Cyprian vitriol** <term>vitriolo Cyprico</term> <note>Blue vitriol or copper sulfate, historically sourced from Cyprus.</note>, or Hungarian vitriol, or at least...

<summary>This page describes a rigorous, multi-day chemical distillation process using glass retorts protected by earthen coatings to extract "spirits" from vitriol. The method emphasizes extreme heat and careful cooling to prevent the loss of volatile medicinal components.</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII.

...[from] the **Goslaric** [vitriol], you have reserved. That residual dregs, mixed with sulfur, should be burned again until the sulfur is completely consumed. This you will then sprinkle and moisten with the aforementioned spirit. For as it is immediately absorbed; in a pan or a glass **cucurbit** <term>cucurbitâ vitreâ</term> <note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation.</note>: so when repeated from there, it yields nothing but an aqueous and useless **phlegm** <note>In alchemy, "phlegm" refers to the tasteless, watery part of a distillate that lacks medicinal or chemical power.</note>, with the absorbed spirit remaining in the **colcotar** <term>colcotare</term> <note>The red or brownish residue—usually iron oxide—remaining after the distillation of vitriol.</note>. Renew this repeatedly six or seven times, until at last the poured-on spirit turns red and floats upon the **Colcotar**. This is the sign that the saturation of the absorption must cease. Therefore, let this rich **Colcotar**, well dried, be placed in a **Retort**, and let this rich **Colcotar** be distilled until the very last spirits, now turning yellowish and smelling with the pleasant odor of honey. <margin>20</margin> Remember, however, that the receiving vessel must be removed from the retort while it is still glowing hot, and the mouth of a stronger flask must be stopped with wax to preserve this spirit. If you then pour water into it, the vessel itself immediately shatters. Therefore, this second spirit is restrained only by the spirit of the first distillation. Scarcely a single pound of this is poured from flask to flask without at least a single ounce being lost. Likewise, unless the receiver is removed from the retort in a timely manner, as I said: you will see the most powerful spirit, once the furnace has cooled, return into the **Colcotar** from which it was driven out by the fire.

<margin>21</margin> Furthermore, the dregs of the **Colcotar** remaining from the second distillation are still entirely copper-like and turn green in many ways. From which:

1. It is established, as I previously warned, that the **fire of Venus** <note>original: "ignem Veneris"; Venus is the alchemical name for copper. Its "fire" refers to its essential active principle.</note> is only drawn forth and obtained through the total destruction and separation of the metal. 
2. Therefore, this must be done by a far more hidden path. 
3. That vitriol rich in copper is less suited for distillation than common vitriol. 
4. That copper vitriol pours out a spirit of the **mineral salt of vinegar** <note>Likely referring to a potent acidic distillate</note>, but not a volatile liquid of copper. 
5. And therefore, the **sulfur of Venus** <note>copper sulfur</note> may rightly be called the **sulfur of the Philosophers** <term>sulfur Philosophorum</term> <note>A purified, active principle sought by alchemists for use in the "Great Work" of transmutation and medicine.</note>,

The author emphasizes the volatile nature of this substance and concludes that the true medicinal "sulfur" of copper can only be obtained through the total chemical breakdown of the metal.</summary>

For although certain brute animals generate small stones within themselves, yet those stones are not born from the causes of **Duelech**, nor are they destined for them as punishment or tribulations: but rather,

### CHAPTER VIII.

...more, brought forth for human benefit. Therefore, if **Duelech** <term>Duelech</term> <note>A term coined by the physician Paracelsus to describe the stony, "tartaric" deposits formed in the body, such as kidney stones.</note> carries the blame for sin—and sin indeed drew its origin from a tree—it also seemed to me that the preservation of health in **Lithiasis** <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>The medical condition of forming stones in the kidneys or bladder.</note> should not only be expected from the seed of **Daucus** <term>Daucus</term> <note>Wild carrot (Daucus carota), traditionally used as a diuretic.</note> and other similar herbs, but from some kind of wood. For this reason, while it is true that **Kidney Wood** <note>original: "lignum Nephriticum"; a wood from the Americas used in early modern medicine to treat kidney ailments.</note> has recently been brought to us from India, I have never been convinced that Divine Goodness would deny Europeans a remedy for so long, or that he would fail to help even a poor man suffering from the stone until, after three thousand years, a remedy finally flew here from the Indians at great expense—a remedy which is otherwise quite sluggish in itself. **Daucus** does indeed preserve health, but only under a continuous and strict obligation, just as **Aroph** <term>Aroph</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for a lithontriptic (stone-dissolving) medicine, often involving saffron or distilled spirits.</note> strengthens the kidneys at great cost. Therefore, I inquired seriously whether there might not be some wood familiar to our own people which might take the place of the **Kidney Wood** finally sent to us by the barbarians? For if the wood of sin and the wood of life were trees, and not shrubs, and much less herbs. For this reason, I once observed that it was a local custom among the princes of Germany <margin>24</margin> that, to combat **Lithiasis**, they would drink a daily draught of the liquid from the bark of a wounded **Birch tree** <note>original: "Betula"</note> every year in May. They preserved this liquid from the corruption of the air by pouring olive oil over it. The tree is called *Bircken-baum* by the Germans, and *Berken-boom* by our people. For the bark of the **Birch** is wounded near the ground, on the trunk, in the month of March, around the time when the wounded **chaste tree** <note>original: "vitex"; likely referring here to the grapevine (vitis) or a similar woody plant that "weeps" sap when pruned in spring.</note> is accustomed to weep new liquid drawn from the earth. But that liquid of the **Birch** is entirely watery and almost tasteless. However, if some branch, three fingers thick, is wounded to its midpoint and filled with inserted wool, it immediately weeps a liquid that is not unpleasant, but rather slightly acidic, which comforts those afflicted even in the very torment of **Lithiasis**, in three [days?]

<summary>The author argues that since the stone disease (Duelech) is linked to original sin (the tree), its cure must be found in a tree rather than an herb. He rejects expensive Indian imports in favor of local European Birch sap, describing how to harvest it for treating kidney stones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into finding a local European botanical remedy for kidney stones (lithiasis). Having previously suggested that a common wood might hold the cure, he now focuses his experiments on the Birch tree (Betula) and the properties of its sap and bark.</meta>

# ON LITHIASI <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>The medical condition of forming stones in the kidneys or bladder.</note>

...or taken in four spoonfuls. Therefore, the liquid that flows from the branches above is purer <note>original: "meracior"; meaning more concentrated or less diluted.</note> than that which flows from the trunk below. However, the liquid that flows out near the ground is clearly watery. For I immediately considered that this happens because, as it ascends, it passes through the reddish inner bark, which acts as the **liver of the tree**. But since that inner bark was without any notable taste throughout the year, whereas the outermost bark—which is white and membrane-like—possessed a flavor and fragrance like the finest **turpentine** <term>therebintinæ</term> <note>A resinous sap from trees, highly valued in early medicine for its aromatic and healing properties.</note>, I stripped away the exterior bark for about half a foot all around the trunk. I observed that, nonetheless, the liquid dripping from the branches retained the same taste as before.

I wondered, therefore, from where this diversity of liquids in one and the same tree could flow. Furthermore, I marveled that a single small branch could easily "weep" eight or ten pounds of liquid in a single day—a branch which otherwise does not require so much nourishment throughout the entire summer. Nor is there a place within it where such a vast quantity of liquid could be stored. Much less does the root grant ten times that amount of liquid to each of the other individual branches; yet, the other branches do not lack sufficient nourishment, even though the root would have had to draw up that massive quantity of liquid and distribute it through those several other branches.

I considered, therefore, that this liquid was similar to **synovia** <term>synovia</term> <note>In this period, the term referred generally to a clear, fluid discharge or "joint-water," here compared to the tree's sap leaking from a "wound."</note> flowing from a wound; indeed, I began to loathe it, as if it contained within itself the contagion of death or rot, and could not provide health if it already bore a defect in its own integrity. However, I discovered that both the wood of the **Birch** <term>Betulæ</term> itself and its red inner bark were devoid of healing power. The white bark, which is like parchment and grows on the outside of the younger branches, is easily flammable and marked by a distinct taste of turpentine, yet it hardly releases its medicinal virtue into a decoction <note>A medicinal preparation made by boiling plant material in water.</note>. I thought, therefore, that the aforementioned power of the liquid did not proceed from the root...

<meta>The text continues on the next page, likely explaining the author's theory on the origin of the sap's medicinal power.</meta>

<summary>The author describes botanical experiments on the Birch tree, observing that its sap varies in purity depending on where it is tapped and noting the mysterious volume of liquid a single branch can produce. He concludes that while the white bark smells of medicine, the healing power against stones likely resides in the sap itself rather than the wood or roots.</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII.

...neither from the wood, nor finally from the reddish bark, nor even from the white bark, since that was not continuous everywhere upon the trunk. I attempted, therefore, to distill that bark, both by itself and with the addition of lixivium of tartar <term>lye of tartar</term> <note>original: "lixivii tartari"; a solution of potassium carbonate used in early chemistry to extract oils or resins.</note>: but truly, the liquid dripped from the wound of a branch far excelled the oil and the distillation of the barks. Thus, I was led to acknowledge that the liquid flowing so abundantly of its own accord from the wounded branches is a pure **balsam for stone-disease** <term>Lithiasis balsamum</term> <note>original: "Lithiasis balsamum"; Lithiasis refers to the formation of stony concretions (stones) in the kidneys or bladder.</note>; and it does not hinder the matter that my own insolence <note>original: "petulantia"; the author refers to his previous skepticism or "rude" comparison of the sap to a bodily discharge.</note> compared it to water flowing from a wound or an ulcer. For whereas a wound or ulcer brings or promises death to us, the **Birch** <term>Betula</term> suffers no such thing. Therefore, that liquid of the Birch is a medicine promised by nature, drawn forth through wounds; and thus it is a matter of urging nature to produce its own natural balsam, which it would otherwise never offer. For this reason, I ordered that the tender, blackish twigs (from which our countrymen make brooms and rods), which had swelling buds but were not yet leafy, should be crushed with a hammer upon a stone or anvil and boiled in the water intended for brewing **beer** <term>cerevisia</term>. If I later added **carrot seed** <term>Dauci semen</term> <note>The seeds of the Wild Carrot (Daucus carota) were widely used as a traditional diuretic to help expel stones.</note> or **brooklime** <term>becapungam</term> <note>A semi-aquatic plant (*Veronica beccabunga*) used in historical medicine to treat scurvy and urinary disorders.</note> to this beer, I obtained the desired effects for the prevention of **stone-disease** <term>Lithiasis</term>. These were even more powerful if that **Martial liquid** <note>original: "liquor Martialis." While "Martial" often refers to iron in alchemy, here it likely refers to the sap collected in the month of March (*Martius*), when the flow is strongest.</note> collected from the higher branches were infused into the beer after the greatest part of its fermentation <note>original: "ebullitionis"; literally "boiling," but referring to the bubbling and foaming of fermenting beer in the barrel.</note>—which wines and beers undergo naturally in casks—had subsided.
<margin>25</margin> For I have found, above all, that a drink of this kind made from the **Birch** removes the fear of **diuretics** <term>diureticorum</term> <note>Medicines that promote the production of urine. Some historical diuretics were considered dangerous or overly harsh.</note>. It does this because it relaxes the pains of **stone-disease** and its contractions, both in the loins and the intestines (for a single stone often stirs up colicky pains, no less than if the source of the pain were inside the intestines themselves). Likewise, it heals **difficulty in urinating** <term>dysuriis</term> and **painful urination** <term>stranguriis</term>, even in the elderly. Heat...

...and it likewise mitigates, and then removes, pain <note>The Latin text begins with "lorem," likely the tail end of "dolorem" (pain) from the previous page's catchword "atten-" (possibly from "attentat dolorem" or similar).</note> of the liver, just as if it were arising from a driven-in thorn. Afterwards, when quarrels arose between the betrothed, the bride said she had hidden the misdeed with the maidservant so that she might explore whether he was "cold" <note>Impotent.</note> or indeed bewitched <note>original: "maleficiatus"; under the influence of a "maleficium" or harmful spell.</note>, and on what grounds she might attempt a divorce. At last, the incantation of Binding was dissolved by drinking the aforementioned beer, and he was found to have been bewitched, but not "cold." 

Furthermore, a certain man, who by habit was passing urine in the corner of a courtyard, was soon afflicted with a bloody and cruel strangury <term>stranguria</term> <note>original: "stranguria"; a condition of painful, frequent, drop-by-drop urination.</note> and took to his bed. Any remedies from the healers were in vain, except that as often as he drank of the aforementioned beer, he perceived significant relief; but as often as he rose from his blankets, walked about, and urinated in the usual place, he immediately suffered relapses. At length, a nail made of old, black oak wood was seen fixed in the spot where he was accustomed to pass urine. Once that nail was pulled out and burned, he remained entirely free from the bloody strangury by the drinking of that beer. I remember then that Karichter <note>Bartholomäus Carrichter (c. 1510–1567), an influential physician and astrologer to the Habsburg emperors.</note> wrote that he had dissolved incantations of this kind solely by urinating through brooms made of Birch <term>Betulæ</term>.

# CHAPTER IX.

### *135. That the causes of cramps have remained hidden. 136. The neglect of the Schools. 137. The degrees of pains.*

---

<margin>1</margin>
**K**idney pain <note>original: "Dolor nephriticus"</note>, one of the primary and most troublesome, is for the most part immense and atrocious. For the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the established medical authorities of the time, typically following Galenic or Aristotelian traditions</note> are content to have blamed the cause of such great pain on rubbing <note>original: "attritum"</note>, caused by sand or a stone.

<margin>2</margin>
However, I have persuaded myself that nothing is at all satisfied by that answer. And therefore I have investigated further. For the reason that some tiny little stone, slipping from the kidney, hurts more severely the first few times than some larger stone does later on—which, nevertheless, undoubtedly twists, scrapes, and stretches the ureter more by its rubbing. Since indeed in adults the "spermatic parts" <note>The structural, permanent tissues of the body believed to be formed from the initial "seed" or life-force, which were thought not to grow or stretch easily after maturity</note> of the first constitution do not grow larger day by day, and thus the ureter is not widened even after the descent of so many stones. Finally, thin sand has often been very troublesome in its pain, and has cast its man into bed wailing before it even exited the kidney—and which therefore was never yet harmful by its friction or rubbing. Nor is it enough to have named "rubbing" as the proper or total cause for such a sharp pain.

<margin>3</margin>
For the ureter, throughout its entire length, has no connection with a nerve inserted into it; which therefore ought to lack sensation, and consequently, even pain.

<margin>4</margin>
Since the Schools define pain as a "distressing sensation caused by a harmful rushing-in." If, therefore, thin and tasteless sand, free of all sharpness and rubbing—or a small clot—is not innocent of causing pain: certainly, to have touched upon the causes, the lineage <note>original: "prosapia"; meaning the source or ancestry</note> of sensation and pain, with their circumstances, will not be inconsistent with a treatise on <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>The condition of forming stones in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones</note>. First, therefore, and at the threshold of the sense of Touch, pain must be evaluated.

<margin>5</margin>
For then the Schools teach that the brain is the primary <meta>The catchword "rium" at the bottom of the page completes the word "primarium" or "primary"</meta> [source] of all senses, movements, and consequently also of pain and lack of sensation—

<summary>In this opening to Chapter IX, the author disputes the traditional medical view that kidney stone pain is caused simply by physical friction, noting that even tiny stones or sand cause immense pain before they have the chance to "rub" against the ureter.</summary>
<keywords>kidney stones, nephritic pain, ureter, lithiasis, medical schools, sensation, anatomy of pain</keywords>

<header>ON LITHIASI <note>The medical condition of stone formation, specifically kidney stones.</note></header>

primary and principal organ. That is, it would distinguish the objects of the senses through **animal spirits** <note>original: "spiritus animales." In early medicine, these were believed to be subtle, vaporous substances that traveled through hollow nerves to convey sensation and movement.</note>, sent out from itself in every direction into all the branches of the nerves, serving as the patrons, messengers, and deciders of all sensations. They <note>The traditional medical Schools</note> presume to have said something grand in this. I will speak more clearly. And yet I shall have said nothing [significant in their eyes]. Or at least I will say something that they consider worth nothing. Indeed, the Schools admit that the **brain** <term>cerebrum</term> feels nothing, or barely anything, within itself; and in this regard, it is like the universal **Prime Mover** <term>Primi Motoris: the Aristotelian concept of the first cause of all motion, which itself remains unmoved</term>, which modern thinkers (even

<margin>7</margin> Catholics), along with **Aristotle** <term>Aristotele</term>, command must be immobile if it is to move all other things (as if the ineffable Prime Mover [God] were unable to move Himself, or had to be unmoved and entirely immobile—whereas in truth, He acts and brings things to perfection through His touch of local motion, governing

<margin>8</margin> the universe in an instant). He who truly moves nothing except by the absolute and most abstract nod of **Omnipotence** <term>Omnipotentiæ</term> (and let this absurdity of the Schools be noted here in parentheses as a blasphemy among the upright). Nevertheless, the brain is not the primary

<margin>9</margin> or adequate organ of sensation and motion, since it is immobile in itself and deprived of feeling. For the Schools, seeing that when a vertebra is dislocated, everything subjected to the nerves lower than that vertebra is likewise without sensation and motion, immediately concluded that the brain itself, and its deputy, the **spinal cord** <term>spinæ dorsalis medullam</term>, is the adequate

<margin>10</margin> organ of sensation and motion. Other writers, however, wishing to observe more closely, recognized and admitted that the substance of the brain is devoid of the sense of touch and does not move voluntarily. But since the double membrane, endowed with the name of **Meninges** <term>Meningis: the protective membranes enveloping the brain and spinal cord</term>, has the sharpest sense of touch although it is immobile, they decreed that every nerve, however slender, is covered by a double membrane of this kind, borrowed from both meninges of the brain. They followed this by claiming that this membrane of the nerves...

<summary>This page critiques the traditional view that the brain is the primary organ of sensation, comparing the Scholastic view of the brain to Aristotle's "Prime Mover." The author argues that because the brain's substance lacks feeling, authorities shifted their focus to the spinal cord and the meninges (membranes) as the true seats of sensation.</summary>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

[...be initiated] <note>This completes the thought from the previous page regarding the "absurdity" of certain traditional views.</note> namely, to be formed under a single act of shaping and the labor of seed fabrication. To such an extent these thinkers also wished that every nerve derives its sensation from the covering membrane <note>original: "membranulâ," likely referring to the epineurium or the meningeal coverings.</note>, and by no means from its own substance, which corresponds to the medullary <note>The soft, inner tissue of the brain or spinal cord.</note> substance of the brain.

<margin>11</margin>
They perhaps noted that in the stomach and the uterus, such great and excellent powers were resident within the bare membranes; therefore, it is no wonder that they believed something very similar happened to the **Meninges** <term>Meningibus: the three membranes (dura mater, arachnoid, and pia mater) that line the skull and vertebral canal and enclose the brain and spinal cord</term>, by the prerogative of the same law.

<margin>12</margin>
I, however, proceeded in an entirely different way toward the investigation of sensation and pain, the organ of motion and sensation, its objects, and its causes. I considered first that while a wound is still fresh, it hardly hurts; but soon, while the lips of the wound swell and burn, the wound hurts acutely. And again, while its lips become flaccid and subside, the wound—though still open—is nevertheless almost painless.

<margin>13</margin>
From this I gathered that the **breach of continuity** <term>continui solutio: a traditional medical term for a wound, fracture, or any mechanical separation of parts that are normally continuous</term> must indeed be in the process of occurring <note>original: "in fieri"</note> for there to be pain; but once it has already occurred <note>original: "in facto esse"</note>, unless some unfavorable condition arrives, the breach hardly hurts. I thought to myself: the breach does not hurt simply as a separation of the continuous parts; much less does the heat that arises in a wound two days later hurt, for its property is surely only to heat, not to cause pain. If, however, some external or foreign heat, brought to a high degree, should burn: it indeed hurts, but not as heat; rather, it hurts by causing breaches of continuity and, moreover, by stirring up and fostering disorders of **acrimony** <note>In early medicine, "acrimony" referred to a sharp, corrosive, or irritating quality in the bodily fluids (humors).</note>, arising from another root that corrupts the **economy** <term>oeconomiam: the internal functional system and balance of the living body</term> of our being.

<margin>14</margin>
Truly, because the body, or the solid part, does not feel of itself. Because it is rather a corpse. Sensation, therefore, certainly belongs to life alone. And since the **Schools** <note>The established universities and their traditional Aristotelian/Galenic doctrines.</note> knew that the **brain** <term>cerebrum</term> has no sensation, or at least a barely obscure one, they preferred therefore to believe that the nerve is the primary subject of sensation, motion, and pain. That is to say, the brain is indeed the source of sensation and motion...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the traditional academic "Schools." On the previous page, the author began discussing the origins of sensation and the role of membranes. This page moves into a specific rebuttal regarding the role of nerves and "animal spirits" in the perception of pain and motion.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
# ON THE STONE DISEASE

[...to be initiated] <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page.</note> yet they have nonetheless made the nerve the immediate subject of pain and sensation. Motion, however, although something more material, they nonetheless wished to depend on the higher decision of the will, and to be subject to it. 
<margin>15</margin> 
So that, for instance, the will is the commanding principle of motion; the nerve is the organ that delivers the arbitrary command; and finally, the muscle is the instrument that executes the will. 

<margin>16</margin> 
But they understand sensation to occur in the nerve, as its subject, by means of the **animal spirit** <term>spiritu animali: in pre-modern medicine, a weightless substance or "vapor" thought to travel through hollow nerves to facilitate sensation and movement</term>. Which they call "animal" <note>original: "animalem," derived from 'anima' or soul/life-force</note> because it is drawn from the arteries, but refined <note>original: "recoctum," literally "re-cooked"</note> in the brain for its own uses. They acknowledged, therefore, that the nerve itself is actually without sensation, just like the brain and other solid limbs; for this reason, they want the animal spirits to be the primary feelers of sensation itself and of motion, as well as the effective movers. 

<margin>17</margin> 
I do not yet agree with them; nor do I agree that the nerve is the organ and primary subject of all sensation. For who does not know that in a healthy person, any part of the skin feels, yet does not have a nerve running directly underneath it? For I do not concede that when a sensation is perceived in parts outside a nerve, the spirit runs back into the nerve in a retrograde motion to communicate the perceived sensation to the brain (as the origin of the senses), so that by returning from there, a feeling of pain or pleasure is finally produced in the injured or touched part. For specifically, the **ureter** <term>ureter: the tube that carries urine from the kidney to the bladder</term> hurts intensely without the insertion of any nerve. Similarly, hollow ulcers are often filled with sensitive flesh, yet nerves do not grow anew into that flesh; for parts of the original constitution, once removed, do not grow back, just as those things of the original constitution that have been eaten away by **decay** <term>cariem: rot or bone tissue death</term> are no longer restored.

<margin>18</margin> 
But the numbness or insensibility of **leprosy** <term>lepræ: a disease known in this period for causing a complete loss of physical feeling in the extremities</term> comes to mind here opportunely. Indeed, they <note>those suffering from the disease</note> do not feel a stylus or needle driven into the flesh. Is it then to be believed...

<summary>The author challenges the traditional medical view that nerves and "animal spirits" are the sole conduits of sensation, arguing that since flesh can feel without nerves, sensation must be inherent to the tissues themselves.</summary>

## CHAPTER IX.

...at it must be believed that lepers are deprived of nerves? Or have their nerves been cut off from the fleshy membrane? Are they devoid of **animal spirit** <term>spiritu animali: a refined substance thought to carry signals through the nerves</term>, or robbed of life? Are they "plugged up," as is said of those with paralysis? Must it then be that the nerves of touch throughout their entire bodies are obstructed, and only the nerves for free motion remain functional? Will there be, I ask, motor nerves that are now destitute of sensation alone? 

<margin>19</margin> 
I indeed admit that from the front part of the brain, nerves are dispersed to the eyes, ears, palate, and tongue which serve sensation alone, and do not turn toward the muscles as the proper organs of motion. But no one can deny that the nerves dedicated to motion, which emerge from both sides through the vertebrae, also bestow sensation. Why is it then, that in Leprosy, the nerve that effects motion—already set in motion through the animal spirit—nonetheless lacks the faculty of sensation? Why then in Paralysis, under an injury to the same nerve, are both motion and sensation taken away, while in Leprosy, sensation alone is removed? 

<margin>20</margin> 
Above all, the **Schools** <note>The traditional academic authorities of the era, largely following the ancient Greek physician Galen.</note> hold that Leprosy is incurable, and is a "universal cancer" <note>original: "cancrum universalem"</note> of the body. For, while they think a particular <note>localized</note> cancer is incurable, they believe a universal one is even more so. This chatter of **Galen** <note>A 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated medicine for over a millennium.</note> was composed for this purpose: so that through the "impossibility of healing," he might excuse his own ignorance, his laziness in undertaking labor, and his lack of mercy. For cancer in the flesh is characterized by the sharpest pain and continuous eating away. 

<margin>25</margin> 
Leprosy, however, is painless in the flesh. I do not see, therefore, how Leprosy can be a "cancer" according to the followers of Galen. Furthermore, **Paracelsus** <note>The famous 16th-century Swiss-German physician and alchemist who challenged Galenic medicine.</note> is in error, for he thinks Leprosy is deprived of all **salt** <term>sal: in Paracelsian chemistry, one of the three "Primes" representing stability and solidity</term>, and thus it possesses that characteristic senseless numbness. He speaks as if the sense of touch itself resided in salt alone! For Leprosy has its own ulcers; and, according to the same Paracelsus, there are as many types of ulcers as there are types of salts. Therefore, according to his own doctrine, Leprosy would flow from an abundance of salt. Let us grant to Paracelsus (without, however, an investigation of the truth) that the excrement of the bowels...

<summary>The author critiques traditional views on leprosy, questioning why it causes a loss of sensation but not motion, and rejects Galen's definition of it as a "universal cancer" and Paracelsus's theory involving a lack of "salt."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of contemporary medical theories, specifically attacking the inconsistencies of Paracelsus and the traditional "Humorists" regarding the loss of sensation in leprosy. He argues against the idea that a lack of "salt" in the body causes the numbness associated with the disease.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>

# ON THE STONE

...of a leper, to teem with granules of salt, and that his urine <term>lotium: urine</term> no longer dissolves any sea salt (both of which were ideas dreamed up by **Paracelsus**): this would still not prove that the leper's flesh or blood lacked its own salt; much less would it prove that the flesh lost its sense of touch for that reason. For first, this opinion of his regarding **Leprosy** completely overturns his own doctrine concerning the **Three First Principles** <term>tria prima: the alchemical triad of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury which Paracelsus believed composed all matter</term> of bodies. Furthermore, just as manifest salts are produced daily within us from tasteless and non-salty things by the law of human digestion, so, even if the waste products of digestion were nothing but pure salt, the blood <term>cruor: the vital or clotted part of the blood</term> would not therefore be deprived of its salt. For the blood does not borrow its own salt, nor the necessities of its constitution, from waste products. Indeed, it would rather follow that since **Leprosy** is such an abundant producer of salt in the waste products, the blood would not lack its own salt either. Similarly, while a constant and clearly salty fluid <term>synovia: here referring to a clear fluid weeping from sores</term> flows from ulcers, the remaining blood does not lack its own salt because of it, nor is the sensation in the flesh diminished; rather, it increases pain and sharp irritation. Likewise, even if in **Dropsy** <term>Hydrope: an old term for edema, or the accumulation of fluid in the body</term> salty water sometimes suddenly distends the abdomen, <margin>22</margin> those suffering from dropsy do not lack the sense of touch. For **Paracelsus** elsewhere defines blood as pure human **Mercury**, from which such excrements—in the form of putrid **Sulfur**, as well as useless and superfluous salt—have been separated. Then again, forgetting his own words, he defines blood as "the salt of a ruby" <note>original: "salem rubini"</note>. As if salt were a ruby-colored tincture, or the tincture of the blood came from salt! In fact, he makes the **Three First Principles** changeable at his whim, just as the **Humorists** <note>Traditional doctors who believed health was governed by the balance of four "humors": blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note> blame their humors and "heats" at will. What is more, they claim these are the causes of disease and death, and also the authors of sensation and motion. Bah! Is this how we are to play at will with nature, diseases, blood, and death?

<summary>The author critiques Paracelsus for contradictory theories on leprosy and salt, arguing that the presence or absence of salt in waste products does not dictate the salt content or sensory function of the blood and flesh.</summary>

### CHAPTER IX.

...neighbor? For Medicine is a plainly serious matter; and at some point a man would give "skin for skin." <note>original: "pellem pro pelle," a biblical reference to Job 2:4, implying one will sacrifice anything to save their own life.</note> For salt does not appear in the blood <term>cruor: the vital or clotted part of the blood</term>, the flesh, the solid parts, etc., except in the final and artificial separation of those "beginnings" after death, and indeed by means of fire. This occurs, clearly, after the sense of touch has long been extinguished. Therefore, these dreams of such "principles" <note>The "principles" refers to the Paracelsian Tria Prima: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> do not serve the investigation of motion and sensation. The **Stigma**, <note>The "Devil's mark" or "witch's mark" believed to be a numb spot on the body of a witch.</note> impressed upon witches by the Devil, is known to betray them because the location of the mark lacks sensation for the duration of one's life. Once impressed, it possesses its own natural causes for this lack of feeling, much like **Leprosy**, though these causes are confined to a specific and small center. For if a needle is thrust all the way into the site of that mark while the witch's eyes are covered, the puncture is made without any feeling. At the very least, that spot would be preserved by a miraculous privilege without salt or rot for the rest of one's life; whereas otherwise, the **Paracelsian** life is a "mummy" <term>mumia: in alchemy, a vital balsam or spiritual essence that preserves the body from decay</term> mixed with a liquor of salts.

Therefore, the doctrine of **Hippocrates** is far healthier, which determines that the spirit—the ethereal and animal breath—is the immediate organ of sensation, pain, motion, pleasure, suitability, harmony, attraction, repulsion, contractions, relaxation, and indeed any alterations whatsoever. This is so much the case that the spirit appropriates sensible objects for itself, and from them, it fashions the sensations themselves. For it happens that if this spirit is perhaps occupied by deep thoughts or by madness, the body does not feel pain, hunger, cold, thirst, etc. Indeed, I remember a robber who eluded the agonies of torture by a draft of **spirit of wine** <term>aqua vitæ: literally "water of life," referring to high-proof distilled alcohol or brandy</term> and a piece of garlic; when he was finally deprived of these, he confessed his crimes. <margin>23</margin> But the stupor and insensibility of **Leprosy** exists subjectively in the condition of the flesh and the nerves; it does not exist merely in the path of the imagination. Effectively and occasionally, however, it resides in a certain poison. This poison is a bloody, icy, and pro[found] <note>The text cuts off here, likely "provocative" or "productive."</note> anodyne. <term>anodynum: a substance that kills pain or numbs sensation.</term>

# ON THE STONE-DISEASE

<meta>catchword: departs-</meta>

...almost mortifying, communicable through a horrendous and foul contagion, and emanating outward. For this reason, the Holy Scriptures <note>Referring to the laws in the Book of Leviticus regarding the ritual uncleanness and isolation of lepers.</note> command that Leprosy be sequestered from the company of men. Moreover, this icy poison begins from the outside; they feel internal pains for that reason, and likewise 
<margin>24</margin> external cold and heat. Yet they do not feel wounds or a blow. There is Psoriasis, and various types of mange <term>scabies: a general term for contagious skin eruptions or mites</term>; as well as the Venereal plague <note>Syphilis</note>, infamous for its polluting poison. But these differ in species, as much by the nature of the poison as by the diversity of the subjects. Mange, to be sure, infects only the skin. Thus the skin cannot turn the nourishment assigned to it into its own sustenance, but converts the greater part of it into a salty and contagious fluid <term>ichor: a thin, watery, or purulent discharge from a sore</term>. This, of course, belongs to the property of a stinging and "nettle-like" salt, etc. Therefore, 
<margin>25</margin> mange does not require internal remedies, but only topical ones, which are meant for killing off that stinging salt. The Venereal plague, however, chiefly affects the vital blood <term>cruor: the thick, clotting portion of the blood associated with life force</term> with a biting, purulent, and putrefying poison. But Leprosy regards the inflowing spirit <term>spiritus influus: the vital breath or pneuma believed to flow through the nerves to provide sensation</term> most especially, by means of an anodyne <note>anodyne: a substance that numbs or kills pain</note> icy poison. 
<margin>26</margin> Pardon me, *Reader*, that due to a meager supply of words, I am forced to use wordplay <note>original: "paronomasia," a rhetorical device where similar-sounding words are used to describe complex ideas.</note>. Because the essences of things are unknown to us *a priori* <note>a priori: from the cause; knowing the nature of a thing before seeing its effects</note>, and therefore proper names for them are lacking, we are forced to borrow and describe the conditions of poisons in diseases from the similarity of their properties: so that if the definition does not proceed from the "reason why" <note>original: "Propter quid," a logical term for explaining the cause</note>, it may at least proceed from related things "because it is so" <note>original: "quia est," explaining the effect when the cause is unknown</note>. Thus I say the poison of the Falling Sickness <note>original: "venenum Caduci," referring to epilepsy</note> is, with a certain constriction, inebriating, sleep-inducing, and simultaneously causes fainting; and yet it is not contagious because it is intrinsic and not fermentative. Thus Leprosy has an anodyne poison—not indeed sleep-inducing, but icy, almost mortifying—with an infection of the sensitive spirit, and for that reason, it is powerfully contagious, especially in a hot and sweat-producing region. For just as cold takes away the sense of touch by freezing, and...

# CHAPTER IX.

...by driving the forces inward: thus also Leprosy has chosen and prepared for itself an anodyne <term>anodyne: a medicine that relieves pain</term>, not of the cooling or sleep-inducing kind, but one called "icy" for another reason. It is not unlike how chilblains <term>perniones: painful, itchy swellings on the skin caused by poor circulation in the cold</term> are pierced with ulcers as if burned by fire; yet these often occur before or after winter in those who, throughout the whole winter, felt no cold in their heated rooms <term>hypocausta: rooms or buildings heated from below by furnaces, common in the early modern period</term>. Therefore, the poison of Leprosy in this respect effectively corresponds to cold, though not in its primary elemental quality <note>original: "non in qualitate prima elementali." In ancient and medieval medicine, the primary qualities were Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry. The author argues leprosy mimics the effects of cold without being a simple temperature change.</note>. 

Nor does Leprosy for that reason cause total death of the tissue in the manner of Gangrene; rather, it only affects the part which it seals with an ulcer. Indeed, it does not immediately spread far from there, because it arises from a constricting icy poison, the author of insensibility <note>The loss of sensation or numbness characteristic of leprosy.</note>. 
<margin>27</margin> However, it is difficult to heal because of this icy contagion, which is almost mortifying and oppressive to the sensitive spirit <term>sensitive spirit: the vital force believed to carry sensory information through the nerves</term>. Since it becomes intimately fermented with the sensitive spirit as it flows toward the extremities, it is removed with difficulty, except through remedies that have access to our innermost recesses <term>penetralia: the deepest, most hidden parts of the body's internal systems</term>. These remedies must strengthen the spirit of life so that it can overcome the aforementioned poison and, like a new spring, melt away the ice of the preceding winter. 

And although this poison is fermentative in nature—and thus, even in a small quantity, attempts to creep into everything thereafter—it is not suited to be fully fermented with the spirit. This is due to the strength and combative nobility of the subject <note>the human vital spirit</note> in which it is received, as well as the constricting and sluggish lethargy of its icy nature. This is the difference among contagious diseases: that the poisons of some may recede and be separated by nature or by art, leaving the bodies they infected; but for others, no spontaneous separation can be hoped for. The ice of Leprosy also occupies the exterior parts more, because it is an icy evil and is pushed outward by the innate heat <term>innate heat: the natural warmth of a living body, seen in ancient medicine as the source of life and digestion</term>; for that reason, it remains more...

<summary>The author explains leprosy as an 'icy poison' that causes numbness by suppressing the 'sensitive spirit.' He argues that because this poison mimics the effects of cold and binds to the body's vital forces, it can only be cured by internal remedies that strengthen the life-spirit from within.</summary>

It transitions from a chemical/alchemical explanation of the disease's "root" to a theological and then physiological discussion of sensation.</meta>

<note>The sentence continues from the previous page: "...and oppresses..."</note> contaminates the outer parts more than the internal organs, which are connected to the root in the unity of life. No healer, however, has ever cured Leprosy who has not possessed the liquor **Alkahest** <term>Alkahest: a hypothetical "universal solvent" in alchemy, capable of dissolving any substance into its primary matter</term>. Since this substance is of the most tedious preparation, no one, even if they are skilled in the art, will reach its attainment unless the Most High, by a special gift, has led them to it. For one must be chosen and endowed with a particular privilege if they are to possess that medium. Through this substance, namely, both sensitive and insensitive sublunary <term>sublunary: earthly things, literally "below the moon"</term> things are equally penetrated, down to the feminine and intrinsic root of the first being <note>original: "radicem feminalem, atque intrinsecam primi entis." This refers to the fundamental vital principle or "seed" of matter in Paracelsian philosophy.</note>. Therefore, it also tames and changes all things under its power, without any reaction from the patient or depletion of the acting agent. For otherwise, whatever hope Leprosy may have persuaded itself of from elsewhere is in vain. Consequently, of old, the healing of lepers was given as a sign to the Messiah alone.

My firstborn daughter, while still five years old, became more and more leprous; and finally, livid ulcers and white horny scales grew out over her whole body. At that time, an image of the Blessed Virgin <note>original: "D. virginis" - Divae Virginis</note> had recently emerged, famous for many miracles in our city, at the hospital of St. James. The girl, then seven years old, desired to go to that place; and so the grandmother and her granddaughter hurried there. She returned after a few hours healthy, and the scales immediately fell off. 

Soon after a year, that same Leprosy suddenly returned. I confessed myself guilty because I had kept silent about the honor of the Blessed Virgin. Therefore, the little daughter returned with her grandmother to the sacred image, and again she returned healed, and so she remained from then on. I, however, fearing the return of the Leprosy, made the miracle known, and in a public writing, I confessed the divine favor and mercy. To whom be all praise and glory, with the sanctification of His name forever! 

<margin>28</margin> I have already said, according to the mind of Hippocrates, that sensation—both in its cause and its reception—consists in the animal spirit <term>animal spirit: in early medicine, a subtle fluid or essence believed to flow through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement</term>. But because a spirit of this kind
