### C H A P T E R IX.

...of every kind, is dead, and a corpse, unless it is illuminated by life itself. And because life itself, in that spirit, is not something proper and inseparable from it—for life is distinct from the vital or animal spirit (I use both names interchangeably here) in its entire subject (a point made more clearly elsewhere regarding long life)—it is therefore primary and clear that the vital spirit does not feel immediately. Rather, it is life itself which feels and suffers pain more closely and immediately within that spirit. Indeed, I have demonstrated in the treatise <margin>29</margin> *On the Forms of Things*, that life, or the form of things, becomes a certain light, a special creature, shining in its lodging <note>original: "hospitio." The author views the body as a temporary dwelling or "inn" for the soul/life.</note>, through all the attendants of its parts. It is not, however, a substance, nor an "accident" <term>accident: in Aristotelian philosophy, a quality that does not belong to the essence of a thing (like color or size)</term>, regardless of how much the **Peripatetic School** <term>Peripatetic School: followers of Aristotle, whom the author frequently challenges</term> might grumble at such a novelty. This paradox I have demonstrated through Mathematics and Mechanically in the book of *Elements*. And so I assume it here as sufficiently proven elsewhere.

Therefore, regarding sensation and sense, I shall speak much more closely to the truth than **Hippocrates**. For if sensation was formerly said to occur through a "passion" <note>original: "passione." In this context, it means a physical undergoings or being acted upon by an external force.</note> of the body, in which the spirit, making an impulse, receives the impression of the sensible thing—which is therefore improperly called the "sensible species" <term>sensible species: the theoretical forms or "images" that medieval philosophers believed traveled from an object to the senses</term>—let us now understand that this impression, in a single moment and at the same point, is introduced into the life dwelling within it. 

Under this introduction, application, and adaptation, sensation first arises, created in life itself and through life. Clearly, the inseparable property of this life is sense itself. And since life is not of the body, nor proper to the body, nor finally the offspring of bodily properties, but is a light arriving by the gift of the **Creator** from above the lot of the elements and the heavens; hence also sensation does not belong to bodies, nor to matter, nor to the "disruption of continuity" <note>original: "solutionis continui." A medical term for a wound or break in the flesh; the author argues that the physical cut isn't what "feels" the pain, but life itself.</note>, etc., but is a plainly vital property, arising from the very trunk of life. Just as it is also not enough for there to be an eye, a medium, and a vital spirit for vision to occur:

<summary>The author argues that sensation and life are not physical properties of the body or the "vital spirit," but a divine "light" or form that acts upon the body. He rejects traditional Aristotelian and Hippocratic explanations of sense as mere physical impressions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of sensation. The author moves from the concept of life as a "light" to the specific role of the "sensitive soul" and "vital spirits" in the act of perceiving the world, arguing that biological organs alone cannot account for the miracle of sight or touch.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE <note>original Latin: DE LITHIASI</note></header>

But furthermore, the application of the visual spirit to life is required. Therefore, the effect of seeing, though entirely ordinary, surpasses the whole of elemental nature: because it contains the image <note>original: iconem</note> and symbol of life itself. This is because vision, taste, smell, touch, etc., are the immediate effects of life playing through its organs; for in every sense, it is necessary that the attractions <note>original: lenocinia; here referring to the way sensory inputs "entice" or are presented to the consciousness</note> of the spirits and the perceived forms <term>species: in older philosophy, these were the mental "images" or "likenesses" of objects that travel to the senses</term> of things be adapted immediately to life, if sensations are ever to occur.

<margin>30</margin>
Indeed, in a matter so difficult and removed so far from common doctrine, grant me, Reader, that I may speak with you even more intimately. For you have perceived that it does not suffice for sense and sensation to say that the brain, and likewise the nerve, are the immediate organs of sense. Nor is it even enough to have invoked the "flowing spirit" <term>spiritum influum: a vital force or fluid thought to flow from the brain to the rest of the body</term>, or even the spirit itself implanted in the body parts (insofar as it is nourished by the flowing power of the brain or nerves), unless life itself concurs with all these. Sensation itself is of such great weight <note>original: tantæ molis; a phrase suggesting a monumental or divinely difficult task</note> that it easily exceeds the scope of all sublunary <term>sublunary: the earthly realm below the moon, governed by change and decay, as opposed to the eternal heavens</term> things, along with the entire power of the heavens and elements. And so, since you have now perceived this, I shall speak further. Regarding what I have already said about life throughout the book *On Long Life* <note>original title: *De vita longa*</note> (to which I refer you, as it will shortly be published), I have described how variously life spreads through nature—namely, as if in the manner of a seed <note>original: seminaliter</note> within the vital spirits themselves, but in the **sensitive soul** itself, as if from a fountainhead <note>original: fontaliter</note>.

<margin>31</margin>
Therefore, speaking properly of sense and sensation, the **sensitive soul** itself is the primary and immediate entity that performs all sensations and, in performing them, endures them within itself. And thus the spirit of the brain is merely a mediate <note>acting as an intermediary or middle-man</note> organ; but life is the organ, or medium, by which the sensitive soul feels external things rushing in upon it. For sensation is not located immediately in the thing contained, nor in the containers, nor even in the spirit diffused through the nerves into the vital parts: because that spirit, making its onset,

He references his upcoming work, "On Long Life," to explain how life acts as a "fountainhead" for these sensations.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R IX.</header>

## CHAPTER IX.

...making [it], from the sensitive soul, no differently than thick material smoke is produced by the flame by which it is ignited. But the soul, the immortal mind, is entirely impassable to human concepts: insofar as it is the likeness <note>original: simulacrum</note> of the incomprehensible God himself. The sensitive soul, however, although it may begin in nature through an occasional seed—that is, by way of disposition—nevertheless, since it is the nearest image of that [immortal] image, it too is entirely unknown to human understanding and utterly stripped of definition. For that very reason, it cannot be defined by causes: but is only described through an absurd circle of reflections on its actions and properties.

<margin>32</margin>
So that, namely, the *sensitive soul* is a formal light, to which the properties of sensitive life primarily belong; but in man, it is the support and host of the *immortal soul*, and the immediate *bond* <term>syndesmion: a Greek term for a link or connection</term> with created corporeal things outside itself. To this soul, therefore, there is a still more remote aspect relative to life: since life and soul are distinct, just as the abstract and the concrete [are distinct]: or rather as the property of a being and the being itself. 

<margin>33</margin>
This soul, therefore, feels through life within the animal spirits, and immediately sees the conceived visible forms <term>species: in older philosophy, these were the mental "images" or "likenesses" of objects that travel to the senses</term> within the optical spirit that dwells in the pupil. For the optical spirit there is a transparent mirror, whose light is the sensitive soul itself present in that very place, acting as the seat and the handmaiden of the immortal mind. Therefore, there is no need for the received sensible forms to travel back through the nerves to the brain: but the soul, being immediately present and bestowing all its power from itself upon the optical spirit, itself sees and discerns.

<margin>34</margin>
The brain, however, is only the workshop and the steward of these spirits. Wherefore the nerves do not serve to carry the forms gathered in sensation back to the brain: but rather to sprinkle the spirits—which have been enlightened in the brain—down to the parts of the body to refresh and strengthen those parts into which they are inserted. Nor is sensation...

<page-num>172</page-num>

# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES
<note>original: DE LITHIASI. While the book title refers to kidney and bladder stones, Van Helmont often detours into the fundamental nature of life and the soul to explain how these diseases form.</note>

The same reasoning applies to external things, entirely in accordance with the power of the imagination and its sisters. For the sensible forms <term>species: in early modern philosophy, these are the "likenesses" or mental images of objects that the senses perceive</term>, perceived from the outside by the soul, are abstracted from their physical sensation; and then at last, as if from the raw material from which they are made, the forms of the sensible thing are forged into an image. Otherwise, sensory inputs entering from without would be conceived in a concrete way within the organs of the senses, and thus they would not only cause displeasure, but would right now even cause pain.

<margin>35</margin>
Regarding the seat of the soul, there has been various debate in favor of either the heart or the brain. I, however, believe that the sensitive soul—conforming to its own seeds and in real actuality—is distinguished from the immortal mind, which is the image of divinity. Yet the sensitive soul (which is the "old carnal man," the "Adamic man," and the "law of the flesh" <note>These are biblical terms used by Van Helmont to describe the biological, mortal part of human nature that we share with animals, as opposed to the immortal spirit.</note>) is not distinguished on either side from the formal and vital light. Nor does it sit immediately in the "influental spirit" <note>The *spiritus influens* refers to the subtle, fluid-like substances that early scientists believed flowed through the nerves to animate the body.</note>, which is surely entirely unstable and fleeting. 

However, the spirit which has grown into the organs from the very first constitution of the body, even if it lives by the "ultimate life of the seeds," does not yet truly live by the "middle animal life" (which is the sensitive one), until the vital light, arriving from above, actually shines upon it. The preparations for this are indeed sent ahead step by step: but nonetheless, in a single instant, they are illuminated by the divine goodness of the Creator. This is as described in the book *On Long Life* <note>original: *de vita longa*</note>. For it happens no differently than when striking flint against steel to create a spark. Therefore, an inexpressible light from the Creator of all things is ignited in the spirit of the noble internal organs: and first, indeed, in the Heart. This light, as it gradually acquires strength, is more powerfully expanded; not otherwise than how the smoke of a lower candle visibly catches the flame sent down from an upper one. Thus, although the organs are separated by their different duties and functions, they nevertheless serve through a mutual harmony for the needs and ends established by the Lord Creator. There is, however, only a single lyre <term>chelys: a Greek word for a tortoise-shell lyre, used here as a metaphor for the harmonious coordination of the body's organs</term> for all the viscera and limbs...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the sensitive soul is a "vital light" ignited by God, primarily in the heart, which coordinates the body's organs like a musical instrument. He distinguishes this mortal, biological soul from both the immortal mind and the fleeting "animal spirits" of the nerves.</summary>

<page-num>173</page-num>

# CHAPTER IX.

...continued, homogeneous life, and the **sensitive soul**; which, in individual things, receives certain determinations, or properties, which it had prepared for itself through seeds, and soon thereafter puts them on. For indeed, just as the flame of a candle does not extend above or beyond its own sphere, nor does it perish as long as it dwells within that sphere—even though the soot arising from it might fly far away outside that sphere, devoid of flame—so similarly, the **influential spirits**, although illuminated by a participation in life, are dispersed and travel abroad. Therefore, they are materially diminished within the body; and indeed, to that extent, the vigor of the vital light withers; yet nothing of the essence of the sensitive soul perishes. For life is not acquired by parts and degrees, nor does it subsist as a mere "accident" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that can change without changing the essence of the thing (like hair color). Van Helmont argues life is not an accident, but an essential substance.</note> does; rather, it is always life: even though more or less vitality may appear in that light.

For it is no different than fire: where a tiny bit is just as much fire as an intense one. Likewise, whatever exhales from the body, having previously enjoyed a participation in life, nevertheless loses that life as soon as it departs outside its own boundaries. In this way, even excrement <note>original: "excrementa" – here referring generally to waste products or fluids leaving the body, like sweat or pus.</note> preserves not life itself, but a shared participation of the vital spirits. For this reason, the order of the lower harmony falls into <term>ataxia</term> <note>original: "ataxian," from Greek meaning disorder or lack of coordination.</note> from that loss. According to the saying: *My spirit shall be weakened, and therefore my days shall be shortened.* <note>A reference to Job 17:1. Van Helmont suggests that the loss of these vital fluids weakens the "spirit" that keeps the body alive.</note> Therefore, an excessive evacuation of pus, and similar fluids, brings about a hasty death. This is true insofar as they do not contain the soul itself, but only the final seminal life of the vital spirits.

Indeed, concerning the immediate indwelling of the immortal mind—or the **divine sculptor**—the dispute is still before the Judge as to whether it resides in the heart or the brain. For I, who know that even <term>empsychosis</term> <note>The process of "ensoulment," or the moment the soul enters or animates the body.</note> occurs at the very instant in which the sensitive soul is present—that is, while that formal, animal, and sensitive light is ignited (as I have written elsewhere in *On the Origin of Forms* <note>original: "De ortu formarum," one of Van Helmont's other philosophical treatises.</note>)—I believe also that the immortal mind is present...

<summary>Van Helmont uses the analogy of a candle flame to explain how the sensitive soul remains whole even as vital spirits (like "soot") leave the body, and argues that losing bodily fluids can cause death by draining vital energy.</summary>

<page-num>174</page-num>

# ON LITHIASI <note>The medical condition of "the Stone" or urinary calculi.</note>

...is such, and in its entirety, it sits immediately in the **sensitive soul**, as an associate. It does not, indeed, sit in some corner of the body, or an organ, a workshop <note>original: "ergastulo" – literally a prison or work-house, here used to mean a functional anatomical site</note>, or a factory of spirits. Instead, I think the **Mind** exists throughout the whole **sensitive soul**, penetrating it, and does not exceed its sphere as long as one lives; and to this extent, the Mind is subject to many inconveniences of circumstances. But in death, the **Mind** is separated: because the **sensitive soul** itself, like the light of a candle, vanishes into nothingness.

<margin>36</margin> These things certainly had to be previewed here before the explanation of sensation. **Pain**, therefore, being the most perceptible sensation, will open the way for us. For pain is a harmful and sorrowful sensation; conceived in the **vital spirit**, and inserted into the **sensitive soul** through the power of life. And strictly speaking, **Sensation** <term>Sensus: the faculty of feeling or perception</term> is a passion <note>In 17th-century philosophy, a "passion" is something the soul "undergoes" or receives from the outside world.</note> of the sensitive soul, conceived in the spirit of life. For nothing can rejoice, be sad, or feel pain, except the soul itself. And so sensation, since it is the first conception of pain or pleasure, occurs entirely in the soul primarily. Therefore, sensation represents to me nothing other than that power of the soul to conceive and judge passively concerning external things rushing in upon it.

Thus, since these things depend on the soul—the whole history of which is hidden from us—it is no wonder that, until now, the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to Scholasticism, the traditional university philosophy based on Aristotle that Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note> have treated the soul and sensation only sluggishly <note>original: "oscitanter" – literally "yawningly" or carelessly</note>. For they have leaped over the investigations of far more manifest things, leaving them untouched; indeed, by subscribing to the dreams of the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum" – here referring to ancient Greek and Roman philosophers like Aristotle or Galen, whose works formed the basis of medieval medicine.</note>, they have neglected them through laziness. In pain, therefore, the **irrational sensitive soul** primarily grieves, rages, grows angry, feels anxious, itches, or fears; and, as it is the fountain-root of all vital actions, it naturally moves and contracts not only the muscles but any parts whatever, according to the tone of its passions.

<margin>37</margin> **Sensation**, then, is the action of external sensible things; and while these are conceived in the soul, the soul itself also suffers, no less than...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the immortal Mind resides within the sensitive soul throughout the body, and that pain is a psychological "passion" felt by the soul rather than a purely mechanical bodily process.</summary>

# CHAPTER IX

...sensation, the social life, the **animal spirit** <term>animal spirit: from the Latin 'spiritus animalis' (spirit of the soul), these were believed to be subtle fluids that transmitted instructions from the soul to the muscles and senses</term>, and the rest of the retinue. However, the **immortal Mind** suffers nothing at all in its own substance; but only in its subject, seat, and guest-house—namely, the **sensitive soul**. Otherwise, all sublunary <note>Earthly things existing below the moon, which were considered corruptible and temporary.</note> things are far too weak to affect an immortal being that is eternal in its duration.

<margin>38</margin>
Yet, what the **sensitive soul** suffers from external sensory things is still a very small matter, unless it becomes, as it were, hostile to itself by overstepping its bounds through impatience. Indeed, it begins to act when it is irritated by sensory things, and thus it suffers. For it shakes the **vital spirit** and the whole body, and finally, like a spendthrift, it squanders its vital provisions <note>original: "suppellectilem" – literally household furniture or equipment; here referring to the body's vital resources</note>, gives birth to diseases for itself, and hastens its own death. Thus, even here the proverb is proven true: *that no one is more harmed than by himself.* And so, from the sensory concepts it receives—since the sensitive soul is pure action once stimulated (for it is entirely irrational, brutal, irascible, and full of desire)—it leaps into furies and, symptomatically or furiously, shakes everything. Sensory things, therefore, are the **occasions** <note>In 17th-century medicine, an "occasion" is a triggering or precipitating factor, rather than the underlying cause of a disease.</note> of injuries and diseases; but the **sensitive soul**, perceiving these same occasions and being unwilling to endure them, stirs up its various ministers in different ways and, by the ideas impressed upon it, wanders from its goal. From this, then, arise the various **seeds of diseases** and their offspring. The soul, therefore, endures and suffers the aforementioned things from the sensory object; being agitated by the stings of sensations, it acts and finally suffers insofar as, in its madness, it squanders its own economy <note>original: "œconomiam" – the internal management and balance of the body's functions</note>. While it feels things that are sweet, plausible, helpful, and pleasing to itself, it is not truly "outside itself" in this sensation; this differs from the **judgment** by which it feels things that are harmful, corrosive, stinging, tearing, or bruising, which are nothing but accidents plainly external to life itself. From this it is easily discerned that sensation occurs through the **judgment** of the **sensitive soul**, which is applied to a sensory concept, and which first changes by itself according to the sensation it has conceived.

<summary>Van Helmont argues that the immortal Mind is unaffected by physical suffering, which instead occurs in the "sensitive soul." He explains that disease is often caused by the soul's own overreaction to external stimuli, which wastes the body's vital resources.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R  IX.</header>

## CHAPTER IX.

...natural, originating from the **Archeus** <term>Archeus: In Van Helmont's system, this is the "master-workman" or internal vital principle that governs the life-functions of an organ or the whole body</term>, from which at last the most ready powers for acting are born. In these cases, the persistence of the emotion is sometimes so prompt and stubborn that it presents a specimen of wonder to the observer, especially if one examines the attributes of life and of the **spiritual seed** <note>Van Helmont believed that "seeds" contained the formal blueprint for all living things and diseases.</note>. For how very quickly do children, women, and the improvident become angry, weep, and laugh? This is because their **sensitive souls** <term>sensitive soul: the irrational part of the soul shared with animals, responsible for processing senses and emotions</term> are recently, and as if immediately, attached to sensible things. It is natural, therefore, for the **sensitive spirit** to be carried away spontaneously and easily into such overflows: because that soul, easily swayed by its own sensual judgment, slides into the spontaneous passions of the material spirits. And, just as these habits grow from childhood, so thereafter that soul grows up irascible, furious, and entirely symptomatic. Otherwise, it would perform all its duties far more safely under the rule of gentleness, rather than aspiring—through its furies—toward diseases and its own subsequent internal destruction. This is frequent and most manifest in **uterine exorbitations** <note>likely referring to "hysteria" or "wandering womb" symptoms, common medical theories of the time</note>, or in the symptoms of certain wounds and other diseases. 

<margin>39</margin>
Therefore, in this context, anger and fury do not belong to the man <note>meaning the rational, immortal part of the human being</note>; rather, they belong to that **sensitive soul**, which is carried into life and performs the **rages** <note>original: "thymoses" — from the Greek meaning rages or spirited impulses</note> of natural sensation. Consequently, it not rarely rises to such a peak that it burns an **eschara** <term>eschara: a dry slough or scab, typically caused by a burn or cauterization</term> and withers a part with **gangrene** <term>gangrene: original "sphacelo," referring to the death of body tissue</term>, just like a fire.

Pain, therefore, is an indubitable **affliction** <term>affliction: original "pathema," a Greek-derived term for a state of suffering or being acted upon</term> of the sense of touch, in which the sensitive soul expresses its displeasure with an object, according to the different types of injury inflicted upon the parts. Furthermore, is this affliction a function of a **judicial power** <note>the ability of the soul to "judge" or evaluate a sensation as good or bad</note>, from which the soul is specifically named "sensitive"? 

<margin>40</margin>
In the same way, the **motive faculty** <note>the power of movement</note> moves at the mere nod of the soul, without an external or foreign exciter. Or, is pain instead an affliction produced immediately by a sensible, painful cause? The Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional Scholastic academic authorities of the universities, whom Van Helmont often criticized.</note> could have investigated this, if they...

<page-num>182</page-num>

<header>ON THE STONE DISEASE</header>

...schools <note>original: "las," likely the end of "scholas" (schools) from the previous page</note>, if the care of investigating such great truth had ever touched them as much as the receiving of a fee from the sick. For my part, however, this matter has long been free from doubt. Since the sense of pain is a judgment of the soul, expressed through sensation within the **sensitive faculty** <term>sensitive faculty: the ability of the irrational soul to perceive and react to the physical world</term>, by which the soul laments over a sensible, harmful, and painful object: therefore, in almost every case, both concur connectedly, and each behaves in its own way regarding the pain.

Specifically, the cause of the sensible injury is the motive for the pain; but the **sensitive soul** <term>sensitive soul: the vital, animal part of a human that governs feeling and involuntary functions</term> itself brings judgment regarding the painful object, with a certain boiling over and impatience of the **suffering** <term>pathema: a state of being acted upon, or a passion/suffering of the soul</term>. This is indeed entirely obvious in a wound, a bruise, a tension, a burn, or from cold, insofar as these are external causes. 

When, however, the moving causes of pain have been applied—whether by the aforementioned impression of external things or by its own internal deviation—the **sensitive spirit** <term>sensitive spirit: the subtle, vaporous substance that carries sensory information through the nerves</term> then becomes entirely sharp, gnawing, biting, and degenerate. It even forms blood <note>original: "cruorem," typically referring to thick or clotted blood</note> similar to itself. Then, of course, the sensitive soul does not merely pass a simple judgment about the pain by suffering; but moreover, being entirely disturbed within itself, it produces from itself a new painful product, no differently than if it had proceeded from an occasional external cause. 

And although both of these usually concur in a greater suffering and a more severe sensation, nevertheless, properly speaking, pain regards more intimately the judgment passed by the suffering sensitive soul. Or rather, pain reflects more closely upon the property of the soul than upon the cause of the pain itself. For many are seriously wounded without manifest pain; just as a madman shows that he hardly feels pain even from harmful causes. Certain people also often evade the pains of torture, and ointments also deceive pains, even though the body parts are being beaten by injury. Therefore, sensation more intimately and properly regards the judgment of the power of the sensitive soul,

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain is an internal psychological and spiritual reaction ("judgment") of the sensitive soul rather than just a physical result of injury. He explains how the soul can "boil over" and create further physical distress or, in some cases (like madness or through ointments), fail to perceive even severe physical damage.</summary>

# CHAPTER IX.

...than the injury of the painful cause. Truly, because of the aforementioned lack of feeling in **Leprosy**, and the lack of movement in **Apoplexy** <term>Apoplexy: a historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness</term>, etc., I turn my attention elsewhere. Truly, the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical establishment following the teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note>, contending that the brain is the primary organ of sensation and pain, note that the brain—touched by its own **internal condition** <note>original: "idiopathia"</note> immediately and as if by a single blow—loses both sensation and motion at once; indeed, it even causes one side of the body to contract.

<margin>41</margin>
They express the manner of this happening thus: The fourth sinus of the brain (a very tiny little sinus) at the beginning of the **spinal cord** <note>original: "spinalis medullæ"</note>, starting from the **cerebellum** <note>the part of the brain at the back of the skull that coordinates muscular activity</note>, is blocked by **phlegm** <note>original: "pituitam"</note>, which results in instantaneous apoplexy. 

<margin>42</margin>
For Nature, either being unwilling or unable to draw back or reduce such phlegm once it has fallen there, at least busies itself diligently to deposit that phlegm into one or the other side of that channel. Consequently, **paralysis** <term>paralysis: the loss of the ability to move or feel in part of the body</term> of that side begins. 

I read these things, indeed, regarding Apoplexy and Paralysis; yet I find nothing regarding the **contraction** <note>a permanent shortening of a muscle or joint</note> arising from a blow to the head. **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged traditional medicine.</note> also, not content with this drowsy doctrine of these three diseases, is turned about inconsistently. 

<margin>43</margin>
For now he says that Apoplexy, and the subsequent Paralysis, are born because the **sensitive spirit** in the nerves, like **water of life** <note>original: "aquæ vitæ," referring here to highly distilled, flammable alcohol</note>, has caught a flame from the fire of Etna, in the manner of sulfurous mines, by the **microcosmic law** <term>microcosmic law: the alchemical belief that the human body (the microcosm) reflects the processes of the entire universe (the macrocosm)</term>. 

By this **inflammation** <note>original: "phlogosi"</note>, the nerves and tendons—as if scorched, burned, and half-dead—are dried out along with the muscles, and therefore remain thereafter deprived of sensation and motion. He certainly places these two diseases (thinking nothing of contraction from a blow meanwhile) not indeed in the "case" of the brain, but in the furthest branches of the nerves, as if they were conditions hastening inward from the outside. 

In other places, however, he does not judge anything sulfurous or flammable to be the cause of Apoplexy; instead, he blames only **Mercury** (one, namely, of the three which he calls his **principles of nature** <term>principles of nature: Paracelsus's "Tria Prima"—Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—which he believed composed all things</term>) having circulated too exquisitely...

<summary>Van Helmont critiques the "Schools" and Paracelsus for their theories on the causes of apoplexy and paralysis, specifically questioning the role of phlegm and alchemical "Mercury" or "Sulfur" in the nerves.</summary>

<page-num>184</page-num>

# ON LITHIASIS
<note>Lithiasis: The formation of stony concretions (calculi) in the body, such as kidney stones. While the header indicates this topic, the text continues the philosophical investigation into sensation and pain.</note>

->ON STONE FORMATION<-

<meta>image quality: good; catchword: toes (digi-)</meta>

with his exuberant subtlety, he <note>Referring to Paracelsus</note> asserts the containing cause of every instantaneous death. Elsewhere, he traces **Apoplexy** back to the stars of the heavens. And elsewhere again, varying his opinion, he teaches that all *Apoplexy* arises from thick vapors blocking the arteries of the throat and the restless, pulsing channels; he even claims it is a lunar eclipse of the brain within us, by a microcosmic necessity <note>The "microcosm" is the Hermetic and Paracelsian idea that the human body reflects the structure and events of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</note>. 
<margin>44</margin>
Therefore, he likewise spun the causes of **Vertigo** into uncertainties, being himself entirely dizzy. 

But I have proceeded differently. Whatever primarily feels is the very thing that is the first recipient of pain, and its effectively efficient cause. Now, a sword, a blow, a bruise, corrosives, etc., are indeed occasional or instrumental effective causes; however, they are not the primary efficient causes of pain. Furthermore, pain is mostly born in an instant, even that which is excited by external things. Therefore, for pain to occur, there is no need for a recourse to the brain, as if it had to work by reflection or by deliberation. 

Wherefore the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical establishment following Galen and Aristotle.</note>, receding only slightly from the brain, preferred to take the nerve as the primary organ for perceiving the objects of sense, insofar as the nerves are continuously sprinkled either by a ray of light or by a material bedewing of spirits <note>original: "spirituum irroratione." This refers to "animal spirits," a subtle fluid then believed to flow through nerves to enable sensation.</note>—for they have not yet determined their position on most of these points—sent down from the brain. And so they claim the brain denies sensation and motion to the lower parts unless it incessantly inspires its favor through these mediator spirits. 

But even in this, I find many difficulties. 
<margin>45</margin>
First of all, I explore the different types of touch in man. Specifically, these are almost unique in individual members: indeed, they exist in the intestines and other parts that are almost entirely destitute of any partnership with the nerves. Such are the teeth themselves; for although a tiny nerve touches the root, it does not touch the outside of the teeth, yet many will unwillingly testify that they feel them. Likewise, the ureters <note>The tubes leading from the kidneys to the bladder.</note> lack a nerve, and the skull itself, under the drill of the **trepan** <term>trepan: a surgical tool used to bore holes in the skull</term>, provides a wonderful sensation all the way down to the 
<margin>toes</margin>

### CHAPTER IX.

fingers, it resonates. Therefore, I believed that such a vast breadth of a single sense of touch could not be distributed from a single and common source, the brain, or from a nerve of uniform texture. I thus assumed what I have already proven: that sensation resides primarily in the **sensitive soul**, which is present everywhere, and to that extent it also resides most closely in the **inborn spirit** <note>original: "spiritu insito"; this refers to a vital, governing force that Van Helmont believed lived within the organs themselves, rather than everything being controlled by the brain.</note> of the body's parts. And I asserted this all the more boldly because the brain itself—the workshop of the "flowing spirit"—possesses such a dull and irregular sense of touch that it has been thought to be entirely without sensation. Therefore, either the axiom collapses: *that because of which everything is such, is itself even more such* <note>original: "propter quod unumquodque est tale, illud ipsum est magis tale." This is a rule of logic suggesting that if the brain gives sensation to the body, the brain itself must be the most sensitive organ. Van Helmont notes that since the brain doesn't feel pain when touched, the logic of his opponents fails.</note>, or the brain is not the primary seat and source of touch. Finally, all pain happens in the specific place and is felt, as it were, instantly. Therefore, touch also happens in that place, and not after a report has been made to the head. 

Furthermore, in nature—or at least in a sphere—there is no "right" and "left." Thus, a specific side cannot be affected by a "leakage of phlegm" <note>A reference to the traditional Humoral theory, which suggested that paralysis on one side of the body was caused by cold, thick fluids (phlegm) blocking nerves.</note> in a case of **Paralysis**, unless there were two channels running the length of the single spinal cord, especially at its beginning, to maintain the necessity of sides. This is ridiculous to even think, especially in that slender cavity of the fourth sinus <note>Referring to the fourth ventricle, a small cavity in the brainstem.</note>. Since motion and sensation exist in one and the same muscle, which receives a single and slender nerve. Yet in fingers affected by **numbness** <term>stupor: a loss of feeling or "falling asleep" of a limb</term>, sensation alone is often suspended, while motion meanwhile remains safe and free. Therefore, it is either necessary that sensation and motion do not depend on the same nerve, the flowing spirit, and their common principle; or it is necessary that from that same single little nerve, motion alone depends and not sensation; or sensation alone and not motion; or there are other strange things, unknown until now, which take away or injure sensation alone and not motion; and others that stop motion alone; and some that affect both. ...defect in motion, are sometimes passions arising from a privative medium <note>A "privative" state refers to the absence or deprivation of something necessary for life, rather than the presence of a disease-causing substance.</note>; and that these are also, by necessity, privative. At the very least, his **fourth ventricle** <note>In traditional anatomy, the fourth ventricle of the brain was thought to be a primary reservoir for the "animal spirits" that controlled movement and thought.</note> of the brain was not then filled with fluid, nor was his **spinal cord** compressed, which remained safe within the vertebrae. A certain **Astrologer**, wishing to test whether death by hanging was painful, placed a noose around his own neck. He ordered his young son to watch closely until he moved his thumb after the small stool was pulled from beneath his feet, so that the boy might immediately cut the rope. The boy, with his eyes fixed on his father's fingers and seeing no movement in them, looked up and saw his father turned blue <note>original: "lividum"</note> with his tongue sticking out. Therefore, the rope having been cut, the Astrologer fell to the ground and barely recovered after a month. 

Drowning <note>original: "demersio"</note> proceeds in almost the same manner. In this case, as soon as water is drawn into the lungs through the mouth at the first pull, the use of the mental faculties is lost, and with the repeated drawing of water, the previous state is confirmed. And yet, they do not die so quickly that, if they are laid face down so that the water flows out, even those who have appeared dead for a long time can usually be revived. Therefore, when the pipes of the lungs are filled with a "foreign guest" <note>Referring to the water entering the lungs.</note>, the **vital radius** <note>Van Helmont's term for a beam of life-force or spiritual energy that connects the organs.</note> is intercepted, which shines perpetually from the **midriff** <note>original: "praecordiis"; the area around the heart and diaphragm, which Van Helmont believed was the true seat of the sensitive soul.</note> into the head. From this, consequently, a **privative Apoplexy** <note>An "apoplexy" (stroke) caused by the sudden cut-off of vital energy rather than a physical clot or burst vessel.</note> immediately arises. It is truly a wonder that the mental functions fail so quickly in both cases. And thus from this, the importunity of continued necessity is conjectured, as well as the depen...

## CHAPTER IX.

...dependence, on the aspiration and vital favor of the lower organs—something not yet recognized in the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional academic medical establishment of the 17th century, which followed the ancient teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note>. For this reason, I prepared the treatise *On the Duumvirate* <note>The "Duumvirate" is Van Helmont’s term for the stomach and spleen, which he believed acted as the true functional center of the body's life force.</note>. I considered, therefore, whether the **brain** is the primary source and seat of the immortal soul, the intellect, and the memory. At the very least, as long as the soul remained in the brain, these faculties ought to stay unimpaired; since for thinking, there is no need for the leg, nor the arm, nor even for breathing itself. Nevertheless, hanging—as if by a single blow—totally takes away the faculties of the mind.

<margin>47</margin>
Indeed, when the **jugular arteries** deny communication with the lower parts, or the **lung** is filled with water, the mental faculties immediately collapse. Not only that, but such a blockage acts in the manner of a universal **apoplexy** <note>original: "apoplexia"; a sudden loss of consciousness or movement, often compared to a stroke.</note>, and does not merely suspend movement on one side only, as occurs in **paralysis**. From this, I confirmed for myself that the influence of the lower organs and their communion is taken away from the brain by the interruption of a bond or obstacle. Consequently, I also suspected that the first conceptions are formed elsewhere than in the head. According to that word of truth: *Out of the heart flow adulteries, murders,* etc. <note>original: "Ex corde manant adulteria, homicidia"; a reference to Matthew 15:19, suggesting the heart/torso is the origin of thought and intent.</note>

<margin>48</margin>
I found, moreover, that **apoplexy**, **stupor**, **paralysis**, **vertigo**, the **falling sickness** <term>Falling sickness: a historical term for epilepsy.</term>, **spasm**, etc., are passions arising from a positive occasional cause. They are very different from states caused by the mere deprivation or constriction of the nerves, channels, and spirits. These causes have been neglected by the Schools until now, because they subscribe to the ravings of the pagans—namely, those who house **phlegm** <note>original: "pituitam"</note> in the **fourth ventricle** <term>Fourth ventricle: "sinulo cerebri"; a small cavity in the brain where traditional medicine believed the "animal spirits" were stored or blocked.</term> of the brain. Meanwhile, similar and positive faculties occur everywhere in **opiates**, as well as in drowsy and epileptic diseases. I remember too, at one time during my **apprenticeship** <note>original: "Tyrociniis"; referring to his early chemical experiments and training.</note>, I distilled certain poisonous substances. If the joints of the apparatus were not properly sealed and they emitted an odor, or if later, while separating the vessels from one another, they struck me unawares—

In a single instant, I was ready to fall, overcome by vertigo and a numbness on my right side. Indeed, if that scent had struck me just once more, I would undoubtedly have fallen down apoplectic <note>The author is likely describing an experiment involving toxic fumes where he nearly suffered a stroke or seizure, illustrating how external vapors can affect the brain instantly without the presence of physical "phlegm."</note>. Truly, my ardor for knowledge once drove me to such recklessness that I did not spare my own life a thousand times over. To stay within the terms of my argument: that scent did not stir up any phlegm <note>original: "pituita"; one of the four humors, which traditional medicine blamed for many diseases by causing blockages.</note> threatening to slip down, nor did a new, fresh breath of air remove it from the cavity of the brain. Therefore, if certain simple substances can bring about numbness, dizziness, the cessation of movement, and the clouding of the senses, it is not absurd to suggest that similar things can arise suddenly from within the body itself. Nor is it right to perpetually attribute all these symptoms to the obstructive blockage of phlegm alone. For I recall

<margin>49</margin> an apoplectic patient who died within two hours; because there was a suspicion of poisoning, a dissection was performed. Once the skull was removed, thirteen students gently peeled back the meninges <note>The membranes enveloping the brain and spinal cord.</note>. Then, after the cerebellum was carefully separated, the diligent investigators found no trace of phlegm in the fourth sinus <note>Likely referring to the fourth ventricle of the brain, where Galenic doctors believed blockages caused strokes.</note>, nor had any drained down into the spinal cord. Therefore, I will never be persuaded to believe, as the Schools do, that **Apoplexy** is a phlegmatic blockage of the fourth sinus of the brain; nor will I believe that **Paralysis** is an obstruction on one side of the spinal cord. First of all, the unsuccessful treatments for these diseases betray a lazy investigation of their causes. Furthermore, Apoplexy has been treated so negligently and ignorantly thus far that even today in the Schools it lacks a proper name of its own. Indeed, it retains its name from the "plexus" or network of arteries

<margin>50</margin> dreamed up by Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (c. 129–216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note>—or perhaps passed on to him while he was in a credulous state—a network that anatomy has never yet seen. But his fabricated network fails Galen, proving him a rash speaker of trifles and a ridiculous dissector. # CHAPTER IX.

it stands, that Galen never saw a human corpse dissected: and that he described his doctrine of anatomy from elsewhere, word-for-word, no differently than he did <margin>51</margin> his botanical work from Dioscorides <note>Galen was the primary medical authority of the time; Dioscorides was the authority on herbs. For I followed the doctrine of the Sacred Scriptures: *By their fruits, you shall know them* <note>original: "E fructibus eorum, cognoscetis eos"; Matthew 7:16</note>. That is to say, I learned from experience and from the effect that the origin of Apoplexy is positive <note>meaning an active, specific cause</note>: it is not a "privative" thing, or a blockage of the sinus of the cerebellum caused by phlegm suddenly falling into that place. This is especially true because Apoplexy and Paralysis not infrequently arise from uterine affections <note>Conditions believed to originate in the womb, often referred to at the time as "hysteria."</note>. When remedies for the womb are applied, the symptoms cease; but when they are neglected, the patients are either truly suffocated by apoplexy, <margin>52</margin> or they languish for the rest of their lives in paralysis. I finally recognized that the threshold of Apoplexy is in the *praecordia* <note>The area of the midriff and stomach, which Van Helmont believed was the seat of the body's vital spirit.</note>, and it is in the brain only through <term>deuteropathia</term> <note>A medical term for a "secondary suffering" where one organ is affected because of a disease in another.</note>, by which the brain in turn obeys the governance of the lower parts. For neither emetics, nor even the aforementioned sweat-inducing remedy, remove anything from the rear sinus of the brain, and much less from the narrow cavity of the spinal cord. And this is known by whoever has ever been present at a dissection of those parts. Likewise, fragrant and helpful essences, if drunk, would never be carried up to the head if it were physically blocked; nevertheless...

Because of this, I have come to believe that **Apoplexy**, **Deep Sleeps**, the **Falling Sickness** <note>original: "Caducum"; a historical term for epilepsy.</note>, as well as **hysterical strangulations** <note>A term for "suffocation of the womb," a historical diagnosis for various physical and emotional symptoms in women.</note> and any kind of fainting fits, are diseases arising from <term>deuteropathy</term> <note>A condition where one organ is affected "in sympathy" with another; a secondary reaction.</note> and the "action of the governance"—and not from a physical gathering of humors and vapors born within the cavities of the brain. To be sure, the womb never rises above the **Diaphragm**, yet the apoplectic person suffers. Therefore, there is no physical contact between the womb and the head. # CHAPTER IX.

In a woman, [the womb] suddenly rose above the height of the mind, which is suited neither to humors nor to vapors. Indeed, the appearance of the **uterus** <note>original: "uterus"; Van Helmont is discussing "uterine suffocation" or hysteria, where the womb was thought to move upward and cause choking or fainting.</note> is like that of its own basilisk, by which the parts swell with an influx of fluid (what this fluid is will be taught elsewhere); just as is natural to many other poisons. It is just as the waters rise and swell at each station of the moon, from the look of the leaves of that star. I will describe my own case in this matter. While I was in my 65th year and was focused on the consideration of **Apoplexy** <note>original: "Apoplexia"; historically, a sudden loss of consciousness or "stroke," often attributed by others to the brain, but here questioned by Van Helmont.</note>: I distinguished between the "positive" kind, which would be caused by a "glacial poison," and another kind which afflicts through the blockage of a nerve, and how they might be recognized. It is just like someone who, sitting with his leg twisted, loses sensation in the leg because of the compression of the nerve; while sensation is being restored to him, "stings" or "pins and needles" are felt, caused by the **vital** or **animal spirit** <note>original: "spiritu vitali vel animali"; the subtle substances believed by traditional medicine to carry life and sensation, though Van Helmont argues this specific theory is false.</note> (which is a false concept, as I showed in my book *On Long Life*). But from the numbness that proceeds from a glacial poison, if sensation returns, no pain of stinging or pricking occurs. 

For I was contemplating in my **study** <note>original: "museo"; literally a "museum," but in this period it referred to a scholar's private study or laboratory.</note> during the cold of the first of January, and a pot loaded with a few coals was standing at a distance, so that at least the very frigid winter weather might be mitigated just a little. One of my household arriving at an opportune moment announced the stench of soot, and immediately took the pot away. I immediately felt a threatened **fainting spell** <note>original: "deliquium"; a sudden loss of strength or consciousness.</note> around the orifice of my stomach. Therefore, rising and going out in a single instant, I fell with my body straight onto the stone floor. Thus, both by reason of the **fainting** <note>original: "syncope"; a medical term for a temporary loss of consciousness caused by a fall in blood pressure.</note> and the blow to the **back of the head** <note>original: "occipitis"; the posterior part of the skull.</note>, I was carried away as if I were a corpse. I returned indeed to signs of life after a quarter of an hour: but with a swelling on the back of the head, and the **cranial sutures** <note>original: "suturas cranii"; the fiber-like joints where the bones of the skull meet.</note> began to ache more and more significantly and perceptibly. Both taste and smell were also totally taken away, and my ears rang continuously. Furthermore, at every single thought, immediately my head spun with vertigo, even with my eyes closed.

[...of the soul.] <note>The text begins mid-word, completing a thought from the previous page.</note> Soon after that, all the nerves, all the way down to the calves, were in pain, so much so that a single sneeze would cruelly rack the entire body. My appetite for eating did indeed return, but a **spinning sensation** <note>original: "rotatio"; here meaning vertigo or dizziness.</note> troubled me for several months. I first learned the following: 

1. That in the evening, before dinner, the dizziness increased—namely, around the completion of digestion. 
2. That although my judgment remained sound <note>original: "stante judicio"; his cognitive abilities were unaffected even while his balance failed.</note>, the dizziness nevertheless grew stronger. 
3. That with any kind of vegetables and unsalted fish, the spinning rose up more cruelly. 
4. I noted that a **Turquoise** gem <note>original: "Turcois gemma"; in early modern folklore and medicine, turquoise was believed to protect its wearer from falls or to crack/change color to warn of danger.</note> remained intact when I fell, nor did it preserve me from the danger of the fall. And I learned that a Turquoise does not help unless a sudden fear of falling seizes the person. This does not happen to those for whom a **fainting spell** <note>original: "syncope"</note> precedes and causes the fall. 
5. That the dizziness was caused by foods prone to corruption <note>original: "corruptelæ obnoxiis"; foods that easily "spoil" or ferment in the gut.</note>. 
6. And I seriously noted that **Apoplexy** <note>a sudden loss of consciousness or a stroke</note>, dizziness, and so on, depend on the **hypochondrium** <note>the region of the upper abdomen</note>, although my head alone seemed to be affected by the concussion of the blow, and the dizziness was sensibly felt to spin in my head. 

Nevertheless, since the dizziness was related to food and the quantity of food, I clearly perceived that shortly after the aforementioned fainting spell, there remained around the stomach an "unnatural guest" <note>original: "hospitem præter naturam"; Van Helmont's way of describing a pathological substance or a "foreign" influence on the body's internal processes.</note>, which was the occasional cause of the dizziness. I confirmed this even more strongly by the fact that whenever I had crossed the sea in the past, I felt nauseated at first during storms, but I never vomited or stopped eating; yet after I was back on land and wandering freely, I always perceived a wandering dizziness, day and night, which mimicked the up-and-down motion of the ship. This lasted until I was finally freed by vomiting through the use of **white vitriol** <note>original: "vitrioli albi"; zinc sulfate, used historically as a powerful emetic to clear the stomach.</note>. For indeed, while sailing, no harm had been done to the head; nevertheless, as if I were drunk, I was threatened with a fall by continuous dizziness, while the operation of my judgment remained constant and unharmed. I was always freed from that dizziness by a single instance of vomiting. But now, in the aforementioned fall, the blow indeed caused a swelling in the back of the head and su-

...and future ones, it revealed its effects in the organs of the senses and the nerves. But all these things did not cause the **spinning of the head** <note>original: "rotationem capitis"; vertigo.</note>, which I observed was provoked or worsened primarily by the choice of foods. I noted this especially because, as was the custom, such spinning would be quieted by a single act of vomiting. 

Thus, I experienced in myself that vertigo—even when the head has been injured—is stirred up and nourished by the stomach, and therefore by the **duumvirate** <term>duumvirate: Van Helmont’s term for the partnership between the stomach and the spleen, which he believed governed the body's vital functions.</term>. However, I realized that the fainting spell itself had caused the blow, and at the same time left a "sealed mark" <note>original: "notam sigillarem"; an impression or lasting medical effect.</note> in the "foreign guest" <note>likely referring to the sensitive spirit or vital force</note> detained there. 

Finally, I concluded that this spinning did not come from a "vapor" carried from the lower parts upward <note>Van Helmont is rejecting the traditional Galenic theory that "vapors" rising from the stomach caused headaches and dizziness.</note>, but from a bodily occasion of a "sealed excrement." Whenever something occurred that was less pleasing to those **lower workshops** <note>the digestive organs</note>, their force and "impressive Idea" would overflow into the brain. 

From this, I distinguished two things: 
1. **Intoxicants**, which are carried from the stomach into the arteries and mixed with the vital spirit; these confound the "economy of the spirit" in the cells of the brain and disturb the imagination because exotic and foreign substances actually travel up through the arteries. 
2. **Vertigo-sufferers**, for whom the intellect remains unharmed and intact, even though they endure dizzy spells, however atrocious. 

For the "occasional causes" of these dizzy spells persist in the **hypochondria** <term>hypochondria: In 17th-century medicine, this refers to the upper abdominal region (below the ribs), then thought to be the source of many "nervous" or emotional disorders.</term>. From there, by the power of their governance, they corrupt the brain itself—but they do not corrupt the abstract faculties of the mind, which are immediately "sealed" in the spirits. 

It is just as when an **elk's hoof** <note>original: "ungula Alcis"; a popular 17th-century folk remedy. It was believed that elks suffered from epilepsy and cured themselves by scratching their ears with their hooves; therefore, wearing an elk-hoof ring was thought to prevent seizures.</note> is tied to a finger; it calms that same rigor of the duumvirate in epileptics. I also considered, as if through an optical inspection, how the first concepts were formed in the **praecordia** <note>the area of the diaphragm and upper stomach</note> and were sent from there to the brain to be refined. And finally, I saw how in delirious and insane "hypochondriacs," these things were agitated in different ways without any spinning of the head. In drunks, however, the spinning accompanied their madness. Elsewhere, the rota-

...tion brought about no stumbling of the mind. In other cases, the memory might stumble even while the person remains otherwise healthy. Indeed, as I pondered these things seriously and with much leisure, I discovered that the qualities in the **excrements** <note>In Van Helmont’s chemistry, "excrements" are not just waste, but the byproducts or "leftovers" of any bodily process.</note> themselves follow their own **Ideas**, and individually play out their own scenes. I then clearly understood that the various properties of these qualities are like **seminal gifts** <term>seminal gifts: "dona seminalia," the inherent capacities or blueprints within matter that allow it to grow and act.</term> and true **formal Ideas** <term>formal Ideas: the guiding spiritual patterns or "blueprints" that give shape and function to physical matter.</term>. It is by these that the strength of the **sensitive soul** <note>The "anima sensitiva" in Helmontian thought is the biological life-force that governs feeling and movement, distinct from the immortal mind.</note>—being a member of that same "formal" class—is corrupted, subdued in various ways, and yields to the persistence of these active Ideas. 

Alas! It was then that the **ground of the soul** <note>original: "fundus animae"; a term famously used by the 14th-century mystic Johannes Tauler to describe the deepest, most essential essence of the human spirit.</note> revealed itself to me. This was nothing other than the immortal mind itself, however much it might be wrapped in "outer darkness," like **garments of skins** <note>original: "tunicis pelliceis"; a biblical reference to the skins given to Adam and Eve, here signifying the heavy, physical body that covers the spirit.</note>, insofar as it is bound and housed, for the duration of life, in the "inn" of the sensitive soul. And so, from this I clearly recognized what I explained in a new way (for the honor of God, the contempt of Satan, and the magnificence of the entire human pilgrimage) in my book *On Long Life*. 

<margin>35</margin> 

I also taught in *On Long Life* that the head is the source of **growth** <term>growth: "accretio," the process by which the body adds to its mass.</term> for the parts situated below it (which even hunchbacks confirm), and thus the state and duration of growth are limited by the head. The boundaries are also marked by the hair, and for that reason, heads that are neglected rarely turn gray. I therefore profess with the Schools of medicine that the **vital light** <note>original: "lumen vitale"; the animating force that allows the body to function.</note> is indeed diffused from the brain as if from a fountain, and dispersed through the nerves. When this light is absent, the faculties that reside in their own "inns" <note>the various organs</note> fall silent, and they immediately fall silent whenever they are deprived of it. For although sensation and movement depend in some way on the **inborn spirit** <term>inborn spirit: "spiritu insito," the localized vital force already present in the organs and limbs.</term> of the parts (both for perceiving and for acting), yet each individual part is vitally nourished by the "dew" of light from the brain. Once the thread or ray of this light is cut off, those parts are also immediately cut off.

...sense and movement. But these things only demonstrate a **privative** <note>original: "privativam"; referring to a condition defined by the absence or loss of a normal function, rather than an active disease state.</note> **apoplexy**, which is not so much a truly diseased state as it is an accidental one, just as I showed earlier regarding the dislocation of the vertebrae. For even when its head is removed, a fly continues to flutter for some time. Even when a man is beheaded, the limbs often leap or contract for a long while, and these innate movements
<margin>56</margin>
still show signs of life. But the cause and nature of **positive and diseased apoplexy** is far different. <margin>57</margin>

Yet a single nerve provides both sensation and movement to the four fingers through the tendon of the palm: why then is sensation alone numbed in just one finger? Finally, what vapor—even one arising from the thickest mucus—could ever be thicker than, and not equal to, the vapor that rises from water? Let all the distillers in the world answer that! Why then should a "thick vapor of phlegm" (which I have demonstrated elsewhere to be a non-entity) be required for numbness, rather than simple water or blood? obstructive power of the nerve: it is certainly sent there, or it flows down there of its own accord. If it is sent, it is at least not from the brain, or from the spinal cord which acts as its deputy. For if that were the case, it would not affect a single finger—and specifically the tip of a finger that was healthy just moments before—so suddenly. Nor is that vapor sent by the vital spirit, the steward of life <note>original: "spiritu vitæ œconomo"; the vital force responsible for governing the body's internal functions.</note>, who would much more willingly and promptly have cast it out through perspiration <note>original: "diaphæresin"; the process of sweating or excreting waste through the skin.</note>. <margin>58</margin>
easily, effectively, and directly sent it through the skin’s natural and ordinary exit <note>original: "emunctorium"; a term for any organ or part of the body that carries off waste products.</note>, as is its custom. For the channel of the nerves, following a straight line and continuous path, ends in the spinal cord <note>original: "medullam spinalem"</note>; it does not have any transverse tubes through which it could transmit that phlegmatic <note>original: "pituitosum"</note> vapor sideways (for otherwise there would be a total loss of the spirits before they reached the muscle that executes movement). Far be it that such a vapor should flee in that direction. Therefore, that humor or vapor cannot be transmitted to a single finger or descend there (and much less
<margin>59</margin>
leap into it suddenly) unless it travels through the passage of the nerves that they share with the spinal cord. But it is like a dream—that is, absurd—to imagine that in a healthy body, one not currently complaining of illness, the sensation of a finger should suddenly fail because of phlegm <note>original: "pituitam"</note> that was not previously noticed in the higher nerves. Or, alternatively, that it was caused by a vapor produced in some irregular way—not sent or descending there—just as it was not generated in that part instantly. For any guest of foreign origin is manifestly troublesome to nature from the very start. But perhaps that phlegm, or that vapor, crept sideways into the nerve at the tip of the finger? If so, then the axiom of the Schools would perish, which states that the Impulse...

ascribes the distributions of any humors to the one performing this action. For truly, these humors do not exist within us, nor in the nature of things: and even if they did exist, they would not possess the power of self-movement <note>original: "vis ambulatoria"; the ability to move from place to place of one's own accord.</note>: and much less so when they are already waste products <note>original: "excrementitiis"</note>: because all natural movements within us obey the vital faculties. For, if phlegm <note>original: "pituita"; in traditional medicine, one of the four humors, often blamed for "cold" diseases.</note> and its thick vapor were present in nature—at least as they are regarded in this context by the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the established university medical authorities who followed the ancient teachings of Galen and Aristotle.</note> as morbid waste—they would have no way to travel, no spontaneous motion, and no path forward: therefore, they would necessarily have to be driven by something else. Certainly not by the Archeus <note>original: "Archeo"; Van Helmont's term for the "vital workman" or internal governing spirit that directs the body's biological processes.</note>, who, since he directs everything properly and well, would not drive toward the nerves that which he is otherwise accustomed to lead regularly toward the skin. Perhaps, then, the phlegm is thinned into a vapor by heat and moves upward? But then it would not go downward into a hanging finger. At the very least, according to the Schools' own theorem regarding catarrhs <note>original: "catarrhis"; traditionally believed to be "down-flowings" of waste fluids from the brain to other parts of the body.</note>, that vapor would immediately condense back into droplets: it would not wander about in the form of a vapor toward the extremities of the nerves, nor would it hurry through the palm of the hand toward only a single finger. 

But why would it rush suddenly, like a weight, into a little nerve thinner than a thread? One, I say, and not another? If, indeed, the vapor enters from the side, why is it absorbed in a single instant without any prior discomfort? Why is it thrust into the tip of a tiny nerve—enclosed in its own membrane—rather than into the flesh? Why does the cause, which produced a single "atom" of phlegm or thick breath, not immediately produce another besides that single one? For often that sudden numbness <note>original: "stupor"</note> begins from the little finger and finally ends there, or it might reach the third or fourth finger. Frequently, too, all the fingers suddenly take on a deathly paleness over half their length or more, with or without numbness, while their movement grows sluggish, etc. If, therefore, this were caused by vaporous matter, it certainly would not happen in the brain or the spinal cord <note>original: "Nucha"; a medieval anatomical term often used for the spinal marrow or the nape of the neck.</note>. Indeed, in that case, it would necessarily—

Nor am I persuaded why, in the tranquility of health, that vapor—existing outside the nerve—would be pressed inward toward the nerve or tendon, especially when there is otherwise a constant outward <term>diaphæreſis <gloss>perspiration or the passage of sweat through the pores</gloss></term> within us. Why, if it is propagated from a single small nerve into all of them, does it not afflict them all at once, but rather subsequently and a long time after? Why is the **sense of touch** damaged but not the **ability to move**, if it is brought about by a single and equal cause, and delivered through one small nerve that is the author of both motion and sensation? The cold of the hands alone causes numbness from without and pain within, without any falling in of vapors or humors. Finally, nerves are not inserted into the fingers, but into the tendons; why then is sensation damaged and not motion? Why does the numbness not extend at the same time through the entire palm, which is covered by a single tendon? If the tendons suffer this threatened <term>paralyſin <gloss>paralysis; a loss of muscle function or sensation</gloss></term>, then the cause has moved away from the shared nature of the nerves toward the dense, unperforated, and non-channeled tubes of the tendons.

<margin>60</margin>
They are not passable, I say, and therefore not subject to the falling in of <term>pituitæ <gloss>phlegm; one of the four traditional humors often blamed for "cold" or "sluggish" diseases</gloss></term>. # CHAPTER IX.

By a substitution of right and action, does he want the delegates to go to the fingers of their own side, or to those opposite? Therefore, will an obstructed **Spleen** evaporate more toward the brain and the <term>nucha <gloss>the spinal cord or the nape of the neck</gloss></term> than a healthy, unobstructed one, burdened by continuous **black bile**? 
<margin>61</margin>
Certainly, I have followed the instances of partial insensitivity and numbness so that we might more rightly understand total **Apoplexy**. In the meantime, I pity the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical authorities who followed the teachings of Galen and Avicenna, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note> for not having more accurately examined their own figments of humors and vapors, and the so hasty and ridiculous "settling" of these things. Nor did they once consider that, just as the cold of the surrounding air is narcotic, they failed to distinguish the nature of paralysis and similar *positive* passions of the nerves from similar *privative* ones. From this, they might have learned that positive affections can by no means exist without a narcotic, dead matter and quality. If that quality is sufficient for creating numbness when it touches the sensitive parts from the outside, what will it not achieve if it strikes the nerve itself locally? Indeed, if touching it merely as a vapor (according to the Schools) it should produce a soon-to-perish effect on the sense, why did they not at least once think that it could stir up a stubborn and durable paralysis with a more tenacious matter? Furthermore, wherever such an <term>anodyne <gloss>a pain-killing or numbing agent</gloss></term> is shut up within the **Duumvirate** of the body 
<margin>62</margin>
(I mean the stomach and the spleen), it will trigger a sudden <term>syncope <gloss>fainting or loss of consciousness</gloss></term> and a positive apoplexy. 

**Paralysis**, however, is usually of one side only, and the failure rushes in like a single stroke of an arrow. Moreover, the speed of such an unexpected fall produces terror in the brain and the **medullas** <note>original: "medullis"; referring here to the spinal marrow or spinal cord.</note>. That is, in the spirit which is the inhabitant of these and the author of that sensation. Therefore, on account of its own terror, the weaker side of the spinal marrow is contracted. **Paralysis** is surely the product of that contraction. 
<margin>63</margin>
And in all people, one side is always weaker than the other. For this reason, women, who are for the most part of a timid mind, frequently collapse into paralysis through terror.

...Paralysis, without Apoplexy. For terror has this property: if it is sudden, it immediately closes the pores. "The hair stood on end, and the voice stuck in the throat" <note>This is a famous line from Virgil's *Aeneid* (2.774), describing a physical reaction to extreme fear.</note>. This is because it is natural for the door to be closed against an approaching enemy. Indeed, in a blow to the skull, the underlying side is loosened, while the opposite side is contracted. The underlying side is loosened because it is more terrified; the opposite side is contracted because it is irritated. 

<margin>64</margin> 
Truly, the common people are accustomed to predict **Apoplexy** <gloss>a sudden loss of consciousness or a stroke</gloss> from the shortness of a person's neck. However, a short neck does not prove that there are fewer vertebrae, but rather that each individual vertebra has a shallower depth. But what does that have in common with **phlegm**? Or with a future obstruction of the fourth sinus of the brain? Should one really be predicted as the cause of the other? For shortness of the neck does not contain a mere sign or a prediction based on **Physiognomy** <gloss>the practice of judging health or character from outward appearance</gloss>; rather, it contains an occasional cause. 

For often after "yesterday’s overindulgence" <note>original: *crapulam*, referring to a hangover or the effects of excessive drinking and eating</note>, dizziness, <term>scotomia <gloss>a dimming of vision or dark spots in the eyes</gloss></term>, vomiting, and numbness of the fingers occur. These things threaten and foretell Apoplexy—not indeed because of a "suitable organ" (as with the shortness of the neck), but because they are a prelude to Apoplexy, differing from it only in degree and intensity. 

If, therefore, that dizziness and numbness after overindulgence comes from the <term>praecordia <gloss>the area of the chest around the heart and diaphragm</gloss></term>, the "occasional matter" is fostered by the **Archeus** <gloss>the internal vital spirit or "master-workman" that Van Helmont believed governed the body's functions</gloss> at a lower degree. Therefore, whenever this <term>anodyne poison <gloss>a numbing or narcotic substance produced within the body</gloss></term> is raised to a higher degree, it causes Apoplexy, which is born from those same seats. By an error of the **sixth digestion** <note>Van Helmont proposed a system of six stages of digestion; the sixth occurred in the individual tissues and members of the body</note>, this anodyne substance is made from food, from which eventually **Paralysis** also arises occasionally. 

Shortness of the neck, therefore, provides a shorter path and a readiness for this substance to move from the internal organs into the head, as is required for Apoplexy. That is, it allows for a more ready passage through the organs. Indeed, the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical authorities following Galen and Avicenna</note> assert that in minor or threatened strokes, the established practice of rubbing the extremities was beneficial when...

He explains that a short neck is a risk factor not because of "humors," but because it provides a shorter physical path for these toxins to reach the brain.</summary>
