sometimes, and from there conjecturing a <term>revulsion <gloss>a medical technique intended to "draw" harmful humors away from one part of the body to another</gloss></term> of **phlegm** and vapors from the head, they prescribe **rubbings**, even to the point of a cruel flaying of the skin, and sharp **enemas**. They skin the person, forsooth, so that sensation does not fail in that spot. Meanwhile, they forget their own rule,
<margin>66</margin> that sensation depends entirely upon the brain: and it is done in vain for the legs to be flayed in order to "pull away" <note>original: *revellant*, referring to the practice of revulsion</note> the phlegm from the **fourth sinus** of the brain. For they do not know toward what place they should pull it. Should they draw it into the anus from the sinus of the cerebellum via enemas; or truly, should they drive that same phlegm out from the sinus of the cerebellum through the skin by rubbing? The whole thing is ridiculous, because the Schools themselves are ridiculous! Meanwhile, let the bystanders witness with me whether they are tearing away the skin rather than the vapors. I know for certain, however, that even if someone were totally flayed, **Apoplexy** <gloss>a sudden loss of consciousness or stroke</gloss> or true **Paralysis** would by no means be removed. Nor do I see how
<margin>67</margin> they defend their theorem: namely, that phlegm in the fourth sinus of the brain is the "containing and adequate" <note>This is Scholastic terminology for a direct and sufficient cause.</note> cause of both these evils. Indeed, I believe that rubbings help very little when that <term>anodyne <gloss>pain-numbing or narcotic</gloss></term> and cadaverous poison is located in the constitution of the body alone. But what will those cruel rubbings do if that "anodyne" is primarily situated in the **midriff** <note>original: *præcordiis*, the area around the heart and diaphragm, often considered the seat of vital spirits</note>? And how do they prove that phlegm is drawn from the sinus of the cerebellum through rubbings? I know, therefore, that rubbings—being instituted without discretion, without knowledge of causes, and without distinction of locations—have been, and always will be, useless. For it is truly ridiculous and cruel to have rubbed the skin to the point of flaying, while assigning the cause of the condition to an obstruction in the very center of the spinal cord. Because no matter how much rubbing is done, if there were any phlegm in the world that had slipped into the aforementioned sinus of the cerebellum, rubbing would never remove it by even a single grain. Rather, according to their own hypotheses, it would continuously increase it. For it would draw the matter more downward (if
<margin>68</margin> "revulsion" contains any truth),

<summary>Van Helmont ridicules the Galenic practice of "revulsion," specifically the use of painful skin-rubbing and enemas to treat apoplexy and paralysis. He argues that flaying a patient's legs cannot possibly remove phlegm from the brain's cavities or the spinal cord.</summary>

and it will strike into the canal of the spinal marrow, at that part where it always becomes narrower—and all the more so because, ordinarily, the distribution of the larger vessels is made into their lower and smaller branches. Then also, because **phlegm** <gloss>original: *pituita*, a thick, cold humor</gloss> of this kind, once separated from the rest of the blood, would be mere waste-matter, and therefore not capable of being dispersed without leaving behind a <term>caput mortuum <gloss>literally "dead head"; the useless chemical residue or dross left after a process</gloss></term>, or a far harder residue. 

Therefore, if **friction** <gloss>medical rubbing</gloss> should draw or pull it away in any manner, it will also feel that ordinary effort of nature—not by a retrograde motion moving upward from the rear and lower cavity toward the brain, but rather that this obstructing phlegm would be drawn toward the narrower and lower tubes of the **spinal cord** <note>original: *Nuchae*, a term often used in medieval Latin for the spinal marrow</note>. This is especially true while in **Paralysis**, the **sensitive spirits** <gloss>subtle fluids thought to carry sensation through the nerves</gloss> flow down sparingly, or not at all, which might otherwise be able to propel that phlegm. Friction, therefore, insofar as it exhausts the lack of sensitive spirits, will rather cause them to accumulate. 

<margin>69</margin> However, an apoplectic **numbing-force** <gloss>original: *anodynum*, typically a painkiller, but here used for a pathological loss of sensation</gloss> is generated in the same manner as other natural things. To be sure, some waste-matter occasionally arises by the specific path of the material; but the **Archeus** <term>Archeus <gloss>Van Helmont's term for the "internal master-workman" or vital force that governs the body’s functions</gloss></term>, sensing that waste-matter and shuddering at it, flees. Conceiving a **death-like image** <note>original: *ideam cadaverosam*</note> of the waste, the Archeus impertinently imprints it upon itself. From this, as if by the strike of a spear, **Apoplexy** is immediately stirred up. 

Most often, several previous dispositions precede the birth of this numbing-force. 
<margin>70</margin> If these dispositions occurred in the brain, there would be no room for the complaint that **Apoplexy** happens in an instant—which is why we call it **the stroke** <note>original: *Den schlag*</note>—because, to be sure, it comes upon one unexpectedly, in the manner of a blow. Therefore, the place where **Apoplexy** is born is in the **vitals** <term>praecordia <gloss>the area of the heart and diaphragm, considered the seat of vital heat</gloss></term>; and for that reason, it has warning signs such as dizziness, numbness, nausea, etc. 

The location of **Apoplexy**, therefore, is in the **Archeus** of the vitals; but in individual parts, it manifests as a particular numbness. Because of an error in digestion, the fluid that is next to be assimilated—

<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

### CHAPTER IX.

<margin>71</margin>
because of a failure of the **Archeus** <note>The "Archeus" is the internal "master-builder" or vital spirit that Van Helmont believed governed all bodily functions</note>, it degenerates into a **numbing poison** <gloss>original: *venenum anodynum*</gloss>, and it becomes the material cause of such a great evil—a waste matter, I say, that is **sealed** <note>Helmont uses "sealed" to describe how a disease-state or "idea" is permanently stamped onto the body's spirit</note>. By the **Idea** of the shuddering Archeus, it is sealed into the dregs, carrying with it a memory of its own hostility that lasts as long as the disease itself. That it does not depart from there, nor obey the remedies known to the **Apothecary** <gloss>original: *Seplasia*, referring to the famous perfume and drug market of ancient Capua</gloss>, let the **Quartan fever** <gloss>a form of malaria where the fever recurs every four days</gloss> itself teach us; for until now, to the disgrace of physicians, it repeats its performance at its own pleasure. 

If the Quartan fever is incurable to the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional medical universities following Galen and Aristotle, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized</note>, how much more so is **Apoplexy** <gloss>a sudden loss of consciousness or stroke</gloss>. For an Apoplectic numbing-force is indeed milder in itself than an **Epileptic** one, but it infests the body far more terribly. For besides stupors, it strikes the mind, and produces a deep sleep and a **frozen state** <gloss>original: *catochum*, a condition similar to catalepsy where the body remains in one position</gloss>. But if, in addition, it acquires a sharpness, it gives birth to malignant ulcers alongside the deadening caused by the numbing poison. 

Because this poison is **harsh** <gloss>original: *ponticum*, an alchemical term for a tart or stinging quality</gloss>, it threatens **atrophies** <gloss>wasting away of the body</gloss>. Indeed, I observed a **Chemist** <gloss>original: *Chymicum*</gloss> who for a long time had been occupied with **royal waters** <note>original: *aquas regias*, a highly corrosive mixture of acids used to dissolve gold</note>; he fell into terrible palpitations of the heart, and finally into pains in his arms. His mouth was twisted toward the right side in a convulsion, and he suffered from sleepless nights and deep pains in his arms, which were not made worse by being touched. 

He had also wasted away with significant thinness because of the harsh qualities he had absorbed from the acid waters. Meanwhile, any external remedies were tried in vain (for I spared no expense or servants for his sake); but by **laudanum** <note>An opium-based medicine</note> alone, administered for thirteen days, he was fully restored; he soon regained his physical condition and his former strength. For because the harsh bitterness of those liquids had contaminated the **sensitive spirit** <gloss>the subtle fluid believed to carry sensation through the nerves</gloss>, the result of which tormented the Archeus, his twisted mouth and his fingers being distorted to the side resembled a form of Apoplexy. But because the disease did not arise from the governor of the **midriff** <gloss>original: *praecordiorum*, the area around the heart and diaphragm</gloss>, but rather only the vapors of the acid waters had mixed themselves with the flowing sensitive spirit, that man’s Apoplexy was not complete, although otherwise

<header>ON THE STONE DISEASE</header>

# ON THE STONE DISEASE

<margin>72</margin> ...quite dizzying. However, what I call a **pontic** <note>Van Helmont uses "pontic" here to describe a sharp or astringent quality, often associated with specific regions or chemical properties</note> numbing agent is bitter in **Opium**, but not so in **Henbane** (*Hyoscyamus*) or **Mandrake**; yet it is very sweet in **Vitriol** <note>Metallic sulfates, often described as having a "sweet" aftertaste in early chemistry</note> and **Sulfur**. This especially reveals the error of the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities following Galenic and Aristotelian doctrines</note>, as they guess the powers of medicines <gloss>original: *simplicium*, meaning raw medicinal herbs or minerals</gloss> based only on common flavors. Indeed, I know that the cycles or stages of maturity in things have not yet been sufficiently examined, nor will the truth please everyone over their cherished falsehoods. Therefore, I will set down some of the anxieties that the traditional rules of the Schools concerning **Apoplexy** <gloss>a sudden loss of consciousness or stroke</gloss> caused me.

<margin>73</margin> For while I was occupied more than usual with the testing of minerals, I experienced—due to the fumes of certain ones—an instantaneous **Apoplexy**, along with a loss of function on my left side. I would have fallen headlong if I had breathed the air of that place just one more time. Because of this, I first learned that **Paralysis** is not subsequent to **Apoplexy** in terms of duration <note>Traditional medicine taught that paralysis was a lingering "leftover" effect of a stroke; Helmont argues they happen at the exact same moment</note>. Furthermore, I learned that there is no **blockage** <gloss>original: *oppilationem*</gloss> in the cavities of the brain. 

For I was already nearly collapsing; and if I had not turned my head away from the vessel from which the foul vapor breathed, I would have fallen as an apoplectic, and I was ready to drop. Even then, my arm was failing me, and my leg was numb, failing in both sensation and movement. But the Schools will never respond to this: if no **phlegm** <gloss>original: *pituita*</gloss> had ever fallen into the fourth cavity of the brain <note>In Galenic anatomy, the fourth ventricle was considered the seat of the "animal spirits," and blockages there were thought to cause strokes</note>, how could the effect exist in me before its cause? 

Or if some of it [phlegm] had fallen, filling at least half of the cavity, why did that phlegm retreat so quickly? Why, by the same logic, is every case of Apoplexy not cured spontaneously as soon as its causative phlegm vanishes? But if they prefer to dodge the issue by saying my Apoplexy came from a foul vapor rather than phlegm, then why was that foul smoke carried more quickly to the fourth cavity than to the forward ones, which are closer to the nostrils and more receptive? Unless, perhaps, the forward ones were diseased, sluggish, and without sensation? On the contrary, all the nerves assigned to the senses alone receive from the forward [cavities]—

<summary>Van Helmont recounts a personal laboratory accident where inhaling mineral vapors caused instant paralysis, using the experience to debunk the Galenic theory that strokes are caused by physical phlegm blocking the brain's ventricles.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R IX.</header>

## CHAPTER IX.

...to the cavities of the sensitive spirits. <note>In the medical theory of the time, "sensitive spirits" were a refined vapor or fluid that traveled through the nerves to enable sensation.</note> In the anterior ventricles of the brain, however, I perceived no sign of injury: yet one cannot reach the fourth ventricle from the outside except through all the anterior ones. <note>The ventricles are hollow spaces in the brain; the "fourth" is located near the brainstem, deeper than the "anterior" ones near the forehead.</note> Likewise, my senses (though dulled on one side) and my movement remained intact, as did the judgment that urged my retreat. Therefore, had the **phlegm** <gloss>original: *pituita*, one of the four humors</gloss> been waiting for some years at the edge of the fourth cavity, so that—once the smell of that smoke was repeated, like a trumpet sounding the signal for battle—it might rush headlong into the hollow? Why then, while I was leaping away as a fugitive, did the phlegm not fall? For in the <margin>74</margin> **falling sickness** <gloss>original: *caduco*, or epilepsy</gloss>, the principal powers of the soul collapse, and sensation is lost on both sides, while motion alone remains—even though every nerve, even those dedicated to motion, also possesses sensation. Therefore, the brain and all its cavities ought to be affected on both sides when the internal senses, along with the external ones, are extinguished as if put to sleep. How, then, does motion alone persevere? How is it that in **epilepsy**, **apoplexy**, and **paralysis**, the senses are lulled, while in apoplexy and paralysis, the organ of motion is only occupied for one half of the body? 

The Schools will say that in epilepsy, the front parts of the brain suffer while the rear parts remain safe. First of all: why then are the limbs contracted if the organs of motion are free? Memory is damaged most of all in the "falling sickness": will it then be located only in the **forehead**? <gloss>original: *sincipite*</gloss> But even if we grant what they ask, why then does every nerve dedicated to motion—leaping from the back of the brain through the **spinal cord** <gloss>original: *medulla spinae*</gloss>—lose sensation but not motion? Therefore, the brain is struck just as much in the back as in the front during the falling sickness, caused by forces in the **precordia**. <note>The *precordia* refers to the area of the chest and upper abdomen, including the heart and stomach; Helmont believed many brain disorders actually originated here.</note> For often a dying person still feels, speaks, and hears, even though motion in the lower parts was taken away long before, having been unfolded through the nerves of the spinal cord. Even when the brain is perfectly healthy, a sudden **fainting fit** <gloss>original: *syncope*</gloss> often rushes up from below, and both sensation and motion fail in a single instant. If that were caused by fumes, sensation ought to fail first, and then motion gradually, because the anterior cavities of the brain are closer to the mouth of the stomach.

<summary>Van Helmont uses his own experience with toxic fumes to challenge the traditional Galenic view that epilepsy and paralysis are caused by phlegm blocking the brain's ventricles. He argues that these conditions often originate in the "precordia" (the chest/stomach region) rather than the brain itself.</summary>

1. The brain does not feel through itself, and scarcely in itself.

sensation. <note>These are the meninges.</note> So much so that 
this very sharp sensation and majesty of great authority is 
found everywhere: in the stomach, the womb, the <term>meninges</term>

intestines—namely, in the bare membranes, etc.

2. Its correlative is this: The <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The subtle, 
vapor-like substance believed to travel through the nerves to 
enable movement and thought.</note>, as long as it is formed or 
wanders within the cavities of the brain, does not feel, nor does 
it become a partaker of sensation; therefore the brain is made so.

3. That <term>animal spirit</term> does not receive sensation 
from the brain, since the brain itself lacks sensation. 
And consequently, that spirit does not receive the final touch 
of its perfection and sensation in the cavities of the brain.

4. The <term>spinal cord</term>, in its internal core, is a 
continued substance of the brain, and is therefore clothed 
with a membrane continuous with the <term>meninges</term>.

5. Every nerve, therefore, is medullary within...

# C H A P T E R I X.

on the outside, however, it is covered by its own little membrane. 6. It is believed that the **spinal cord** <gloss>original: *spinalis medulla*</gloss>, through its middle, is permeable as long as one lives, through which the **motive spirit** <note>A subtle, vaporous substance believed by early modern physicians to transmit commands from the brain to the muscles.</note> is distributed and extended equally along the length of that marrow and the nerves. Indeed, this vital light of its own, radiating outward, carries the command or the "nod" of the **will** <gloss>original: *voluntatis*</gloss> to the muscles, the executive organ of that movement which the soul has arbitrarily proposed for itself. 7. For although movements occur by the service of the spirits, their obedience would be far too slow. Therefore, by a single ray of light, the command or "nod" of the soul is carried in an instant. Just as objects of sight are perceived in a moment, even from a distance. 8. Since there is no sensation—or at least it is "deaf"—in the brain itself, yet it is most acute in the **Meninges** <term>Meninges</term> <note>The three membranes (dura mater, arachnoid, and pia mater) that line the skull and vertebral canal and enclose the brain and spinal cord.</note>; therefore, the light of sensation does not flow through the medullary and central substance of the nerve and its tube. Instead, the **sensitive soul** radiates sensation, and it is communicated primarily from the Meninges, through the membranes that are the coverings of the nerves, to the parts contiguous and annexed to the nerve. 9. Therefore, by a double substance and a double "nod" of sensitivity, the light is formed, radiating to the attendants of sensation and motion. From this it happens that sensation may be harmed while motion remains safe, or vice versa, because of the diversity of the shared light carried through different organs. For this reason, the Most High is never to be praised enough, who placed such noble faculties in the membranes of the brain, the stomach, and the womb—faculties containing within themselves life, the soul, and the whole governance of man! <margin>75</margin> Indeed, if there is a fundamental truth in **Chiromancy** <note>The art of palm reading.</note> or **Physiognomy** <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or health from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note>, there are lines, both on the forehead and in the hand, which sometimes portend a future **Apoplexy** <term>Apoplexy</term> <note>A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note>. There is, however, a "signed" mark <note>original: *signatum*; referring to the "Doctrine of Signatures," the belief that physical signs on the body or in nature reveal hidden truths or future ailments.</note> of this kind,

## ON THE STONE

...marked by the **Signer** <note>original: *Signante*; in Helmont’s philosophy, this refers to the creative power (Nature or God) that stamps a specific identity or "signature" onto a created thing.</note>, who naturally constitutes us.
However, the **Archeus** <term>Archeus</term> <note>The internal "master-builder" or vital spirit that Van Helmont believed governed all physiological processes.</note> of the seed cannot foresee effects of this kind; especially those arising from accidental chance (suppose if **anger**, a **disordered life**, and **excessive use of Tobacco** brought about the beginnings of **Apoplexy** <term>Apoplexy</term> <note>A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note>). Therefore, it is at least necessary that the principles of Apoplexy are not from a "privative" <note>A cause defined by the absence of something.</note> cause, if they are hidden within the seminal beginnings themselves, and are destined to erupt at the time of their maturity.
Which is to say: *Apoplexy* actually lies hidden in the Archeus, or the seed, in the manner of **hereditary diseases**, and so it spreads through entire families. At the very least, let it be known that *Apoplexy* is not a blockage <margin>77</margin> of a passage caused by **phlegm** <term>pituita</term> <note>The humor of phlegm, which traditional Galenic medicine blamed for "clogging" the brain and causing strokes.</note>, nor is it a "privative" effect; rather, it consists of true and seminal principles. Moreover, an obstructing phlegm (if such a thing were in a person) or a blockage depending on it, does not pre-exist in the seed, and much less would it be fit to outline such late-arriving, monstrous effects in the fetus. And so, they [the moderns] very much exclude the idea of phlegm slipping into the **fourth ventricle** <gloss>original: *quartum sinum*</gloss> of the **Brain**. And consequently, they also exclude the **Academies** <note>The established medical schools and universities of the time.</note>, who have until now remained ignorant of the cause of this disease and its remedy. Finally, it is not even <margin>78</margin> intelligible by what means or gap nature could so restrain phlegm (a disobedient and non-vital excrement) to only one side of that tiny and narrow ventricle, such that it would never flow down to the opposite side by its own weight and the fluidity of its liquid. Indeed, since *Paralysis* occurs on the right side, the patient is then always lying on the left: it would therefore be impossible for that phlegm not to immediately fall toward the left side, which would either kill the patient or at least produce a "walking" or shifting *Paralysis*. Why, after all, would that ventricle always drive that phlegm to the right or left side, but never forward or backward? Especially since in nature, there is no inherent "right" or "left"—

<summary>Van Helmont argues that apoplexy and paralysis are caused by inherent "seminal" disorders in the Archeus rather than physical blockages of phlegm, noting that gravity would move a liquid blockage if it existed.</summary>

-> ### CHAPTER IX. <-

...our [constitution]; but all things, with respect to the whole, are round. From this, it is clear that in the organs themselves—namely in the **vital Archeus** <term>Archeus</term> <note>The internal "master-builder" or vital spirit that Van Helmont believed governed all physiological processes.</note>—and not in the fictional **phlegm** <note>original: *pituita*; one of the four traditional humors that Galenic doctors blamed for most diseases.</note> of that sinus, lies hidden the effective reason why the **Apoplectic Archeus** always turns its follower, **Paralysis**, to the side. Indeed, whatever the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical universities of the 17th century, which Helmont frequently criticized for following ancient Greek theories blindly.</note> have dreamed regarding the fourth small cavity of the **Brain** is ridiculous.

<margin>79</margin>
Therefore, the entire reason for the variety in these diseases depends on how the **Archeus** forms and perfects the **seminal idea** <note>The internal blueprint or "seed" of a disease that directs how it develops in the body.</note>, which it mostly finds somewhat corpse-like in foods and their transformations. Then, indeed, it causes dizziness; and these are more persistent if the same happens in the excrements on the journey from food to nourishment. **Apoplexy** <term>Apoplexy</term> <note>A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note> is by far the most ready to strike when it forms its own sealed idea in the **Archeus** of the **Duumvirate** <term>Duumvirate</term> <note>Van Helmont’s term for the two-part governing center of the body, located in the stomach and spleen.</note> itself. Because the entire Archeus in the internal organs is immediately made corpse-like, as it were.

<margin>80</margin>
From what has been unfolded, I finally conclude that **Apoplexy** is in no way a "privative" disease <note>A disease defined by the absence of something (like the absence of flow).</note>. Far different from this are the blockages of the nerves, such as in the twisting of the vertebrae, hanging, etc. Nor does either disease arise from an obstruction of the **fourth sinus** <note>A cavity or channel.</note> in the cerebellum, at the beginning of the **spinal cord**.

<margin>81</margin>
Instead, **Apoplexy** is generated occasionally from a poisonous, pain-killing, and corpse-like prelude conceived in the **precordia** <note>The region around the heart and diaphragm, which Helmont saw as a primary site of vital activity.</note>. When this has acquired its perfection and required maturity there, it infects the **Archeus** of that place, which immediately, by that very fact, overcomes and strikes down the powers of the brain. However, it is not because the brain primarily suffers and draws the subordinate parts into a conspiracy for its own death.

<margin>82</margin>
**Paralysis**, however, is a contraction of the sensitive parts caused by terror alone. In the specific weakening of the limbs, this is clear. In those parts, local generations of the aforementioned **Apoplectic poison** occur.

<margin>83</margin>
Furthermore, the **Schools** remember only one kind of **anodyne** <note>A pain-relieving substance or state.</note>, which is sleep-inducing, narcotic, so... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword indicates the next word is "loque," likely part of *loquuntur* (they speak).</note>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that stroke and paralysis are caused by the corruption of the Archeus in the digestive organs or heart, rather than by physical blockages in the brain as traditional Galenic medicine claimed. He asserts that these conditions arise from "corpse-like" toxins that overwhelm the body's vital spirit.</summary>

...[in a certain] degree, distinguished between **Opium**, **Mandrake** <note>original: *Mandragora*; a root often used as a narcotic and anesthetic in the early modern period.</note>, and **Henbane** <note>original: *Hyoscyamus*; a poisonous plant in the nightshade family used for its sedative effects.</note>, not because they deny—even if they pass over—many other simple substances. For there are some things which, in a short duration of time, take away the senses and the health of the mind while leaving motion intact, as seen in **epileptics** <term>epileptics</term> <note>Those suffering from "the falling sickness."</note>. Some only cloud motion; others cloud both sense and motion; and many also cause madness while leaving the senses and motion alone. These do not need to be named here, nor do others that are intoxicating. 

Furthermore, the fluid to be assimilated by us is easily infected in the **chest** <note>original: *praecordiis*; the region of the heart and diaphragm, considered a seat of emotion and vitality.</note> by the **Archeus** <term>Archeus</term> <note>The internal "master-builder" or vital spirit that governs the body's functions.</note> when it conceives a corpse-like, pain-killing image. A varied fate of poison is born into fellowship with this image, which is frequently seen during the **Plague**. In other places, however, it does not strike the head but is "sealed" into the physical constitution: where then, by the same privilege of degeneration, the icy virus of **Leprosy** is born. 

But when this pain-killing influence is in the **Duumvirate** <term>Duumvirate</term> <note>Van Helmont's term for the "dual-rule" of the stomach and spleen, which he believed was the true center of life and governance in the body, rather than the brain.</note>, it throws the brain into a tailspin and, according to its variety, generates **dizziness**, the **falling sickness** <note>original: *caducum*; epilepsy.</note>, **palpitations**, **fainting**, **trances** <note>original: *catochos*; a state of rigid unconsciousness or catalepsy.</note>, and **Apoplexy** <term>Apoplexy</term> <note>A stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note>. Just as fear of the parts accompanies these, so too does **paralysis**. Outside of the **Duumvirate**, however, it kills its own seat with numbness and **cold gangrene**, etc.

<margin>84</margin>
Therefore, those who strive to restrain **mania** <note>Insanity or psychosis.</note> with **opiates** err significantly. For every opiate is, in itself, mindless. Because madness is nothing but a "waking dream." Indeed, scarcely a tenfold dose of **opium** can bring sleep to a madman, while with a smaller dose, nothing is achieved. But if sleep should creep up on a madman because of an increased dose, it will only increase the madness and the sleep of the wakeful, as well as the scattered vanities of dreams. 

<margin>85</margin>
However, the **Schools** <note>The traditional Galenic medical universities.</note> were deceived by sleep that comes to a madman of its own accord. For as that sleep arises from a good cause, so it is a precursor and index of health, promising that the madness is about to be dissolved. Add to this that in **opium**, besides the sleep-inducing quality, there is another poisonous element joined to it. 

<margin>86</margin>
Hence, the "lethal poppies" are sung of by the Poets for their sleep. But in the **sulfur of Vitriol** <term>sulfur of Vitriol</term> <note>likely referring to "Sweet Spirit of Vitriol" or diethyl ether, which Van Helmont discovered had anesthetic properties long before its modern use.</note>, there is a sleep-inducing...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that narcotics like opium affect the body by corrupting the Archeus in the "Duumvirate" (stomach and spleen). He criticizes the medical establishment for using opiates to treat insanity, arguing that the drugs themselves induce a state of "mindless" dreaming that worsens madness.</summary>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

## CHAPTER IX.

...saccharine liquor, which brings sweet sleep along with the restoration of the principal faculties. It is similar with sulfur, which is recommended for lung afflictions for this reason, provided it is prepared in such a way that it can "play along" with us. Sleep that brings labor or distress—such as that caused by opiates—is bad. Such a sleep signifies a poison, disturbances, and storms within the body. Therefore, a sweet, creeping sleep is to be attributed to favorable causes. 

Therefore (I will say it again), **Apoplexy**, **Epilepsy**, **coma**, **Vertigo** <note>original: *Vertigo*; a sensation of spinning or loss of balance.</note>, **Heart Tremor**, and so on, each have their own unique poisons and **anodynes** <term>anodynes</term> <note>Pain-killing substances or medicines.</note>. Indeed, Vertigo sometimes strikes a man down just like Apoplexy, but without **Paralysis**. This is because it does not possess a "corpse-like" pain-killer, but rather an intoxicating one, such as is found in **Tobacco**. If this substance becomes more harmful in its degree, number, or quantity, it even becomes Apoplectic. Furthermore, I have spoken of garlic and **water of life** <note>original: *aquâ vitæ*; high-proof distilled alcohol, often used as a solvent or stimulant.</note>, and their ability to cause insensibility; yet garlic is not apoplectic, because the specific poison and its constant root are absent.

<margin>87</margin> At the very least, I bring this up here as a side point: that although anodynes cause a state of stupor similar to the effect of cold, they are erroneously placed by the **Schools** <note>The academic medical establishment of the time, which followed the ancient Greek teachings of Galen.</note> among the "extremely cold" substances. On the contrary, the sleep-inducing sulfur in **opium** is not cold; rather, it is very bitter, and its salt is sharp and induces sweating. According to the Schools, however, bitter things are notably "hot" in temperament. The power to induce sleep, therefore, whether in opium or elsewhere, is a specific power and a gift of the Creator—it is <margin>88</margin> not an effect of cold. I have openly demonstrated this elsewhere in my writings on sleep. 

<margin>89</margin> The pain-killer used in epilepsy differs from the one for Apoplexy, because its primary characteristic is its intoxicating nature. Forgive me, Reader, if I name the powers of things based on their similarity to simple substances; I do this because proper names are lacking, just as we lack a prior knowledge of the properties of these things which ought to dictate their names.

<margin>90</margin> After these treatises on insensibility, pain-killers, and certain poisons, I must now resume my discussion of pain.

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s exploration of the nature of sensation and pain. While the previous page discussed how specific substances (like opiates or tobacco) affect the body's internal spirits and cause insensibility, this page pivots to a physiological and philosophical argument: pain is felt locally by the "sensitive soul" and does not require the brain's "permission" or "consultation."</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>ON THE STONE</header>

# ON THE STONE

I repeat, therefore, that pain and sensation occur immediately in the injured place, or center, without seeking the **consensus** <term>consensus</term> <note>From the Latin for "agreement." In early medicine, this refers to "sympathy," where one part of the body reacts to an injury in another part.</note> of the brain. For it is sufficient that the vital light of the **sensitive soul** <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>In Helmontian philosophy, this is the part of the soul responsible for sensation and involuntary movement, present throughout the body rather than just in the head.</note> itself is diffused in all directions into every part, according to the requirements of necessity. For every "governor" of the parts <note>The "Rector" or local Archeus, the internal directing force of an organ.</note> also had to be an observer and discerner of objects, because it has the soul present and presiding over it on both sides. For in what way will the soul manifest that it feels harmful things, unless it has stirred up an aversion or a pain conceived within that injured center?

Therefore, the **spirits** <term>spirits</term> <note>Subtle, vaporous substances that were thought to carry life and sensation through the nerves and limbs.</note> inserted into the limbs had to serve the needs of the members without consultation or recourse to the brain; since it is not the brain, but the soul itself, present everywhere, that feels immediately. Indeed, there was a need for extreme speed to turn away and guard against harmful things; therefore, to send a messenger to the brain would have been troublesome.

I admit, indeed, that a pain in the intestine may draw other parts into **sympathy** <note>original: *consensum*</note>, and either relax them into a persistent paralysis or contract the parts serving voluntary motion. Thus, when a **kidney** is in pain, the stomach may feel nausea and a desire to vomit; the intestines <note>original: *ilia*; the flanks or entrails.</note> may be twisted; and the thigh situated below may become numb. A painful nail may even rouse a distant gland. To be sure, the presence of the soul confirms, but does not take away, the sympathy of the parts. 

<margin>91</margin> Therefore, that sympathy in pains is foreign to the pain itself and occurs by accident; it does not, for that reason, touch or alter the essence or cause of the pain. This is because this sympathy occurs after the pain, and is therefore separable from it. Consequently, the particular spirits of the parts feel things without the commerce of the **inflowing spirit** <note>The *spiritus influus*, or the spirit sent from the brain to the rest of the body.</note>. This is seen in the teeth, and in new flesh restored to a hollow ulcer. Truly, because the parts everywhere "live by their own measure" <note>original: *propriâ vivunt quadrâ*; an idiom meaning they function independently or according to their own nature.</note>, sensation exists according to the diversity of the organ. 

<margin>92</margin> For this reason, there are also many pains concentrated by the weather, which respond to the inequalities of the Moon.

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain is an immediate local reaction of the soul and the organ's own "spirit" rather than a signal that must travel to the brain. He explains that "sympathetic" symptoms in other organs (like nausea from kidney pain) are secondary effects, not the essence of the pain itself.</summary>
<keywords>sensitive soul, consensus, sympathy, Archeus, kidney pain, spirits, sensation, physiology, Jean Baptiste van Helmont</keywords>

-> # CHAPTER IX. <-

...because they are received centrally within our **astral spirit** <term>astral spirit</term> <note>In Helmontian medicine, the "astral spirit" is a subtle, celestial substance that acts as a bridge between the immortal soul and the physical body.</note>.

<margin>93</margin>
Finally, neither gore <note>original: *cruor*, often used to refer to blood that has been shed or clotted.</note> nor the blood of the arteries itself possess animal sense or touch, although they may feel through **sympathy** <term>sympathy</term> <note>The idea that two things can affect one another across a distance or without direct contact due to a shared hidden nature.</note>, even when drawn from the veins. This is because they flourish only by an **inflowing spirit** <term>inflowing spirit</term> <note>original: *spiritu influo*. A vital force that flows from the organs into the fluids, rather than being an inherent part of the fluid itself.</note>. Therefore, theologians have long asked: is blood **informed** <term>informed</term> <note>In a philosophical sense, "to inform" means to provide a soul or a life-giving "form" to raw matter. If something is "informed," it is considered a living part of the person.</note> by the soul? I would think, subject to the correction of better judgment, that nothing is informed by the soul of a living being which does not participate in the **sensitive soul**. That is, nothing is informed by the soul unless it feels through the spirit which is implanted in the parts and animated. For the act of "informing" necessarily proves life in a living being, just as life proves sensation—at least a dull one, such as exists in the bones and the brain. If indeed the food in the stomach, the abundance of seed in the **epididymides** <note>original: *parastatis*</note>, hunger, and even **urine** <note>original: *lotium*</note> produce their own dreams in the soul, and stir the soul during sleep according to their whim, it does not follow from this, vice-versa, that the soul therefore "informs" the food or the urine. For although the soul has felt the abundant and pressing urine, the urine itself does not feel its own objects. For the soul also feels a piercing knife, which it nevertheless does not inform. Therefore, for something to be "informed" by the soul, it is necessary that it live and feel as a subject of that life itself.

<margin>94</margin>
Sensation and pain, therefore, are in the containing parts **subjectively** <term>subjectively</term> <note>Meaning the part itself is the "subject" experiencing the feeling.</note>, but in the contained parts only **objectively** <term>objectively</term> <note>Meaning the substance is the "object" or cause of the feeling, but does not feel it itself.</note>. Indeed, although the things contained are most intimate to us and vital in an immediate way, they are nonetheless external with respect to the things containing them and to the sensitive soul.

<margin>95</margin>
However, it is not enough to say, along with the common folk, that a harmful cause is painful; nor is it sufficient to know that the sensitive life primarily feels, and from there, the spirit implanted in the parts, and finally the stable organs; and that thus, certainly, sensation testifies to the presence of something harmful. Since the **Schools** <note>Referring to the traditional University-based Scholastic philosophers of the time.</note> and the common people know these things in a way. But it must be made more clearly evident what immediately causes pain, and in what **way** <note>Catchword "pacto" (way/manner) connects to the start of the next page.</note>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that fluids like blood and urine are not "alive" or "informed" by the soul because they do not possess their own sensation, even though they can trigger sensations in the body. He distinguishes between the body's tissues (the containers) which feel pain, and the fluids (the contents) which do not.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont's investigation into the nature of sensation. -> ### ON THE FORMATION OF STONES <-

...the way pain is produced through feeling. Regarding the former, a needle pricks, and from there comes pain. A little bee stings and wounds like a needle, yet each hurts in a far different way. Therefore, it is not the very **breach of continuity** <term>breach of continuity</term> <note>original: *solutio continui*. A traditional medical term for any physical injury where tissues that should be joined are separated, such as a cut or puncture.</note> or division (which is common to both punctures) that primarily causes pain. For the division of continuity produces nothing other than, or short of, a breach. This breach in **lepers** <term>leprosy</term> <note>original: *Leprosis*. In the 17th century, this often referred to conditions involving nerve damage or loss of physical sensation.</note> and in **paralysis** exists without feeling or pain. If indeed the breach of continuity should hurt or not, that is incidental to the knife; nor does the knife primarily cause the breach except as a **necessary condition** <term>necessary condition</term> <note>original: *sine qua non*. An essential circumstance without which the effect—in this case, pain—cannot occur.</note>. Therefore, the fact that a bee's sting hurts differently than an equal breach made by a needle certainly depends on a deeper judgment of the sense.

<margin>96</margin>
Thus, from this it is immediately clear that the **sensitive soul** itself immediately feels, appraises, and judges the object of pain. Moreover, in the **Schools** <term>the Schools</term> <note>A reference to Scholasticism, the university system based on the teachings of Aristotle that Van Helmont frequently challenged.</note>, sensation is said to happen passively, just as motion happens actively. But I have already shown that sensation is produced by the power, or the primary sensitive being, through action; even though the limbs suffer subjectively through the application of sensible objects.

<margin>97</margin>
Therefore, sensation happens **actively**, because sensation itself is the soul's active appraisal. That the limbs meanwhile suffer is incidental to the sensation, and it cannot prevent sensation from occurring through the sensitive faculty. Indeed, the same specific agent exists in that sensitive action of both sensation and pain: because the agent itself is the soul. And sensation differs from pain through the soul's judgment of sensible objects. Thus, sensation is the judgment of the soul itself—an action, truly, not a **passion** <term>passion</term> <note>In Helmontian philosophy, a "passion" is a state of being acted upon or being passive, the opposite of an "action."</note>. The Schools, along with the common people, know that violent causes bring about pain, just as they know that water is liquid; but to have shown the internal **wrathful impulse** <term>wrathful impulse</term> <note>original: *thymosin*. Derived from the Greek *thymos*, referring to a spirited, sudden, or vital reaction of the life-force within the body.</note> of the sensitive faculty, and to have revealed pain at its root—this they have not yet attempted until now. To which end the following consideration leads.

<margin>99</margin>
Living flesh is very easily burned and flayed by...

### C H A P T E R  IX.

...with boiling water. Moreover, **dead flesh** is burned more slowly. There is also a different kind of burning if a living hand is scorched than if a corpse's is. For the former burning <note>of the living</note> raises **blisters** with even the slightest heat, such that the same thing happens even under the Sun. But the latter combustion <note>of the dead</note> grips the flesh just as if it were being roasted: namely, without blisters and without the skin peeling away.

<margin>100</margin>
The **Schools** <term>the Schools</term> <note>original: *Scholæ*. Refers to the established academic authorities of the 17th century whose theories Van Helmont often challenged.</note> have not yet noted this difference because they have not paid attention. They might say, perhaps, that it is easier to heat what is already warm, and that living flesh is therefore burned more quickly <note>because it already possesses body heat</note>. But let us suppose dead flesh is first warmed to the same degree of heat, exactly as if it were alive: it still is not burned as easily, nor in the same way as living flesh. Therefore, the aforementioned excuse has no place. Wherefore, since different effects occur from the same degree of heat acting upon the same subject of flesh—distinguished only by life—it is therefore necessary that **life alone** is the cause of that difference.

<margin>101</margin>
This is to say that life is the proper agent of sensation in sentient beings; and life is that kind of cause which moreover has the power of creating **burns** <term>burns</term> <note>original: *ambustiones*. Van Helmont distinguishes between a "burn" as a vital reaction and "roasting" as a mechanical one.</note> in living things and in **medical matter** <term>medical matter</term> <note>original: *materia medica*. Here referring to the physical substance of the body as it relates to medical study.</note>, and even of resisting it, or not. Therefore, I find life to be the **primary efficient cause** and the immediate cause of sensation and pain. For the force of fire, when received and introduced into a corpse, is not felt; indeed, it is not even properly "burning" as it is in the living, but rather "roasting" or "toasting." For in the living, the fluid of the flesh, through the **indignation** <note>Helmont describes the soul's reaction to injury as a form of "anger" or "indignation" that causes physical changes.</note> of the **sensitive soul** <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>original: *anima sensitiva*. The aspect of the soul that governs physical senses and involuntary reactions.</note>, is very quickly converted into a sharp liquid and substantially transformed; this fluid in the dead is not subject to such a **vital transformation** <term>vital transformation</term> <note>original: *vitalis transmutatio*. A change in matter caused specifically by the presence of life/soul.</note>. Thus, by boiling and frying among the fibers, fire scorches and roasts the meat. For dead flesh suffers by degrees in the same way that other non-sentient bodies suffer from fire. But in the living, even

...boiling water immediately produces **blisters**; then the solid part is quickly contracted and burned. Therefore, this process of burning, in living and sentient beings, is brought about *efficiently* by life itself. Fire, on the other hand, acts *effectively* as an active, occasional, and external medium. For life itself, feeling the harshness of the fire, sharpens its own fluids and transforms them into a blistering liquid, and then into a scab-forming <note>original: *escharoticum*. Referring to an "eschar," the dry crust or scab formed after a burn.</note> liquid. And as much as is damaged by the fire, just as much is afterwards corrupted through the state left behind, because it is dead.

<margin>102</margin> But because touch is the primary judge of things known by the senses, logical demonstration hardly has a place here; thus the history and root of pain has remained neglected by causes until now. I will therefore repeat certain things which I wrote previously regarding this great paradox under the word **contraction**. Wherefore, to investigate the proper agent of pain, I observed that *Ranunculus* <note>buttercup</note>, *Apium* <note>wild celery</note>, *Flammula* <note>spearwort, a type of buttercup known for its acridity</note>, etc., do not cause blisters on a corpse. Yet they blister living flesh.

<margin>103</margin> I therefore judged that the difference in this action lies within the <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>original: *anima sensitiva*. In Van Helmont’s system, this is the vital principle that mediates between the mind and the physical body, governing sensation and involuntary reactions.</note> itself, and not primarily in the spearwort. Indeed, the plant only causes blisters insofar as it harasses the **sensitive spirits** with a biting sensation sharper than is fair. To mitigate, blunt, or extinguish that sensed sharpness, the soul rages within them and consequently dissolves the vital substance of the limbs into a corrosive curd <note>original: *labumen*. A technical term used by Van Helmont to describe a biological byproduct or "sourness" created when the vital spirit reacts to disease or injury.</note> (as I have written elsewhere *concerning the plague*). Therefore, the sensitive soul itself—as the immediate sensitive substance—is the "efficiently effective" cause of the blister. The spearwort, however, which does nothing to a corpse, is merely the effective, occasional, external, and exciting cause.

<margin>104</margin> On what grounds, then, have the stupefied **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional academic establishment following Galen and Aristotle, whom Van Helmont frequently criticized.</note> taught that medicines are entirely inert and as if dead, unless they are first prepared by our internal heat (as if by a cook), and once excited, are sharpened? This certainly is not without its own difficulties. They established

<page-num>213</page-num>
### CHAPTER IX.

...for they <note>Referring to the "Schools" or traditional medical authorities mentioned later.</note> believed the same thing: that medicines, when taken or applied, do not immediately discharge their powers upon us like fire, but require a certain amount of time to produce their effects through preliminary **dispositions** <note>original: *dispositiones*. In Helmontian philosophy, this refers to the preparatory state of matter that allows a specific vital or chemical action to take place.</note>. However, even if a period of time is required for the "alteration" (which is the effect of the medicine) to occur, this certainly in no way proves that the medicine’s action is caused by our own body heat. It is not as if heat were necessary for the medicine to obtain its innate gift, or the license to act which it has held safe, full, and free since its creation.

But (as I have said) a substance works differently—indeed, frequently far differently—in the living than it does in the dead or in non-sentient things. And so, the effects of medicines do not occur unless they are first properly applied, and then, through a more refined "appropriation," imprint their power upon us. From this, a "disposition" clearly arises, which the <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>original: *anima sensitiva*. The vital principle that governs the body's involuntary reactions and perceptions.</note> arouses by its own judgment, and afterwards even explains and perfects. 

<margin>105</margin> For the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the Scholastic academic establishment, particularly the followers of Galen and Aristotle.</note> of medicine erred because they looked at external and "occasional" <note>An "occasional cause" is a secondary trigger, rather than the primary source of an action.</note> causes as if they were the principal and vital ones. Therefore, they neglected to connect the effects produced in the living—and in the processes of healing themselves—to their proper "efficient" causes through the necessary stages of progression. 

It is therefore foolish to suggest that pepper, vinegar, and so on, must borrow their activities and the gifts they received for acting from our own body heat. As if a single, uniform heat could be the primary cause of such a wide variety of effects! In truth, for a substance to act upon us, it does not need any other external thing; rather, just as it does fundamentally, so it immediately exerts its powers through the stages of its dispositions without delay, provided it is properly applied. (I have demonstrated this at length both regarding the action of vital governance and in my treatise showing that heat does not perform digestion in sentient beings.) 

But because the **sensitive soul** (which the Schools disgracefully confuse with heat) has receiv...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that medicines have inherent powers that do not require body heat to "activate" them; instead, the sensitive soul perceives these substances and directs the body's reaction. He criticizes traditional medicine for oversimplifying vital processes by attributing them all to the single cause of heat.</summary>

He is transitioning from the general concept of vital action to how the soul interprets medicinal "impressions."</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>

# ON THE STONE
<note>original: *De Lithiasi*. While the book is about the formation of kidney and bladder stones, Van Helmont often detours into the fundamental principles of how life and medicine interact.</note>

...[vi]tal powers to itself, and from there constructs a certain new action proper to itself and entirely vital. Therefore, the powers that the <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>The vital principle that governs the body's involuntary reactions and perceptions.</note> receives from a **medicine**, are only occasional effective causes; it could, if it wished, bypass and neglect them. 
<margin>107</margin> 
This is evident in the more robust, who digest laxatives—even violent ones—without discomfort and with the drink being ineffective, just like food. Likewise in the dying: to whom medicines are indeed applied, but there is no **appropriation**; specifically because of the neglect and failure of the sensitive power. For in the robust, the exciting heat is not lacking, and yet there is no effect. 

Indeed, the powers of the medicine are otherwise immediately received and proceed more and more by degrees; then, once those powers are taken up by the sensitive soul, it soon behaves well or poorly toward them. 
<margin>107</margin> 
If well: it then uses its objects to nourish its powers or to defeat what is harmful. But if wrongly: the sensitive life then behaves morosely, furiously, angrily, anxiously, etc., and scatters the **seminal ideas** <note>original: *Ideas seminales*. In Helmontian thought, these are the "blueprints" or vital impulses that direct the body to produce either healthy tissue or disease.</note> of these sufferings into the <term>regulating spirit</term> <note>original: *spiritum enormon*. A term borrowed from Hippocrates to describe the vitalizing force that flows through the body and directs its functions.</note>, into the blood, and into the organs affected by the medicines; the effects remain, conforming to the aforementioned ideas. 

This I have clearly demonstrated in the treatise *On Diseases*, as well as in the one *On the Plague*. 
<margin>108</margin> 
And so, if a delay occurs between the application of a medicine and its effect, this is by no means due to a defect or lack of activity in the things themselves; rather, it happens because of the necessity of the vital activity, emerging and following from the impression made by the medicine. For the **virulent force** is not lacking in the bite of a serpent, though sometimes it does not work due to some hindrance. For an agent, in order to act upon us, requires application, an appropriated impression, and the sensitive power as a sort of receiver of the other agent's power. And this, so that it may bear a product, which is truly and immediately a new fruit, or—

<summary>Van Helmont argues that medicines only work if the body's "sensitive soul" actively accepts and processes them, explaining why the same drug might affect a healthy person, a strong person, and a dying person differently. He asserts that disease and medicinal effects are "vital" responses rather than simple chemical or thermal reactions.</summary>

## CHAPTER IX.

...vital, as a testimony of the soul’s perception. For indeed, many become so accustomed to **laxatives** that they eventually have no effect. This is not because the heat in a person has failed, or because the laxatives have lost their original powers, but because the soul has developed a familiarity through frequent use. Consequently, the soul eventually reacts more mildly <note>original: *mitiùs excandeſcat*. Literally "glows" or "burns" less brightly, implying a less fiery or angry vital reaction.</note> to these poisons than it did the first few times. 

<margin>109</margin> 
Up to this point, it is a true and eternal principle that every sensation consists in vital action and judgment rather than in passive reception. This remains true whether that sensation occurs in the exterior senses, or in some emotion of the mind, or finally, in the natural or sympathetic perception of inanimate things. 

<margin>110</margin> 
At the very least, it is clear that **medicines** do not require our pre-existing heat simply to act. Rather, the **sensitive power**—which is the primary actor—needs agents and sensible objects so that it may perceive, and through perceiving, take action. Therefore, the action of sensible things serves as an <term>occasional cause</term> <note>A philosophical term meaning a trigger or "occasion" that prompts the true cause (the soul) to act, rather than the medicine possessing the power to heal on its own.</note> for the sensitive soul. For this reason, medicines do not work on a corpse, due to the lack of the principal and immediate agent, which is life, or the **soul**. 

<margin>111</margin> 
From this, it is also quite clear how preposterously the powers of medicines have until now been attributed to the medicine itself <note>original: *agenti*. The "agent" or active ingredient.</note>, rather than to the vital and principal efficient power. We see how much the principal agent has been neglected, both in the healing and the creation of **diseases**. Truly, if a medicine were activated by our heat alone, any medicine would always and everywhere act equally upon every warm human subject—no different than how a specific weight always balances itself uniformly. Yet a **laxative**, given in the same dose, acts violently on one person and not at all on another, even though it was sufficiently stimulated by heat in both cases. Indeed, it often rages more fiercely in the weak, the same thing which...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that medicine acts as a stimulus ("occasional cause") rather than a mechanical force, and its effectiveness depends entirely on the "sensitive soul's" vital response, which can be dulled by habit.</summary>

In stronger individuals, it lacks any effect. Furthermore, what I have said regarding <term>colocynth</term> <note>The bitter apple, a plant used as a violent purgative.</note>, <term>spearwort</term> <note>original: *flammula*; a species of buttercup known for causing skin blistering.</note>, <term>buttercup</term> <note>original: *ranunculus*</note>, and so on, must also be applied generally to other agents. In the living, the iron primarily harms the **sensitive soul**, which, being wonderfully impatient, rages, breaks down, and exasperates its own vital fluids into a sharp poison. It then contracts the fibers of the flesh and turns them into an <term>eschara</term> <note>A dry, leathery scab or slough produced by a burn or caustic substance.</note>, or even into the state of charcoal. A corpse, however, is burned by a red-hot iron no differently than if it were wood or some other insensitive thing. That is to say, it burns by the fire's own action, but not by the action of life. This distinction is a prerogative that the **Schools** 

<margin>113</margin>
have not attended to, yet it is the only one worthy of consideration at the very threshold of healing. They have only considered the fire's own action in their usual way, just as they consider the abstract powers of medicines. <term>Quicklime</term> <note>original: *calx viva*; calcium oxide, which reacts heat-intensively with water.</note>, as long as it remains dry, does not corrode a corpse; yet it quickly corrodes living flesh and produces an eschara. In a corpse, the whole body is dissolved into a liquid <note>original: *liquamen*</note> by the lime and is soaked up, except for the bone and cartilage. But in living flesh, the lime does not consume it into a liquid; instead, it transforms it into an eschara. Therefore, in general, the vital sensitive power is first affected by a sensible object; and from that point, it fabricates an effect, or a "new being"—that is, one unique to itself. It shapes this according to the conceived <term>pathema</term> <note>A Greek term for "suffering" or "passion," referring here to the soul's internal reaction to an external stimulus.</note>, in proportion to the injury or harm it imagines. And this effect is proper to the sensitive soul, though it is mediated by the exciting occasion.

### CHAPTER IX.

mixed, from indignation and fear: or proceeding from agony <note>original: *agonia*; here referring to the soul's internal struggle or distress</note>. I have indeed carefully observed that there is a contraction in almost every kind of pain. So much so that when a harmful cause is presented, the injured part immediately manifests its pain, as if contracted and wrinkled by a **cramp**. Nature is, in fact, so prone to this cramping everywhere, that no man strains to defecate <note>original: *cacaturiat*</note> without his scrotum—however loose it may be—immediately shriveling and wrinkling. Because of the natural aversion of the indwelling spirit toward a painful object, pain perpetually has the shriveling of the limb as its companion. 

Furthermore, there is a type of pain that is stable in a part, as in an ulcer, a wound, an abscess <note>original: *apostemate*</note>, and so on. This pain seethes from pure indignation alone. In this case, the part does not contract itself as properly as in convulsive pains; rather, it melts its own nutritive fluid and changes it into a sharp, salty, virulent, and finally blistering or <term>escharotic</term> <note>capable of producing a dry scab or slough, like a chemical burn</note> liquid, as the introduced evil transforms it. There are, moreover, some pains that are obtuse, deep, moderately gnawing, and more dull, which wander by an error of the digestions, following a morose anger rather than the fury of life. Therefore, all pain is caused occasionally by the sad affection of objects, but it proceeds immediately from life itself as a testimony of the sense. Indeed, pain not infrequently denotes the <term>passions</term> <note>original: *pathemata*; the sufferings or reactive states</note> of the sensitive soul to the destruction of its own organs. Because that soul perceives those ill-affected parts as if they were rising up by their own fault and, as it were, "murmuring," it strives to correct, punish, and often even destroy them.

<margin>117</margin>

<note>Lithiasis refers to the formation of stones (calculi) in the body, particularly the bladder and kidneys.</note>

...[child]ren’s <note>The previous page's catchword "bre-" suggests the original word was *puerorum*</note> bladder, impatient of pain from retained urine, naturally contracts and compresses itself. But the bladder of the elderly, being less shocked by a condition they have become accustomed to, allows the urine to slip out of its own accord. Otherwise, if it were only a matter of the bladder's <term>sphincter</term> <note>the circular muscle that keeps the bladder closed</note> being relaxed, there would be no reason why boys urinate to a distance and old men near to themselves, were it not for the aforementioned contraction of the child's bladder—a painless and spontaneous compression from behind that is as yet unfamiliar to the organ.

<margin>118</margin>
On the occasion of pain, the **Spasm** is not to be neglected. First of all (I shall not repeat what I taught concerning griping pains <note>original: *torminibus*; referring to intestinal cramps or colic</note> in my treatise *On Winds*), a part does not hurt when contracted simply because of the contraction itself (as is evident from the scrotum, which contracts without pain), but because of an offense inflicted upon the spirit and the life. For contraction is indeed the effect of sensation or pain, although it may happen that when a contraction occurs, the pain is also increased. My own age, because it is fertile with perverse minds, will laugh at this paradox as it does at many others. Which, however, the following

<margin>119</margin>
posterity will willingly embrace. Indeed, the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the established academic medical authorities of the 17th century, primarily those following Galenic and Aristotelian tradition</note> claim that a spasm occurs through the executive organs of voluntary motion, to the extent that they say healthy and morbid functions belong to the same faculty, even if they are excited by different external causes. Therefore, since the muscle alone is the executive member of voluntary motion, and the nerve is the organ that carries the command of the will; it follows (as they teach) that the muscle, even in a spasm and acting against our will, is nevertheless not contracted except by that same motor faculty of choice which moves it while it is healthy.

<margin>120</margin>
In this, the Schools contradict themselves in a hallucinatory manner: while they define a spasm as a symptom of the movement of the will, they nevertheless say it arises from <term>plethora</term> <note>an excess of bodily fluids, specifically blood</note> or <term>inanition</term> <note>exhaustion or emptiness due to lack of nourishment</note> as the proximate and containing causes. However, it is well enough known that repletion and emptiness are natural causes, and not voluntary ones; which natural causes, if they [shorten] the nerve...

<header>C H A P T E R   I X.</header>

### C H A P T E R   I X.

...should they shorten, or contract (as they clearly teach), at least the contraction <note>original: *attractio*; literally a drawing-together or tension</note> of the nerves will not happen through voluntary motion. I also marvel, in this regard, at the stupidities of the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional universities that taught the medical theories of Galen and Aristotle</note> celebrated thus far. For in the first place, a nerve differs from a muscle no differently than a vein does. This [the vein] indeed carries blood to the muscle, and the other [the nerve] carries motion. Then, besides the two causes of spasm found perhaps by *Hippocrates*, *Galen* added a third besides, which has been admitted in the *Schools*: namely, a poisonous <term>virulent quality</term> <note>Galen argued that some spasms were caused not by physical fullness or emptiness, but by the "quality" of a poison or venom</note>. For *Galen* had seen a spasm follow from a snake bite; yet, even if the limb swelled from the bite, he still could not believe that <term>repletion</term> <note>original: *repletionē*; a state of being over-full, one of the two classical causes of spasm</note> had caused the spasm. He is first of all defective
<margin>121</margin>
because he does not know whether the nerve must be struck in order to be convulsed, or rather the muscle. Then, there is the fact that fatal convulsions occur in griping pains <note>original: *torminibus*; intestinal cramps or colic</note> and after taking **hellebore** <note>A toxic plant used in antiquity as a powerful purgative</note>, without any injury to the nerves, or any <term>inanition</term> <note>emptiness or depletion of fluids</note>, repletion, or colic poison. Thirdly, he is also defective because, since a retraction of the limb and a shortening of the muscle occur in a spasm, he did not distinguish (as otherwise befitted a "Prince of Medicine") why a poison would contract or shorten a muscle, thus leaving the previous obscurity. Indeed, *Galen* says that the name of a **Physician** belongs to the "discoverer of the occasion" <note>This refers to the medical skill of identifying the right moment and cause for treatment</note>. Which 
<margin>122</margin>
name he has lost in this instance. Fourthly and finally: if a spasm happens from an emptied or over-filled nerve—that is, a proper suffering of the nerve itself—then must the poisonous quality be imprinted on the nerve, or on the muscle, for the spasm to result from it? Fifthly, *Galen* remained defective, and with him the following **Schools**, as to why a snake bite, the poisonous quality of a drug, etc., should become a "proper suffering" of voluntary motion and its organs. For if the poison must be imprinted on the muscle, then the nerve ceases to be the "proper subject" of the spasm; and consequently, its emptiness or <term>plethora</term> <note>excess of bodily fluids</note> is supposed and required in vain.

But if indeed a poison strikes the nerve itself, how will **hellebore** <note>a toxic plant used in the ancient world as a violent purgative</note>, as it wanders through the intestines, affect the nerve primarily? How will a medicine, while still held in the stomach, cause a contraction <note>original: *contracturam*</note> and yet grant freedom from vomiting? It is at least ridiculous that a difference in the affected muscles should create a distinct species of "evil" <note>meaning a distinct type of disease</note>, if the very essence of that evil depends upon the affected nerves. And it is foolish to think that **Emprostotonus**, **Tetanus**, and **Opisthotonus** <note>These are Greek terms for different types of rigid spasms: bending forward, staying rigid, and bending backward</note> are specifically different diseases just because a different muscle is involved. For a muscle always pulls in the same way: namely, from the "tail" toward the "head" <note>referring to the insertion and origin points of muscle fibers</note>.

<margin>123</margin> Truly, such childish nonsense necessarily flows from an ignorance of the disease and the recklessness of a juvenile judgment. Therefore, nature has distinguished the species of diseases according to the kind of "occasional causes," not because of a difference in their physical locations.

<margin>124</margin> And so, since **Emptiness** and **Fullness** <term>Inanitio and Repletio</term> <note>These were the two primary causes of disease in Galenic medicine: either the body had too little fluid/spirit or too much</note> are entirely opposite terms, they could not possibly produce a single type of **Spasm**. It is hard to believe that the emptying of a nerve—which is a total deprivation—could be just as much a cause of a spasm as the filling of that same nerve. Just as it is equally stupid to claim that a nerve can be filled to such an extent that it shortens, or that a muscle is shortened because a tiny nerve has been stretched wide by fullness. As if all the nerves could suddenly be emptied, and likewise filled, and stretched to an immense degree in every single seizure of **epilepsy**, by means of these fictitious "humors"? As if a spasm were merely a shortening of the muscle, following the shrinking of a dried-out or moistened nerve? And indeed, as if an unusual filling of the nerve would shorten it, just as the other cause—which by its dryness would diminish the nerves no less in length than in width—would be the cause of a convulsive retraction? Would it not be more likely that, through the error of both these supposed causes, the nerves would instead suffer an inexcusable **paralysis**? Namely, by the blockage of the nerve, from a
