## CHAPTER IX.

...filling it with <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: *pituita*; in Galenic medicine, one of the four humors thought to be cold and moist</note> (as they say), rather than through the compression of a dried-out nerve? As if such a sudden drying of a nerve were credible, or even possible in a living person! Moreover, how could a nerve, once it has been dried out (suppose by the excessive and unusual force of laxative **hellebore**, as they claim), soon admit a restoration of its dried-up <term>radical moisture</term>? <note>The "radical moisture" was considered the fundamental life-fluid within organs; its total loss was thought to be irreversible and fatal.</note> Why was it necessary to invent and admit to the "filling" or "emptying" of a nerve, if a poisonous quality grants a spasm without either? <margin>125</margin> Therefore, the dogma of the **Schools** <note>the traditional academic medical authorities</note> regarding the causes of Spasms—recorded as the emptying and filling of nerves—is ridiculous. For even if they acknowledge, along with **Galen** <note>the 2nd-century physician whose theories dominated medicine for over a millennium</note>, a third cause which is of a "malignant quality," they nevertheless remain stuck and convicted by their reliance on the first two. Indeed, they are hallucinating regarding the matter, the object, the efficient cause, and the method of occurrence. That is to say, in the whole thing. 

As if a tiny nerve extending to a muscle—often barely equaling the thickness of a triple-thread—could become so much shorter than itself by being moistened too much or dried out too much, that the muscle would contract the limbs by perhaps a <term>span</term>! <note>original: *spithamum*; a measurement of about 9 inches, the distance between the tip of the thumb and the little finger</note> Indeed, as if the impaction of a hostile humor, as much as the emptying of a nerve, would cause the pains of a spasm! <margin>126</margin> They bring up the ridiculous example of dried mud here; although such dryness is impossible in the living, and restoration would also be impossible; yet spasms of this kind not infrequently occur even spontaneously. 

The **Schools** thought that those fictional moistenings and dryings of a tiny nerve (which would barely amount to the width of a straw) could contract a muscle into a foot-long convulsion. Nor is the example valid that the **strings of a lute** <note>original: *fides testudinis*, literally "strings of a tortoise-shell," referring to the instrument's classical origins</note>, becoming damp in rainy weather, might snap and break because they have been shortened by the absorbed liquid. For first of all, the string could have been stretched twice as long before its fictional extension in width would have shortened it; the Schools do not notice that a damp membrane is fragile, just as a dry one is; and therefore...

<vocab>Spasm, Galen, nerve, muscle, convulsion, Schools, hellebore</vocab>

<summary>The author mocks the Galenic theory that spasms are caused by the physical shrinking or swelling of nerves, arguing that the tiny size of nerves makes it impossible for them to pull a muscle across such a large distance. He dismisses the common medical analogies of his day, such as comparing human nerves to drying mud or lute strings.</summary>

<keywords>spasms, nerve anatomy, muscle contraction, Galenic medicine, radical moisture, hellebore, medical polemic</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's refutation of the traditional Galenic theory that spasms are caused by the physical shortening of nerves through either "emptying" (dryness) or "filling" (excess moisture). He uses logical arguments to show that these physical changes do not match clinical observations.</meta>

-># ON THE STONE<-
<note>original: DE LITHIASI. Although the header refers to "The Stone" (kidney stones/calculus), the text continues the discussion on spasms from the previous chapter.</note>

...[their] integrity be preserved, as oily things in oil, lest they become wet or dry out. Away with those examples, which have no place in a living being! 
<margin>127</margin> 
1. For in a living being, the nerves cannot be dried out to such an extent that their dryness causes any shortening. 
2. Once they have been dried out, they never again receive moisture afterwards, any more than dry old age itself does. 
3. They deny that a **spasm** <note>original: *spasmum*</note> arising from a laxative is caused by poison: for if they recognized a toxin in a purgative <note>original: *solutivo*</note>, they would be cutting their own purse-strings <note>an idiomatic way of saying they would lose their income by admitting their treatments are harmful</note>. Therefore, they claim a **spasm** from a purgative occurs only insofar as it empties the body, but not as if from a virulent drug; it would be from the "emptying," namely, and the dryness of the nerves. But quite often, a spasm arising from a purgative is restored [cured]. Therefore, it is not born from the dryness of the nerves. 
4. Every thin, emaciated old man would be pulled back by a perpetual and universal spasm [if dryness alone caused contraction]. 
5. Since a nerve is not the executive organ of motion, a shortening of the nerve does not prove a convulsion of the limbs, as if the arm or leg were required to follow the shortening of a tiny nerve. 
6. If a nerve were moistened (such that it became shorter in length as it was extended in width by the absorption of foreign moisture), since such a moisture would be clearly against nature, it would produce <term>paralysis</term> <note>original: *paralysin*; a loss of motion or sensation</note> rather than a **spasm**. However, paralysis is diametrically opposed to the spasm itself, both in sensation and in motion. 
7. How could a blow to the <term>skull</term> <note>original: *calvariae*</note>, in a single moment, immediately dry out all the nerves on one side, while immediately stretching out the others opposite to them by moistening them, so that they cause a **spasm** and **paralysis** at the same time? And since both "emptying" and "filling" are fabricated as causes of **spasm**, a blow to the skull ought to produce a spasm on both sides. 
8. It is no wonder, therefore, that such unlucky remedies are applied to **spasm**, if the Academies <note>the traditional medical universities</note> have thus far ignored all the requirements of diseases. For they ought to have known that every **spasm** is a <term>vital Blas</term> <note>original: *Blas vitale*; Van Helmont’s term for a rhythmic, motive power or internal "pulse" of life within the organs</note> of the muscles, stirred up from the <term>innate Archeus</term> <note>original: *Archeo innato*; the internal "vital director" or spirit-like force that governs the health and function of a specific body part</note>: of which...
<margin>128</margin>

<summary>The author presents eight logical arguments against the Galenic idea that spasms are caused by the physical drying or moistening of nerves, instead proposing they are caused by the "vital Blas" of the muscles governed by the "Archeus."</summary>
<keywords>spasm, paralysis, nerves, anatomy, Galenic medicine, vital Blas, Archeus, purgatives, skull injury</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the Galenic view of spasms. The previous page (222) argued that nerves cannot physically shorten enough through "dryness" to cause the visible contraction of a limb.</meta>

### CHAPTER IX.

[The] occasion is some malignant thing rushing into the <term>Archeus</term> <note>In Helmontian medicine, the Archeus is the "master-workman" or vital principle that governs the life and functions of an organ.</note>, laying snares for the life of the muscles. Even if Hippocrates attributed the cause of a **Spasm** to "emptying" and "filling," he was looking at the preceding circumstances of life. Namely, because among those who caroused and lived decadently, and likewise after much depletion of the veins <note>referring to bloodletting</note>, convulsion was frequent. And Galen, not understanding the mind of the Old Man <note>original: *Senis*, referring to Hippocrates</note>, corrupted it with his own later commentary regarding the "filling" and "emptying" of the nerves by humors.

Therefore, having rejected these kinds of old wives' tales, which gullible youth are convinced of in the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional university system of the 17th century, which relied heavily on Galenic and Aristotelian theories.</note>, I say: 

<margin>129</margin> 
That there is a twofold motion in muscles: one, namely, insofar as it is the organ of **voluntary motion** <note>original: *motus arbitralis*; motion directed by the will</note>; and the other, proper to itself. Through which, even if it pulls itself back toward its own "head" <note>the origin point of the muscle</note>: nevertheless nothing prevents the spirit, implanted in those motive parts, from pulling and moving them: just as was said before concerning the scrotum. For it is not contrary to nature for parts to leap with their own local motion even when the soul is absent: specifically, that through a distressing sensation, parts that are usually movable by another commander <note>the soul or conscious will</note> are furiously contracted. Since muscles specifically lack any other visible motion by which they might signal a harm inflicted upon them, except for that motion by which they carry out the voluntary movement of the soul. 

Nay rather, it is entirely natural to all members, and proper to the common effort of the parts, that they contract because of a distressing feeling of injury inflicted. This is a point which the Schools have left untouched. 

<margin>130</margin> 
Therefore, I have judged it erroneous to believe, along with the Schools, that a **Spasm** is an affliction of the head <note>the brain</note>. For they now depart from their own theses in this, by which they suppose a **Spasm** to be from filling, emptying, or a poisonous quality of the nerves: unless they would rather, with the tables turned, have the **Spasm** arise from the filling or evacuation of the head. 

A **Spasm**, however, is an "accident" <note>a symptom or secondary phenomenon</note> of the sensitive spirit, which is manifested especially by punctures of the nerves or tendons, and likewise by fevers, the taking of laxative poisons, or the bite of a serpen[t]—

<summary>The author argues that spasms are not caused by physical changes in the nerves (as Galen claimed), but by the Archeus (vital spirit) reacting to distress or injury. He posits that muscles have an innate power to contract independently of the soul's will when they are harmed.</summary>

<keywords>Archeus, Hippocrates, Galen, muscle motion, voluntary motion, sensitive spirit, spasm, Scholasticism, anatomy, convulsion</keywords>

## ON THE DISEASE OF THE STONE

<margin>131</margin> ...forth, and other things equal to these. Nor does it meanwhile argue to the contrary that a blow to the head also induces a **spasm**; for the head has no less authority than the intestines do during <term>griping pains</term> <note>original: *torminibus*; severe, twisting abdominal pains</note> for the "architecture" of a spasm. Specifically, a spasm arising from the head is just as much a servant of the muscles as one born from the **sensitive soul** <note>The "sensitive soul" or *anima sensitiva* was believed to be the vital force responsible for sensation and involuntary movement.</note> when it shrinks back in horror from a <term>venom</term> <note>original: *virus*; in this context, a morbid or toxic influence rather than a biological virus.</note>.

In a blow to the head, what "venom" has immediately contaminated the contracted side? Or what has suddenly emptied or filled all the nerves of that side? Does the brain not shake during a sneeze? Is the membrane surrounding the lung not contracted in a "dry" **asthma**? In **pleurisy**, the <term>pleura</term> <note>the membrane lining the thoracic cavity</note> is wrinkled and contracted, and so far as it goes, it tears itself away from the ribs of its own accord. Does the **mediastinum** <note>the partition between the lungs</note> not also contract quite frequently? Is the **diaphragm** not also constricted by a notable "anxiety of pressure"? A name for this [sensation] is still lacking, although the affliction is frequent during heart palpitations.

The pains of a dulled **spleen** are also, at times, indicators of a contracted internal organ. The **stomach** is likewise contracted by hiccups, vomiting, and stomach pains. Truly, there are intermittent contractions in these membranes whenever the annoying "occasional cause" <note>an external trigger or stimulus</note> is moved or returns. Also, at the onset of **dropsy** or **jaundice**—even before the water or "wind" is produced—the abdomen often contracts and hardens on one side. Finally, the **intestines** manifest intermittent griping pains, not solely from the distension of "winds" (which produces no pain if the bowels are not constipated), but rather from their own contraction. I have written elsewhere that I observed this across the navel of an infant. I saw,

<margin>132</margin> namely, that whenever the griping pains were most urgent, **epileptic**-like attacks were stirred up. The intestines, however, according to the measure of the pain, were variously rolled up and contracted as if "walking," while at other times the intestines were settled and completely at rest. For in **colic**, an acid and sharp excrement pricks the sensitive soul...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that spasms and internal pains are caused by the involuntary contraction of membranes and organs (like the stomach, pleura, or intestines) reacting to irritation, rather than Galenic theories of nerve fluid. He supports this with clinical observations of pleurisy, asthma, and a case study of an infant with colic.</summary>

<keywords>spasm, sensitive soul, pleurisy, asthma, diaphragm, colic, infant observation, anatomy, contraction, Archeus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Van Helmont’s argument that bodily contractions and spasms are active responses of the "sensitive soul" to irritation, rather than passive mechanical failures. He is specifically distinguishing between "windy" colic and the pain caused by the "Duelech" (his term for a stone).</meta>

# CHAPTER IX.

...this [contraction] produces pain, and as if through intervals, it contracts the intestine; the wind then trapped there, by some chance, stretches the intestine out. Therefore, "windy colic" (distinguished from a descending **Duelech** <note>A term coined by Van Helmont to describe the specific stony matter formed in the body, distinct from ordinary sand or gravel.</note>, as it is called) does not take its name from its cause, but from a later and accidental symptom. Likewise, the pain of <term>griping</term> <note>original: *torminum*; severe, twisting abdominal distress</note> often recurs from the use of laxatives along with a contraction, and it is cured by things that soothe that contraction. **For** windy colic is scarcely distinguished from the colic of a stone, because the same symptom of pain appears equally in both, with the intestines being curled and contracted. Thus, drinking almond milk eases the pains, because it pacifies the contracted intestines by coating them. Therefore, since pain produces contraction, and this in turn produces new pain, we see that pain often gives birth to pain, and to something like itself.

Next, whenever an injury happens to the skin, veins, arteries, or nerves, they contract into wrinkles through the power of the **sensitive soul** <note>The *anima sensitiva*; the vital force Van Helmont believed governed the body's unconscious reactions to stimuli.</note>. For how remarkably does an artery immediately harden under any kind of pain? This hardness does not prove a dryness of the artery (as the **Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional Galenic and Aristotelian medical authorities of the university system, whom Van Helmont frequently challenged.</note> believe), but rather its tension, or a singular **spasm**. Because of this, when a sweat follows, the artery displays a relaxation of that contraction along with softness. Otherwise, if the arteries were dried out and hardened, there would be as much possibility of re-moistening them as there is hope of taking away old age.

Has the contracted bladder not also frequently deceived the **Stone-cutters** <note>original: *Lithotomos*; surgeons who specialized in the manual extraction of bladder stones.</note>? Likewise, the <term>vessels of the prostate</term> <note>original: *parastata vasa*; here referring to the ducts associated with the male reproductive system</note> are contracted in **gonorrhea**, excited by the stimulus of the seed. <margin>134</margin> **The penis also**, having contracted inward, sometimes vanishes so completely that nothing remains visible except the foreskin. So too are there cramps of the muscles. And thus a woman in labor suffers her own immense contractions through intervals, whenever the womb wrinkles itself from the back so that it may expel the hidden burden. The **pubic bone** <note>original: *os pectinis*; the pectineal or pubic bone area</note> also, by a spontaneous contraction of its own, opens a passage for the coming fetus, with atrocious pain...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that many medical conditions, including windy colic and arterial hardening, are actually caused by active muscular contractions or spasms triggered by the "sensitive soul" rather than mechanical causes like trapped wind or dryness. He provides examples ranging from arterial tension to the contractions of the bladder and the uterus during childbirth.</summary>

<keywords>Duelech, windy colic, sensitive soul, arterial tension, spasm, lithotomy, bladder stone, labor contractions, Van Helmont, Galenic medicine</keywords>

<page-num>226</page-num>
-># ON THE FORMATION OF STONES<-

# ON THE FORMATION OF STONES

...pain. I have also seen, in **hysterical women** <note>In the 17th century, "hysteria" was a specific medical diagnosis believed to be caused by the womb (hystera) wandering or affecting the nervous system.</note>, tendons spontaneously leap from their natural positions, contracted with immense pain, and then return to their original place. I noted that when they had endured this more often, they complained of a milder pain. (Perhaps the **Schools** <note>The traditional medical authorities of the time, whose theories Van Helmont frequently challenged.</note> will recognize in this jumping of the nerves and wandering displacement a sudden emptying, a filling, or the presence of some virulent quality? And the sudden expulsion of these things?) It is also common in <term>nephritis</term> <note>Kidney disease or inflammation.</note> for the **ureters** to contract with the most ferocious pain, even when perhaps nothing is pressing on them except for very fine sand. I have always considered it a mark of unusual ignorance to claim (as the Schools do) that **winds** arise in the nerves and tendons, or are conceived in the nerves from the outside; they claim these winds are the causes of <margin>135</margin> **cramps** (which they therefore call "flatulent"), and even that they are removed almost at will. 

For the **sensitive spirit** <note>The vital force Van Helmont believed governed the body's unconscious reactions to stimuli.</note>, recoiling from pain, furiously contracts the veins, arteries, tendons, and membranes. And while it finds no hoped-for relief during such fits of rage, it provokes an increase in the pain. Thus, for instance, when a thorn is driven into a finger, as long as it hurts, it curls and hardens the artery; it makes the pulse hard—even though it wasn't noticeable there before—solely because of the tension of the contracted artery. For it is the nature of pain to pluck and contract. So much so, that the bone above the pubic area and in the loins contracts spontaneously in a woman giving birth, even though there is no muscle acting as a guide or motor. Indeed, pain, by its own nature, is a contractor of the limbs; and this happens by a natural movement, not at all by the will. This is seen most clearly in the lips of wounds; for they are painless as long as the lips remain flaccid and not contracted. 

The **Schools**, however, have overlooked these contractions in the nature of <margin>136</margin> pain, just as they have also overlooked the sensitive soul. They run instead toward "winds," the settling of waste humors, sharpness, harmony, and <term>deuteropathy</term> <note>A medical term for a "secondary suffering," where one part of the body is affected because of a primary disease in another part.</note> of parts—things which are entirely [beside] the mark of pain...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom "fus" likely begins "frustra" (in vain) or "fusus" (spread out) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain is an active contraction of the body's tissues triggered by the "sensitive spirit" rather than a passive result of "internal winds" or humors as traditional medicine claimed. He uses examples like childbirth, cramps, and kidney stones to show that the body's own reaction to irritation is what causes the most severe symptoms.</summary>

<header>C H A P T E R IX.</header>

## C H A P T E R IX.

...foreign winds <note>original: "flatus." Continuing from the previous page's discussion on "flatulent" cramps.</note>. Because they are only abstract names, and for the most part, do not contain the cause in the slightest point, as I have demonstrated in my treatise on the causes of disease, as if in a wrestling arena <note>original: "certaminis palestra"; a metaphor for a place of vigorous debate or exercise.</note>. For indeed, for the ureters (to use the terms of **Stone-formation** <term>Lithiasis</term> <note>The clinical formation of stones in the kidney or bladder.</note> as an example) there is no relationship, dependence of lordship, patronage, usurpation, possession, habit, or community of channels or waste products with the intestines or the stomach. For if, when the left side of the throat is in pain, even the right side is not immediately afflicted in such a narrow passage; why should we not rightly suspect the proximity and dependence of parts that are distinct and differing in their assigned duties and location? 

It is sufficient then for now that every pain, which is the author of contraction, presupposes a hateful guest <note>Van Helmont uses the term "hateful guest" to describe a foreign substance or irritant that the body's governing spirit—the Archeus—tries to expel or reacts to with hostility.</note>. To be sure, there are also painless contractions (as mentioned before regarding the scrotum), which take their origin not so much from pain as from mere discomfort. But painful contractions occur from hostile causes. For those things cause pain which strike the **sensitive spirit** <note>The "spiritus sensitivus" was Van Helmont's term for the unconscious vital force that feels and reacts to stimuli, distinct from the rational soul.</note>—so called because of its connection to the soul—with sharpness, bitterness, or degrees of heat or cold. 

<margin>157</margin> Moreover, the most intense pain is from fire; then from **alkalis** and **corrosives**, because they are closest to fire. Next, from harsh, bitter, and acid things, because they are closely related to contractions. Then soon after from salty things; then finally from sharp things, and at last from some bitter things. From poisons, however, as such, an immense pain arises—which is common in the plague—and because such great pain arises from poisons often without any actual sharpness <note>Meaning without physical acidity or caustic properties.</note>, the truth is revealed. Namely, that pain flows from the judgment of the sensitive soul. For **corrosives** <term>Corrosives</term> <note>Chemical substances that wear away or destroy tissues.</note>, since they erode the sensitive soul itself, consume the parts like fire. Moreover, **alum** <term>Alumen</term> <note>A chemical compound used as an astringent, which causes tissues to shrink or "curl."</note>, **vitriol** <term>Vitriolum</term> <note>Sulfuric acid or a metal sulfate, known for its burning and corrosive properties.</note>, **strong waters** <term>Aquæ fortes</term> <note>Nitric acid, a powerful solvent and corrosive.</note>, and finally **verjuice** <term>Omphacium</term> <note>The highly acidic juice of unripened grapes.</note>, as well as any sharp things, insofar as they curl the fibers of the organs by themselves, and con...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain and muscle contractions are the result of the "sensitive spirit" reacting to an irritant or "hateful guest." He rejects the idea that proximity of organs or "winds" cause these reactions, and he categorizes various chemical irritants by the intensity of pain they provoke.</summary>

<header>ON THE STONE-DISEASE <term>Lithiasi</term> <note>Lithiasis refers to the formation of stony concretions (stones) in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note></header>

...they tear. Hence, such wastes are convulsive and painful. There are also Alkalis <term>Alcalia</term> <note>Substances that neutralize acids. Van Helmont was a pioneer in identifying the chemical nature of digestion and disease as a struggle between acids and alkalis.</note> which soothe pains, certainly in those cases where they break down the greatest acidities of putrefaction. For indeed, under the dog-star <note>The "dog days" of summer (late July/August), associated in the ancient and early modern world with extreme heat and the spoiling of food.</note>, while meats threaten imminent corruption, the broths made from those meats become sour with an unpleasant taste. Whence in gout <term>podagra</term>, colic, and gnawing or putrefying ulcers, I first conceived that pains proceed from acid. 

Likewise, the sensitive soul <term>anima sensitiva</term> <note>The non-rational life-force that governs sensation and bodily reactions. Van Helmont believed this spirit, rather than mechanical pulleys, controlled the body.</note> feels pain first, which eventually, having become accustomed to it, grows less enraged—just as an unaccustomed horse recoils at the spurs. For nature in itself is entirely furious and prone to symptoms, but gradually, having grown accustomed to pains, it grows mild. Therefore, Self-love <term>Philautia</term> <note>From the Greek word for "self-love"; here referring to the biological instinct for self-preservation.</note> and revenge are prior to, or older than, sensation: because they are intimately present within the "seeds" <note>The "semina" are the internal blueprints or vital principles that Van Helmont believes direct the growth and function of all things.</note> in the lap of nature even before sensation [develops]. 

For the characters or images of anger, agony, revenge, sadness, and fear give birth to contractions equal to those very Ideas <note>In Helmontian medicine, an "Idea" is a mental or spiritual image formed by the internal spirit (the Archeus) that has immediate physical effects on the body.</note>. For by the same knowledge through which a mouse shudders at a cat it has never seen before, the provoked spirit is stirred into anger, dread, and so on; and this spirit, by its own "Idea"—as if by a brand—exerts its fury upon the limbs. 

1. The hand grows cold because the heat nurtured there by life is extinguished by the cold; but not because the vital spirit <term>spiritus vitalis</term> flees inward, abandoning the blood to which it had been wedded. And much less [does it grow cold] because heat, as a naked quality <note>A critique of the "Schools" (traditional Galenic/Aristotelian medicine), which treated heat and cold as abstract qualities that could move around independently of matter.</note>, migrates inward, departs, and returns as if in a comedy. 

2. Once the heat has been diminished and the cold persists, the cold grows strong; and then sensation in the hand is numbed. To be sure, the withdrawn sensitive spirits are compressed: specifically those which are in the nerves, but not those in the arteries. For the spirit has wedded the arterial blood more firmly, and it is the property of the veins, even after death, to preserve the gore from thickening <note>original: "concretionem"; the clotting or hardening of blood.</note>. For the vital spirit is sustained from behind by the nourishment of the heart as much as possible. And therefore, in that numbness, life is still maintained. 

3. Movement in the hand falters because...

<summary>Van Helmont argues that pain and bodily contractions are caused by "acidities" and the "sensitive soul's" emotional reactions (Ideas) rather than mechanical shifts of heat. He uses the example of a cold hand to show how the vital spirit remains tied to the blood even when sensation is numbed.</summary>

-> # CHAPTER IX. <-

The spirits condensed within the flesh, when they are not sufficiently warmed from behind by the heart, gradually perish and are gradually altered. 4. Then movement perishes at the same time, and sensation is extinguished, while clearly the blood outside the veins <note>Van Helmont refers to "extravasated" blood, or blood that has left its proper vessels.</note> threatens to clot; yet life still remains. 5. And so at last the limbs are totally deprived of life by the cold, while indeed the blood has already turned into clots and passed away. Therefore, in the aforementioned third and fourth degrees <note>Referring to stages of numbness or frostbite discussed previously.</note>, once the hand is warmed, pain emerges. For insofar as the heart from behind breathes in a new **sensitive spirit** <term>spiritum sensitivum</term> <note>In Van Helmont's system, this is the vital force that mediates between the soul and the physical body to produce feeling and movement.</note>, which, when it notices death about to arrive, as if angry in that very place, immediately constructs an **Idea** of its own indignation, and thus casts off its native sweetness or complacency. As I will demonstrate more clearly in the *Treatise on Morbid Ideas*, in the work *On the Rise of Medicine* <note>original: *Ortus Medicinae*, Van Helmont's major posthumous collection of works.</note>. Thus the sensitive spirit, which was not destroyed by the cold but repelled by compression, stirs up another **Idea** of its indignation upon its return, and another pain, like a stinging needle. Meanwhile, let the reader forgive me for having to borrow the name **icy poison** <term>veneni glacialis</term> <note>A metaphorical term for something that numbs or "freezes" the spirit's function without necessarily involving cold temperature.</note> without the necessity of preceding cold. For I do not call that poison "icy" as if it were chilled, as I already said regarding the numbness of the hands, but because it is "chilling"—at once numbing and removing sense and motion. Therefore, the similarity of the name takes its origin not from its root, but from its effect. And finally, in this digression <term>parergo</term>, as an epilogue to this work and to sensation, let us at least ponder the remedies of Physicians in **Apoplexy**, numbness, vertigo, catalepsy, **catochus** <note>An ancient term for a condition where a patient is conscious but unable to move or speak, similar to a trance.</note>, coma, contractions, and the **twitching** <term>vellicatione</term> of the eyelids, eyes, tongue, and lips. For you will find that **blood-letting** <term>venæ-sectionem</term> and an **enema** <term>enema</term> are immediately prescribed. They seem to wonder, forsooth, whether the dung of the anus twitches the tongue and lips in the mouth, or fashions sleeps and stupors in the sick man, just as it has produced stupidity and neglect in the Physician; or whether indeed these symptoms arise from the blood...

<meta>catchword: orian-</meta>

<summary>Van Helmont explains that pain and numbness arise when the "sensitive spirit" reacts to threats with "indignation," creating a mental "Idea" of suffering. He concludes by critiquing traditional physicians who use blood-letting and enemas to treat neurological symptoms like twitching or coma.</summary>

<page-num>230</page-num>

# CHAPTER IX. ON LITHIASIS <note>The formation of stony concretions in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note>

...should arise. Therefore, they immediately attempt both at the same time. On the following day, they then administer purgatives. And on the third day, they promote sweating after rubbings, filled with uncertainty. Their defenses are indeed only universal ones, because they lack others and are ignorant of specific remedies. Therefore, their "total" and "usual" treatments are merely generalities. Due to a lack of knowledge regarding efficient causes <term>efficient causes</term> <note>In the philosophy of the time, the "efficient cause" is the agent or force that actually brings something into being—in this case, the process that creates a disease.</note>, they wander only among the products <note>The "products" here are the physical symptoms or the stones themselves, rather than the internal process that created them.</note>; unconcerned with the radical maker <term>fabricator</term> <note>A term Van Helmont uses for the internal "Archeus" or vital spirit that, when disturbed, builds or "manufactures" a disease within the body.</note> and the prior cause, they are occupied only with removing the effect. 

It is not that they hope for the disease to return, leaving the root behind so they might pluck profit from it later (for I would not wish to suspect such a thing of an honest man); rather, they are too breathlessly intent on profit, and thus far have learned nothing from the Schools <term>the Schools</term> <note>Traditional medical universities based on the ancient teachings of Galen and Aristotle, which Van Helmont frequently criticized as being outdated and ineffective.</note> regarding the maker of diseases. Nor is it any wonder! For Medicine (as I said at the beginning, so I conclude again) is a gift of God. But He withdraws His gifts from those intent on profit, who do not once think of the mandate: 

> Be merciful, just as your Father, who is in heaven, is merciful. <note>original: "Estote misericordes, sicut Pater vester, qui est in coelis, est misericors." A quote from the Gospel of Luke 6:36.</note>

From Him every good and luminous gift descends. This is, therefore, what I have called the mournful modern tragedy of insensibility and pain, with a truly tragic outcome for the sick.

->THE END.<-

***

*The treatises of the most illustrious and expert Lord Jan Baptist van Helmont, in which he exactly investigates and demonstrates the principles of Lithiasis, Apoplexy, Paralysis, and other diseases, are worthy to be published by the press. So I judge.*

->**P. Coens, Licentiate of Sacred Theology, Canon**<-
->**and Censor of Books for Antwerp.**<-

<summary>Van Helmont concludes his chapter by criticizing physicians who treat only the symptoms of stone-disease and profit from patients without understanding the divine nature of healing. The page includes the official ecclesiastical approval for the publication of his works on lithiasis, apoplexy, and paralysis.</summary>

<keywords>lithiasis, kidney stones, medical ethics, divine gift of healing, the Schools, efficient causes, censorship, Antwerp, Jan Baptist van Helmont</keywords>

--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page contains the "Errata" (list of corrections) for Jan Baptista van Helmont’s treatise "De Lithiasi" (On Lithiasis), following the conclusion of his arguments in Chapter IX. This is a common feature in 17th-century printing where the author or printer corrects typographical errors found after the main text was set.</meta>

# CORRECTIONS
## On Lithiasis <note>The medical study of stone formation in the kidneys or bladder.</note>

**PAGE 9.** line 5. for *lege* read **luge** <note>original: "lege... luge"; a shift from "read" to "mourn".</note>. l. 21. for *for* twice read **for these**. **p. 11.** l. 9. for *they will fall* read **let them fall**. **p. 13.** l. 29. for *oolias* read **Ooliab** <note>A biblical craftsman mentioned in Exodus; Van Helmont often uses biblical metaphors for the "internal craftsmen" of the body.</note>. **p. 26.** l. 13. for *of efficiency* read **efficacy**. **p. 35.** l. 1. for *themselves* (masculine) read **themselves** (feminine). l. 25. for *thing* read **kidney** <note>original: "rem... ren"; a significant medical correction.</note>. l. 28. for *they are sought out* read **they complain**. **p. 41.** l. 25. for *uromancy* read **uronomancy** <note>Divination or diagnosis by inspecting urine.</note>. **p. 48.** l. 27. for *uniting* read **living**. **p. 49.** l. 10. for *from others* read **others**. **p. 53.** l. 28. for *changed* read **unchanged**. l. 36. for *thievish* read **voracious**. **p. 56.** l. 27. for *Cathecamenis* read **catechumens** <note>Students or converts receiving basic religious instruction.</note>. **p. 57.** l. 33. for *be turned* read **be converted**. **p. 58.** l. 3. for *that* (feminine) read **that** (masculine). l. 25. for *breath-like* read **vaporous**. **p. 60.** l. 2. for *When* read **Then**. **p. 67.** l. 16. for *at the same time at the same time* read **at the same time**. **p. 73.** l. 24. for *wars* read **beasts**. **p. 75.** l. 6. for *strength* read **redness**. **p. 79.** l. 3. for *of great* read **of great**. **p. 86.** l. 31. for *ash* read **gore**. **p. 88.** l. 32. for *threatened* (feminine) read **threatened** (masculine). **p. 89.** l. 4. for *filth* read **filth** <note>spelling correction from "squalores" to "squallores".</note>. **p. 90.** l. 27. for *Tormentor* read **of the Tormentor**. **p. 99.** l. 35. for *urine* read **sand** <note>original: "urina... arena"; this is vital to Van Helmont's theory that "sand" (not just urine) is the material cause of stones.</note>. **p. 101.** l. 21. for *places* read **of the place**. **p. 104.** l. 3. for *urino* read **urine**. **p. 105.** l. 6. for *icy* read **gravelly** <note>original: "glacialem... glarialem".</note>. **p. 107.** l. 19. for *nourished* read **is nourished**. **p. 116.** l. 24. for *he preferred* read **they would prefer**. **p. 121.** l. 16. for *of membranes* read **of limbs**. **p. 122.** l. 27. for *shaped* read **confirmed**. **p. 125.** l. 20. for *infinite things* read **infinite times**. **p. 126.** l. 9. for *bruised* read **bruised**. **p. 127.** l. 17. for *through steps* read **not through steps**. **p. 133.** l. 4. for *Let them be* read **They are**. l. 19. for *dissect* read **burst apart**. **p. 134.** l. 3. for *discover* read **to be discovered**. **p. 138.** l. 17. for *extreme* read **external**. **p. 141.** l. 3. for *thin* read **thin**. **p. 147.** l. 18. for *god* read **for a long time**. l. 29. for *commission* read **commission**. **p. 148.** l. 3. for *what those eyes* read **because he with eyes**. l. 19. for *practeas* read **bracteas** <note>thin plates or scales.</note>. **p. 150.** l. 12. for *but let it be vitrified* read **or let it be vitrified**. **p. 161.** l. 18. for *it ought* read **it may ache**. l. 19. for *with pain* read **to be in pain**. **p. 164.** l. 29. for *ruby* read **of a ruby**. **p. 168.** l. 24. for *hours* read **an hour**. **p. 171.** l. 16. for *Syndesmion* read **Syndesmon** <note>A Greek-derived term for a ligament or bond.</note>. **p. 172.** l. 19. for *ultim* read **ultima**. **page 219.** line 8. for *discovered* (masculine) read **discovered** (feminine). **page 221.** line 26. for *they were* read **let them cease**. **page 123 <note>Likely a typo for 223</note>.** line 14. for *& if* read **even if**. line 21. for *muscles* read **muscle**.
